### AREA: Direct competitor landing page teardown (competitors)
FINDINGS:
  - METHOD NOTE FIRST: markdown-extraction (WebFetch) got the light/dark theme WRONG on 4 of 5 sites I cross-checked in a real browser — it called Sonar 'light' (actually #031426 navy), Cursor Bugbot 'light' (actually #14120B), cubic 'light' (actually #060707), Qodo ambiguous (actually #111013). Every colour/theme claim below marked as browser-verified came from getComputedStyle in a rendered page, not from markup guessing.
  - CodeRabbit's base is oklch(0.177 0.0084 305) — a PURPLE-tinted near-black, computed as lab(4.9349 1.26021 -1.99696). Their whole neutral ramp is Radix's 'mauve' relabelled: --color-cui-mauve-1 #121014 → mauve-6 #3C3841 → mauve-11 #B5B2B9 → mauve-12 #EFEDF0, with a light-mode pair for all 12 steps. The category's three darks are mutually distinct and all taken: CodeRabbit purple-black #121014, Graphite pure-neutral #0A0A0A (lab(2.75381 0 0), zero hue), Cursor warm olive-black #14120B. A BLUE-tinted near-black (hue ~265) is unoccupied.
  - CodeRabbit's hero 3D is FAKE and it is the best trick in the category: a conic-gradient rim light over flat panels — `conic-gradient(from 0deg at 0 0, transparent 0deg, transparent 102deg, var(--home-hero-rim-soft) 114deg, var(--home-hero-rim-core) 135deg, var(--home-hero-rim-strong)...)`. The rim tokens are barely-there whites: --home-hero-inner-rim-core #efedf01f (12%), --rim-strong #efedf00e (5.5%), --rim-soft #efedf008 (3%), --rim-falloff #b5b2b905 (2%). No WebGL in the hero. This is how you get 'cinematic/3D' at 60fps on hotel wifi.
  - CodeRabbit's motion vocabulary is quantised and that is why it reads expensive: ONE expo-out easing cubic-bezier(.23,1,.32,1) used 11 times, then (.22,1,.36,1) ×8, (.16,1,.3,1) ×6, (.2,0,0,1) ×5. Durations are .28s / .48s / .62s / .72s / .82s with an 80ms stagger. Radii: --radius-xs .125rem, --radius .5rem, --radius-xl .75rem, --radius-2xl 1rem. Copy the discipline, not the look.
  - CodeRabbit's hero diff colours are lifted verbatim from GitHub's dark theme and then re-tinted: add bg #122B26 / text #2DE3A1, delete bg #44171B / text #FF6256, plus literal GitHub tokens --home-hero-github-accent #58A6FF, -success #3FB950, -attention #D29922, -danger #F85149. Every competitor does this. Diff-green/diff-red is the category's entire visual language.
  - ito.ai IS PRGuard's M1, already shipped and already marketed. Title: 'Runtime Code Review That Runs Your App on Every PR'. H1 'Catch all runtime bugs before merge.' Subhead: 'Ito is the only AI code review tool that builds your app on every PR, runs the impacted flows, and attaches the failing test to anything that breaks.' Their 3-up is literally 'Catch runtime errors no one else can / Detailed proof on every finding / Removes the worst 3 hours of your week', and they have a section headed 'The problem: review without execution' vs 'The solution: execution as infrastructure'. They run programmatic /vs-coderabbit, /vs-greptile, /vs-cursor-bugbot, /vs-qodo, /vs-mabl, /vs-claude-code-review pages.
  - BUT ito.ai does NOT do the thing that makes PRGuard defensible: they run whole-app E2E flows and attach video+logs; they never publish a DISPROVEN finding, never compute a deterministic severity, have no fingerprint/suppression ledger and no audit chain. Their proof is 'we ran your app and it broke'. PRGuard's proof is 'passes at base SHA, fails at head SHA — and when it passes at head we publicly refute ourselves'. The base/head differential and the self-refutation are the unoccupied ground.
  - Qodo is the closest POSITIONING collision, not ito: H1 'Code review for agents. Governance for humans.' (Figtree 72px/500/-1.44px, #111013 base, #7968FA purple). Their section list includes 'The governance layer to keep codebases healthy' with sub-cards literally named 'Risk intelligence', 'Audit trail', 'Repo relationship mapping', 'System of record'. PRGuard's M4 language must NOT be generic 'governance' — it must be the hash chain (event_hash = sha256(prev_hash + canonical_json), chain.verified, broken_at_seq), which Qodo does not have.
  - Graphite has RETIRED the Diamond brand. diamond.graphite.dev 301s to graphite.com/features/ai-reviews, whose H1 is 'AI Reviews - Instant AI code review.' and subhead 'Graphite Chat finds critical bugs and security issues before they reach production.' graphite.dev also 301s to graphite.com. Any competitive deck still saying 'Graphite Diamond' is stale.
  - Two named competitors have LEFT the category. CodeAnt.ai is now 'Autonomous Preventive Security Platform' (pentest product, CTAs 'START PENTEST'/'BOOK A DEMO'). Bito.ai is now 'Cut your agent bill in half' — a model router. Entelligence.ai is now 'Cost and quality optimization engine for engineering agents'. The AI-code-review middle is collapsing into either governance (Qodo/CodeRabbit) or cost-routing.
  - Font map — and Geist is BURNED: CodeRabbit = Geist + Lato + 'hack' mono; cubic = Geist + Fragment Mono; Snyk = Geist. Three of the biggest sites share one free typeface. The differentiated choices are Greptile's Anybody (variable, OFL, 96px/700/-2.4px, blur-in reveal), ito's fontRepro (ABC Repro, Dinamo, commercial), Graphite's matterFont (Matter, Displaay, commercial), Cursor's CursorGothic (proprietary), and Sonar's Sentient — the only SERIF H1 in the entire category, 57.3px/400, free under the ITF licence.
  - H1 typography converges hard: 56–96px, weight 400–700, letter-spacing -0.02em to -0.03em. Exact reads: Greptile 96/700/-2.4px, Qodo 72/500/-1.44px, ito 64/400/-1.92px, Snyk 64/700, Graphite 60/500/-1.5px, Sonar 57.3/400, DeepSource 56/700/-1.4px, Cursor 36/400/-0.72px. Cursor at 36px/400 is the outlier and reads the most confident thing in the set.
  - Page-length spread is a positioning tell: Cursor Bugbot 5,080px (shortest, most confident), DeepSource 8,056px, Graphite 8,569px, ito 9,241px, Greptile 9,311px, Qodo 11,874px, cubic 14,570px (longest, and its H1 is 29px at weight 200 — invisible hierarchy). Median ≈ 8,500px.
  - Greptile is the only LIGHT hero among the serious players: base #E9E9E9 with exactly ONE dark inset section at #3D3B4F (a 1,927px pinned block, 'How Greptile reviews every PR'). Accents are acid lime #DAFF01 / #ECFFA3, pink #FFCFFE, mint #28E99F / #C5FFD6, plus an emerald grid overlay at --grid-color #10b9810d and #10b9811f. Animation is Rive (5 canvases) + named Lottie files: /features/agent/continuous-learning.lottie, full-codebase-context.lottie, swarm-agents.lottie, /greptile-lizard.lottie.
  - Greptile's page renders as a BLANK GREY RECTANGLE under fast scrolling — every section below the hero is IntersectionObserver-gated and did not fire across repeated programmatic scrolls with 2s waits. If a hackathon judge scrolls quickly, they see nothing. Do not gate primary content behind scroll reveal.
  - CodeRabbit's 'trusted by' band sits on a 1439×1088 canvas-2D force-directed code graph (class `trusted-force-graph trusted-force-graph--codegraph`), and 'Fast code. Faster chaos.' sits on a separate `FastCodeChaos gridShimmerBackdrop` canvas. Their bespoke CSS-module keyframes are named: HomeHero (stage-scene-in, studio-landing-reveal, screen-landing-reveal, shadow-body-landing-reveal, review-card-enter, review-stack-panel-enter), AsciiRabbit (flip-a/flip-b, 1.6s step-end), CodeRabbitColorBar (color-bar-travel, 18s linear infinite), ROIAnimatedDiagrams (draw-cost-arrow, draw-merge-line, reveal-arrow-head, dot-column), PriorityQueuePreview (priority-metric-bar-fill).
  - EVERY SINGLE SITE visualises code review as a fake GitHub PR comment or a unified diff. CodeRabbit: 'coderabbitai [bot] commented 2 minutes ago' + '⚠ Potential issue | ● Major' + hunk `@@ -42,2 +42,3 @@ export async function createSession`. Cursor: a macOS-chrome window titled 'GitHub Pull Request' with red/green hunks. GitHub Copilot: a PR mockup with the reviewer dropdown. DeepSource, Graphite, Qodo, Greptile, Korbit: same. ZERO of 18 sites show a terminal executing a test. That is the open lane.
  - Pricing is monotonous: per-seat/month, 3–4 tiers, last one 'Custom', annual toggle at −20%. CodeRabbit $24/$48/$72 annual (Essentials/Team/Advanced) + $0.40/agent-minute; Graphite $0/$20/$40; Greptile $0/$30 + $1 per extra credit (TREX review = 3 credits, standard = 1); Qodo $30/mo base + $0.012/credit; cubic $40/$99 on /pricing; Korbit $15/$24 monthly. cubic's homepage advertises $30/$79 while /pricing says $40/$99 — a live inconsistency on the site of the company claiming '#1 on every independent benchmark'.

RECOMMENDATIONS:
  - (high) Make the hero a LIVE TERMINAL running the repro test at base SHA then head SHA — not a fake GitHub PR comment. Three-column hero: (1) the claim, (2) a real monospace terminal that types `git checkout <base_sha> && pytest tests/test_repro_null_deref.py` → green PASS, then `git checkout <head_sha>` → red FAIL with the actual stack trace, (3) a PROVEN verdict chip. Autoplay on load, loop, ~14s cycle. | WHY: All 18 sites visualise review as a PR comment or a unified diff. Zero show a terminal executing anything. This is the only genuinely unoccupied hero visual in the category, and it is the literal rendering of M1 and of the stage moment ('Tool A says this MIGHT null-deref. We say it DOES'). It also means your marketing hero and your dashboard's proof pane are the same component. | TRADEOFF: Terminal-as-hero risks reading 'developer blog' rather than 'expensive product' if typeset badly. Mitigate with a proper window chrome, generous padding (32px), a rim-lit panel, and a large mono at 14–15px with 1.65 line-height — not a raw <pre>. Also: a looping typed animation costs ~1.5s before the payoff lands, which fights the 3-second rule; solve it by having the FAIL state pre-rendered on first paint and the animation replaying after.
  - (high) Base on a BLUE-tinted near-black at oklch(0.145 0.012 265) with a neutral ramp at chroma 0.012 hue 265, and take a cold instrument teal oklch(0.78 0.16 168) as PROVEN plus a deliberate amber oklch(0.70 0.19 35) as DISPROVEN. Define all 12 steps as tokens on :root before writing any component. | WHY: The three occupied darks are CodeRabbit's purple-black #121014, Graphite's zero-hue #0A0A0A and Cursor's warm #14120B. A cold blue-black is measurably unoccupied. On accents: CodeRabbit owns orange #FF570A + mint #46E1A5, Greptile owns acid lime #DAFF01 + pink #FFCFFE, cubic orange #F26625, Qodo purple #7968FA, Snyk purple/magenta, Sonar blue. A high-chroma cold teal at high lightness is free, reads as instrumentation rather than 'AI', and pairs with amber to make proven/disproven a COLOUR SYSTEM rather than two labels. | TRADEOFF: Teal-green sits near CodeRabbit's #46E1A5 at a glance; you must push lightness up (0.78 vs their ~0.82 but colder hue 168 vs ~160) and never use it on large fills, only on 1px borders, text and 2px status rails, or you will look like a CodeRabbit skin. Avoid green=good/red=bad for diffs — you will inherit the category's GitHub palette. Use teal=proven / amber=disproven / neutral=inconclusive instead, and say so in a legend.
  - (high) Put a 'WE WERE WRONG' section ABOVE the logo wall — a real disproven finding with its terminal output showing the test passing at head, and the live disproven_rate from GET /v1/accuracy with its n and its caveat printed. | WHY: Not one of the 18 sites shows its own tool failing, publishes a false-positive rate with an n, or refutes a finding. cubic claims '#1 on every independent benchmark' without rendering the benchmark; DeepSource claims 'Highest F1 score' as a bare assertion. Self-refutation is uncopyable by anyone whose architecture cannot execute, which is everyone except ito.ai — and ito never publishes its own misses. Placing it above the logo wall also solves your real problem: you have no customer logos, and this section is stronger proof than logos would be. | TRADEOFF: Publishing a disproven_rate invites a judge to ask 'so your tool is wrong X% of the time?'. Pre-empt it in the copy: the number is the rate at which the system CAUGHT ITSELF before shipping the claim, which is the opposite of a defect. Requires the /v1/accuracy caveats to be rendered, not hidden behind a tooltip — half-disclosing is worse than not disclosing.
  - (high) Build the 9-stage pipeline as a LIVE state machine driven by a recorded real run's JSON (stage_index / stage_total / stage_label / heartbeat), not a static diagram. Show the 4 specialists fanning out in parallel at S3 and reconverging at S4, with the heartbeat text ticking 'Stage 3/9 — specialists, 12s since last output'. Ship the identical component in the dashboard's run view. | WHY: Sonar and Qodo both ship static circular loop diagrams; nobody in the category animates a named-stage pipeline with live state. This is the animated architecture diagram the owner asked for, it is honest (real timings), and building it once for two surfaces is the highest-leverage component in the project. The fan-out at S3 is also the only place the parallel-specialist architecture becomes visible. | TRADEOFF: A 9-node rail is wide; at phone width it must reflow to vertical, which loses the parallel-fan-out reading — accept a stacked variant with the four specialists as an indented group. Driving from recorded JSON risks looking canned if the timings are too clean; keep the real jitter and the real degradation events.
  - (medium) Render M2 as a horizontal stacked CONTRIBUTION BAR (each input's value × weight as a segment summing to the score, with band thresholds .15/.35/.55/.75 marked on the axis), not a radar chart. Print the human sentence under it in mono. | WHY: Radar charts read as 'analytics dashboard template' and make six values hard to compare against fixed weights. A stacked bar makes the weighting visible — a viewer can see that path_sensitivity (.25) is the biggest lever and churn (.10) the smallest — which is the actual argument: severity is arithmetic, not an adjective. The threshold ticks let someone see WHY it landed in 'critical' rather than 'high'. | TRADEOFF: Six segments in one bar needs six distinguishable colours, which fights the disciplined two-accent palette. Solve it with a single-hue lightness ramp (all teal, six steps of L) plus labels on hover, rather than six hues. You lose the instant 'shape' recognition a radar gives across many findings — add a tiny 6-segment sparkline as the list-view glyph if you need at-a-glance comparison.
  - (medium) Do NOT use Geist. Pick a display face nobody in this category uses and pair it with a mono that carries the brand, since your entire story is terminal output. Set the H1 at 64–72px, weight 500, letter-spacing -0.03em. | WHY: CodeRabbit, cubic and Snyk all ship Geist — three of the category's most visible sites on one free typeface. Meanwhile the sites that read expensive bought type: Graphite (Matter/Displaay), ito (ABC Repro/Dinamo), Cursor (CursorGothic, proprietary). Sonar gets differentiation free by using Sentient, a serif, under the ITF licence. -0.03em (ito's setting) sits tighter than the category's -0.02/-0.025em cluster and reads more editorial. | TRADEOFF: Commercial type is a real cost and a real licensing risk for a hackathon; free-but-distinctive is the right trade. Whatever you pick, VERIFY the licence on the foundry's own page before shipping — I did not verify licences for the specific alternatives worth considering, and a wrong licence claim is worse than a boring font. Also: a serif H1 (the Sonar move) is the single fastest way to look different here, but it fights 'tech-savvy/cinematic' and I would not do it for PRGuard.
  - (high) Target 6,000–7,000px total page height and seven sections maximum. Order: hero terminal → the one-sentence positioning → 'we were wrong' / disproven → 9-stage live pipeline → severity contribution bar → audit chain + never-approve → closing CTA. | WHY: Cursor Bugbot is 5,080px and reads the most confident page in the category; cubic is 14,570px with a 29px weight-200 H1 and reads the least. Category median is ~8,500px. At a hackathon a judge scrolls once — length is a liability, and every section after the fourth dilutes the four mechanics. Seven sections maps exactly to M1–M4 plus hero, positioning and CTA. | TRADEOFF: A short page leaves out the security/compliance block and the FAQ that enterprise buyers look for — acceptable for a hackathon, not for a real launch. Keep them as linked sub-pages so the information exists without costing hero-to-CTA distance.
  - (high) Never gate primary content behind IntersectionObserver scroll-reveal. Render everything at full opacity by default; enhance with motion only where it already-visible content can afford to lose. | WHY: Greptile's page returned a blank #E9E9E9 rectangle across repeated programmatic scrolls with 2-second waits — every section below the hero is reveal-gated and did not fire. A judge who scrubs the scrollbar sees an empty page. This is a self-inflicted wound on an otherwise strong site. | TRADEOFF: You lose the staged-reveal choreography that makes a page feel authored. Get it back with CSS-only entrance animations that run once on load for above-the-fold content and `animation-timeline: view()` where supported, with a no-op fallback — never opacity:0 as the default state in the stylesheet.
  - (high) Replace the logo wall with a VERIFIABILITY WALL: a row of hash-chained audit event cards showing event_hash = sha256(prev_hash + canonical_json), the chain.verified flag, and a control that lets the viewer recompute one hash themselves. | WHY: Every competitor's credibility slot under the hero is customer logos (CodeRabbit's NVIDIA/Adyen/BMW row, Greptile's 20,000+ teams, Qodo's Intel/Walmart/Box carousel). You have none, and a thin or fake logo row is instantly read as a student project. A tamper-evident chain the viewer can verify is the one credibility artifact that does not require customers — and no competitor has it, including Qodo whose 'Audit trail' card has no mechanism behind it. | TRADEOFF: Hash chains are abstract and can read as cryptographic theatre. It only lands if the viewer can actually break it — include a control that mutates one event and shows chain.verified flip to false with broken_at_seq populated. That interaction is the whole point; without it this is just a list of hex strings.
  - (high) Get the 'cinematic 3D' look from CSS conic-gradient rim lighting on flat panels, not from Three.js or WebGL. Copy CodeRabbit's exact technique at their exact opacities. | WHY: CodeRabbit's hero — the most expensive-looking thing in the category — has NO WebGL in it. It is flat panels with a conic-gradient rim light at 2–12% white plus a 1px inner highlight. Their only canvases are elsewhere (a 1439×1088 canvas-2D force graph behind the trust band, and a grid-shimmer backdrop). A WebGL scene that drops frames on a demo laptop or a projector will actively cost you the judging moment; CSS gradients will not. | TRADEOFF: Pure CSS caps how far the 3D illusion goes — you get lit panels and depth, not parallax geometry or a rotating object. Given the owner's stated 3D/realistic preference this may feel like under-delivering; counter by spending the saved effort on the terminal and pipeline animations, which carry actual product meaning rather than decoration.
  - (high) Adopt one quantised motion vocabulary and enforce it: entrances on cubic-bezier(.23,1,.32,1) at 280/480/620/820ms with an 80ms stagger; exits on cubic-bezier(.4,0,1,1) at 200ms; honour prefers-reduced-motion by disabling transforms and keeping opacity. | WHY: This is directly measured from CodeRabbit's stylesheet and it is the mechanical reason their site reads as designed rather than assembled — one easing appears 11 times, durations land on five values, staggers are all 80ms. Sites that read cheap (CodeAnt, Korbit) have no such consistency. This is free to adopt and is the highest ratio of perceived polish to effort available. | TRADEOFF: Quantised timing can feel mechanical on genuinely playful moments; allow exactly one exception (the terminal typing cadence, which should have human jitter) and keep everything else on the grid.
  - (medium) Visualise S8's comment ladder (inline_exact → inline_nearest → file_level → summary_only) and the gate ladder (check_run → commit_status → dry_run) as lit/dimmed rails showing which rung actually fired and why the ones above it were skipped. | WHY: No competitor visualises graceful degradation at all. Showing the fallback path — and that nothing was silently dropped — is a trust artifact that costs almost nothing to render and directly dramatises 'never silently dropped'. It also gives the dashboard a distinctive surface that is not another table. | TRADEOFF: It only reads as meaningful if the demo actually degrades at least once; a run where every ladder hits rung one shows four lit steps and no story. Seed the demo data with one inline_nearest and one commit_status fallback so the mechanism is visible.
  - (low) If you show pricing at all, do not price per-seat per-month. Price on proven findings delivered, or omit the page entirely for the hackathon. | WHY: Every single competitor is $12–$99 per seat per month with 3–4 tiers, a 'Custom' last column and a −20% annual toggle. Matching that structure silently concedes that you are the same kind of product. Pricing on PROVEN findings is the only pricing axis that restates the positioning ('you pay for proof, not opinions') and it is consistent with 'only proven findings can block'. | TRADEOFF: Outcome-based pricing raises immediate objections (gaming the count, unpredictable bills) that you cannot answer in a hackathon demo. Omitting pricing entirely is the safer play and is what GitHub Copilot's code review page does. Only ship a pricing page if you are prepared to defend the axis on stage.
ANTI-PATTERNS / AVOID:
  - The perspective grid floor / Tron horizon receding under the hero text. Qodo ships it in 2026. It was tired in 2022 and it now reads as 'we bought a template'.
  - The #7968FA-to-#0693E3 purple-to-blue gradient on near-black. This is the default AI-SaaS palette and at least four sites in this category are inside a few degrees of hue of each other. Using purple here makes you invisible.
  - Concentric radial glow rings behind the hero headline (Snyk). Gradient-filled words inside an otherwise white H1 ('Securely' in orange-to-red) is the same crime — it dates the page to the 2023 gradient-text era.
  - Starfield of 1px white dots as a dark-page background (Graphite). It adds nothing, costs paint, and reads as a placeholder texture.
  - Animated GIFs as the product demo (Korbit). Banded colour, no loop control, enormous file size. In 2026 this is the clearest possible signal that nobody on the team owns design.
  - Framer templates with five or more font families on one page. CodeAnt ships Nunito Sans, IBM Plex Mono, Mona Sans, Plus Jakarta Sans, Geist Mono, Gochi Hand, Fragment Mono AND Inter simultaneously. cubic is also Framer-built and its H1 is 29px at weight 200 with positive tracking.
  - Shipping the untouched Tailwind / Untitled-UI gray ramp as your brand (#111827, #6B7280, #9CA3AF, #E5E7EB, #F9FAFB, #344054, #98A2B3). CodeAnt does exactly this. It is the visual equivalent of shipping default Bootstrap.
  - Using Geist. It is excellent and it is now the uniform — CodeRabbit, cubic and Snyk all ship it. Same for Inter as a display face.
  - Mascots on a governance product. Qodo's skateboarding character and Greptile's lizard Lottie are charming and they actively undercut 'keeps the human in charge on the record'. You cannot be cute and be the system of record.
  - Cookie-consent walls and OneTrust overlays that fire before the hero finishes animating (Snyk, Sonar). You get one first impression and they spend it on a modal.
  - The drop-shadowed browser-chrome screenshot floating on a gradient. Nine of these sites do some version of it. Glassmorphism and frosted-blur cards belong in the same bin.
  - Asserting a benchmark win without rendering the benchmark. cubic's site-wide banner says '#1 AI code reviewer on every independent benchmark' and DeepSource claims the 'Highest F1 score on the OpenSSF CVE Benchmark' — neither shows the chart, the n, or the date. For a product whose entire thesis is PROOF, an unbacked number on your own page is self-refuting.
  - Investor logos presented as social proof (Korbit shows eight VC firms). It tells a buyer that no customer would agree to be named.
  - Gating every section below the fold behind IntersectionObserver so a fast scroller sees a blank page (Greptile). Actively dangerous at a hackathon where judges scrub.
  - Pricing that contradicts itself across two pages of the same site (cubic: $30/$79 on the homepage, $40/$99 on /pricing).
  - The mandatory closing stack — 3-or-4-up icon grid, testimonial carousel, SOC 2 badge block, FAQ accordion, repeat-the-hero CTA — in that exact order. All 18 sites. Shipping it signals that you filled in a template rather than made an argument.
  - Per-seat-per-month, three tiers, last one 'Custom', −20% annual toggle. Universal in this category. Copying the pricing structure concedes the category framing.
  - Using GitHub's dark-theme diff palette verbatim (#3FB950 / #F85149 / #58A6FF / #D29922, add #122B26 delete #44171B). Everyone does it, so 'green good / red bad' carries zero brand. For PRGuard it is worse than neutral: it collides with proven/disproven, which is the distinction that actually matters.
  - SPAs that render an empty <body> without JS (baz.ai) and vanity domains that are 2.8KB redirect stubs (baz.co). Both are visible to anyone who looks.
  - Single 'Request a Demo' CTA with no self-serve path (Korbit). In a developer-tools category where CodeRabbit, Greptile, cubic, Graphite and Cursor all offer a free tier, demo-only reads as either enterprise cosplay or a product that cannot be shown.
FACT-CHECK CORRECTIONS:
    [UNVERIFIABLE] CodeRabbit's hero does not use WebGL; earlier 'webgl: true' was a self-inflicted false positive from calling getContext('webgl2') -> Cannot re-test canvas context types without a rendered browser this session. The researcher's self-correction is methodologically sound (calling getContext does create a context) and is the safe read — do not claim CodeRabbit uses WebGL. Partial corroboration: cubic-bezier(0.23,1,0.32,1) appears 10 times in CodeRabbit's served HTML, consistent with a CSS-driven rather than GPU-driven aesthetic.
    [CORRECTED] Graphite's 'matterFont' is Matter by Displaay Type Foundry — inferred from the name only; Matter is commercial, exact price not confirmed -> Matter by Displaay Type Foundry is confirmed real: a sans-serif grotesque first drawn June 2015, released Aug 2016, and it IS commercial. Exact prices now confirmed and were NOT before: EUR 55 per single upright or italic style; EUR 242-352 for uprights-only or complete family packages; EUR 1,189 personal / EUR 5,280 commercial for the complete collection; volume discounts 20-50%. The Graphite-CSS-token-to-Displaay-Matter mapping itself remains name inference only and is still unconfirmed.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] 'CursorGothic' is proprietary/custom to Cursor — inferred from the name only -> No foundry page, licence page or font-file metadata found for a typeface named CursorGothic. Treat as unconfirmed. Do not state it as fact in a deck; 'Cursor ships a custom-named face' is the defensible phrasing.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] DeepSource's font stack begins with 'Google Sans'; unverified whether it loads or falls through, and no information on licensing -> Could not confirm font loading or licence status. The researcher's instruction not to repeat this as an accusation is correct and should be followed. MATERIAL CONTEXT THAT MAKES IT MOOT: DeepSource announced on 9 September 2026 that it is joining Harness — see the separate verdict on the acquisition.
    [CORRECTED] Font licences NOT verified and appearing only in recommendations: JetBrains Mono, Berkeley Mono (and any price), Instrument Sans, Bricolage Grotesque — treat all licence/price statements as unverified -> Three of the four are now verified and all three are free/OFL, so the warning can be relaxed for them. JetBrains Mono: typeface under OFL-1.1 (free for commercial and non-commercial use), source code under Apache 2.0. Instrument Sans: 'Copyright 2022 The Instrument Sans Project Authors' under SIL OFL v1.1. Bricolage Grotesque: 'Copyright 2022 The Bricolage Grotesque Project Authors' under SIL OFL v1.1. Berkeley Mono REMAINS UNVERIFIED: berkeleygraphics.com now 301-redirects to usgraphics.com and that site returns HTTP 403 to automated fetches, so no price could be confirmed — it is a paid commercial face and the researcher's warning stands for this one only.
    [REFUTED] cubic pricing is a 'live contradiction': homepage reads Free/$30/$79/Custom while /pricing reads Team $40 and Pro $99 — one view may be stale -> Not a contradiction and nothing is stale. The two views are the same prices at different billing periods, and the homepage states both explicitly: Starter Free; Team '$30/month (billed annually) or $40/month per developer'; Pro '$79/month (billed annually) or $99/month per developer'; Enterprise Custom. /pricing defaults to the monthly view ($40 and $99, 'billed monthly') with a 'Yearly -20%' toggle. The only real wrinkle is cubic's own arithmetic: $40 to $30 is a 25% discount, not 20%, while $99 to $79 is ~20%. Drop the 'contradiction' framing entirely — asserting it on stage would be a factual error about a competitor.
    [CORRECTED] Vendor self-reported metrics repeated verbatim: CodeRabbit '17K customers / 6M repositories / most installed AI app on GitHub+GitLab'; Greptile 'OVER 20,000+ TEAMS'; Sonar '7M developers / 75% Fortune 100'; Korbit '22K developers / 331M+ lines / 661K+ PRs / 37K+ bugs'; ito '81% / 50% / 10x'; cubic '#1 on every independent benchmark'; DeepSource 'Highest F1 on OpenSSF CVE Benchmark'; CodeAnt 'Gartner Cool Vendor 2026'; Entelligence 'MindsDB 43% / Clodo 77% / Hobbes 59% / -43% per merged PR' -> Nearly all confirmed as claims the sites actually make, with two corrections and one gap. CORRECTION 1 — Greptile says 'Over 22,000+ teams use Greptile', not 20,000+. The researcher repeats 20,000+ twice (claim list and recommendation 9 rationale); both need fixing. CORRECTION 2 — CodeRabbit's page says 'Most installed AI App on GitHub'; GitLab is not part of the rendered claim. GAP — Entelligence's '-43% per merged PR in 12 weeks' did not appear in my extraction; the other three (MindsDB 43% bugs resolved, Clodo 77% findings fixed, Hobbes 59% comment action rate) are confirmed with named executives attached. Confirmed verbatim: CodeRabbit 17K customers / 6M repositories; Sonar 'over 7 million developers' and '75% of the Fortune 100'; Korbit 22K / 331M+ / 661K+ / 37K+; ito 81% / 50% / 10x; cubic banner '#1 AI code reviewer on every independent benchmark'; CodeAnt Gartner Cool Vendor 2026 + SOC 2 Type II + HIPAA; DeepSource 'Highest F1 score on the OpenSSF CVE Benchmark' with a specific 84.51% F1 figure the researcher did not capture. INDEPENDENT DATA POINT: CodeRabbit's GitHub Marketplace listing reports 328,320 installs — a third-party number you can cite instead of their self-claim.
    [REFUTED] CodeRabbit funding: $143M at a $1.5B valuation co-led by Atomico and Smash Capital with BMW i Ventures and Datadog participating (Aug 2026); Sacra estimate of $50M ARR July 2026 -> The ARR half is CONFIRMED; the round is NOT. Sacra — which is actively tracking CodeRabbit through July 2026 — states total funding of $88M across all rounds, with the most recent being a $60M Series B in September 2025 led by Scale Venture Partners at ~$550M post-money (participants NVentures, CRV, Harmony Partners, Flex Capital, Engineering Capital, Pelion), preceded by a $16M Series A in August 2024 led by CRV. Sacra explicitly shows no 2026 round. TechCrunch's CodeRabbit coverage shows the same two rounds and nothing later. A Bing query for the specific figures returned no matching headlines. CONFIRMED separately: Sacra's ARR estimate — 'hit $50M in annual recurring revenue in July 2026, up from $25M at the end of 2025' — and it is indeed an estimate, not a company disclosure. ONE PARTIAL SIGNAL FOR the round: BMW i Ventures does list CodeRabbit as an active portfolio company, which is not explained by the disclosed September 2025 investor list, so some later round may exist. But $143M and $1.5B are uncorroborated by every source I could reach. DO NOT put these figures in a deck or on stage.
    [CORRECTED] 'Graphite has retired the Diamond brand' — inferred from the 301 redirect, the 'AI Reviews' H1 and the 'Graphite Chat' naming, though a 'diamond' substring was still found in body text -> The researcher's own hedge was right and should be kept — do not assert 'retired'. Redirect chain confirmed in full today: diamond.graphite.dev -> 301 -> graphite.dev/features/ai-reviews/ -> 301 -> graphite.com/features/ai-reviews/ -> 308 -> /features/ai-reviews -> 200 (note the first hop is graphite.DEV, not .com as stated). H1 confirmed as 'AI Reviews - Instant AI code review.' and the reviewer is called 'Graphite Chat' throughout. BUT Diamond survives in live body copy in the 'How it works / Get instant reviews' section: 'Diamond analyzes every pull request in seconds, flagging only real issues.' The accurate statement is 'Graphite is mid-rebrand from Diamond to Graphite Chat / AI Reviews, with residual Diamond copy still live' — not 'retired'.
    [CORRECTED] ito.ai renders LIGHT despite a dark computed body token; colour list is from markup greps plus one screenshot, not a full computed-style audit -> Light theme CONFIRMED. Stats CONFIRMED (81% of bugs found are unique to runtime, 50% fewer regressions, 10x more coverage) plus two the researcher missed: '30% more features per sprint' and '70% fewer production regressions'. THREE CORRECTIONS: (1) The H1 is 'Catch all runtime bugs before merge.' — the researcher never recorded ito's H1 text at all, only its size/weight/tracking. (2) The customer list is wrong. Named customers on the live page are DoltHub, Truemed, CNaught, Inkeep and Temi. Chatwoot, Sybill and SignalFire were NOT found — and SignalFire is a venture capital firm, so it was almost certainly an investor logo misread as a customer under the 'TRUSTED BY ENGINEERING TEAMS AT' band. (3) The closing CTA 'Ship with total behavioral certainty.' was not found; the live closing CTA is a 'GET STARTED' button with 'no credit card required' under copy about connecting your repo and getting a runtime report with video, screenshots and failure details in the PR.
    [CORRECTED] Korbit, Bito, Entelligence and GitHub Copilot code review were assessed via markdown extraction only; treat their dark/light and visual claims as LOW confidence -> Three of the four hold up well; one theme claim is disputed. KORBIT fully confirmed: H1 'Deliver better code faster with AI powered code reviews', single 'Request Demo' CTA, all four stats, and pricing. ENTELLIGENCE fully confirmed: H1 'Cost and quality optimization engine for engineering agents', subhead 'One engine, one memory. The most efficient model, every session. Bugs that never repeat.', CTAs 'Start for free' / 'Book a demo', and all three named customer metrics. GITHUB COPILOT fully confirmed: H1 'Copilot Code Review', the exact subheadline, both CTAs, and no pricing on the page. BITO — content confirmed (H1 'Cut your agent bill in half', the exact subhead, 'Get access' / 'Get a demo', 48% cost / 100% task success / 25 min to 8.5 min) but the THEME IS DISPUTED: my extraction reads bito.ai as a LIGHT theme with white backgrounds and dark text, the researcher recorded DARK. Neither read is from a rendered browser. Do not state Bito's theme either way without rendering it. Also confirmed: Bito has no pricing on the homepage but does link a pricing page from the nav.
    [CORRECTED] Section-order heading lists pulled from the live DOM are reliable for CodeRabbit, Greptile, cubic, Qodo, Graphite, Cursor, ito, Sonar, Snyk, DeepSource -> Broadly reliable, with one systematic discrepancy you must resolve before using any H1 quote on stage. On BOTH CodeRabbit and Sonar my markdown extraction inverts the researcher's H1/eyebrow assignment. CodeRabbit: extraction reports H1 = 'Agentic change management' with 'The future isn't writing code. It's reviewing it./securing it./prioritizing it.' as the surrounding phrase; researcher has these swapped. Sonar: extraction reports H1 = 'Guide. Verify. Solve' with eyebrow 'Introducing the Agent Centric Development Cycle'; researcher has these swapped. The inversion runs the same direction on both sites, which suggests my markdown extractor is picking the wrong heading node rather than the researcher's DOM read being wrong — the per-character keyframe animation on 'reviewing/securing/prioritizing' and Sonar's 57px Sentient setting both point to the researcher being right. But it is unresolved, so verify in a rendered browser before quoting a competitor H1. Everything else checks out: CodeRabbit's section run (Review/Prioritize/Understand/Secure -> 'Fast code. Faster chaos.' -> pillars -> Customer outcomes -> 'Get started with CodeRabbit'), Greptile's run through to FAQ, Snyk's run ending on 'Can you trust what you're shipping?', Graphite's H1/sub/CTAs/microcopy/banner verbatim, Qodo's two-tone H1, Cursor's H1/sub/CTA and all six testimonials with correct names and companies. CodeRabbit's logo wall is LARGER than recorded — NVIDIA, Indeed, Adyen, JFrog, Gilead, BMW, Swiggy and Visma are all confirmed, plus Trivago, Campfire, Life360, Groupon, Bun, Clerk, Dialpad and TaskRabbit. Greptile's logo wall (not captured by the researcher) is Substack, Klaviyo, Retool, Nvidia, Brex, Scale, PostHog, Mintlify, Zapier, Joby, WorkOS, Compass, Bilt, Crossmint, Browserbase, Material Security.
    [CORRECTED] 'Every site shows a fake PR comment or diff; zero show a terminal' — verified directly on 5 of 18, inferred for the rest -> The 'zero terminals' half is not safe as written, and it is contradicted by the researcher's own reference sheet. Their Entelligence entry states the hero is a 'dashboard/terminal with routing metrics and cost breakdowns'. So at least one of the 18 already shows a terminal-like surface. Two further caveats on the comparison set: Entelligence and Bito have both pivoted off code review onto model routing and cost control (confirmed — Bito H1 'Cut your agent bill in half', Entelligence H1 'Cost and quality optimization engine for engineering agents'), so they are weak members of a code-review category set either way. The defensible, still-strong version of the claim is: 'no dedicated code-review competitor uses an executing terminal as its hero proof device' — which preserves the whole force of recommendation 1 without a checkable error in it.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] Qodo's hero sits on a 'perspective grid floor' (Tron horizon), read from one 400x300 downscaled screenshot -> Cannot re-verify a visual treatment without rendering the page. Qodo's text content is confirmed (two-tone H1 'Code review for agents.' / 'Governance for humans.', and the governance-layer section the researcher correctly flagged as a danger zone for PRGuard positioning). The grid-floor reading is uncorroborated; it is a low-stakes claim since it only supports a 'do not do this' instruction.
    [REFUTED] oklch(0.145 0.012 265) / oklch(0.78 0.16 168) / oklch(0.70 0.19 35) are 'unoccupied' in this category — researcher's judgement, not an exhaustive audit -> The proposed base is NOT unoccupied, and the researcher's own reference sheet contains the counter-evidence they did not use. Converting the collected hexes to oklch: Baz #15171F = oklch(0.206 0.0162 273.9) — a cold blue-tinted near-black at hue 274, chroma 0.016, which is the same family as the proposed oklch(0.145 0.012 265), only lighter. Sonar #031426 = oklch(0.187 0.0443 250.1) — also blue, at much higher chroma. The researcher analysed only three darks (CodeRabbit #121014 = oklch(0.177 0.0087 307.9), Graphite #0A0A0A = oklch(0.145 0 89.9), Cursor #14120B = oklch(0.182 0.0139 94.0)) and excluded Baz and Sonar from the comparison, which is what produced the 'measurably unoccupied' conclusion. The accurate statement is that the proposed base is DARKER than the two existing cold darks (0.145 vs 0.187-0.206) and sits at a hue between them, not that the region is free. The recommendation still works — it just needs to be argued on lightness separation from Baz, not on novelty. The teal and amber accent claims remain unaudited and the researcher's own caveat stands.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] The shared browser tab was navigated to reactbits.dev by something outside the researcher's control mid-task; nothing from that page informed the findings and the affected Greptile capture was re-run in an isolated tab -> No way to audit another session's tab state. Disclosing it was the right call and the stated remediation (re-run in an isolated tab) is the correct one. Flagging it here only so it is not lost: an unexplained third-party navigation into a shared browsing context during a research task is a contamination vector worth noting in the methodology section of anything that ships.
    [CORRECTED] Recommendation 11 premise: CodeRabbit uses one easing cubic-bezier(.23,1,.32,1) appearing 11 times, durations landing on five values, all staggers 80ms -> Directionally right, slightly overstated. In the served HTML I count cubic-bezier(0.23,1,0.32,1) exactly 10 times — but also a SECOND easing, cubic-bezier(0.77,0,0.175,1), once. So 'one easing' is not literally true; 'one dominant easing at roughly 10:1' is. The 10-vs-11 gap is likely just HTML-versus-external-stylesheet scope. The duration set (.28/.48/.62/.72/.82s) and the 80ms stagger could not be re-extracted from the served HTML (they live in the external stylesheet) and remain UNVERIFIED. The recommendation itself is unaffected — adopting a quantised motion grid is sound regardless — but do not claim on stage that CodeRabbit uses exactly one easing eleven times.
    [REFUTED] Recommendation 13 premise: 'Every single competitor is $12-$99 per seat per month with 3-4 tiers, a Custom last column and a -20% annual toggle' -> The $12-$99 range is right (Korbit Pro $12 annual at the floor, cubic Pro $99 monthly at the ceiling) and the 'Custom' last column and -20% toggle are widespread. But 'every single competitor is per-seat' is false, and the category has ALREADY moved toward usage pricing — which materially weakens this recommendation's differentiation argument. Qodo is not per-seat at all: $30/month for up to 30 users plus $0.012/credit pooled across the team, i.e. base-plus-usage. Greptile is $30/seat but gates work behind credits ($1/credit, 1 credit per review, 3 per TREX review). CodeRabbit layers $0.40/agent-minute and $0.25/reviewed-file on top of seats. GitHub Copilot code review shows no pricing on its page at all (confirmed). So outcome- or usage-adjacent pricing is not an unoccupied axis — three of the largest players already bill on consumption. Pricing on 'proven findings' is still a sharper articulation, but it is a refinement of where the category is heading, not a break from it. The researcher's own 'low' confidence rating on this one was appropriate.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] Recommendation 8 premise: Greptile's page returned a blank #E9E9E9 rectangle across repeated programmatic scrolls because every section below the hero is IntersectionObserver reveal-gated -> Cannot reproduce a browser-rendering symptom without a rendered browser. One corroborating data point: Greptile's full section content IS present in the served markup and extracts cleanly as text (H1 'The AI Code Reviewer.', the 22,000+ teams band, the full logo wall, all eleven sections through the FAQ). That is consistent with the researcher's diagnosis — the content ships, the reveal is what fails — rather than with a server-side rendering problem. The underlying advice (never make opacity:0 the default state for primary content) is sound regardless of whether Greptile specifically is guilty.
    [CORRECTED] Recommendation 8 fallback premise: use 'animation-timeline: view()' where supported, with a no-op fallback -> The 'where supported' hedge is doing more work than the recommendation implies. MDN classifies animation-timeline as LIMITED AVAILABILITY and explicitly NOT Baseline, with the stated reason 'it does not work in some of the most widely-used browsers' — MDN is even soliciting developer signals asking for wider support. Scroll-driven animations are a Chromium-only story in practice; do not plan any judged demo around them. If you use it at all, gate it behind @supports (animation-timeline: view()) and make the un-enhanced state the designed one. Given that a hackathon demo may run on an unknown laptop or a projector, this is the same risk class the researcher correctly flagged for WebGL in recommendation 10.
VERIFIER ADDITIONS:
    + BIGGEST SINGLE CORRECTION: the CodeRabbit funding figures. $143M at $1.5B co-led by Atomico and Smash Capital is uncorroborated by Sacra (which tracks CodeRabbit through July 2026 and states $88M total, latest $60M Series B at ~$550M in Sept 2025 led by Scale Venture Partners) and by TechCrunch (same two rounds, nothing later). If this went into a competitive deck it would be the most falsifiable thing in it. The $50M ARR July 2026 estimate IS confirmed and is the safe number to use, clearly labelled as a Sacra estimate.
    + SECOND BIGGEST: the palette recommendation's core premise is refuted by the researcher's own data. Baz's #15171F converts to oklch(0.206 0.0162 273.9) — a cold blue-tinted near-black nine degrees of hue and 0.004 chroma from the proposed oklch(0.145 0.012 265). The researcher recorded Baz's base in their reference sheet but excluded Baz from the three-dark comparison that produced the 'measurably unoccupied' claim. The recommendation survives on lightness separation (0.145 vs 0.206) but the novelty argument does not. Worth re-running the comparison across ALL collected bases before committing the token set — I converted eight and it took one script.
    + THIRD: recommendation 13's premise that the category is uniformly per-seat is wrong, and this is the one place where a corrected fact should change the product decision rather than just the copy. Qodo already bills base-plus-credits ($30/mo for up to 30 users + $0.012/credit), Greptile gates work behind credits, CodeRabbit bills $0.40/agent-minute and $0.25/reviewed-file. Usage pricing is the direction the category is already moving. 'Price on proven findings' is a sharper articulation of an existing trend, not a break from the category — pitch it that way and it survives the obvious judge question; pitch it as unoccupied and someone who knows Qodo's pricing page will correct you live.
    + METHODOLOGICAL: the H1/eyebrow inversion on BOTH CodeRabbit and Sonar is unresolved and should be settled before any competitor H1 is quoted on a slide. My markdown extraction and the researcher's DOM read disagree in the same direction on both sites, which points at my extractor rather than at them, but neither of us has a rendered browser reading with computed font sizes attached. Quoting a competitor's headline wrongly is a cheap, avoidable error. Same applies to every docH page-height figure in the teardown (Cursor 5,080px, cubic 14,570px, the ~8,500px median that underpins recommendation 7's entire length argument) — none of those are verifiable without rendering, and recommendation 7 is rated 'high' confidence on top of them.
    + A COMPETITOR THE TEARDOWN TREATS AS LIVE IS BEING ACQUIRED: DeepSource announced it is joining Harness on 9 September 2026, thirteen days before this analysis. That moots the Google Sans licensing question entirely, and it means DeepSource's 17-section feature wall is about to be absorbed into a much larger platform. Conversely it makes Harness a competitor that appears nowhere in the 18-site set. Worth a look before the set is called complete.
    + TWO SITES IN THE SET ARE NO LONGER CODE-REVIEW PRODUCTS. Bito's H1 is now 'Cut your agent bill in half' and Entelligence's is 'Cost and quality optimization engine for engineering agents' — both have pivoted to model routing and cost control. Keeping them in an 18-site code-review comparison inflates the denominator on every 'N of 18 sites do X' claim in the teardown, including the load-bearing 'zero show a terminal'. Recommend restating those as 'N of 16 dedicated code-review sites'.
    + PANTO IS UNREACHABLE, NOT ABSENT. DNS resolves to AWS addresses (76.223.54.146, 13.248.169.48) while HTTP times out at 20s from this environment on both apex and www. The researcher was right to collect nothing, but the downstream inference 'Panto is dead / does not exist' would be wrong. It is a live registered domain that this network cannot reach — most likely bot or geo blocking. If Panto matters to the positioning, it needs a check from a different network before anyone concludes anything.
    + THE OKLCH BACKGROUND CAVEAT CAN BE RETIRED, BUT NOT FOR THE STATED REASON. Conversion proves oklch(0.177 0.0084 305), #121014 and lab(4.9349 1.26021 -1.99696) are all the same colour to within rounding, so the researcher's number is correct. What is wrong is the provenance: in the served HTML that oklch literal appears only as the fallback of --not-found-bg, the 404 page's background. The right claim is 'CodeRabbit's base is #121014, which is --color-cui-mauve-1 and also what the 404 page falls back to' — the mauve-1..12 token ramp is independently confirmed present in the live markup.
    + SMALL FACTUAL FIXES TO PROPAGATE: Greptile says 22,000+ teams, not 20,000+ (appears twice in the researcher's output). CodeRabbit claims 'most installed AI App on GitHub', not GitHub+GitLab — and GitHub Marketplace independently reports 328,320 installs, which is a better number to cite than either. The diamond.graphite.dev redirect's first hop is graphite.dev, not graphite.com. ito's customer list is DoltHub/Truemed/CNaught/Inkeep/Temi — Chatwoot and Sybill were not found and SignalFire is a VC firm that was almost certainly an investor logo misread as a customer. ito's H1 is 'Catch all runtime bugs before merge.' and its closing CTA is 'GET STARTED', not 'Ship with total behavioral certainty.' CodeRabbit's logo wall has sixteen names, not eight. Snyk's hero carries six stats (288%, 80%, 75%, 60%, 52%, 3), not two. DeepSource's F1 figure is 84.51%.
    + LICENSING IS IN BETTER SHAPE THAN THE TEARDOWN SAYS — three of the four 'unverified' alternatives are free. JetBrains Mono is OFL-1.1 for the typeface (Apache 2.0 for the source), Instrument Sans is OFL 1.1, Bricolage Grotesque is OFL 1.1. Only Berkeley Mono remains unverified and it is genuinely commercial with no price obtainable (berkeleygraphics.com now redirects to usgraphics.com, which 403s automated requests). Matter's prices are now known and are the real number for the 'commercial type is a real cost' tradeoff: EUR 55 per style, EUR 242-352 per family, EUR 1,189 personal or EUR 5,280 commercial for the full collection. One genuine remaining gap: the full text of the ITF Free Font Licence could not be read (Fontshare is JS-gated), so if Sentient is a serious candidate, read the actual licence terms on self-hosting and commercial use rather than relying on the word 'Free' in Fontshare's tagline.
    + SCROLL-DRIVEN ANIMATIONS ARE A DEMO RISK IN THE SAME CLASS AS WEBGL. Recommendation 8 tells you to avoid WebGL for fear of dropped frames on a demo laptop, then proposes animation-timeline: view() as the fallback mechanism. MDN classifies that as limited availability and explicitly not Baseline. The recommendation's own logic argues against it. Use plain CSS entrance animations that run once on load, and keep the un-enhanced state as the designed state.
    + ONE THING NOBODY CHECKED: the teardown never establishes whether any competitor publishes a false-positive rate anywhere off the landing page — in docs, a benchmark post, or a trust page. Recommendation 3's whole claim to uncopyability is 'not one of the 18 sites shows its own tool failing or publishes a false-positive rate with an n'. That was assessed from landing pages only. Cursor's Bugbot page markets 'a low false positive rate' as a headline property, which is exactly the kind of claim a vendor sometimes backs with a number one click deeper. Worth ten minutes on the docs and blogs of Cursor, CodeRabbit and cubic before staking a section on being the only one who does this — it is the single most load-bearing 'nobody else does X' claim in the whole set.

### AREA: Elite dark dev-tool landing pages — the bar to clear (elite-devtools)
FINDINGS:
  - THE BIGGEST FINDING, AND IT CONTRADICTS THE OWNER'S BRIEF: essentially nobody in the elite tier ships a 3D/WebGL hero. I counted `<canvas>` and `webgl` occurrences in the live homepage HTML of 22 sites. linear.app 0 canvas / 0 webgl. vercel.com 0/0. clerk.com 0/0. raycast.com 0/0. resend.com 0/0. supabase.com 0/0. turso.tech 0/0. browserbase.com 0/0. railway.com 0/0. The ONLY genuine WebGL hero is e2b.dev (6 canvas, 3 webgl refs, shipped with a `webgl-off:block hidden [image-rendering:pixelated]` raster fallback class). warp.dev has 3 canvas + Rive. The premium signal in 2026 is a DOM-rendered, pixel-exact recreation of the real product UI, not a Three.js scene.
  - The depth recipe on a great dark page is FOUR stacked layers per surface, never one gradient. Linear literally ships them as separate sibling divs: (1) surface from a 5-step near-black ladder, (2) a 1px hairline border, (3) `<div class="grain grainSubtle" style="inset:1px">`, (4) `<div class="glow">`. I pulled the exact markup from linear.app: `...</div><div class="SPbJba_grain SPbJba_grainSubtle qM9FAa_boardGrain" style="top:1px"></div><div class="qM9FAa_glow MwJdiW_glow"></div>`.
  - Linear's grain, verbatim from https://static.linear.app/web/_next/static/css/Grain.D_EBlr94.css : `.grain{pointer-events:none;border-radius:inherit;opacity:.9;mix-blend-mode:overlay;background-size:256px 256px;position:absolute;inset:0}` with `.grain:after{content:'';background:#ffffff0f;position:absolute;inset:0}` and a Safari kill-switch `@supports((-webkit-hyphens:none)){.grain:after{background:0 0}}`. The subtle variant is `opacity:.6` with `:after{content:none}`. A 256x256 tile at overlay blend is the whole trick — not feTurbulence (zero sites used feTurbulence for page grain).
  - Linear's cursor-tracked card glow, verbatim: `.MwJdiW_glow{--glow-opacity:.04;background:radial-gradient(circle, rgba(255,255,255,var(--glow-opacity)) 0%, transparent 50%);mix-blend-mode:lighten;pointer-events:none;width:400px;height:400px;position:absolute;top:0;left:0;transform:translate(-50%,-50%)}`. Variants use `#ffffff0a` at 50%/90% stops and an 800x320px ellipse version. Four percent white. That is the entire glow budget on the most-copied dark site on the internet.
  - Linear's spotlight border (the effect everyone tries to reverse-engineer): `.shine{--shine-beam-length:200px;border:1px solid var(--color-border-translucent-strong);pointer-events:none;-webkit-mask-image:radial-gradient(ellipse var(--shine-beam-length) var(--shine-beam-length) at var(--mask-x) var(--mask-y), #000 0%, #0009 30%, #0003 50%, #0000 ...)}`. It is a full 1px border masked by a cursor-positioned radial — not a gradient border.
  - Linear's live dark ramp (from its own CSS chunks, not a third-party token scraper): `--bg-base:#0f1011`, `--color-bg-panel:#161718`, `--bg-shade:#191d20`, `--row-bg:#111213` / `#18191a`, `--border-frame:#151616`, `--border-faint-thin:#191d21`, `--border-thin:#24282c`, `--border-solid:#2a2e33`, `--kbd-bg:#3b3d45`, `--anchor-glass-bg:#ffffff14`. Its syntax tokens are worth stealing wholesale for a code hero: comment `#8B93A1`, constant `#8FA6FF`, entity `#83dcdc`, keyword `#F79CE0`, string `#ffdf9f`, variable `#f7bf8b`, text `#E2E4E7`, punctuation `#D2D7DE`, plus `--diff-new-color:#27A644` on `rgba(39,166,68,0.07)` and `--diff-old-color:#F34E52` on `rgba(243,78,82,0.1)`.
  - Vercel's alpha-on-black ramp is the single most copyable dark system in existence because every step is white-at-alpha, so it composites correctly over ANY background: `--ds-gray-alpha-100:#ffffff0f` (some contexts `#ffffff12`), `-200:#ffffff17`, `-300:#ffffff21`, `-400:#ffffff24`, `-500:#ffffff3d`, `-600:#ffffff82`, `-700:#ffffff8a`, `-800:#ffffff78`, `-900:#ffffff9c`, `-1000:#ffffffeb`. Solid dark equivalents: `--ds-gray-100:#1a1a1a`, `-200:#1f1f1f`, `-300:#292929`, `-400:#2e2e2e`, `-500:#454545`, `-600:#878787`, `-700:#8f8f8f`, `-900:#a0a0a0`, `-1000:#ededed`; `--ds-background-100:#000`, `--ds-background-200:#000`.
  - Vercel's semantic scale for a pass/fail merge gate, dark values: `--ds-blue-600:#0090ff`, `--ds-blue-700:#0070f7`, `--ds-blue-100:#06193a` (tinted surface); `--ds-amber-700:#ffb200`, `--ds-amber-800:#f90`, `--ds-amber-100:#291800`; `--ds-green-100:#00250a`, `--ds-green-200:#003110`, `--ds-green-1000:#00370d`. The pattern: a 100-200 step is the tinted CARD BACKGROUND, the 600-800 step is the TEXT/ICON. Copy that pattern for PRGuard's proven/disproven/inconclusive states.
  - Raycast has the coldest and best near-black ramp of anyone, and it is measurably blue-tinted rather than neutral: `--grey-900:#07080a`, `-800:#0c0d0f`, `-700:#111214`, `-600:#1b1c1e`, `-500:#2f3031`, `-400:#434345`, `-300:#6a6b6c`, `-200:#9c9c9d`, `-100:#cdcece`, `-50:#e6e6e6`; `--color-bg-100:#101111`, `--color-bg-200:#18191a`, `--color-border:#242728`. Their card fill is top-lit: `radial-gradient(100% 100% at 50% 0%, var(--grey-700) 0%, var(--grey-600) 150%)` — note the 150% end stop, which flattens the falloff. They also use real backdrop blur at scale: `blur(2px)`, `blur(5px)`, `blur(10px)`, `blur(36px)`, `blur(48px)`.
  - Warp parameterizes chroma so one variable tints the whole neutral ramp: `--gray-950:oklch(7% var(--gray-chroma) <hue>)`, `-900:oklch(12% ...)`, `-800:oklch(20% ...)`, `-700:oklch(30% ...)`, `-600:oklch(40% ...)`, `-500:oklch(53% ...)`, down to `-50:oklch(97% ...)`. Change `--gray-chroma` from 0 to 0.006 and the entire site warms or cools without touching 10 hex codes. This is the correct 2026 way to build a dark ramp, and PRGuard should do exactly this.
  - Warp's animated aurora, verbatim: `.scanner-fluid{background:radial-gradient(#b978ff8c 0%,#0000 80%) 0 0/320% 320%, radial-gradient(#8c4bfa6b 0%,#0000 75%) 0 0/260% 260%;animation:4s ease-in-out infinite scanner-fluid}` with `@keyframes scanner-fluid{0%{background-position:10% 20%,90% 80%}25%{background-position:80% 10%,20% 90%}50%{background-position:60% 85%,40% 15%}75%{background-position:15% 70%,85% 30%}to{background-position:10% 20%,90% 80%}}`. Zero JS, zero WebGL, GPU-cheap, and it reads as a living gradient. This is the drop-in replacement for a Three.js hero.
  - Depot puts the glow BEHIND the terminal as a blurred solid, not as a box-shadow: `.terminal-glow{background:linear-gradient(81deg, var(--color-depot-purple) 6.83%, var(--color-depot-light-blue) 93.13%);filter:blur(12px);border-radius:4px}` where `--color-depot-purple:#a033ff` and `--color-depot-light-blue:#0af`. Their base is `--color-depot-primary-dark:#080808` with neutrals `#111` / `#1b1b1b` / `#1e1f1f`. `filter:blur()` on a gradient div is far richer than any `box-shadow` glow and is the single highest-ROI trick for making a code/terminal block look expensive.
  - Modal ships a REAL ANSI palette in production CSS because they render actual terminal output — directly reusable for PRGuard's proof terminal: `.ansi-black-fg{color:#1d1f21}`, `.ansi-red-fg{#cc342b}`, `.ansi-green-fg{#198844}`, `.ansi-yellow-fg{#fba922}`, `.ansi-blue-fg{#3971ed}`, `.ansi-magenta-fg{#a36ac7}`, `.ansi-white-fg{#c5c8c6}`, `.ansi-bright-black-fg{#969896}`. That is the Tomorrow Night palette. Modal's neutral ramp for log surfaces: `--color-c-gray-0:#181818`, `-2:#1c1c1c`, `-5:#242424`, `-7:#272727`, `-10:#2f2f2f`, `-15:#3b3b3b`, `-20:#464646`, `-30:#5d5d5d`, `-50:#8b8b8b`, `-80:#d1d1d1`, `-90:#e8e8e8`.
  - Clerk's hero background is one enormous OFF-CANVAS radial plus a vignette, and it is two lines: `radial-gradient(100% 100% at 50% 0,#0000 31.25%,#0006 100%), radial-gradient(200% 160% at -20% -80%,#5de3ff 0%,#561ee3 100%)`. Note the 200%x160% size anchored at -20%/-80% — the color source is outside the viewport, so you only ever see the smooth middle of the ramp. Clerk's neutral ramp: `--color-gray-950:#131316`, `-900:#212126`, `-850:#27272d`, `-800:#2f3037`, `-750:#373840`, `-700:#42434d`.
  - Resend's hero is deliberately near-monochrome and still reads premium: `radial-gradient(100% 100% at 50% 0,#ffffff14 0%,#fff0 100%), linear-gradient(210deg,#080808 0%,#000 100%)`. Their dot grid is `radial-gradient(#2b2b2b 1px,#0000 1px)`. Their animated gradient border uses `@property --angle{syntax:'<angle>';inherits:false;initial-value:0deg}` driving `.rainbow-border{background:linear-gradient(var(--angle), #02fcef70 0%, #ffb52b70 50%, #a02bfe70 100%);animation:rotate 30s linear infinite}` — 30 SECONDS, i.e. almost imperceptible. Their Radix-style gray ramp: `--gray-1:#141517`, `-2:#191b1e`, `-3:#212629`, `-4:#293034`, `-5:#333b3e`, `-6:#3b4345`, `-7:#434a4d`, `-12:#f0f0f0`.
  - Every house has ONE signature easing and uses it everywhere. Counted occurrences in production CSS: Raycast `cubic-bezier(.23,1,.32,1)` x12 and `cubic-bezier(.16,1,.3,1)` x8 (expo-out family); Clerk `cubic-bezier(.33,1,.68,1)` x10 (cubic-out); Warp `cubic-bezier(.16,1,.3,1)` x8; Modal `cubic-bezier(.215,.61,.355,1)` x20 and `cubic-bezier(.645,.045,.355,1)` x18; Resend and Dub fall back to Tailwind's `cubic-bezier(.4,0,.2,1)` 60x and 44x respectively (i.e. they barely customize). Linear's interaction spec is `scale(0.97)` on active, 100-150ms ease-out. Pick ONE expo-out curve and a 150-250ms duration and never deviate.
  - CODERABBIT IS ALREADY DARK IN THE HERO — the brief's assumption that dark alone beats them is wrong. From their live CSS: `--cr-triage-card-background:#121014`, `--cr-home-hero-feature-backdrop:#1a181c`, `--cr-home-hero-prioritize-row:#1a181d`, orange CTA `--cr-ketch-cta:#ff570a`, `--home-hero-diff-add-bg:#122b26` / `--home-hero-diff-add-text:#2de3a1`, `--home-hero-diff-delete-bg:#44171b` / `#ff6256`, `--home-hero-github-accent:#58a6ff`. Their base is WARM-tinted near-black (red/violet cast) with a cream brand ramp `#311c15 -> #6e564d -> #a1887e -> #dacdc6 -> #ebe7e6 -> #f7f2ef`. Fonts: Geist Sans + Hack (mono) + Lato. The differentiation lever is therefore HUE and OBJECT, not darkness: go cold/instrument-blue and put a PROOF TERMINAL + SEVERITY RADAR in the hero instead of a diff card.
  - Greptile went the opposite way — LIGHT, whiteboard, hand-drawn: fonts are DM Sans + Space Mono + Anybody + **Nanum Pen Script** (an actual handwriting face). Their 'how it works' is three numbered text steps, no diagram. Their hero h1 is literally 'The AI Code Reviewer'. Graphite's is 'The next generation of code review.' cubic's is 'AI code reviews for complex codebases'. Every competitor headline is a CATEGORY LABEL. PRGuard's headline should be a CLAIM ('We don't think it's a bug. We proved it.') — that alone differentiates in the first 3 seconds.
  - The recurring structural grammar across 20 sites is 8-11 sections, and it is remarkably rigid. Verified section order from live H1/H2/H3 extraction: (1) hero: 6-10 word headline + 1-sentence subhead + 2 CTAs + ONE hero object; (2) logo wall immediately after the fold (Warp: 'Trusted by 800k+ devs' + 8 logos; Depot: ~14 logos with case-study links; Clerk: 11 named companies); (3) the PROBLEM restated as a one-liner (Depot: 'The bottleneck has shifted from writing code to everything after.'; turso: 'The database wasn't built for a world where every agent wants its own.'; CodeRabbit: 'Fast code. Faster chaos.'); (4-7) three to five MECHANISM sections, each one H2 + one visual, alternating image side; (8) 'how it works' as 3 numbered steps; (9) proof — metrics, case study, or testimonial wall; (10) security/compliance grid (Modal, Depot, Greptile, cubic, e2b ALL have one — SOC2/isolation/self-host); (11) final CTA, a restatement of the hero with one button. Linear's is literally 'Built for the future. Available today.'; Resend's 'Email reimagined. Available today.'
  - Product screenshot placement is universal: the hero object is a cropped, top-lit, rounded-16px panel that BLEEDS off the bottom or right edge of its container, masked with `mask-image:linear-gradient(#000 0% 45%,#0000 90%)` (Raycast uses that exact stop pattern in five variants; Vercel uses `linear-gradient(#000 0% 28%,#0000 88%)` and `#000 0 calc(100% - 24px),#0000 100%`). Nobody shows a full uncropped screenshot in the hero. The crop + fade is what signals 'there is more product than fits here'.
  - Font reality check: the expensive-looking sites almost all pay a foundry, which is a budget trap for a hackathon. Warp uses 'The Future' + 'Matter'; Resend uses 'ABC Favorit' + 'Commit Mono'; Depot uses 'Neue Montreal' + 'ABC Favorit Mono'; e2b uses 'PP Supply Sans' + 'Neue Haas Grotesk'; Clerk uses 'Suisse' + 'Soehne Mono'; Mintlify uses 'ABC Arizona Flare' + Geist Mono; Speakeasy uses 'Tobias' + 'ABC Diatype'; val.town uses 'iA Writer Mono/Quattro'; Linear ships its own 'Linear Display/Text/Mono'. The free stack that still reads expensive, all SIL OFL: Inter (or Geist) for UI, Geist Mono / JetBrains Mono / Commit Mono for code, Instrument Serif or Manrope/Sora for a display accent. Berkeley Mono — the one every dev covets — is PAID ($75 personal tier at usgraphics.com) and its commercial license is restricted, so do not ship it.
  - CSS scroll-driven animations (`animation-timeline: view()/scroll()`) are the right engine for a 9-stage pipeline scroll narrative but are NOT yet safe alone: Chrome/Edge 115+, Safari 18+ (Safari 26 shipped it Sept 2025, threaded in 26.4), but Firefox still sits behind `layout.css.scroll-driven-animations.enabled` in stable as of Firefox 152 (June 2026), at roughly 84% global support. Ship it as progressive enhancement with an IntersectionObserver fallback, or use Motion (npm `motion`, v13.4.0, formerly framer-motion, import from `motion/react`).
REFERENCES:
  - [must-steal] Linear https://linear.app :: STEAL: The entire four-layer depth stack, the grain tile, the 4%-white cursor glow, the masked spotlight border, and the near-black surface ladder. This is the single highest-value reference and you should treat it as the floor, not the ceiling. :: SPECIFICS: Grain: `.grain{opacity:.9;mix-blend-mode:overlay;background-size:256px 256px;position:absolute;inset:0;pointer-events:none;border-radius:inherit}` + `:after{background:#ffffff0f;inset:0}` + `@supports((-webkit-hyphens:none)){:after{background:0 0}}`; `.grainSubtle{opacity:.6}` no :after. Glow: `radial-gradient(circle, rgba(255,255,255,.04) 0%, transparent 50%)`, 400x400px, `mix-blend-mode:lighten`, `translate(-50%,-50%)`. Shine border: `border:1px solid` + `-webkit-mask-image:radial-gradient(ellipse 200px 200px at var(--mask-x) var(--mask-y), #000 0%, #0009 30%, #0003 50%, #0000)`. Surfaces: --bg-base #0f1011, --color-bg-panel #161718, --bg-shade #191d20, --row-bg #111213/#18191a. Borders: --border-frame #151616, --border-faint-thin #191d21, --border-thin #24282c, --border-solid #2a2e33. Accent #5e6ad2 (hover #828fff). Syntax: comment #8B93A1, constant #8FA6FF, entity #83dcdc, keyword #F79CE0, string #ffdf9f, variable #f7bf8b. Diff: new #27A644 on rgba(39,166,68,.07); old #F34E52 on rgba(243,78,82,.1). Interaction: active scale(0.97), 100-150ms ease-out. Font: InterVariable with `cv01` + `ss03` on. Sections: hero -> 3 pillars -> intake -> planning -> AI/automations -> build/review/ship -> changelog -> testimonials -> '40,000 teams' -> 'Built for the future. Available today.'
  - [must-steal] Vercel https://vercel.com :: STEAL: The alpha-on-black token system and the semantic 100/700 pairing pattern for status chips. Do not steal their page — it is currently a fairly plain 'Agentic Infrastructure' hero with three customer-framed feature blocks and no hero visual. :: SPECIFICS: Alpha ramp: #ffffff0f/12, #ffffff17, #ffffff21, #ffffff24, #ffffff3d, #ffffff82, #ffffff8a, #ffffff78, #ffffff9c, #ffffffeb. Solid: #1a1a1a, #1f1f1f, #292929, #2e2e2e, #454545, #878787, #8f8f8f, #a0a0a0, #ededed. Backgrounds both #000. Status dark values: blue-600 #0090ff, blue-700 #0070f7, blue-100 #06193a; amber-700 #ffb200, amber-800 #f90, amber-100 #291800; green-100 #00250a, green-200 #003110. Glass specular on cards: `radial-gradient(58% 46% at 24% 16%,#ffffff21 0%,#5aa0ff14 30%,#0000 72%)` and a brighter `#ffffff57 0%,#70d2ff1a 28%,#0000 72%`. Vignette: `radial-gradient(140% 100% at 50% 52%,#0000 26%,#00000075 100%),linear-gradient(#00000085 0%,#0003 42%,#0000009e 100%)`. Screenshot fades: `mask-image:linear-gradient(#000 0% 28%,#0000 88%)`. Section rhythm observed: padding-block 90px / 100px / 135px. Fonts Geist Sans + Geist Mono (SIL OFL, npm `geist`).

Closest in temperature to what PRGuard should feel like. :: SPECIFICS: Ramp: --grey-900 #07080a, -800 #0c0d0f, -700 #111214, -600 #1b1c1e, -500 #2f3031, -400 #434345, -300 #6a6b6c, -200 #9c9c9d, -100 #cdcece, -50 #e6e6e6. --color-bg-100 #101111, --color-bg-200 #18191a, --color-bg-300 #313133, --color-bg-400 #494b4d. --color-border #242728. --window-background #111217. Keycap gradient: --key-bg-start-color #121212/#151515/#161616 -> --key-bg-end-color #0d0d0d. Card fill: `radial-gradient(100% 100% at 50% 0%, var(--grey-700) 0%, var(--grey-600) 150%)`. Ambient: `radial-gradient(#ffffff0a 0%,#0000 70%)`. Bottom vignette: `radial-gradient(180% 95% at 0 100%,#0000 2%,#00000021 22%,#00000047 44%,#000000bf 76%,#000 100%)`. Masks: `linear-gradient(#000 0% 45%,#0000 70%|88%|90%)`. Blur stack: 2/5/10/36/48px. Easing: cubic-bezier(.23,1,.32,1) x12, (.16,1,.3,1) x8, (.4,0,.22,.96) x10.   - [must-steal] Warp https://warp.dev :: STEAL: The oklch parameterized-chroma gray ramp AND the zero-JS animated aurora. These two together replace a WebGL hero at a fraction of the cost and risk. :: SPECIFICS: Ramp: `--gray-950:oklch(7% var(--gray-chroma) <h>)` through `--gray-50:oklch(97% var(--gray-chroma) <h>)` at 12/20/30/40/53/66/78/91/95%. Aurora: `background:radial-gradient(#b978ff8c 0%,#0000 80%) 0 0/320% 320%, radial-gradient(#8c4bfa6b 0%,#0000 75%) 0 0/260% 260%; animation:4s ease-in-out infinite scanner-fluid` with keyframes stepping background-position through 10%20%/90%80% -> 80%10%/20%90% -> 60%85%/40%15% -> 15%70%/85%30%. Dot grid: `radial-gradient(circle at 1px 1px, var(--line-soft) 1.2px, transparent 0) 0 0/22px 22px`. Top wash: `radial-gradient(circle at 50% 15%,#c0aad62e,#0000 48%)`. Semantics: --color-background #121212, --color-surface #08090a, --color-foreground #f8f5ee (warm off-white, not pure white), --color-muted-foreground #a5a39d, --color-accent-purple #cbb0f7, --color-theme-accent #00c2ff, --accent-2 #7267ff. `@keyframes factory-run-pulse{0%,to{opacity:0;transform:scale(.85)}50%{opacity:1;transform:scale(1.06)}}` is a perfect 'stage is running' pulse for PRGuard's 9-stage diagram. Hero holds a `factory.yaml` config file, not a shell prompt. Easing cubic-bezier(.16,1,.3,1).
