  - [strong] Paper Shaders https://shaders.paper.design/ :: STEAL: If you want animated mesh-gradient depth without writing GLSL or shipping Three.js. This is the 2026 answer to 'make it feel expensive and alive'. :: SPECIFICS: npm `@paper-design/shaders-react`, latest 0.0.81 (published ~2026-09-19). Zero runtime dependencies, 30+ WebGL effects, React and vanilla exports. MeshGradient component takes `colors`, `distortion`, `swirl`, `speed`. Still 0.0.x, so pin the exact version. Trade-off vs Warp's pure-CSS aurora: heavier, needs a canvas, but genuinely animated color transport rather than moving background-position.
RECOMMENDATIONS:
  - (high) Kill the 3D/WebGL hero. Build the hero as a DOM-rendered composite of three real PRGuard surfaces — a proof terminal, a severity radar, and a merge-gate chip — laid out as a floating, top-lit, 16px-radius panel that bleeds off the bottom edge under a `mask-image:linear-gradient(#000 0% 45%, #0000 90%)` fade. Put the motion budget into Warp's pure-CSS aurora behind it and Linear's 4%-white cursor glow on top. | WHY: I counted canvas/webgl in the live HTML of 22 elite sites: Linear 0/0, Vercel 0/0, Clerk 0/0, Raycast 0/0, Resend 0/0, Supabase 0/0, Turso 0/0, Browserbase 0/0. Only e2b.dev ships real WebGL, and even they ship a pixelated raster fallback. A Three.js hero in 2026 reads as agency-portfolio, not as infrastructure. It also actively hurts you in a hackathon: it costs the most build time, is the most likely thing to jank on a judge's laptop, and communicates nothing about proof. The judges' signature moment is 'here's the failing test and the stack trace' — that is a TERMINAL, and a terminal must be readable DOM text, not a texture. | TRADEOFF: You are directly contradicting the owner's stated 3D/realistic preference, so this needs to be sold, not just asserted. Mitigation: you can still deliver 'cinematic' via depth stacking, a 4s aurora drift, and scroll-choreographed stage reveals. If the owner will not budge, the compromise is `@paper-design/shaders-react` (pin 0.0.81) as a background-only mesh gradient — enhancement, never load-bearing, and never in front of text.
  - (high) Own COLD. Base the entire brand on a blue-cast near-black in the #07080a - #0a0b0f range (Raycast's #07080a, Mintlify's #08090a, Unkey's #040406 are the 2026 consensus), built as an oklch ramp with a single `--chroma` and `--hue` variable the way Warp does it. Use exactly ONE chromatic accent for 'proven' and borrow Vercel's amber/red only as semantic status. | WHY: CodeRabbit is ALREADY dark — their hero card is #121014 and #1a181c with #ff570a orange and a cream ramp from #311c15 to #f7f2ef. That is a WARM, red-violet-cast near-black. Going dark alone differentiates nothing. Going cold and instrument-grade against their warm and friendly is an immediate, legible, first-3-seconds difference, and it matches the product's actual claim: this is a measuring instrument, not a helpful rabbit. Warp's `oklch(7% var(--gray-chroma) <hue>)` pattern means one variable retunes the whole site, which is worth an hour on day one. | TRADEOFF: Cold near-blacks can read sterile and can lose warmth on cheap projector displays at a demo. Counter it the way Warp does with an off-white foreground (#f8f5ee rather than #ffffff) and by keeping the proven-state accent genuinely saturated. Also, #040406-class bases crush on poorly-calibrated screens — test on the actual demo hardware.
  - (high) Make the hero object the PROOF TERMINAL, and make its single visual moment the base-SHA/head-SHA split. Two stacked panes: top shows the test PASSING at base, bottom shows it FAILING at head, with a `proof_status: proven` stamp between them. Render real ANSI output using Modal's production palette — red #cc342b, green #198844, yellow #fba922, blue #3971ed, magenta #a36ac7, white #c5c8c6, bright-black #969896 — on a #181818 surface, with Depot's blurred-gradient glow behind it (`filter:blur(12px)` on a linear-gradient div, radius 4px). | WHY: Every competitor's hero object is a diff or a comment thread — CodeRabbit ships `--home-hero-diff-add-bg:#122b26`, Graphite shows a PR page, cubic shows review comments. A terminal with a failing assertion is a categorically different object and it IS the positioning: 'we prove it'. Depot's `.terminal-glow` is the single highest-ROI trick for making a code block look expensive, and Modal's ANSI classes exist in production precisely because they render real terminal output. The base/head split is also the literal thing that makes proof_status meaningful, so the visual teaches the mechanic for free. | TRADEOFF: Terminal text is small and a hackathon room is a bad reading environment — you will need to set the mono at 14-15px minimum in the hero, well above the 13px these sites use, and accept less content on screen. Also, a fake-looking terminal is worse than no terminal: use real captured output from an actual run, including timing lines and a real traceback.
  - (high) Ship Linear's four-layer depth stack as a single reusable `<Surface>` component and use it for EVERY panel on both the site and the dashboard: (1) surface fill from a 5-step ladder, (2) 1px hairline, (3) a grain div at `opacity:.6; mix-blend-mode:overlay; background-size:256px 256px; inset:1px`, (4) a glow div at `radial-gradient(circle, rgba(255,255,255,.04) 0%, transparent 50%)`, 400x400, `mix-blend-mode:lighten`, cursor-tracked. Include the Safari guard `@supports((-webkit-hyphens:none)){ .grain:after{background:0 0} }`. | WHY: This is verbatim from linear.app's shipped CSS and markup — I pulled both the Grain stylesheet and the actual DOM where the grain and glow divs sit as siblings inside every product card. It is the difference between a dark page that looks flat and one that looks lit. It costs about 40 lines of CSS and one React component, and it is the cheapest possible path to 'more expensive than CodeRabbit'. | TRADEOFF: `mix-blend-mode` forces compositing layers; on a page with 20+ surfaces you will want `will-change` discipline and should drop the glow layer under `prefers-reduced-motion`. The grain tile is an image request — inline it as a base64 PNG under ~4KB rather than a separate fetch, and remember Linear's own Safari guard exists because the `:after` white wash misbehaves in WebKit.
  - (high) Use the canonical 10-section grammar and do not invent a new one: (1) hero + one object, (2) logo/social-proof strip immediately below the fold, (3) ONE-SENTENCE problem negation, (4-7) the four mechanics M1-M4 as four alternating mechanism sections, each one H2 + one visual, (8) the 9-stage pipeline as the 'how it works' section, (9) the accuracy self-report as the proof section, (10) final CTA restating the hero in five words. Write H2s as full sentences, not labels. | WHY: I extracted the H1/H2/H3 order from 20 live sites and the grammar is rigid: 8-11 sections, logos right after the fold, a problem negation at section 2-3 (Depot: 'The bottleneck has shifted from writing code to everything after.'; Turso: 'The database wasn't built for a world where every agent wants its own.'; CodeRabbit: 'Fast code. Faster chaos.'), a 3-step how-it-works, a security grid, and a 5-word final CTA (Linear: 'Built for the future. Available today.'; Resend: 'Email reimagined. Available today.'). Neon and Unkey both write H2s as full sentences and it reads noticeably more confident than label-style headings. PRGuard's problem negation writes itself and it is the positioning line. | TRADEOFF: Following the grammar means the page's shape is familiar, so ALL differentiation has to come from the hero object, the color temperature, and the copy. That is the correct trade — a hackathon judge scrolls in a known rhythm and you want their attention on the claim, not on figuring out your navigation. Risk: with four mechanics plus a pipeline plus an accuracy section, the page gets long. Cap each mechanic section at one screen.
  - (high) Lead with a hard trust number in the hero, not an adjective. Put the accuracy self-report — proven_rate, disproven_rate, execution_verified_precision, self_caught_false_positive_rate, each with its n — into a compact stat strip directly under the fold, and make the DISPROVEN number prominent. 'We publicly refuted N of our own findings' is the most differentiating sentence available to this product. | WHY: The category's documented failure mode is false-positive fatigue, with reported behavioral thresholds where developers treat every finding as real below ~10% FP, call tools noisy at 10-30%, triage with suspicion above 30%, and dismiss by default above 50%. CodeRabbit shipping 'Source lines' (traceability of each comment to its triggering rule) in July 2026 is a competitor tacitly admitting the trust problem. A tool that publishes its own refutation rate is doing something no competitor's marketing does, and it is literally what S7 + /v1/accuracy already produce. Turso's proof section (real numbers inside prose: 'Over 2 million databases created') outperforms a generic stat bar. | TRADEOFF: Publishing self_caught_false_positive_rate invites a judge to ask 'so how often are you wrong?' — you must be ready to answer that being able to answer at all is the product. Also, demo-scale n values will be small; show the n honestly rather than hiding it, since hiding it is exactly the behavior the positioning attacks. Flag: I did not verify the 10/30/50% thresholds against a primary study — treat as directional.
  - (medium) Animate the 9-stage pipeline with a scroll-driven timeline: stages illuminate sequentially as the section scrolls, the active stage pulses with Warp's `@keyframes factory-run-pulse{0%,to{opacity:0;transform:scale(.85)}50%{opacity:1;transform:scale(1.06)}}`, and the connecting rail fills. Implement with CSS `animation-timeline: view()` as progressive enhancement behind an IntersectionObserver + Motion fallback. | WHY: The pipeline is PRGuard's 'how it works' section and a 9-stage DAG is the natural visual. Scroll-driven CSS is the right engine because it is GPU-composited and jank-free, but support is Chrome/Edge 115+, Safari 18+ (Safari 26 shipped Sept 2025, threaded in 26.4), while Firefox is still behind `layout.css.scroll-driven-animations.enabled` in stable as of Firefox 152 (June 2026), at roughly 84% global. A demo laptop running Firefox with a dead pipeline animation would be a self-inflicted wound. Motion (npm `motion`, v13.4.0, import from `motion/react`) is the safe fallback; GSAP is also now fully free including all plugins since v3.13 under Webflow's ownership if you need SplitText. | TRADEOFF: Two implementations of the same animation is duplicated work. Cheaper alternative: skip scroll-driven CSS entirely and use Motion's `useInView` + staggered variants for everything — slightly less silky, one code path, and it is what Trigger.dev and Inngest actually do. Given hackathon time pressure I would take the single Motion path and spend the saved hours on the proof terminal.
  - (high) Use a fully SIL-OFL type stack: Inter or Geist for UI, Geist Mono or Commit Mono or JetBrains Mono for all code and all numerics, and optionally Instrument Serif or Manrope for one display accent. Set the mono with tabular figures everywhere a severity score, a token count, a cost, or a hash appears. Do NOT ship Berkeley Mono. | WHY: Almost every site that looks expensive is paying a foundry — Warp uses 'The Future' + Matter, Resend uses ABC Favorit, Depot uses Neue Montreal, e2b uses PP Supply Sans + Neue Haas, Clerk uses Suisse + Söhne Mono, Mintlify uses ABC Arizona Flare, Speakeasy uses Tobias + ABC Diatype, Linear ships its own. You cannot buy those for a hackathon and you should not pirate them. Supabase proves a 100% free stack (Inter + Manrope + Source Code Pro) can carry a multi-billion-dollar brand. Berkeley Mono is the one every developer covets but it is paid (~$75 personal tier at usgraphics.com) with commercial restrictions, so it is a licensing landmine on a public site. | TRADEOFF: Inter and Geist are the two most-used faces in the category, so the type alone will not differentiate you — the color temperature, the hero object and the copy have to. If you want one point of type distinction at zero cost, set the display line in Geist Mono (as several 2026 sites do for headings) rather than reaching for a serif; it reinforces the instrument-grade read. Verify every font's license file before shipping.
  - (medium) Give M2 (computed severity) the marketing site's single best custom visual: a six-axis radar over reachability .20 / blast_radius .15 / path_sensitivity .25 / coverage_gap .15 / churn .10 / proof_confidence .15, with the weights printed as small mono labels on each axis and the band threshold rings (.15 / .35 / .55 / .75) drawn as concentric hairlines. | WHY: The brief is right that this is inherently visualisable, and it is the one thing in the product that no competitor can show because no competitor has a deterministic score. It is also the perfect antidote to 'AI slop' suspicion: printing the weights and the thresholds on the chart is a visible commitment to determinism. Structurally this is Turso's move — argue with a diagram rather than list features. Build it as inline SVG so it is crisp, themeable via currentColor, and animatable by scroll. | TRADEOFF: Radar charts are genuinely poor for comparing magnitudes across axes and a data-viz-literate judge may say so. Mitigate by pairing the radar with a horizontal weighted-bar breakdown showing the arithmetic (input x weight = contribution, summing to the score) directly beneath it — the radar is the poster, the bars are the proof. Do not use the radar in the dashboard's dense finding list; use a sparkline-style stacked bar there.
  - (high) Build the dashboard from the SAME token file as the marketing site but with the ornament stripped: keep the surface ladder, the hairlines and the semantic status colors; drop the grain to opacity .3 or remove it, drop the aurora entirely, drop all scroll animation. Use Scalar's three-step ladder (#0f0f0f / #1a1a1a / #272727, border #2d2d2d) as the density model and Mintlify's alpha borders (rgba(255,255,255,.08) and .1) rather than hex borders. | WHY: The two surfaces have opposite jobs: the site must impress in 3 seconds, the dashboard must be readable for 3 hours. Alpha borders composite correctly over the tinted status backgrounds you will need for proven/disproven/inconclusive rows, where fixed hex hairlines visibly break. Raycast's 2/5/10/36/48px blur stack and Linear's `scale(0.97)` active state plus 100-150ms ease-out transitions are the right interaction budget for a keyboard-fast tool — anything slower fights the 'keyboard-fast' requirement. | TRADEOFF: Sharing a token file means a marketing tweak can regress the dashboard. Guard it by splitting tokens into a `core` layer (ramp, hairlines, semantics, type) that both import and a `marketing` layer (aurora, glow, grain opacity, display sizes) that only the site imports. Slight upfront cost, saves you at 2am before the demo.
  - (high) Write the hero headline as a CLAIM with a verb, not a category label, and put the refutation promise in the subhead. Something in the shape of 'We don't think it's a bug. We proved it.' with a subhead naming the mechanism: 'PRGuard writes a repro test, runs it at base and at head in a sandbox, and attaches the terminal output. Only proven findings can block your merge.' | WHY: Every competitor headline is a noun phrase naming the category: Greptile 'The AI Code Reviewer', Graphite 'The next generation of code review.', cubic 'AI code reviews for complex codebases'. CodeRabbit's is an animated typing gimmick. The elite infra sites that punch above their weight all lead with a claim or a negation — Inngest 'Unbreakable Agents. Invisible Infra.', Turso 'Millions of Databases. One Architecture.', Depot 'Software delivery built for machine scale'. In a room where five teams built an AI code reviewer, a noun phrase is indistinguishable and a verb is memorable. | TRADEOFF: A strong claim invites immediate scrutiny, so the hero object must pay it off within the same viewport — the terminal has to be visible without scrolling, or the headline reads as bluster. Also keep it under ~10 words; the elite set runs 5-9 words in the H1.
  - (medium) For the second demo moment — catching an agent that deleted a test to make CI pass — give it its own full-bleed section with a before/after code pane and a governance stamp, modeled on Inngest's before/after diagrams and Trigger.dev's `needsApproval: true` hero. Frame it as the governance rail (M4) proving itself, not as a feature. | WHY: This is the most viscerally legible thing the product does and it maps onto a real 2026 anxiety about agent-written code. Trigger.dev putting human-in-the-loop approval in their hero code block is evidence the category already treats 'the human stays in charge' as a headline-grade claim rather than a footnote. Structurally, before/after panes are the single clearest way to show a system that prevented something — you cannot photograph an absence, so you have to show the counterfactual. | TRADEOFF: It competes with the proof terminal for the 'signature moment' slot. Resolve it by hierarchy: the proof terminal is the hero, the deleted-test catch is the mechanism section for M4 further down. Trying to make both the hero halves the impact of each.
ANTI-PATTERNS / AVOID:
  - A Three.js / react-three-fiber hero scene. Zero of Linear, Vercel, Clerk, Raycast, Resend, Supabase, Turso, Browserbase and Railway ship one. In 2026 it reads as agency showreel, not infrastructure. For a product whose entire claim is 'we show you the receipts', a floating abstract 3D object is the exact opposite signal. It also eats the build time you need for the proof terminal and it is the single most likely thing to stutter on a judge's machine.
  - The purple-to-blue blob gradient at 40% opacity floating behind a centered headline. This was 2021-2023 and it is now the default output of every AI site generator, which means judges have seen it forty times this month. If you want a hero gradient, construct it the way Clerk does — a 200%x160% radial anchored OFF-CANVAS at -20%/-80% so only the smooth middle of the ramp is visible — or animate background-position the way Warp does. Never a visible elliptical blob with a hard center.
  - Frosted-glass cards with `backdrop-filter: blur(20px)` over a colorful background as the primary design language. The original glassmorphism wave peaked around 2021. Raycast still uses backdrop blur, but at 2/5/10px for chrome and popovers — as UI utility, not as the aesthetic. Glass as the whole identity in 2026 reads as a template.
  - Pure #000000 as the page background. Vercel gets away with it because their entire system is white-at-alpha on top and they have a decade of brand equity. For everyone else it crushes on real displays, kills the surface ladder, and makes hairlines either invisible or harsh. The 2026 consensus base is #07080a - #0f1011. Raycast #07080a, Mintlify #08090a, Linear #0f1011, Depot #080808, Unkey #040406.
  - Pure #ffffff body text on a dark base. Warp uses #f8f5ee. Linear uses #f7f8f8. Vercel's top step is #ededed and its alpha top is #ffffffeb, not fully opaque. Pure white at body size on near-black causes halation and looks cheap at exactly the moment you want it to look expensive.
  - More than one accent hue. Sentry has ONE violet field plus one accent. Linear has ONE #5e6ad2 and declares everything else grayscale. Depot has purple-to-blue and nothing else. A site with a green 'proven', a blue 'info', a purple brand, an orange CTA and a pink highlight is indistinguishable from a Bootstrap theme. Pick one brand accent, then let the SEMANTIC states (proven/disproven/inconclusive) be the only other color on the page — which conveniently is also the product's story.
  - Generic AI-slop illustrations: neural-net node clouds, glowing brains, circuit-board traces, robot mascots, hexagon grids, and 'digital transformation' particle fields. The category is drowning in this. Greptile's hand-drawn Nanum Pen Script annotations are a deliberate rebellion against it and they are the second-biggest player. Every visual on PRGuard's page should be a real product surface or a real diagram of a real mechanism.
  - A noun-phrase headline that names the category. 'AI Code Review, Reimagined.' / 'The AI Code Reviewer.' / 'Next-generation code review.' Greptile, Graphite and cubic all ship exactly this and they are therefore interchangeable at a glance. If your headline would also work on a competitor's site, it is worth zero.
  - Vague trust adjectives with no mechanism attached: 'intelligent', 'context-aware', 'best-in-class', 'enterprise-grade', 'powered by frontier models', 'understands your codebase'. Every competitor says these. PRGuard's entire differentiator is that it can replace adjectives with numbers and artifacts — using the adjectives anyway is throwing away the only advantage.
  - An uncropped, fully-visible product screenshot centered in the hero with a drop shadow. Nobody elite does this. The move is a cropped, top-lit, 16px-radius panel bleeding off an edge under a `mask-image:linear-gradient(#000 0% 45%, #0000 90%)` fade — Raycast ships five variants of exactly that stop pattern, Vercel ships `#000 0% 28%, #0000 88%`.
  - Fast, loud, or looping-fast animation. Resend's animated gradient border runs at 30 SECONDS — almost imperceptible. Linear's glow is 4% white. Warp's aurora is a 4s ease-in-out drift. If a judge can consciously notice the animation as animation, it is too fast. And nothing should bounce: `cubic-bezier(.175,.885,.32,1.275)` appears exactly twice in Clerk's entire stylesheet and nowhere in Linear's.
  - A stat bar of round marketing numbers with no denominators — '10x faster', '99.9% accurate', '1M+ PRs reviewed'. For a product whose thesis is trust, an unsourced stat bar is self-refuting. Turso's approach is correct: real numbers embedded in prose with the context attached ('Over 2 million databases created, one for every prompt'). PRGuard should show proven_rate WITH its n and its definition, exactly as /v1/accuracy already returns them.
  - Berkeley Mono. It is the font every developer wants and it is paid with a restricted commercial license. Shipping it on a public marketing site is a licensing problem for the sake of a detail almost nobody will consciously register. Geist Mono and Commit Mono are free, OFL, and read nearly identically at 14px.
  - Cookie/consent banner styling left at library defaults. Trigger.dev's stylesheet is full of `--cc-*` CookieConsent defaults (#161a1c, #30363c, #242c31) that do not match their brand tokens. It is a small thing that instantly reveals the seams, and a judge who opens the site in a fresh browser sees it FIRST, before the hero.
  - Building the dashboard with the marketing site's ornament intact. Grain at .9 opacity, aurora, cursor glow and scroll animation on a data-dense table with 200 findings is unreadable and slow. Strip to the ladder, the hairlines and the semantics.
  - Treating dark mode itself as the differentiator. CodeRabbit's hero is already #121014 with #1a181c cards. Dark is table stakes in this category — it is the price of entry, not the edge.
FACT-CHECK CORRECTIONS:
    [CORRECTED] Library versions: @paper-design/shaders-react 0.0.81; motion 13.4.0; shiki 4.4.3; lenis 1.3.26; three r184 (2026-04-16); @react-three/fiber 9.7.0 with a 10.0.0 alpha line; @rive-app/react-canvas 4.31.0 -> Checked against registry.npmjs.org on 2026-09-22. CONFIRMED: @paper-design/shaders-react 0.0.81 (single dep @paper-design/shaders 0.0.81); shiki 4.4.3; lenis 1.3.26; @react-three/fiber latest 9.7.0 with dist-tags alpha=10.0.0-alpha.5 and canary=10.0.0-canary.24d1f81. WRONG: motion is 13.4.1, not 13.4.0. three is 0.186.0 (r186), published 2026-09-08 — r184 was real and published 2026-04-16T16:27:55Z but is two releases stale. @rive-app/react-canvas is 4.34.3, not 4.31.0. Net: 3 of 7 version numbers were stale or wrong; treat all of them as needing `npm view` before pinning, exactly as the researcher warned.
    [CORRECTED] Berkeley Mono: $75 personal license tier, commercial use restricted to UI elements, IDE/terminal/text-editor products generally excluded -> usgraphics.com is behind a Cloudflare challenge (403 to both WebFetch and curl), so I verified via a Wayback snapshot of the live product page (June 2026). THE RESTRICTION WORDING IS CONFIRMED ALMOST VERBATIM: 'Commercial use restricted to UI elements only. If you're building an IDE, Terminal app, Text Editor, etc., we generally do not allow it, please contact...'. THE TIER NAME IS WRONG: there is no 'personal license' tier — the tiers are Trial, Developer (Personal Use, 'Commercial use is not covered'), then commercial tiers by headcount: Indie (sole proprietor), Startup (2-7), Medium (8-50), Enterprise (50+). THE $75 IS NOT A PUBLISHED PRICE: prices sit behind 'Purchase →' links; the only '$75' on the page is inside a Tobi Lutke testimonial ('Yes, worth the $75'). A MATERIAL RESTRICTION THE RESEARCHER MISSED: 'Commercial licenses are not compatible with open-source apps.' If PRGuard's marketing site or dashboard is ever open-sourced, Berkeley Mono is disqualified on that ground alone. The 'do not ship Berkeley Mono' recommendation stands and is if anything understated.
    [CORRECTED] Scroll-driven animation support: Chrome/Edge 115+, Safari 18+ (landing in Safari 26 Sept 2025), Firefox still behind layout.css.scroll-driven-animations.enabled in stable as of Firefox 152, ~84% global -> The conservative weighting was right; one number is badly wrong. MDN browser-compat-data for css.properties.animation-timeline AND css.properties.scroll-timeline both give: chrome {version_added: '115'}, edge 'mirror', safari {version_added: '26'}, safari_ios 'mirror', firefox {version_added: 'preview'}. SAFARI 18+ IS WRONG — it is Safari 26, and webstatus.dev dates Safari 26 / Safari iOS 26 availability to 2025-09-15, which confirms the researcher's own parenthetical and contradicts their headline number. FIREFOX CONFIRMED UNSUPPORTED IN STABLE: BCD records 'preview' (Nightly only) with no release version, so the 'universal cross-browser support in 2026' source the researcher saw is simply false. The ~84% FIGURE IS UNSOURCEABLE: caniuse has no feature entry for this at all (css-animation-timeline, scroll-driven-animations and css-scroll-timeline all 404 in the caniuse-db features-json directory), so 'verify on caniuse.com' is not actionable advice. USE THIS INSTEAD: webstatus.dev gives Baseline status 'limited' for feature id scroll-driven-animations — a citable, unambiguous fact that makes the same argument more strongly than a made-up percentage. The recommendation to take the single Motion code path is correct and the evidence for it is stronger than the researcher realised.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] False-positive behavioral thresholds: <10% all findings trusted, 10-30% 'noisy', >30% triaged with suspicion, >50% dismissed by default -> No primary source located. I could not find any study, survey or dataset defining this four-step ladder. The ACM DL and CACM copies of 'Lessons from Building Static Analysis Tools at Google' (the one paper in this space that does publish a hard false-positive bar) both return 403, so I could not even confirm the widely-repeated <10% figure at source. Verdict: do not put any of these four numbers on the site, in the deck, or in the README. The researcher's own flag was correct and should be escalated from 'treat as directional' to 'do not use'. If a threshold number is needed for the pitch, cite nothing and instead state PRGuard's own measured rate with its n — which is the positioning anyway.
    [REFUTED] CodeRabbit shipped a 'Source lines' feature on July 2, 2026 tracing each comment to its triggering rule -> No such feature exists. I pulled the full CodeRabbit changelog (2.6 MB) and 'source line' appears zero times anywhere in it. There ARE six entries dated July 2, 2026, and they are: 'Role management API' (self-hosted instances can apply a role change across every active organization in one API request via org_id: "ALL") and 'Quiet review profile'. DO NOT CITE 'Source lines' ANYWHERE — it is a hallucinated feature from a secondary blog. USE THE REAL ONE INSTEAD, WHICH IS BETTER FOR THE ARGUMENT: the actual July 2, 2026 ship is the Quiet review profile — 'only the most important comments — critical and major issues that are also high-impact — are posted inline. Every other comment is grouped into collapsed sections in the review summary.' That is a competitor shipping comment suppression as a headline feature, i.e. a far more direct admission of false-positive fatigue than rule traceability would have been. The recommendation's rationale survives intact once the citation is swapped.
    [CORRECTED] Sentry's lime accent #c2ef4e is unverified — not found in the CSS pulled; Dammit Sans and Rubik were confirmed -> The researcher under-claimed. #c2ef4e IS in Sentry's live production CSS — 24 occurrences in the concatenated stylesheets from sentry.io, and it is the only lime/chartreuse value in the entire bundle (the nearest other greens are #d1fae5 and #a7f3d0, one occurrence each). The lime is real and can be cited. Also independently re-confirmed in the same pull: 'Dammit Sans' (2), 'Rubik' (23), and the violet ramp — #1f1633 (52), #181225 (35), #150f23 (4), #362d59 (37), #6a5fc1 (74), #4e2a9a (102), #422082 (5), #fd44b0 (35), #f2b712 (7). Every Sentry hex in the reference block checks out.
    [CORRECTED] Linear's hero gradient rgb(8,9,10) 10% to rgb(208,214,224) 100%, and Linear's type scale (display-xl 80px/600/-3.0px letter-spacing), came from third-party scrapers; the surface/border hexes and grain/glow/shine CSS are from Linear's live stylesheets -> Both halves of the researcher's self-assessment are right, and I can now be precise about why. THE HERO GRADIENT IS REFUTED: rgb(8,9,10) appears nowhere. #D0D6E0 (= rgb(208,214,224)) does appear 24 times, but every occurrence is a `stroke="#D0D6E0"` on <path> elements inside an inline SVG diagram in the page body — it is a line colour in an illustration, not a hero background gradient. The scraper sites mangled it. THE TYPE SCALE IS UNVERIFIABLE: no font-size between 64px and 89px appears anywhere in Linear's CSS, and there are no --title-* or display-xl tokens. THE LIVE-CSS CLAIMS ARE CONFIRMED: --bg-base #0f1011, --color-bg-panel #161718, #191d20, #24282c, #2a2e33 and accent #5e6ad2 are all present; scale(.97) appears 9 times.
    [REFUTED] Linear font names: only InterVariable.woff2?v=4.1 confirmed; the mono face and cv01/ss03 OpenType features are unverified -> Stronger than 'unverified' — the extra claims are affirmatively false. InterVariable.woff2?v=4.1 from static.linear.app is the ONLY font file linear.app preloads; there is no second font URL in the document and no @font-face src for any mono face in the stylesheet bundle. 'cv01' and 'ss03' appear ZERO times in Linear's CSS, so the OpenType feature-settings claim is fabricated. 'Berkeley' appears zero times in linear.app's HTML. There are no custom 'Linear Display/Text/Mono' font files. Linear's mono is `font-family: var(--font-monospace)` (69 declarations) resolving to a generic system monospace stack — i.e. Linear does not ship a paid or custom mono at all. This actually strengthens the free-type-stack recommendation: the site the researcher holds up as the ceiling is running one variable Inter file plus system mono.
    [CORRECTED] Warp's --gray-chroma and hue values were not captured — the extraction regex truncated before the hue argument; the technique is confirmed but the numbers are not -> I recovered the missing values. Warp's live CSS declares --gray-chroma: .007 and --gray-hue: 220. The ramp is --gray-950: oklch(7% var(--gray-chroma) ...) through --gray-900: oklch(12% ...) to --gray-50: oklch(97% ...), exactly as described. Hue 220 is a blue cast, which is direct quantitative support for the 'own COLD' recommendation rather than an inference from hex inspection. These two numbers are the single most directly reusable thing in the whole report — PRGuard can adopt chroma .007 / hue 220 as a starting point and retune from one variable. Everything else in the Warp block also verified: @keyframes factory-run-pulse{0%,to{opacity:0;transform:scale(.85)}50%{opacity:1;transform:scale(1.06)}} is present VERBATIM; scanner-fluid appears 4 times; the aurora stops #b978ff8c and #8c4bfa6b are both present; --color-background #121212 (26), --color-surface #08090a (3), --color-foreground #f8f5ee (12), --color-muted-foreground #a5a39d (5), --color-accent-purple #cbb0f7 (2), --color-theme-accent #00c2ff (12), #7267ff (1).
    [CORRECTED] Rive pricing: free tier, $9/mo Cadet plan (annual), paid plans from $24, export to production requires a paid plan, runtimes open-source with no runtime fees -> Checked rive.app/pricing directly. CONFIRMED: there is a Free tier ($0/seat/mo, 3 collaborative files, no export); Cadet IS $9/seat/mo and is the entry paid tier; export IS gated — Free has no .riv export, Cadet unlocks unlimited files and .riv export. WRONG: 'paid plans from $24' — the next tier up is Voyager at $32/seat/mo (25-seat max), and above it Enterprise at $120/seat/mo ($10M+ annual revenue). The $24 figure is stale or invented. UNVERIFIED: the pricing page does not state that the runtimes are open-source or that there are no runtime fees — it only lists the runtimes (JavaScript, Flutter, iOS, Android, React, React Native, C++, Defold, Unity, Unreal) as included features. Do not repeat 'no runtime fees' without checking the runtime repos' licenses. Note also Cadet caps at 3 seats, which matters for a team demo.
    [CORRECTED] Modal's ANSI palette (#cc342b, #198844, #fba922, #3971ed, #a36ac7, #c5c8c6, #969896) matches the Tomorrow Night theme — hexes are from live CSS, the attribution is an inference -> The hexes are perfect; the attribution is half wrong. THE CSS IS CONFIRMED EXACTLY, including the aliasing the researcher described: .ansi-black-fg{#1d1f21} .ansi-red-fg{#cc342b} .ansi-green-fg{#198844} .ansi-yellow-fg{#fba922} .ansi-blue-fg{#3971ed} .ansi-magenta-fg{#a36ac7} .ansi-white-fg{#c5c8c6} .ansi-bright-black-fg{#969896}, with bright-red/green/yellow/blue aliasing to the same values and .ansi-cyan-fg and .ansi-bright-cyan-fg both resolving to #3971ed. @keyframes v2-glow-pulse{0%,to{opacity:.3}50%{opacity:.85}} confirmed verbatim. THE ATTRIBUTION IS WRONG FOR THE CHROMATIC COLOURS: against the canonical base16 scheme, Tomorrow Night is base00 #1d1f21, base03 #969896, base05 #c5c8c6 — so the three GREYS match exactly — but Tomorrow Night's chromatic slots are #cc6666 red, #b5bd68 green, #f0c674 yellow, #81a2be blue, #b294bb purple, none of which match Modal. Tomorrow (light) is #c82829/#718c00/#eab700/#4271ae/#8959a8 — also no match. Modal's five chromatic values come from somewhere else (the .ansi-*-fg class naming is the convention nbconvert/JupyterLab emit, which is the likeliest origin). Use the hexes, drop the 'Tomorrow Night' label from any copy.
    [CORRECTED] Turso's section order and Neon's eyebrow micro-labels ('WHERE STARTUPS START', 'WHERE AGENT PLATFORMS DEPLOY', 'WHERE THE FORTUNE 500 SCALES') -> TURSO IS FULLY CONFIRMED — the H2 sequence on the live page is, in order: 'The database wasn't built for a world where every agent wants its own.' / 'Five properties of the architecture, not features layered on top.' / 'From zero to a database per entity in under a minute.' / 'Replicate to the device. Sync to the cloud. One database, everywhere it needs to be.' / 'The pattern is the same. The use cases are everywhere.' / 'Built for modern enterprise architecture.' / 'Start where you already are. Scale to where you're going.' / 'Built in the open.' / 'Build with the database you'll never outgrow.' Two of these are slightly longer than quoted (the researcher truncated the 4th and 7th). 'Over 2 million databases created' confirmed present. NEON'S EYEBROWS ARE REFUTED — 'WHERE STARTUPS START' and 'WHERE AGENT PLATFORMS DEPLOY' both return zero hits in any casing on neon.tech. The site has been rewritten; the current equivalent H2 is 'Scale from your first users to the Fortune 500. Startups ship on the same Neon primitives that enterprise teams run in production.' Neon's full-sentence-H2 claim IS confirmed verbatim for both quoted examples ('Not just a Database. Neon is a complete backend platform with Authentication, Object Storage, Functions and an AI Gateway.' and 'Ready for coding agents. Create and branch environments the way you work with PRs, with full access via MCP, CLI, and SDK.'), so the section-grammar recommendation survives — only the eyebrow examples are stale.
VERIFIER ADDITIONS:
    + BIGGEST SINGLE WIN: Warp's missing numbers are recoverable and I got them — --gray-chroma: .007 and --gray-hue: 220. The 'own COLD' recommendation can now be implemented literally rather than approximated from hex inspection, and hue 220 is quantitative proof of the blue cast the recommendation is built on.
    + THE ONE CITATION THAT MUST BE REPLACED: 'CodeRabbit shipped Source lines on July 2, 2026' is a hallucinated feature (zero occurrences of 'source line' in a 2.6 MB changelog dump). The real July 2, 2026 ship is the 'Quiet review profile', which suppresses all but critical/major high-impact comments into collapsed summary sections. That is a STRONGER piece of evidence for the false-positive-fatigue thesis, so swap the citation rather than dropping the argument.
    + THE ONE NUMBER THAT MUST BE DROPPED ENTIRELY: the 10/30/50% false-positive behavioural ladder has no locatable primary source, and caniuse has no entry for scroll-driven animations either, so '~84% global' is equally unsourceable. Replace the browser-support number with the citable fact: webstatus.dev gives feature id scroll-driven-animations a Baseline status of 'limited', with Firefox absent from browser_implementations entirely.
    + SAFARI VERSION ERROR WORTH CATCHING BEFORE IT REACHES A JUDGE: the report says 'Safari 18+' in one breath and 'Safari 26, Sept 2025' in the next. MDN BCD and webstatus.dev both say Safari 26, available 2025-09-15. An internal contradiction like this in a technical claim is exactly what a sharp reviewer picks at.
    + LINEAR'S GRAIN TILE IS 696 KB, NOT 4 KB. The recommendation tells you to inline it as a base64 PNG 'under ~4KB'; Linear's actual grain-default.mxaw2efG.png is 696,402 bytes served from static.linear.app. The advice to keep it small is right but the implied precedent is backwards — generate your own tileable noise, or use an inline SVG feTurbulence and skip the image request entirely.
    + THE 'NOBODY SHIPS CANVAS' FRAMING IS SLIGHTLY OVERSTATED AND EASY TO ATTACK. trigger.dev ships 23 canvas elements, mintlify.com 15 canvas plus 8 Rive, sentry.io 1 canvas and 6 webgl references. None of these is a hero object, so the recommendation survives, but state it as 'elite dark sites don't put WebGL in the hero' rather than 'elite dark sites don't use canvas' — the stronger claim is falsifiable in about ninety seconds.
    + BEST PRECEDENT THE REPORT ALREADY HAD BUT DIDN'T USE: CodeRabbit's single decorative canvas (gridShimmerCanvas) ships alongside a shimmer-fallback.svg, and e2b's WebGL hero ships a pixelated raster fallback behind a webgl-off: utility class. Both are direct, citable precedents for the 'enhancement, never load-bearing' rule — from the site to beat and from the one site that does ship WebGL.
    + GSAP IS FREE BUT NOT OPEN SOURCE. The npm license field is the literal string "Standard 'no charge' license", and the license prohibits use in tools that let users visually build animations in competition with Webflow. Harmless for a landing page; worth knowing if PRGuard's roadmap ever includes an animation or visual-config editor. Also, latest GSAP is 3.15.0 (2026-04-13), not 3.13.
    + BERKELEY MONO HAS A SECOND LANDMINE THE REPORT MISSED: beyond the UI-elements-only restriction, the license page states 'Commercial licenses are not compatible with open-source apps.' If any part of PRGuard is open-sourced for the hackathon — which is common — Berkeley Mono is disqualified on that ground alone, independent of price. Also note the tier is called 'Developer (Personal Use)' and explicitly says 'Commercial use is not covered'; the $75 figure appears on the page only inside a customer testimonial, not as a listed price.
    + RIVE'S SECOND TIER IS $32/seat/mo (Voyager, 25-seat cap), not $24, and Cadet at $9 caps at THREE seats. If the plan is 'one person builds the Rive assets', Cadet works; if two designers and an engineer all need editor access, you are at the seat cap immediately. The pricing page also does NOT state that the runtimes are open-source or free of runtime fees — verify in the runtime repos before repeating that.
    + THREE.JS IS TWO RELEASES STALE IN THE REPORT (r186, published 2026-09-08, is current; r184's 2026-04-16 date was correct but superseded), motion is 13.4.1 not 13.4.0, and @rive-app/react-canvas is 4.34.3 not 4.31.0. Since the report's own advice is to pin exact versions, shipping it with three stale pins would be self-undermining.
    + LINEAR IS A BETTER ARGUMENT FOR THE FREE TYPE STACK THAN THE REPORT REALISES. The report lists Linear under 'ships its own' fonts. It does not: linear.app preloads exactly one font file, InterVariable.woff2?v=4.1, has no @font-face for any mono, and sets code in var(--font-monospace) resolving to a system stack. cv01 and ss03 appear zero times. The ceiling site in this survey runs one variable Inter plus system mono — use that when selling the free-stack recommendation.
    + SENTRY'S LIME IS REAL — #c2ef4e appears 24 times in sentry.io's live CSS and is the only lime value in the bundle. The report flagged it as unverified and under-claimed. If a lime/chartreuse 'proven' accent is under consideration, there is a shipped precedent at a company with a comparable audience.
    + METHOD NOTE FOR ANY FOLLOW-UP: every CSS claim in this report was checkable in minutes by fetching the homepage, extracting <link href="*.css">, concatenating and grepping. The claims that failed verification are, without exception, the ones sourced from third-party token-scraper sites (designmd.cc, awesome-design-md), secondary blogs, or design-system aggregators — Linear's hero gradient, Linear's type scale, Linear's cv01/ss03, CodeRabbit's 'Source lines', Neon's eyebrows, Rive's $24, Berkeley Mono's tier name. The live-CSS claims were right essentially every time. Apply that split as a confidence heuristic to anything in the report I did not reach.

### AREA: Awards + curation gallery sweep for dark/3D/cinematic tech sites (awards-galleries)
FINDINGS:
  - THE BRIEF'S CORE ASSUMPTION IS NOW FALSE: CodeRabbit's marketing site is already DARK as of 2026-09-22. Measured body background = lab(4.9349 1.26021 -1.99696) = #111014, Geist at weight 500, h1 64px / line-height 68px / letter-spacing -3%, with a per-character rotating verb in the headline ('The future isn't writing code. It's reviewing it. / securing it. / prioritizing it.'). They also already ship film grain: an inline data-URI SVG, 180x180 viewBox, feTurbulence type='fractalNoise' baseFrequency='1.16' numOctaves='4' stitchTiles='stitch', used as both background-image and mask-image. 'Be dark and grainy' does not beat them.
  - QODO HAS ALREADY TAKEN THE WORD 'GOVERNANCE' VERBATIM. qodo.ai h1 (measured live) reads: 'Code review for agents. Governance for humans.' — Figtree 500, 72px / 73.44px line-height, letter-spacing -1.44px, on #111013. PRGuard cannot lead with governance as the headline noun. Lead with PROOF (proven/disproven), which nobody in the category is claiming.
  - The AI-code-review category is now 5-of-6 dark and converging on the same near-black: CodeRabbit #111014, Graphite #0A0A0A (Tailwind neutral-950, text #FAFAFA, borders #ffffff1a), cubic.dev #060707, Qodo #111013, CodeAnt (Framer, h1 tint #8B8BF5). Only Greptile is light: #E9E9E9 with Anybody 700 at 96px/96px, ls -2.4px, ink #3D3B4F, DM Sans body. Dark is table stakes, not differentiation — the differentiation has to come from hue, type and the signature moment.
  - EMPIRICAL LIBRARY CENSUS of 21 Awwwards Sites of the Day, 23 Aug – 22 Sep 2026 (grepped from initial HTML): lenis 13/21, motion (motion.dev/Framer Motion) 9/21, Rive 7/21, GSAP 3/21, Three.js 3/21, Lottie 2/21. GSAP + Three.js is NO LONGER the award stack — Lenis + Motion + Rive is. Rive now outnumbers Lottie 7:2 and outnumbers GSAP 7:3 in current winners.
  - PRELOADERS ARE BACK BUT ONLY AT ~38%: 8 of the same 21 winners ship a loader element — illoca.unseen.co ('loader'), pensatori-irrazionali.com ('loader-wrapper' on bg-[#111111]), trevornoah.com ('preloader_wrap'), era-residence.com ('master-preloader theme_on-dark'), unitedcarriers.com, seasats.com ('Preloader_preloader__6jxhM'), cerebrium.ai ('c-initial-loader'), why.zero.university ('webgl-loader-overlay'). Note the last one's class name: loaders survive where they are FUNCTIONAL (gating a WebGL asset payload), not decorative.
  - CUSTOM CURSORS ARE DEAD IN THE WINNER SET: only 2 of 21 (lxlcreative.co.uk, unitedcarriers.com) ship a custom-cursor element. Every other 'cursor' hit was a Tailwind `cursor-pointer` utility. SOUND IS COMPLETELY DEAD: 0 of 21 winners contain an <audio> element, .mp3 reference, or Howler/Tone.js. Do not build either.
  - MOST 'MOTION' ON EXPENSIVE DARK PRODUCT SITES IS PRE-RENDERED MP4, NOT REAL-TIME 3D. Resend (#000) serves cube.mp4, 3d-broadcast.mp4, 3d-react.mp4, 3d-control.mp4 and has ZERO canvases. Modal (#000) has 8 videos + 2 canvases. Dimension.dev (#0C0C0C) has 8 videos, 0 canvases. Linear (#08090A) has 0 canvases and 0 videos above the fold. The perceived motion budget is bought with Blender renders and CSS, not WebGL.
  - LINEAR'S EXACT 'EXPENSIVE' RECIPE, EXTRACTED FROM PRODUCTION CSS: base #08090A, --bg-base #0f1011, --bg-shade #191d20, --color-bg-panel #161718, text #F7F8F8; Inter Variable at font-weight 510 (not 500 — a variable-axis value); h1 64px/64px, ls -1.408px (-0.022em); card edge = `box-shadow: inset 0 0 0 1px #ffffff08, inset 0 1px #ffffff0a, 0 0 0 1px #0009, 0 4px 4px #0000...` (white at 3% all round + 4% top highlight = the glass bevel); ambient panel fill `radial-gradient(50% 50%, #ffffff0a 0%, #fff0 90%)`; mouse-tracked border `radial-gradient(ellipse var(--edge-glow-width,50%) var(--edge-glow-height,50%) at var(--edge-highlight-x,0) var(--edge-highlight-y,0), var(--edge-glow-tint))`; header `backdrop-filter: saturate(1.8) blur(20px)`; grain is a static PNG tile (grain-default.*.png) on a `.grainOverlay` — NOT feTurbulence; one dominant easing, cubic-bezier(.32,.72,0,1), used 7x.
  - THE THREE SIGNATURE EASINGS OF 2026 DARK UI, BY MEASURED FREQUENCY: cubic-bezier(.32,.72,0,1) — Linear (7x) and e2b.dev (6x); cubic-bezier(.23,1,.32,1) (expo-out) — Raycast (12x), CodeRabbit (11x), e2b (3x); cubic-bezier(.16,1,.3,1) (expo-out, softer) — Warp (8x), Raycast (8x), Chainguard (4x). Raycast's second-most-used is the unusual cubic-bezier(.4,0,.22,.96) at 10x. Using Tailwind's default ease-in-out (.4,0,.2,1) everywhere is the single clearest tell of a cheap site.
  - NOBODY EXPENSIVE SETS THE HEADLINE IN THEIR BODY FONT ANYMORE. Warp h1 = matterMono (a MONOSPACE display) 56px/58.8px ls -2px. Railway h1 = IBM Plex Serif 500, 54px/60.48px, ls -1.96px (-0.036em), on #13111C. Resend h1 = Domaine (serif) 400 at 96px/96px. Modal h1 = 'Goga'. Cursor uses a bespoke 'CursorGothic' on a WARM #14120B. Meanwhile Geist-for-everything is the category uniform: CodeRabbit, cubic, basement.studio, Dimension, USAvionix, trigger.dev all use Geist.
  - VERCEL'S GEIST TOKENS, READ LIVE OFF :root: --ds-background-100 hsla(0,0%,4%,1) = #0A0A0A; --ds-background-200 #000; --ds-gray-100 hsl(0 0% 10%) = #1A1A1A; --ds-gray-1000 hsl(0 0% 93%) = #EDEDED; --ds-blue-900 oklch(71.7% 0.1648 250.794); and the famous hairline --ds-shadow-border = `0 0 0 1px #ffffff25`. Their h1 is GeistSans 400 at 64px/64px with letter-spacing -3.84px — that is -0.06em, far tighter than anyone's default.
  - THE ONE 3D-FEELING TECHNIQUE THAT IS ACTUALLY HACKATHON-CHEAP IS AN SVG DISPLACEMENT REVEAL, AND GRAPHITE ALREADY SHIPS IT ON THE DIAMOND PAGE: `<feTurbulence baseFrequency='0.02 0.01' numOctaves='3' seed='1'><animate attributeName='baseFrequency' values='0 0;0.02 0.01' dur='1.2s' fill='freeze' begin='indefinite'/></feTurbulence>` feeding `<feDisplacementMap scale='15' xChannelSelector='R' yChannelSelector='G'>` over an 800x300 graphic, triggered from JS via beginElement(). Zero libraries, ~1.2s, reads as a thing materialising out of noise.
  - @paper-design/shaders-react is the correct 2026 answer to 'cinematic without Three.js': v0.0.81 published 2026-09-17, Apache-2.0, 3,471 GitHub stars, zero-dependency canvas shaders. It ships GodRays, GrainGradient, MeshGradient, StaticMeshGradient, Dithering, PulsingBorder, Metaballs, LiquidMetal, GemSmoke, SmokeRing, Voronoi, NeuroNoise, Warp, ColorPanels, DotOrbit, DotGrid, HalftoneDots, ImageDithering, FlutedGlass, LensDistortion, PaperTexture. GodRays + GrainGradient + PulsingBorder alone covers a cinematic dark hero in an afternoon.
  - EXACT TOOLING STATE ON 2026-09-22 (npm registry, verified): next 16.3.6 MIT; tailwindcss 4.3.3 MIT (docs are v4.3, palette is oklch: --color-neutral-950 oklch(14.5% 0 0), --color-zinc-950 oklch(14.1% 0.005 285.823), --color-slate-950 oklch(12.9% 0.042 264.695)); motion 13.4.1 MIT (142,690 B min / 47,727 B gzip for the full package, 2 deps); framer-motion 13.4.1 MIT (same version, it is the alias); lenis 1.3.26 MIT (now darkroomengineering, the old @studio-freight/lenis is frozen at 1.0.42 since 2024-03-17); three 0.186.0 MIT; @react-three/fiber 9.7.0; @react-three/drei 10.7.8; gsap 3.15.0 under the free 'Standard no-charge license'; @rive-app/react-canvas 4.34.3 MIT; shiki 4.4.3; @xyflow/react 12.11.6; @tanstack/react-table 9.2.4; @number-flow/react 0.6.2; cmdk 1.1.1; sonner 2.0.8; nuqs 2.10.1; recharts 3.10.1; ogl 1.0.11 (Unlicense).
  - PLATFORM REALITY CHECK, FROM THE WEB-FEATURES EXPLORER: View Transitions reached Baseline 'Newly Available' on 2025-10-14 (Chrome 111+, Safari 18+, Firefox 144+) — safe to use with a progressive-enhancement guard, and only ~1.34% of page loads use it, so it still reads as novel. CSS scroll-driven animations are NOT Baseline: blocked by Firefox since September 2025, supported only in Chrome/Edge 115+ and Safari 26+. Do not build the 9-stage pipeline scroll on animation-timeline alone.
  - AWWWARDS' PUBLISHED SCORING WEIGHTS ARE Design 40% / Usability 30% / Creativity 20% / Content 10%. Usability+Content = 40%, equal to Design. A data-dense, legible, fast product site is NOT handicapped against a WebGL showpiece under this rubric — and B2B SaaS is chronically under-represented in the winner pool (the SOTD sample above is overwhelmingly agency, portfolio, luxury, architecture and defense).
  - THE CURATION LANDSCAPE ITSELF MOVED: godly.website now 301-redirects to recent.design, which is a component/effect-level gallery (Web Interface, 3D, Motion, OG images, app icons) rather than a whole-site gallery. If the owner is still mentally referencing 'Godly', that reference point no longer exists in the same form. saaslandingpage.com's dark tag is the most PRGuard-relevant gallery and surfaces Linear, Resend, Oxide, Evervault, Volta, Dimension, Shape, Frame.io.
  - TINTED FOREGROUNDS, NOT WHITE, ARE A CHEAP EXPENSIVE-SIGNAL: Modal's h1 color is rgb(221,255,220) = #DDFFDC (mint-white) on #000, not #FFF. Linear's is #F7F8F8. Graphite's is #FAFAFA. Cursor's whole base is warm olive-black #14120B. Pure #FFF on pure #000 is the most common giveaway of a template.
