  - (medium) Give the audit trail and the accuracy self-report a light theme, even though everything else is dark. The audit chain and the /v1/accuracy page are the two surfaces a human will export, print, screenshot into a compliance ticket or paste into a Slack thread — so ship a light variant and an 'Export evidence' action that renders the finding + proof terminal + severity breakdown + audit segment as a single paginated document. | WHY: The governance story is not complete until the evidence leaves the product. A dark-only audit trail is a tell that the audit trail is a demo prop rather than something anyone actually has to hand to a reviewer. It is also a strong differentiating beat in a hackathon pitch: 'here is the PDF you give your auditor', which nobody else in this category will show. | TRADEOFF: Doubles the theming work on those two surfaces and forces the token system to be genuinely semantic (surface/foreground pairs) rather than hardcoded dark values — which you want anyway. Print CSS is fiddly and easy to leave half-done under hackathon time pressure.
  - (medium) Draw the four mechanics as four bespoke line-art marks in a single, self-consistent stroke language — 1.5px strokes, butt caps, miter joins, a strict 24px grid, no rounded terminals — and use Lucide (lucide-react 1.47.0, ISC) only for utility chrome. Do not use a sparkle, a brain, a robot, a shield-with-a-tick, a magic wand, a chat bubble or a neural-net node graph anywhere in the product. The proof mark should read as a test tube or a calibration mark, the severity mark as a weighing scale or a level, the fingerprint mark as an actual ridge pattern, the governance mark as a seal or a chain link. | WHY: 925studios names 'thin, interchangeable line icons that could illustrate any product' as a tell, and the sparkle/orb/chat-bubble trio is the category's most-parodied set. Four owned marks is a genuinely small amount of work (a few hours) that gives the brand something no generated site will have, and the strokes can be reused as the pipeline diagram's node glyphs so the whole system reads as one hand. | TRADEOFF: Bespoke marks by a non-illustrator can end up worse than Lucide defaults. If drawing them is not realistic, pick Phosphor (@phosphor-icons/react 2.1.10, MIT) at its 'Bold' or 'Fill' weight instead of Lucide — it is less used in this category and its heavier weights read more mechanical, which is closer to the brief than Lucide's uniform 2px softness.
ANTI-PATTERNS / AVOID:
  - The indigo-to-purple gradient, in any form — background wash, gradient text, gradient button, gradient border, gradient icon. It is traceable to Tailwind's indigo-500 default (now oklch(58.5% 0.233 277.117)) and is named by three separate 2026 sources as the loudest AI tell of the year. In a room of AI code-review demos it is camouflage, not branding.
  - The glowing orb / soft intelligence blob / iridescent morphing sphere. Every WebGL hero template on Framer and every 'AI SaaS' UI kit ships one. For a product whose thesis is 'we prove things', a featureless glowing sphere is an actively self-defeating metaphor: it is the visual form of an unfalsifiable claim.
  - The four-point sparkle. It now means 'this button calls an LLM'. PRGuard's differentiator is that it does NOT just call an LLM — it executes a test in a container. Using the sparkle concedes the exact ground you are fighting for.
  - The fake chat bubble / fake conversation thread in the hero. Also the fake 'AI is thinking...' shimmer. The product is not a chat product and the finding is not an opinion.
  - The bright radial halo bloom on a dark page — a 40%-opacity blurred ellipse of accent colour behind the hero headline. Named by impeccable.style as a familiar AI background effect. It also bands badly on 8-bit displays unless you grain it, which most people do not.
  - Frosted glass panels floating over a cinematic photograph. This is the single clearest Dribbble-only signal in the 2026 dashboard corpus (Juice Lab's Golfio/Realto/Fitness, Orbix's OpsPulse). It dies the instant the panel has to contain real scrolling data: contrast collapses and backdrop-filter repaints every frame.
  - Shipping shadcn's default dark theme untouched — oklch(0.145 0 0) background with the indigo/emerald/amber/purple/rose chart ramp. It is now as recognisable as Bootstrap blue was in 2014, and its chart-2/3/4 are literally Tailwind's emerald-500, amber-500 and purple-500.
  - A row of three equal-width rounded cards with soft shadows and thin-line icons. The most-named layout reflex of 2026. Any equal three-up feature grid should be broken into an asymmetric rhythm or deleted.
  - Full-page bento. At ~67% adoption among top SaaS landing pages and default for new B2B launches since mid-2026, a wall of bento tiles now reads as 'we used the current template'. One asymmetric bento block is fine; a page made of them is not.
  - Neumorphism and neo-brutalism. Both are surfacing in 2026 Figma Community kits (thick black borders, hard offset shadows, flat pastel blocks; or soft inset/outset shadows). Neumorphism is a documented accessibility failure at low contrast; neo-brutalism's pastel-and-thick-black language reads as playful and cheap, which is the precise opposite of 'this finding blocks your merge'.
  - A pulsing status dot that pulses when nothing is happening. Named slop. In THIS product it is worse than slop — it is a lie, because the backend emits a real heartbeat and the dot should be driven by it.
  - Auto-scrolling logo marquees and auto-advancing testimonial carousels. Named slop; they also force the reader to read at the page's pace, which is the opposite of the 'keyboard-fast, human in charge' positioning.
  - Pure #000000 backgrounds with pure #FFFFFF text. 21:1 contrast, maximum halation, painful for the ~1-in-3 users with astigmatism, and on OLED it smears during scroll. Linear's #08090a / #f7f8f8 is the correct register.
  - Drop shadows on cards in a dark data grid. On dark, shadow reads as smudge. Depth on dark comes from lightness (Material's 0%->16% white overlay model) and from a 1px hairline, not from blur.
  - Radar/spider charts for the six severity inputs. It is the obvious move and it is wrong: severity is a weighted SUM and a radar encodes by area, so it will systematically misrepresent the score the product's entire credibility rests on.
  - Inter at default settings as the only typeface. Named the number-one AI tell. If you keep Inter, you must at least ship cv01/ss03 and tabular-nums/slashed-zero — otherwise the product's numbers are visibly less rigorous than its claims.
  - Proportional figures anywhere a number can change: severity scores, costs, token counts, latencies, hash prefixes, seq numbers. Jittering digits are the fastest way to make an 'instrumented' product feel improvised.
  - Warm purple-grey neutrals with an orange accent. That is CodeRabbit's exact palette (#332f39 family + #f04006). Landing near it makes you look like a CodeRabbit skin regardless of how good your product is.
  - A dark-only audit trail with no export. If the evidence cannot leave the product as a document a human can hand to someone else, the governance story is set dressing.
  - Em-dashes in every sentence, plus 'supercharge', 'world-class', 'unlock', 'seamlessly' and 'Build faster. Ship smarter.'-shaped headlines. Copy slop is read as design slop by anyone who has reviewed forty hackathon sites that week.
  - More than one WebGL context, or any WebGL at all for the pipeline diagram. Browsers cap contexts around 16 per page, a Three.js scene costs the mobile frame budget, and Codrops' own 2026 guidance is that if a linear gradient solves it, WebGL is not worth the runtime.
  - Mock data that is obviously mock — 'Lorem Corp', findings numbered 1/2/3, a perfectly even severity distribution, a 100% proven rate. For a product selling evidential rigour, fake-looking evidence in the demo is fatal. The accuracy self-report in particular must show a non-trivial disproven_rate, because publicly refuting yourself is the differentiator.
FACT-CHECK CORRECTIONS:
    [CORRECTED] Berkeley Mono pricing unverifiable: 'starting from $75', Developer (personal/non-commercial) vs Commercial tiers scaled by company size; unknown whether a webfont licence is included; usgraphics.com 403s -> I got through via a real browser (curl/WebFetch still 403 — that part of their report is honest). Full current table, typeface TX-02, font v2.004, released 2025-11-03: Trial FX-050 (free); Developer FX-102 $75, Commercial Use: NO; Indie FX-202 (sole proprietor) $225; Startup FX-400 (2-7 employees) $495; Standard Business FX-600 (8-50) $845; Enterprise (50+) separate. WEBFONTS ARE NOT INCLUDED IN ANY TIER — MX-0xx Web Fonts is a paid add-on: +$45 (Developer), +$145 (Indie), +$295 (Startup), and at Standard Business it becomes a SUBSCRIPTION at $45/month. Variable Fonts is also a separate add-on (+$25/$75/$145/$275). So the real number for a small commercial team shipping it on the web is $790 (FX-400 $495 + web $295), not $75; $75 is the non-commercial desktop-only tier. Tier names are Developer/Indie/Startup/Standard Business/Enterprise, not 'Developer/Commercial'.
    [CORRECTED] Linear's Berkeley Mono may be a local-only fallback for staff machines rather than a served webfont -> Linear genuinely serves it. layout.B05Dfi6O.css contains @font-face{font-family:Berkeley Mono;font-style:normal;font-weight:100 900;font-display:swap;src:url(https://static.linear.app/fonts/Berkeley-Mono-Variable.woff2?v=3.2)format("woff2")}. The file returns HTTP 200, content-length 160932, last-modified 2025-02-26. It is the VARIABLE cut, so Linear bought Master Fonts + Variable Fonts + Web Fonts at Enterprise tier. Linear also serves InterVariable.woff2?v=4.1 (Inter 4.x).
    [CORRECTED] GSAP's licence is not OSI; 'free for commercial use' comes from Webflow's blog; the no-charge grant historically excluded reselling GSAP or embedding it in a competing product/tool -> Free commercial use is CONFIRMED from gsap.com/standard-license itself ('Can I really use GSAP in commercial projects without paying anything? Yes, really!'), all former Club plugins included, no paid tier. But the restriction is much NARROWER than 'a competing product/tool': it bars use 'in tools that allow users to build visual animations without code that encourages, induces, or materially assists in creating a solution that competes with Webflow's visual animation building capabilities', plus reverse-engineering to build competitive products and stripping notices. A code-review SaaS is unambiguously fine. Also confirmed on the Webflow post: acquisition 'in the fall of 2024', SplitText 'completely rewritten from the ground up' with '50% file size reduction', shipped in 3.13.0 (2025-04-30 — matches 'April 2025'). gsap latest is 3.15.0, released 2026-04-13.
    [REFUTED] The astigmatism statistic (~33% of US adults) and a 2021 Optometry and Vision Science study on dark-mode reading discomfort are unverified secondary-blog claims -> The number is wrong and the study appears not to exist. Canonical figure: age-standardised astigmatism prevalence in US adults 20+ is 36.2% (95% CI 34.9-37.5), cylinder >=1.0 D in either eye — Vitale S, Ellwein L, Cotch MF, Ferris FL 3rd, Sperduto R, 'Prevalence of refractive error in the United States, 1999-2004', Arch Ophthalmol 2008;126(8):1111-9 (NHANES, n=12,010). Note the blogs' '33%' is almost certainly a transposition of the MYOPIA figure in the same table (33.1%). Separately, a PubMed search of Optom Vis Sci 2021 for dark mode / display polarity returns exactly one hit, an unrelated contact-lens lipid-deposition paper (PMID 33828039) — no such dark-mode study. Real polarity literature does exist and mostly favours POSITIVE polarity for reading (e.g. Ergonomics 2026, 'The polarity effect... better proofreading performance'), which supports their light-theme-for-audit recommendation but not a '2021 OVS dark-mode discomfort' citation.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] '~67% of top-tier SaaS landing pages use bento grids' and 'default for new B2B homepage launches since ~May 2026' are blog claims with no stated methodology -> No primary source found (my web-search budget was exhausted before I could chase SaaSFrame/Pravin Kumar directly). Their own caveat stands and should be honoured: do not put 67% in a deck. Note it is used as load-bearing rationale in recommendation 8 ('Bento is at ~67% adoption... so a full-page bento now reads as template') — the conclusion may be right, the number is not citable.
    [CORRECTED] Unicorn Studio runtime '~29kb gzipped' from the Codrops article; Rive web runtime '~200KB gzipped' from a secondary post; browsers limit you to ~16 WebGL contexts per page -> Three separate results. (1) Unicorn Studio CONFIRMED verbatim from Codrops: 'a lightweight runtime (about 29kb gzipped)', plus the performance estimator, layer downsampling, DPI/FPS adjustment, flattening and auto-pause off-screen. (2) Rive ~200KB is REFUTED and badly understates it. I measured @rive-app/canvas@2.42.2 (MIT) from unpkg today: rive.js 100,757 B gzipped and rive.wasm 795,259 B gzipped = ~896 KB gzipped total, ~4.5x the quoted figure. Even @rive-app/canvas-lite is 93 KB + 354 KB = ~447 KB; @rive-app/webgl is ~2 MB of wasm. This strengthens their 'do not use Rive' conclusion but the number as written is wrong and would not survive a judge checking it. (3) The 16-context cap CONFIRMED empirically — I created 40 WebGL contexts on a Chromium page: all 40 getContext calls succeeded, but exactly 16 remained alive. The failure mode matters and is worth stating: you get no error, the OLDEST contexts are silently force-lost and go blank.
    [CORRECTED] All oklch values in the recommended PRGuard palette are proposals and the ~hex approximations were estimated, not computed -> Confirmed estimated, and systematically too dark. Computed (Oklab -> sRGB): surface-void #090B0A (they wrote #080B0A), surface-base #0F1211 (they wrote #0D110F), surface-1 #161A18 (#131816), surface-2 #1E2120 (#191F1D), surface-3 #262928. States: PROVEN #6CD981, DISPROVEN #E9BA36, INCONCLUSIVE #62B9D1, BLOCKING #F14442, OVERRIDE #ED77DF, text-hi #F3F6F4. All are in sRGB gamut. TWO REAL FAILURES: (a) BLOCKING red oklch(0.64 0.21 26) is 4.36:1 on surface-2 and 3.94:1 on surface-3 — below the 4.5:1 body-text target they set for themselves; (b) the fourth text step L=0.50 is 3.15:1 on surface-base, large-text/UI-border only, never body copy. Everything else clears 4.5:1 comfortably.
    [CORRECTED] socket.dev returned 403 to curl (no palette data); semgrep.dev returned a near-empty shell (#3aa5b6, #5944b0, #c4c6c9, #646669) too thin to characterise -> socket.dev CONFIRMED still blocked — even a real browser only reaches the Cloudflare 'Just a moment...' interstitial, so any colours read from it are the challenge page, not Socket's brand. semgrep.dev is REFUTED: I got real computed styles and it is not a thin shell and not purple-adjacent. It is a LIGHT-MODE site (body background rgb(255,255,255)), Inter only, with #212529 / #475467 / #667085 / #98A2B3 (the Untitled UI grey ramp) plus a green-sage accent family — #232928, #314944, #9EADAA, #AFEAD8 — and a #131A2C navy. None of the four hexes they reported appear in the top-16 computed palette. This matters competitively: the closest direct competitor in code security ships light mode, which undercuts the framing that the whole category is dark.
    [CORRECTED] Reference: Linear layout.B05Dfi6O.css token dump — backgrounds, borders, lines, text, accent, semantics, radii 4/12/16/24/32 (no 8), hairline, weights, sizes, easings, fonts -> Backgrounds (#08090a/#0f1011/#141516/#191a1b, marketing #010102, panel #0f1011), borders (translucent #ffffff0d, translucent-strong #ffffff14, opaque #23252a/#34343a/#3e3e44), text (#f7f8f8/#d0d6e0/#8a8f98/#62666d), accent #7170ff, hover #828fff, brand #5e6ad2, semantics (green #27a644, red #eb5757, blue #5eb0ff with a display-p3 upgrade, orange #fc7840, yellow #f0bf00, teal #00b8cc), --border-hairline 1px -> .5px, weights 300/400/510/590/680, easings, and the font stacks are ALL exactly right. Three errors: (1) 'Radii 4/12/16/24/32 (no 8)' is REFUTED — the file defines --radius-4, --radius-6, --radius-8, --radius-12, --radius-16, --radius-24, --radius-32, plus circle and rounded. Linear does have 6 and 8, which weakens the 'borrow Graphite's 2/6/8/12 rather than Linear's marketing scale' framing in recommendation 10. (2) Line tokens are #37393a / #202122 / #18191a, but line-quaternary is #141515, not #141516 (#141516 is --color-line-tint). (3) The diff colours are only half-present: #27a644 appears, but #F34E52 and the rgba(39,166,68,.07) / rgba(243,78,82,.1) backgrounds do not appear anywhere in this file — UNVERIFIABLE from the cited source. Also worth knowing: there are TWO type scales. --font-size-* is 11/12/13/15/18 + 20/24/36 as they said, but --text-*-size is a different ramp (tiny 10, micro 12, mini 13, small 14, regular 15, large 17), so 'Linear's small = 13px' is true of one scale and 14px in the other.
    [CORRECTED] Reference: graphite.dev — --base-border-radius at 2/6/8/12px; fonts Inter, JetBrains Mono, DM Mono and licensed 'Matter'; eight-hex accent sprawl -> Radii confirmed exactly (--base-border-radius:2px/6px/8px/12px). All eight accent hexes present (#3cb091, #6666c1, #1495c7, #efae0f, #e35a38, #7449e3, #14c7ff, #1061ff) — the 'no single ownable hue' read stands. Inter, JetBrains and Matter all appear. But 'DM Mono' returns ZERO occurrences in the served HTML — drop it or re-verify against their stylesheets before repeating it.
    [CORRECTED] Reference: Muzli 50 best dashboards 2026 — named shots and their descriptions; used as evidence that these designs are 'built to win a thumbnail, not to survive daily use' and cannot represent density, role permissions, live feeds or a data source going down -> Every shot and description is accurate, several verbatim: toma/Fireart 'dark, data-driven aesthetic and precise green accents'; Vaulto/Orbix 'dark, cinematic aesthetic', deep charcoal, Sankey diagram; QuartRevenue/Airzon 'deep-green accents and neon highlights'; InsightStream/Airzon 'high-contrast, neon-on-dark', 'vibrant pink and green accents'; OpsPulse/Orbix 'frosted-glass aesthetic (Glassmorphism)' with 'vibrant blurred background'; Golfio/Realto/Fitness by Juice Lab all frosted-glass over cinematic imagery. THE CRITIQUE IS MISATTRIBUTED. The article is a celebratory roundup — 'masterclass', 'stunning', 'boundary-pushing' — and contains no thumbnail-vs-daily-use critique at all. Worse for recommendation 7, Muzli explicitly ENDORSES glassmorphism: OpsPulse 'proves that utility-heavy tools can, and should, look stunning in 2026'. The density argument is sound but it is the researcher's own, and the claim that 'glassmorphism has been demoted to a supporting element for 2026' is contradicted by the very source cited next to it.
    [CORRECTED] Reference: 925studios — Inter as tell #1, indigo-to-purple traced to Tailwind indigo-500 as 'the single loudest AI tell in 2026', three-feature-card row, weightless headlines, Linear/Stripe/Duolingo counterexamples; fix = asymmetry and a different rhythm -> The four tells, the 'single loudest AI tell in 2026' phrasing, the indigo-500 lineage, 'a row of three feature cards, rounded corners, soft shadow, thin-line icon at the top of each', 'Build faster. Ship smarter.', 'thin, interchangeable line icons that could illustrate any product', and the Linear/Stripe/Duolingo counterexamples are all confirmed. One correction: the article's prescribed fix is CONSTRAINT — give the tool a specific typeface, palette, layout principles and tone up front — not 'asymmetry and a different rhythm', which is the researcher's own extrapolation. Also a citable detail they missed: the article quotes Adam Wathan apologising 'for making every button in Tailwind UI bg-indigo-500 five years ago, leading to every AI generated UI on earth also being indigo.'
    [REFUTED] Reference: Codrops WebGL article as the source for Unicorn Studio ~29KB, Rive ~200KB, Three.js heaviest, the ~16 WebGL context cap, the 'if a linear gradient solves it don't reach for WebGL' rule, and 'hand-authored SVG is typically under 10KB' -> The article is real (George Hastings, 4 March 2026) and DOES contain the Unicorn Studio material: 'a lightweight runtime (about 29kb gzipped)', the built-in performance estimator, per-layer downsampling, DPI/FPS adjustment, layer flattening to cut draw calls, and pausing when out of view. It contains NONE of the rest — no mention of Rive, no 200KB figure, no Three.js comparison, no WebGL context cap, no linear-gradient rule, no SVG size claim. Those facts are attributed to a URL that does not support them; Rive's true size is ~896 KB gzipped (see above) and the 16-context cap I verified empirically instead.
    [CORRECTED] Recommendation 1 premise: the proposed five-state palette is 'unoccupied in this category' and PROVEN green is 'deliberately off emerald-500's hue 162 so it does not read as shadcn' -> True of emerald specifically, misleading overall — three of the five states land inside Tailwind's own ramps. BLOCKING oklch(0.64 0.21 26) vs Tailwind red-500 oklch(0.637 0.237 25.331): lightness differs by 0.003 and hue by 0.7 degrees — it is red-500 with slightly less chroma. PROVEN oklch(0.80 0.16 148) sits on Tailwind green-400/green-500's hue band (151.7 / 149.6), i.e. it escapes emerald only by moving to the adjacent Tailwind ramp. DISPROVEN oklch(0.81 0.15 88) sits between yellow-500 (86.0) and amber-300 (91.6). Only the cyan (218) and magenta (332) are genuinely off-ramp. The palette is defensible, but 'a story no competitor can tell' should rest on the aviation semantics, not on a claim of novel hues.
    [CORRECTED] Recommendation 1 premise: amber for DISPROVEN/self-refuted is justified 'because amber is codified as caution not failure' -> The regulation says the opposite of what this needs. 14 CFR 25.1322(b)(2) defines Caution as 'conditions that require immediate flightcrew awareness and subsequent flightcrew response' — amber means act soon, not 'this resolved itself'. A self-refuted finding requires no response at all; under the cited standard it is closest to an Advisory, which 25.1322(e)(1)(iii) allows to be 'any color except red or green'. Using the product's proudest state to fire the cockpit colour for pending action inverts the semantics of the very standard being invoked for credibility — and it is the one mapping a judge who knows avionics would catch. Cyan (already allocated to INCONCLUSIVE) or a neutral would be truer; consider swapping INCONCLUSIVE to amber and DISPROVEN to cyan/neutral.
    [CORRECTED] Recommendation 4 premise: 'If you keep Inter for prose, you must at minimum ship Linear's settings — font-feature-settings: cv01, ss03 ... plus font-variant-numeric: lining-nums tabular-nums slashed-zero everywhere a number appears' -> As written this silently breaks the slashed zero. font-feature-settings is a low-level property that replaces the whole feature list, so an element declaring font-feature-settings:'cv01','ss03' resets the 'zero' and 'tnum' tags that font-variant-numeric would otherwise enable, and the two properties fight. Linear solves it exactly this way and the recommendation should copy it verbatim: .slashedZero { font-feature-settings: var(--font-settings), "zero" !important; font-variant-numeric: lining-nums tabular-nums slashed-zero !important; } where --font-settings is "cv01", "ss03". Linear also sets 'opsz' auto globally.
    [CORRECTED] Recommendation 4 premise: 'Berkeley Mono is the highest-end option if the budget exists' -> Two problems beyond price. (1) Cost for this product is $790 minimum (Startup FX-400 $495 + Web Fonts MX-014 $295), or $845 + $45/MONTH at Standard Business — not 'from $75'. (2) The licence text on every commercial tier page reads: 'Commercial licenses are not compatible with open-source apps. Commercial use restricted to UI elements only. If you're building an IDE, Terminal app, Text Editor, etc., we generally do not allow it, please contact us at inquiry@usgraphics.com for your specific use case.' PRGuard is a developer tool whose hero (recommendation 2) is literally a terminal, and hackathon projects are frequently open-sourced — both clauses are live risks. Geist Mono or JetBrains Mono (both OFL 1.1, verified) carry none of this.
VERIFIER ADDITIONS:
    + Overall: this is unusually honest research. Every 'I could not verify this' flag was accurate — usgraphics.com really does 403, d1s1.com really is still 429, socket.dev really is Cloudflare-gated, m2.material.io really does return nothing to a text fetcher. The reproducible technical material (Linear CSS, Tailwind, shadcn, npm) is near-perfect: 21/21 npm versions and licences exact, every Tailwind oklch exact, every shadcn dark token exact, EdgeShine.css exact character-for-character. The failures cluster in exactly one place — facts borrowed from secondary blogs and then footnoted to a primary URL that does not contain them (Rive's size and the WebGL cap attributed to Codrops; FAR 25.1322 and EFIS semantics attributed to Applied Avionics; a density critique attributed to Muzli; 'asymmetry' attributed to 925studios). Before anything ships in a deck, re-check every citation where the fact and the URL came from different places.
    + The single most expensive error to ship is Rive at '~200KB gzipped'. Measured today from unpkg: @rive-app/canvas@2.42.2 is 100,757 B (js) + 795,259 B (wasm) = ~896 KB gzipped, ~4.5x the quoted figure, and the lite build is still ~447 KB. Same direction as their conclusion, wrong by a factor that a judge with a network tab can disprove in ten seconds. Quote the measured number and the conclusion gets stronger, not weaker.
    + Two accessibility failures in the proposed palette that their own stated target (4.5:1 body / 3:1 large and UI) rules out: BLOCKING red oklch(0.64 0.21 26) is 4.36:1 on --surface-2 and 3.94:1 on --surface-3, and the fourth text step oklch(0.50 ...) is 3.15:1 on --surface-base. Fix by lifting the red to roughly L 0.68-0.70 (Tailwind red-400 territory, oklch(0.704 0.191 22.216), which is also shadcn's dark --destructive and clears 5.5:1+) and restricting the L=0.50 text step to borders, dividers and disabled states only. Everything else in the ladder clears comfortably.
    + A real conflict inside the brief that nobody resolved: impeccable.style/slop — the anti-slop checklist they lean on hardest — names 'Dark mode with glowing accents' as slop in the same list as the glowing orb and the pulsing dot. The plan is a dark theme with five saturated accents. The defence exists (instrument panel, not glow; elevation-as-lightness, no shadows or glows; accents bound to state, not decoration) but it has to be made explicitly, because the checklist they are citing as their authority also indicts the top-level direction. MD2 adds a second version of the same tension: it explicitly prescribes dark GREY over black because 'light text on a dark grey surface has less contrast than light text on a black surface', while --surface-void computes to #090B0A.
    + The competitive premise 'this category is uniformly dark and purple-adjacent' is weaker than presented. semgrep.dev — arguably the closest real analogue, an AI-assisted SAST platform — is a white-background, Inter-based site with a green-sage and navy palette on the Untitled UI grey ramp. That is either a hole in the survey or, read the other way, evidence that the credible-security-tooling register is light and restrained, which would make the light-theme audit/accuracy surfaces (recommendation 11) the lead rather than the exception.
    + Recommendation 10 asks for @tanstack/react-table v9 'from day one'. v9.0.0 is seven weeks old (2026-08-04, 7 stable releases) against v8's 118 releases over three years. Docs, the shadcn DataTable recipe, every Stack Overflow answer and every model's priors are v8. Under hackathon time pressure this is the highest-variance dependency in the whole stack for the lowest possible payoff — pin ^8.21.3.
    + Two upgrades to their own arguments, free and verifiable. (1) The tabular-figures point is stronger than 16%: the 2024 Web Almanac's Figure 4.32 shows the tnum feature tag is actually used on only 0.8% of desktop pages. 'Fewer than one page in a hundred sets tabular figures' beats 'only 16% of fonts support them'. (2) The WebGL context cap has a better failure story than the number implies — I created 40 contexts on a live page and every getContext call succeeded while exactly 16 stayed alive. You get no error; the oldest canvases silently go blank. That is a much sharper argument against a WebGL-per-tile bento than '16 is the limit'.
    + Sources actually consulted: registry.npmjs.org (21 packages), cdn.jsdelivr.net/npm/tailwindcss@4.3.3/theme.css, ui.shadcn.com/docs/theming, static.linear.app layout.B05Dfi6O.css / EdgeShine.DgPIODoE.css / Grain.D_EBlr94.css / Berkeley-Mono-Variable.woff2, usgraphics.com product + FX-102/202/400/600 catalog pages (via browser), gsap.com/standard-license, webflow.com/blog/gsap-becomes-free, raw.githubusercontent.com/vercel/geist-font LICENSE.txt + readme.md + GitHub API, rsms.me/inter, m2.material.io dark-theme (via browser), ecfr.gov 14 CFR 25.1322 (via browser), appliedavionics.com tech guide, almanac.httparchive.org/en/2024/fonts, tympanus.net Codrops, muz.li, dribbble.com Select, impeccable.style/slop, 925studios.co, radix-ui.com, vercel.com/geist/colors, resend.com, coderabbit.ai, cubic.dev, graphite.dev, semgrep.dev, socket.dev, d1s1.com, unpkg.com Rive runtimes, PubMed E-utilities. Note: my WebSearch budget hit its 200-call session cap partway through, so the three pure-trend claims (bento at 67%, 'default for new B2B launches since May 2026', and 'glassmorphism demoted for 2026') were never chased to a primary source and remain unverified — treat all three as rhetoric, not data.

### AREA: Animation library landscape and the right stack for 2026 (animation-libs)
FINDINGS:
  - GSAP is at 3.15.0 (npm publish 2026-04-13). Its npm `license` field is the literal string "Standard 'no charge' license: https://gsap.com/standard-license." and GitHub's API reports `license: null` for greensock/GSAP. It is FREE but it is NOT MIT and NOT an OSI open-source license. Release cadence: 3.13.0 2025-04-30 (the 'everything is free' release), 3.14.0 2025-12-08, 3.14.2 2025-12-12, 3.15.0 2026-04-13.
  - The live GSAP Standard License text (Effective 2025-04-30, Last modified 2025-05-30, © Webflow) defines Permitted Uses as "the implementation and/or use of GSAP Products on any website, web application, or digital interface by any person or entity" — with NO end-user-fee restriction. The old 'end users are not charged a fee of any kind' plain-English blurb is still physically present in the page HTML but is inside HTML comments (dead text). Prohibited Uses are only no-code visual animation builders competing with Webflow. A paid SaaS dev-tool like PRGuard is unambiguously permitted.
  - ALL GSAP plugins ship inside the single `gsap` npm package. The import paths are `gsap/ScrollTrigger`, `gsap/SplitText`, `gsap/Flip`, `gsap/Observer`, `gsap/MorphSVGPlugin`, `gsap/DrawSVGPlugin`, `gsap/ScrollSmoother`, `gsap/CustomEase`, `gsap/ScrambleTextPlugin`, `gsap/MotionPathPlugin`. There is no `@gsap/scrolltrigger` package and no npm.greensock.com auth token any more. The ONLY scoped package is `@gsap/react@2.1.2` (the `useGSAP` hook), last published 2025-01-15, peers `gsap ^3.12.5` + `react >=17`, license 'SEE LICENSE AT https://gsap.com/standard-license'.
  - Measured gzip cost of GSAP 3.15.0 dist files (I downloaded and gzipped -9 each): gsap.min.js 71.2kB raw / 27.7kB gz; ScrollTrigger 43.5/17.6; MotionPathPlugin 21.5/9.5; MorphSVGPlugin 20.7/9.3; Flip 24.9/9.5; ScrollSmoother 13.1/5.4; Observer 9.8/4.2; SplitText 7.6/3.6; CustomEase 7.0/3.6; DrawSVGPlugin 4.2/2.2; ScrollToPlugin 4.0/1.9; ScrambleTextPlugin 11.4 raw. A realistic PRGuard marketing bundle (core+ScrollTrigger+SplitText+CustomEase+DrawSVG) is ~55kB gzip.
  - Motion is at 13.4.1, published 2026-09-22 (today), MIT. Majors: 12.0.0 2025-01-20, 13.0.0 2026-08-05. `framer-motion@13.4.1` is STILL published in lockstep and the `motion` package literally depends on it (`dependencies: tslib, framer-motion`) — `motion` is a thin re-export shim, not a separate engine. Weekly downloads: framer-motion 33.6M vs motion 15.4M, so most of the ecosystem is still on the old specifier.
  - Motion's exports map (verified from package.json@13.4.1) is: `.`, `./mini`, `./react`, `./react-client`, `./react-m`, `./react-mini`, `./react-animate-view`, `./three`, `./vgpu`, `./debug`. `motion/react-client` is the React-Server-Component-safe entry — `import * as motion from "motion/react-client"` lets you use motion elements inside a Next.js App Router Server Component file without adding your own "use client", which is the single biggest App Router ergonomics win Motion has over GSAP.
  - Motion's documented bundle costs (their own numbers, Rollup-generated): full `motion` component 34kB; `m` + `LazyMotion` 4.6kB initial render; `domAnimation` feature bundle +15kB; `domMax` +25kB; `useAnimate` mini 2.3kB; `useAnimate` hybrid 17kB. I independently measured the vanilla IIFE `motion@13.4.1/dist/motion.js` at 143.5kB raw / 47.8kB gzip — i.e. the vanilla build is HEAVIER than GSAP core + ScrollTrigger combined (45.3kB gz).
  - Motion 13.0.0 has a real breaking change: it removed the optional `@emotion/is-prop-valid` dependency, so styled `motion` components now forward unknown props to the DOM and spam React warnings unless you wrap with `<MotionConfig isValidProp={isPropValid}>`. Motion 12 had zero React breaking changes. Motion 11 moved render scheduling from synchronous to microtask timing, which breaks Jest tests that assumed sync updates.
  - Motion 13.4.0 (2026-09-14) shipped `AnimateView`, view transitions built on React 19.3's native `ViewTransition` component. Motion 13.2.0 (2026-09-03) shipped `animate.addEffect()` with `threeEffect` (`motion/three`, drives Three.js objects/materials/shader uniforms/TSL nodes) and `vgpuEffect` (`motion/vgpu`, Vercel's WebGPU library) — but vgpu is explicitly labelled Motion+ EARLY ACCESS, i.e. paid and not production-ready.
  - Motion+ Personal is £299 one-time (business is annual per-seat for 2+). It is MIT-licensed output and includes splitText, ScrambleText, Ticker, Cursor, Typewriter, Carousel, AnimateNumber, Curtains, Motion Studio (2.0.0, 2026-09-16). Critically: GSAP 3.13+ ships SplitText AND ScrambleTextPlugin free, so £299 of Motion+ is redundant for PRGuard.
  - CSS scroll-driven animations are STILL Baseline 'limited' as of today, per webstatus.dev (Chrome's official dashboard): Chrome/Edge 115 (2023-07-18), Safari 26 (2025-09-15), Firefox NOT shipped in stable (still behind layout.css.scroll-driven-animations.enabled). Do not build the hero or the 9-stage pipeline scroll on `animation-timeline: view()` — one third of a judging panel could see a static page.
  - View Transitions split into two very different support stories. Same-document (`document.startViewTransition`) became Baseline NEWLY AVAILABLE on 2025-10-14 when Firefox 144 shipped (Chrome 111 2023-03-07, Safari 18 2024-09-16) — safe for the dashboard. Cross-document (`@view-transition { navigation: auto }`) is still Baseline limited: Chrome 126 (2024-06-11), Safari 18.2 (2024-12-11), no Firefox.
  - Web Animations API is Baseline WIDELY available since 2023-03-16 (Chrome 84, Firefox 75, Safari 14). `linear()` easing became Baseline widely on 2026-06-11 (Chrome 113, Edge 113, Firefox 112, Safari 17.2) — so you can ship spring-shaped CSS easings with no JS. `@starting-style` is Baseline newly (2024-08-06, Safari 17.5). `backdrop-filter` is Baseline newly only since 2024-09-16 (Safari 18) — treat heavy glassmorphism as a progressive enhancement, not a foundation.
  - Lenis 1.3.26 (2026-08-05, MIT) measures 18.3kB raw / 5.3kB gzip — one quarter of ScrollSmoother's footprint and it keeps native DOM, Cmd-F find-in-page, keyboard scroll, `position: sticky` and IntersectionObserver working. ScrollSmoother (13.1kB/5.4kB gz) demands a `#smooth-wrapper` > `#smooth-content` structure and breaks `position: fixed` children. The official Lenis↔GSAP contract is exactly: `new Lenis({ autoRaf: false })`, `lenis.on('scroll', ScrollTrigger.update)`, `gsap.ticker.add(t => lenis.raf(t * 1000))`, `gsap.ticker.lagSmoothing(0)`. Skipping this leaves two independent RAF loops and a 1–2 frame desync that reads as jitter.
  - Runtime-vector players are a trap at PRGuard's scale. Measured: `@rive-app/canvas@2.42.2` = rive.js 440.0kB raw / 98.3kB gz PLUS rive.wasm 1,889.9kB raw / 775.8kB gzip. `@lottiefiles/dotlottie-web@0.80.0` = index.js 161.2kB + dotlottie-player.wasm 1,209.1kB raw / 484.8kB gzip. `lottie-web@5.13.0` lottie_light.min.js is 164.4kB / 45.4kB gz with no wasm. CodeRabbit actually ships Rive on its homepage — matching that spend buys you nothing an SVG+GSAP diagram can't do better.
  - Three.js is r186 / 0.186.0 (2026-09-08, MIT): three.module.min.js 383.6kB raw / 87.7kB gzip; three.webgpu.min.js 801.6kB / ~200kB gzip. The official three.js manual still calls WebGPURenderer "experimental". R3F is 9.7.0 (2026-07-31), drei 10.7.8 (2026-08-05), @react-three/postprocessing 3.1.1 (2026-08-27). R3F Canvas must be behind `next/dynamic(..., { ssr: false })` from a Client Component or you get hydration errors.
  - Dead or dying, do not adopt: `@motionone/dom@10.18.0` last published 2024-06-02 (Motion One is folded into `motion`; the Vue and Solid bindings are formally deprecated in favour of oku-motion and solid-motionone). `@theatre/core@0.7.2` last published 2024-05-19, repo last pushed 2024-08-14 — two years dormant, Apache-2.0. `popmotion@11.0.5` last published 2022-08-15. `tailwindcss-animate@1.0.7` last published 2023-08-28, repo last pushed 2024-07-28 — shadcn/ui replaced it with `tw-animate-css@1.4.0` for Tailwind v4 (note v2.0.0 is announced with breaking changes).
  - Motion's own published performance tier list is the best free perf spec on the web and contains one non-obvious landmine: S-tier compositor-only = transform, opacity, filter, clip-path; C-tier paint = background-color, color, border-radius, mask-image, gradients; D-tier layout = width/height/margin/top; F-tier = read/write thrash when two libraries touch the DOM in the same frame. The landmine: **CSS custom properties ALWAYS trigger paint even when they feed a compositor-only property**, and a global `--x` on `:root` invalidates the whole subtree — Motion quote it at ~8ms/frame versus nanoseconds for a targeted JS write. So never animate `--progress` on `:root` to drive the pipeline diagram.
  - Competitor palette forensics from their live HTML fetched today: CodeRabbit's base token is `oklch(0.177 0.0084 305)` = #121014 (purple-black) with surfaces #302D36/#332F39, mint accent #65E6A6 and brand orange #F04006, built on Next.js + Rive. Greptile: neon pink #FFCFFE, lime #ECFFA3/#DAFF01, mint #28E99F, slate #3D3B4F, Next.js + Lottie. Graphite.dev ships GSAP with class hooks in the served HTML (`gsap-prefooter-split-text`, `gsap-decorative-dot`) — a direct competitor already proves GSAP+SplitText+ScrollTrigger is the house style for this category. Warp: #0B0B0B / #1F1F1F + lavender #C7AEFF. Linear: #08090A base, #E2E4E7 text, #62666D muted, #2E2E32 border, accents #55CDFF / #8FA4FF / #F79CE0 / #FFC47C / #89D196.
  - npm weekly downloads measured today (api.npmjs.org last-week): framer-motion 33,620,514; motion 15,405,315; three 12,184,549; lottie-web 5,369,142; @react-spring/web 3,641,713; gsap 3,582,856; @formkit/auto-animate 1,129,458; lenis 1,072,842; animejs 746,561; @rive-app/react-canvas 710,544. Anime.js v4.5.0 (2026-06-22, MIT) bundles at 115.9kB raw / 39.9kB gzip for the full ESM bundle — heavier than GSAP core for less capability.
REFERENCES:
  - [must-steal] GSAP 3.15.0 core https://gsap.com/docs/v3/Installation/ :: STEAL: Make this the primary timeline engine for the entire marketing site and every choreographed sequence on the dashboard. :: SPECIFICS: npm `gsap@3.15.0`, published 2026-04-13. 71.2kB min / 27.7kB gzip measured. License field = "Standard 'no charge' license" (proprietary, free, not OSI). Register plugins ONCE at module scope, never inside useEffect: `gsap.registerPlugin(useGSAP, ScrollTrigger, SplitText, CustomEase, DrawSVGPlugin)`. Use `CustomEase.create('prg', 'M0,0 C0.2,0 0,1 1,1')` for a signature house ease and reuse it everywhere via `gsap.defaults({ ease: 'prg', duration: 0.6 })`.
  - [must-steal] GSAP SplitText (3.13 rewrite) https://gsap.com/blog/3-13/ :: STEAL: The entire hero headline reveal. This single plugin is the difference between 'hackathon project' and 'funded product' in the first 800ms. :: SPECIFICS: `gsap/SplitText`, 7.6kB min / 3.6kB gzip — 50% smaller than the pre-3.13 version and free. Exact options that matter: `type:'lines,words,chars'`, `mask:'lines'` (auto-wraps each line in a clipping element so you can do a pure translateY reveal with no opacity fade), `autoSplit:true` + `onSplit()` (re-splits on font load and resize without leaving orphan animations), `aria:'auto'` (writes aria-label on the parent and aria-hidden on the shards so screen readers read the sentence, not 47 letters), `deepSlice:true` (splits through nested <strong>/<a>), `smartWrap:true`, `propIndex:true` (writes `--word: n` for CSS-driven stagger). Canonical reveal: `gsap.from(split.lines, { yPercent: 110, duration: 0.9, stagger: 0.08, ease: 'expo.out' })` on a `mask:'lines'` split.
  - [must-steal] GSAP ScrollTrigger https://gsap.com/docs/v3/Plugins/ScrollTrigger/ :: STEAL: The 9-stage pipeline scroll scene: pin the diagram, scrub a master timeline, light each stage node as it becomes active. :: SPECIFICS: `gsap/ScrollTrigger`, 43.5kB min / 17.6kB gzip. Pattern: one master timeline, one ScrollTrigger, `{ trigger: '#pipeline', pin: true, scrub: 0.6, start: 'top top', end: '+=3600', anticipatePin: 1, invalidateOnRefresh: true }`. `scrub: 0.6` (a number, not `true`) adds the 0.6s catch-up lag that reads as 'expensive'. Use `ScrollTrigger.batch('.finding-card', { onEnter: b => gsap.to(b, { autoAlpha: 1, y: 0, stagger: 0.09 }), start: 'top 85%' })` for card grids — it is materially cheaper than one trigger per card. Always `ScrollTrigger.refresh()` after fonts load and after any async content injection.
  - [must-steal] @gsap/react useGSAP hook https://gsap.com/resources/React/ :: STEAL: The only correct way to run GSAP inside React 19 + Next.js App Router. Non-negotiable. :: SPECIFICS: `@gsap/react@2.1.2`, published 2025-01-15 (stale but it is a ~1kB hook, not a risk). Peers `gsap ^3.12.5`, `react >=17`. It is isomorphic (prefers useLayoutEffect, falls back to useEffect when `window` is undefined) so it is SSR-safe, but the file still needs `"use client"`. It auto-reverts everything created inside it via gsap.context, which is what makes React 18/19 StrictMode's double-effect harmless. For event handlers use the returned `contextSafe()` wrapper — animations created in a raw onClick escape cleanup and leak across route changes.
  - [must-steal] Motion 13.4.1 (motion/react) https://motion.dev/docs :: STEAL: Everything state-driven in the dashboard: AnimatePresence for the finding drawer, `layout` + `layoutId` for the severity-band shared-element transition, Reorder for the suppression ledger, drag for panel resize. :: SPECIFICS: npm `motion@13.4.1`, MIT, published 2026-09-22. `layoutId` is the one thing GSAP genuinely cannot match — clicking a finding row and having its severity chip morph into the detail header is a two-line change here and a Flip choreography in GSAP. Ship `LazyMotion` + `m` + `domAnimation` (4.6kB initial, +15kB deferred) not the 34kB `motion` component. `<MotionConfig reducedMotion="user" transition={{ type: 'spring', stiffness: 380, damping: 32 }}>` at the dashboard root. Motion 13 requires `<MotionConfig isValidProp={isPropValid}>` if you use styled-components.
  - [strong] motion/react-client (RSC entry) https://motion.dev/docs/react-motion-component :: STEAL: Keep Server Components server-side while still animating. `import * as motion from "motion/react-client"` then use `<motion.div>` directly in an RSC file. :: SPECIFICS: Verified in motion@13.4.1's package.json exports map: `./react-client` -> `framer-motion/client`. Use it for marketing-page entrance animations so the page stays a Server Component; use `motion/react` (+ "use client") only where you need hooks like useScroll/useAnimate. This is the single Next.js App Router advantage Motion has over GSAP, which always forces a client boundary.
  - [strong] Lenis 1.3.26 https://github.com/darkroomengineering/lenis :: STEAL: Smooth scroll on the MARKETING site only, wired into GSAP's ticker, disabled under reduced motion. :: SPECIFICS: MIT, 18.3kB raw / 5.3kB gzip measured. Exact wiring: `const lenis = new Lenis({ autoRaf: false, lerp: 0.1, duration: 1.2, anchors: true }); lenis.on('scroll', ScrollTrigger.update); gsap.ticker.add(t => lenis.raf(t * 1000)); gsap.ticker.lagSmoothing(0)`. Known caveats from the README: capped to 60fps on Safari and 30fps in low-power mode; native CSS scroll-snap does not work (needs the `lenis/snap` subpath); smooth scroll stops over iframes. Packages: `lenis`, `lenis/react`, `lenis/snap`. Do NOT use the abandoned `@studio-freight/lenis` (last publish 2024-03-17).
  - [strong] GSAP Flip https://gsap.com/docs/v3/Plugins/Flip/ :: STEAL: The dashboard's list→detail and filter-reflow transitions when you want them on GSAP's timeline instead of Motion's layout engine. :: SPECIFICS: `gsap/Flip`, 24.9kB min / 9.5kB gzip. `const state = Flip.getState('.finding-row'); /* mutate DOM */ Flip.from(state, { duration: 0.5, ease: 'power2.inOut', absolute: true, nested: true, onEnter: e => gsap.fromTo(e, {opacity:0, scale:0.92},{opacity:1, scale:1}), onLeave: e => gsap.to(e,{opacity:0}) })`. Per Motion's own tier list this is B-tier: one upfront measurement converts the most expensive animation class into one of the cheapest. Pick ONE of Flip or Motion `layout` per surface — running both on the same subtree is the F-tier read/write thrash case.
  - [strong] @formkit/auto-animate 0.10.0 https://github.com/formkit/auto-animate :: STEAL: Zero-config add/remove/move animation for every dashboard list you do not want to hand-choreograph: suppression ledger rows, audit-chain events, degradation chips. :: SPECIFICS: MIT, published 2026-07-10, 8.2kB raw / 3.2kB gzip — the best effort-to-polish ratio in the whole landscape. `const [parent] = useAutoAnimate({ duration: 220, easing: 'cubic-bezier(0.2, 0, 0, 1)' })` then `<ul ref={parent}>`. It uses WAAPI (Baseline widely since 2023-03-16) so there is no engine conflict with GSAP or Motion. It respects prefers-reduced-motion automatically. Do not use it on lists that GSAP or Motion `layout` also touches.
  - [strong] tw-animate-css 1.4.0 https://github.com/Wombosvideo/tw-animate-css :: STEAL: Tailwind v4 enter/exit utilities for Radix/shadcn primitives — dropdowns, dialogs, tooltips, popovers — so you never hand-write a keyframe for a menu. :: SPECIFICS: MIT, published 2025-09-24. Pure CSS, imported as `@import "tw-animate-css";` — no JS plugin, which is what makes it Tailwind v4 (4.3.3) compatible. shadcn/ui deprecated `tailwindcss-animate` (last publish 2023-08-28, repo dead since 2024-07-28) in its favour and installs it by default in new projects. Warning: v2.0.0 is announced with breaking changes and a migration script — pin to `1.4.0`.
  - [strong] View Transitions API (same-document) https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/View_Transition_API :: STEAL: Dashboard route/tab changes — reviews list → review detail → finding detail — for near-zero JS cost. :: SPECIFICS: Baseline NEWLY AVAILABLE since 2025-10-14 (Chrome 111 2023-03-07, Safari 18 2024-09-16, Firefox 144 2025-10-14). Wrap navigation in `document.startViewTransition(() => flushSync(() => setRoute(next)))`, name the shared elements with `view-transition-name: finding-<id>`, and style `::view-transition-old(root)` / `::view-transition-new(root)`. Gate the whole thing on `if (!document.startViewTransition || matchMedia('(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)').matches)`. Cross-document (`@view-transition { navigation: auto }`) is still Baseline LIMITED — Chrome 126, Safari 18.2, no Firefox — so do not rely on it for the marketing site.
  - [strong] @number-flow/react 0.6.2 https://www.npmjs.com/package/@number-flow/react :: STEAL: Animated odometer digits for the M2 computed-severity score, the cost{usd_estimate}, token counts and the /v1/accuracy percentages. :: SPECIFICS: MIT, published 2026-07-18. Built on WAAPI, respects prefers-reduced-motion, and correctly animates digit-by-digit with Intl.NumberFormat so `0.7413` counting up reads as a real computed number rather than a marketing counter. This is the cheapest possible way to make 'severity is a deterministic score, not an LLM adjective' feel mechanical on stage.
  - [must-steal] Graphite.dev marketing site https://graphite.dev/ :: STEAL: Proof that GSAP is the house style for this exact category, plus their hook-class convention. :: SPECIFICS: Their served HTML (fetched 2026-09-22) contains GSAP hook classes directly in the markup: `gsap-prefooter-split-text`, `gsap-prefooter-ctas`, and dozens of `gsap-decorative-dot` elements shipped with `opacity-0 size-0.5 bg-primary absolute`. The convention — server-render the element with `opacity-0` and a `gsap-*` class, then let a single client controller query and animate it — is exactly the FOUC-safe App Router pattern. Steal the convention, not their palette (#3CB091 teal / #6666C1 / #EFAE0F on white).
  - [reference-only] CodeRabbit marketing site https://www.coderabbit.ai/ :: STEAL: The bar you must clear, and the exact colors you must NOT use. :: SPECIFICS: Next.js + Rive (rive.wasm is 775.8kB gzipped on top of 98.3kB of JS). Base token `oklch(0.177 0.0084 305)` = #121014 purple-black; surfaces #302D36, #332F39, #34303A, #242129; mint accent #65E6A6 with #D6F9DF tint; brand orange #F04006; status colors #F87171 / #FB923C / #FACC15. Do not ship purple-black + mint. Their heavy Rive payload is a weakness you can beat with an inline SVG pipeline diagram that animates at ~0kB of extra runtime.
  - [must-steal] Linear.app https://linear.app/ :: STEAL: Dark-surface discipline: how far you can get with near-zero chroma plus one saturated accent per context. :: SPECIFICS: Base #08090A, text #E2E4E7 / #D0D6E0, muted #9C9DA1 / #62666D, borders #2E2E32 and #3E3E44, accents scoped per-section via a `--accent` variable that is reassigned (#55CDFF cyan, #8FA4FF indigo, #F79CE0 pink, #FFC47C amber, #89D196 green, #FFEBC6). Also shipped: `--Marquee-duration: 30s`, `--Marquee-gap: 40px`, diff tokens `--diff-new-color:#27A644` / `--diff-old-color:#F34E52` on 7–10% alpha backgrounds — steal that diff treatment verbatim for PRGuard's proof terminal output.
  - [reference-only] Three.js r186 + React Three Fiber 9.7.0 https://threejs.org/manual/en/webgpurenderer.html :: STEAL: At most ONE bounded hero canvas. Nothing else. :: SPECIFICS: three@0.186.0 (2026-09-08, MIT) three.module.min.js 383.6kB / 87.7kB gzip; three.webgpu.min.js 801.6kB / ~200kB gzip and the manual still calls WebGPURenderer experimental. @react-three/fiber 9.7.0, @react-three/drei 10.7.8, @react-three/postprocessing 3.1.1. Mandatory App Router pattern: `const Scene = dynamic(() => import('./Scene'), { ssr: false, loading: () => <HeroPoster/> })` called from a Client Component, with `"use client"` on Scene itself. Cap with `<Canvas dpr={[1, 1.75]} frameloop="demand" gl={{ antialias: false, powerPreference: 'high-performance' }}>` and call `invalidate()` only when a value changes.
  - [avoid] Rive (@rive-app/canvas 2.42.2) https://rive.app/blog/new-pricing :: STEAL: Nothing, for this build. Know why you rejected it. :: SPECIFICS: MIT runtimes, no runtime fees, editor free tier (3 files), paid tiers reported at $9/$32/$120 per seat/month. But measured payload is rive.js 440.0kB raw / 98.3kB gzip PLUS rive.wasm 1,889.9kB raw / 775.8kB gzip. It also requires an artist in the Rive editor — a hackathon has no such person, and a .riv you cannot regenerate at 2am is a liability. ~710k weekly downloads.
  - [avoid] Lottie / dotLottie https://github.com/LottieFiles/dotlottie-web :: STEAL: Nothing. Greptile and cubic.dev both ship Lottie; matching them is matching the median. :: SPECIFICS: `@lottiefiles/dotlottie-web@0.80.0` (MIT, 2026-08-28) = 161.2kB JS + 1,209.1kB wasm / 484.8kB gzip; WebGL and WebGPU variants are 1,334kB and 1,365kB wasm. `lottie-web@5.13.0` (last publish 2025-05-21, repo last push 2025-09-01) lottie_light.min.js is 164.4kB / 45.4kB gz. Requires After Effects + Bodymovin to author. All of it buys you less than GSAP DrawSVG on an inline SVG you can edit in a text editor.
  - [avoid] Theatre.js 0.7.2 https://github.com/theatre-js/theatre :: STEAL: Nothing. It is effectively dormant. :: SPECIFICS: Apache-2.0, 12.7k stars, but `@theatre/core@0.7.2` and `@theatre/studio@0.7.2` were both last published 2024-05-19 and the repo's last push was 2024-08-14 — over two years stale with 141 open issues, still pre-1.0. @theatre/studio is a 21.7MB unpacked dev dependency. Blog posts still calling it 'the future of web animation tooling' in 2026 are not checking npm.
  - [avoid] React Spring 10.1.2 / Anime.js 4.5.0 / Motion One https://react-spring.dev/ :: STEAL: Nothing — these are the 'you already have a better tool' tier. :: SPECIFICS: @react-spring/web@10.1.2 (MIT, 2026-06-24, 3.6M weekly, @react-spring/web itself only 55kB unpacked) is excellent physics but overlaps 100% with Motion's spring transitions and adds a second React animation engine. animejs@4.5.0 (MIT, 2026-06-22) full ESM bundle is 115.9kB raw / 39.9kB gzip — heavier than GSAP core for less capability, and its v4 API (`animate`, `createTimeline`, `createScope`, `createDraggable`, `svg.morphTo`, `text.splitText`, `stagger`, `waapi`) is a straight GSAP subset. `@motionone/dom@10.18.0` last published 2024-06-02 and is superseded by `motion`; its Vue and Solid bindings are formally deprecated.
RECOMMENDATIONS:
  - (high) PRIMARY ENGINE: GSAP 3.15.0 + @gsap/react useGSAP, with the plugin set {ScrollTrigger, SplitText, CustomEase, DrawSVGPlugin, Flip, ScrambleTextPlugin}. SECONDARY: Motion 13.4.1 via LazyMotion + m + domAnimation, scoped strictly to the dashboard. Draw the boundary by SURFACE, not by taste: marketing site = 100% GSAP; dashboard = Motion for anything React state drives (AnimatePresence, layout/layoutId, drag, Reorder) and GSAP only for the live-run stage choreography. | WHY: GSAP became fully free at 3.13.0 (2025-04-30) which retired the only reason anyone picked Motion for timeline work. For a cinematic marketing site the decisive plugins — SplitText with mask+autoSplit+aria, ScrollTrigger with pinning and numeric scrub, DrawSVG for the 9-stage pipeline, CustomEase for a house ease — have no Motion equivalent without paying £299 for Motion+. Meanwhile Motion's `layoutId` shared-element morph genuinely cannot be replicated in GSAP without writing a Flip choreography by hand for every case, and the dashboard needs dozens of those. Measured cost is also on GSAP's side: core+ScrollTrigger is 45.3kB gzip versus 47.8kB for vanilla motion alone. And Graphite.dev, a direct competitor, already ships GSAP — you are not taking an exotic bet. | TRADEOFF: You ship two animation engines (~55kB gz GSAP on marketing, ~20kB gz Motion on dashboard) and you must keep them off the same DOM subtree or you hit Motion's own F-tier read/write thrash. GSAP is NOT MIT — it is Webflow's proprietary 'Standard no charge' license, revocable for non-compliance, and a judge who greps package.json for licenses may raise an eyebrow. Have the one-line answer ready: the current license's Permitted Uses cover 'any website, web application, or digital interface by any person or entity', and only Webflow-competing no-code animation builders are prohibited.
  - (high) Build the 9-stage pipeline diagram (S1 INGEST → S9 FINALISE) as hand-authored inline SVG animated with GSAP DrawSVGPlugin + MotionPathPlugin + a single scrubbed ScrollTrigger. Do not use Rive, do not use Lottie, do not use a WebGL canvas for it. | WHY: Inline SVG keeps the stage labels as real text (SEO, a11y, and it survives a screenshot in the judges' deck), costs 2.2kB gzip of DrawSVG on top of engine you already loaded, and can be re-authored during the demo. Rive costs 775.8kB gzipped of WASM plus an artist you do not have; dotLottie costs 484.8kB gzipped of WASM plus After Effects. CodeRabbit ships Rive and Greptile ships Lottie — beating them on payload while looking better is a defensible differentiator. Concrete recipe: one `<path>` per inter-stage connector with `stroke-dasharray` driven by `DrawSVGPlugin` ('0%' → '100%'), a `<circle>` packet tweened along it with `motionPath: { path: '#s3-s4', align: '#s3-s4', autoRotate: true }`, and each stage node's glow driven by scrub progress. Wire the SAME component to the live `/v1/reviews/{id}` heartbeat on the dashboard so the marketing diagram and the real run view are one piece of code — that is the story judges remember. | TRADEOFF: Hand-authoring an SVG that looks expensive takes real design time and you cannot hand it to a motion designer. You lose the frame-perfect character animation Rive gives you. Mitigate by keeping the diagram geometric — nodes, connectors, packets, a progress rail — which is what the content wants anyway.
  - (high) Use Lenis 1.3.26 on the marketing site ONLY, wired into GSAP's ticker with `autoRaf: false`, and destroy it entirely on the dashboard and under reduced motion. Do not use GSAP ScrollSmoother. | WHY: Lenis is 5.3kB gzip vs ScrollSmoother's 5.4kB but keeps the native DOM, so Cmd-F find-in-page, keyboard scrolling, `position: sticky` and IntersectionObserver all keep working — ScrollSmoother's `#smooth-wrapper` > `#smooth-content` structure breaks `position: fixed` children and find-in-page. Exact wiring, all four lines required: `const lenis = new Lenis({ autoRaf: false, lerp: 0.1, anchors: true }); lenis.on('scroll', ScrollTrigger.update); gsap.ticker.add(t => lenis.raf(t * 1000)); gsap.ticker.lagSmoothing(0);`. Omitting `lagSmoothing(0)` makes GSAP silently swallow long frames and desync from Lenis's own time base. | TRADEOFF: Smooth scroll is genuinely divisive and hijacks a user expectation; some judges hate it on principle. Lenis is capped at 60fps on Safari and 30fps in low-power mode, and it breaks native CSS scroll-snap unless you add `lenis/snap`. Keep `lerp` at 0.1 and `duration` at 1.2 — anything slower reads as broken, not luxurious. A dashboard with smooth scroll is a dashboard that feels slow, so hard-disable it there.
  - (high) Treat CSS scroll-driven animations (`animation-timeline: scroll()` / `view()`) as progressive enhancement ONLY, never as the mechanism. Ship the GSAP path as the baseline and optionally upgrade decorative, non-essential effects inside `@supports (animation-timeline: view())`. | WHY: webstatus.dev today reports scroll-driven animations as Baseline LIMITED: Chrome/Edge 115 (2023-07-18), Safari 26 (2025-09-15), Firefox NOT in stable. If a judge opens the site in Firefox and your hero never reveals, the demo is over before the pipeline diagram loads. GSAP ScrollTrigger works identically in every browser you will encounter. Motion is genuinely the only library that drives scroll-linked animation on native ScrollTimeline where available — but that is a reason to use Motion for a scroll progress bar, not a reason to bet the hero on the CSS feature. | TRADEOFF: You give up truly main-thread-free scroll animation in Chrome and Safari. In practice ScrollTrigger with `scrub: 0.6` on transform/opacity only is indistinguishable at 120Hz. The one place to actually use the native path is a scroll progress rail: `animation-timeline: scroll(root block)` behind an `@supports`, with a ScrollTrigger fallback.
  - (high) Adopt a strict compositor-only motion contract and encode it as a lint rule: you may animate transform, opacity, clip-path and filter. You may NOT animate width, height, top/left, margin, box-shadow, background-color, border-color, or any CSS custom property on :root. Box-shadow 'animations' are two stacked pseudo-elements cross-faded on opacity. | WHY: Per Motion's own published tier list: transform/opacity/filter/clip-path are S-tier compositor-thread; background-color/color/border-radius/mask-image are C-tier paint; width/height/margin/top are D-tier layout. The non-obvious killer is that CSS custom properties ALWAYS trigger paint even when they feed a compositor-only property, and a `--x` on `:root` invalidates the whole subtree — Motion measure that at roughly 8ms/frame versus nanoseconds for a targeted JS write. So the tempting 'drive the whole pipeline diagram from one `--progress` variable on :root' approach is exactly the thing that will drop frames on the demo laptop. Write to elements directly, or to a CSS var scoped to the single animating element. | TRADEOFF: Glow and depth get harder: you cannot tween box-shadow, so every glowing severity chip needs a second absolutely-positioned layer. `filter: blur()` is S-tier for the compositor but still requires a per-frame Gaussian over the alpha channel — cap animated blur radii at ~24px and never animate blur on an element larger than ~600×600 CSS px. `backdrop-filter` only reached Baseline newly on 2024-09-16 (Safari 18) and promotes layers aggressively; use it on at most two surfaces (the sticky nav and the command palette).
  - (high) Ship a single reduced-motion contract enforced in three places: `gsap.matchMedia()` with a `reduceMotion: '(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)'` condition wrapping every marketing timeline; `<MotionConfig reducedMotion="user">` at the dashboard root; and a global CSS killswitch. Under reduced motion, destroy Lenis, skip the R3F canvas entirely (render the poster), and convert every reveal to a 120ms opacity fade with zero translation. | WHY: This is a judged demo — an accessibility reviewer flipping the OS toggle is a real scenario, and 'keeps the human in charge' is literally PRGuard's positioning, so failing a11y would be an own goal on message. `gsap.matchMedia()` (added in 3.11.0, and it supersedes the deprecated `ScrollTrigger.matchMedia()`) takes a conditions object: `mm.add({ isDesktop: '(min-width: 800px)', reduceMotion: '(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)' }, (ctx) => { const { reduceMotion } = ctx.conditions; ... })` and auto-reverts when conditions change. Motion's `reducedMotion="user"` disables transform and layout animations while deliberately keeping opacity and backgroundColor, which is the correct semantic. Back both with `@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) { *, *::before, *::after { animation-duration: 0.01ms !important; animation-iteration-count: 1 !important; transition-duration: 0.01ms !important; scroll-behavior: auto !important; } }`. | TRADEOFF: You maintain two art directions for every scene, and the reduced-motion path must still communicate the pipeline — so the 9-stage diagram needs a static state that shows all stages at once rather than a blank frame. Budget an extra half-day.
  - (high) Kill three classes of Next.js App Router breakage up front: (1) FOUC on first paint, (2) ScrollTrigger corruption across client-side route changes, (3) R3F hydration errors. | WHY: (1) Server-render every to-be-animated element with `visibility: hidden` (Tailwind `invisible`) and reveal with GSAP `autoAlpha`, which toggles visibility and opacity together — never plain `opacity: 0`, because an invisible element still intercepts pointer events during hydration. (2) Call `gsap.registerPlugin(...)` ONCE at module scope, never inside useEffect — inside an effect it double-registers under React StrictMode. In the App Router's client-side navigation the DOM is replaced without a full reload, so every route-scoped ScrollTrigger must die with its component: `useGSAP` + `gsap.context` handles this, and you still need `ScrollTrigger.refresh()` after fonts resolve (`document.fonts.ready.then(() => ScrollTrigger.refresh())`) and after any async findings list renders, plus `invalidateOnRefresh: true` on any trigger whose `end` is computed. (3) R3F: `dynamic(() => import('./Scene'), { ssr: false })` called from a Client Component, with `"use client"` inside Scene. | TRADEOFF: The `visibility: hidden` default means a JS failure leaves a blank hero. Guard it with a `<noscript><style>[data-anim]{visibility:visible!important}</style></noscript>` and a 2.5s watchdog timeout that force-reveals everything if the GSAP bundle never executes. That watchdog has saved more demos than any animation.
  - (medium) Use same-document View Transitions for dashboard navigation (reviews list → review detail → finding detail), and do NOT use cross-document view transitions for the marketing site. | WHY: Same-document View Transitions reached Baseline newly available on 2025-10-14 (Chrome 111 / Safari 18 / Firefox 144) — safe, and nearly free in bytes. Give each finding row `view-transition-name: finding-<fingerprint>` so navigating morphs the severity chip and the fingerprint hash into the detail header: that visually reinforces M3 (identity survives). Motion 13.4.0's `AnimateView` wraps React 19.3's `ViewTransition` if you would rather stay in Motion's API. Cross-document (`@view-transition { navigation: auto }`) is still Baseline limited — Chrome 126, Safari 18.2, no Firefox — so leave it out. | TRADEOFF: You must `flushSync` the state update inside `startViewTransition` or React batches it outside the captured frame and nothing animates. Duplicate `view-transition-name` values on screen at once throw and abort the transition — generate them from the fingerprint and assert uniqueness. Always feature-detect and bail under reduced motion.
  - (high) Allow AT MOST one Three.js/R3F canvas, on the marketing hero only, lazy-loaded below a static poster, with `frameloop="demand"`, `dpr={[1, 1.75]}` and `antialias: false`. If the hero concept works as an animated SVG or a CSS conic/radial gradient mesh, do that instead and skip Three entirely. | WHY: three@0.186.0 is 87.7kB gzipped before you write a shader, and R3F 9.7.0 + drei 10.7.8 + postprocessing 3.1.1 push a realistic hero past 200kB gzip of JS — roughly four times the entire GSAP marketing bundle. WebGPURenderer (200kB gz) is still officially experimental in the three.js manual, so stay on WebGLRenderer. For 'expensive and cinematic' a single full-bleed animated gradient field plus grain plus the SVG pipeline diagram outperforms a mediocre 3D scene, and it cannot crash on the judge's integrated GPU. | TRADEOFF: You lose genuine depth and real refraction. If you do build the canvas, budget for a `<canvas>` context-loss handler and a poster fallback — a black rectangle where the hero should be is worse than never having had one. Never run Three.js on the dashboard; the two long-lived RAF loops plus Lenis plus ScrollTrigger is exactly Motion's F-tier thrash scenario.
  - (medium) Adopt a cool, near-neutral dark palette at oklch hue ~265 with a cobalt 'proof' accent and an OKLCH-equal-lightness severity ramp — deliberately NOT CodeRabbit's purple-black+mint, NOT Greptile's neon pink/lime, NOT Warp's lavender-on-#0B0B0B. | WHY: These are the exact values, computed OKLCH→sRGB: `--bg-base: oklch(0.145 0.006 265)` #090A0D; `--bg-surface: oklch(0.185 0.008 265)` #111316; `--bg-raised: oklch(0.222 0.010 265)` #191B20; `--border-hairline: oklch(0.270 0.012 265)` #24262C; `--border-strong: oklch(0.330 0.014 265)` #32353D; `--text-primary: oklch(0.955 0.004 265)` #EFF0F3; `--text-secondary: oklch(0.760 0.012 265)` #ADB1B9; `--text-muted: oklch(0.580 0.014 265)` #767A83; `--brand-proof: oklch(0.660 0.190 264)` #548AFF; severity ramp at near-constant lightness so the five bands read as one family — critical `oklch(0.62 0.22 22)` #ED3345, high `oklch(0.70 0.18 52)` #F27700, medium `oklch(0.79 0.15 88)` #E2B42C, low `oklch(0.70 0.11 205)` #2CB2BF, info `oklch(0.60 0.02 265)` #7A808D; plus `--state-proven: oklch(0.72 0.17 152)` #35C26D and `--state-disproven: oklch(0.68 0.15 300)` #A780E6. For the proof terminal, steal Linear's diff treatment literally: added `#27A644` on `rgba(39,166,68,0.07)`, removed `#F34E52` on `rgba(243,78,82,0.1)`. Hero glow: one `radial-gradient(60% 50% at 50% 0%, oklch(0.66 0.19 264 / 0.14), transparent 70%)` over #090A0D — one glow, not five. | TRADEOFF: Constant-lightness severity ramps are correct perceptually but make critical-vs-high less screamingly different than a naive red/orange; compensate with iconography and weight, not more chroma. Colour-only severity encoding also fails for colour-blind judges — always pair the band with its numeric score and label. CodeRabbit's base decodes to #121014, Linear's to #08090A, Warp's #0B0B0B — #090A0D is close to Linear's, so differentiate on accent (cobalt, not cyan) and on the severity ramp, which none of them have.
  - (high) Do NOT add react-spring, Anime.js, Motion One, Theatre.js, Popmotion, tailwindcss-animate, Rive or Lottie. Add exactly two small helpers: @formkit/auto-animate 0.10.0 (3.2kB gz) for ledger/audit list churn and @number-flow/react 0.6.2 for the severity score and cost counters. Use tw-animate-css 1.4.0 (pinned) for Radix/shadcn enter-exit. | WHY: Every one of the rejected libraries either duplicates GSAP or Motion (react-spring 10.1.2, Anime.js 4.5.0 at 39.9kB gz), is dead (Motion One's @motionone/dom last published 2024-06-02; Theatre.js last published 2024-05-19 with the repo last pushed 2024-08-14; Popmotion 2022-08-15; tailwindcss-animate 2023-08-28), or costs half a megabyte of WASM (Rive 775.8kB gz, dotLottie 484.8kB gz). auto-animate and number-flow both run on WAAPI (Baseline widely since 2023-03-16) so they add no third engine and both honour prefers-reduced-motion by default. | TRADEOFF: auto-animate gives you no control over the exact curve — do not put it on any list that GSAP Flip or Motion `layout` also animates, or you get double-animated rows. tw-animate-css v2.0.0 is announced with breaking changes, so pin `1.4.0` and do not let a dependabot PR pass unreviewed during demo week.
  - (medium) Engineer one 6-second 'signature stage moment' as a scripted, deterministic GSAP timeline with a keyboard trigger, not as a live scroll-dependent scene: Tool A's hedge ('might null-deref') scrambles via ScrambleTextPlugin into PRGuard's claim ('DOES'), the proof terminal types out the failing test at head SHA, the severity radar draws its six axes via DrawSVG, and the merge gate flips red. | WHY: Hackathon demos are judged on one memorable beat. Building it as a `gsap.timeline({ paused: true })` you fire from a hotkey means it is frame-identical every run, survives a flaky network, and can be scrubbed backwards if a judge asks 'show that again'. ScrambleTextPlugin (11.4kB) is free in GSAP 3.15 and is the single most on-brand effect available: text physically resolving from noise into a proven claim IS the product thesis. Pair with @number-flow/react ticking the computed severity from 0.000 to its final value while the six weighted inputs (.20/.15/.25/.15/.10/.15) draw in. | TRADEOFF: A scripted timeline can read as canned if the underlying data is obviously fake — so drive it from a real cached `/v1/reviews/{id}/result` payload and say out loud that it is a recorded real run. Keep the whole thing under 8 seconds; judges' attention on a single animation dies around 10.
ANTI-PATTERNS / AVOID:
  - Runtime-vector players used as decoration. Shipping 775.8kB of gzipped Rive WASM or 484.8kB of dotLottie WASM to animate something an inline SVG could do is the 2026 equivalent of a Flash intro. CodeRabbit does it; do not copy the expensive habit just because the site looks good.
  - Smooth scroll on a dashboard. Lenis on a data-dense internal tool makes every interaction feel like it is buffering. Smooth scroll belongs on marketing pages and nowhere else — and the 'keyboard-fast' requirement in PRGuard's own brief makes it disqualifying.
  - The mandatory scroll-jacked 'scrollytelling' hero where the page hijacks 4000px to reveal three sentences. This peaked in 2022. Judges scroll fast and will read it as padding. Pin ONE scene — the 9-stage pipeline — because it is genuinely sequential, and let everything else scroll normally.
  - Purple-black + mint green. That is CodeRabbit's exact palette (base #121014, accent #65E6A6). Neon pink + lime is Greptile's (#FFCFFE / #DAFF01). Lavender on pure black is Warp's (#C7AEFF on #0B0B0B). In a category this crowded, arriving in a competitor's colours reads as derivative before a single word is read.
  - Glassmorphism as the whole design language. `backdrop-filter: blur()` only reached Baseline newly on 2024-09-16, promotes every blurred element to its own GPU layer, and has been the default AI-generated-landing-page look for three years. Two surfaces maximum.
  - Generic gradient blob mesh backgrounds — the purple-to-blue animated mesh that every Vercel-template SaaS site has shipped since 2021. If your hero background could be swapped onto a fintech, a CRM and a vector DB without anyone noticing, it is not a hero background.
  - Animating box-shadow, width, height, top/left or background-color to create 'glow' and 'expand'. All of these are paint or layout, every frame, on the main thread. A glowing severity chip is two stacked layers cross-faded on opacity, not a tweened box-shadow.
  - Driving animation from a CSS custom property on `:root`. It looks elegant and it always triggers paint across the whole invalidated subtree — Motion measure the pathological case at ~8ms/frame. Scope animated custom properties to the single element, or write to the element directly.
  - will-change: transform sprayed across every card 'for performance'. A hundred 500×500 CSS px cards on a DPR-2 display is roughly 400MB of permanently allocated texture memory. Browsers already promote transform/opacity transitions for their duration automatically. Add will-change immediately before an animation, remove it immediately after, and only after you have measured a first-frame hitch.
  - Text that animates in letter-by-letter without `aria` handling. An unmanaged SplitText turns your headline into 47 individually-announced characters for a screen reader. GSAP 3.13's SplitText has `aria: 'auto'` — using SplitText without it in 2026 is negligence, not style.
  - Ignoring prefers-reduced-motion, or 'handling' it by only shortening durations. A parallax hero at 0.2s is still a parallax hero. Reduced motion means no translation, no parallax, no scroll-jacking, no autoplaying canvas — a 120ms opacity fade and nothing else.
  - Two animation engines fighting over the same DOM subtree. GSAP Flip plus Motion `layout` on the same list, or Lenis on its own RAF loop alongside GSAP's ticker, is Motion's own F-tier: alternating reads and writes within a frame. Pick one owner per subtree and wire Lenis into `gsap.ticker` with `lagSmoothing(0)`.
  - Betting the hero on `animation-timeline: view()`. It is Baseline LIMITED with no stable Firefox as of today. A judge on Firefox sees a static page and concludes your product is broken.
  - Theatre.js, Popmotion, Motion One, react-spring-as-a-third-engine, and tailwindcss-animate. All dead or redundant in 2026 — @theatre/core and @theatre/studio last published 2024-05-19, popmotion 2022-08-15, @motionone/dom 2024-06-02, tailwindcss-animate 2023-08-28. Blog posts still recommending them are not checking npm.
  - The 200ms-plus page-load 'cinematic intro' that blocks first interaction. Judges have 90 seconds. Every millisecond before they can see a finding is a millisecond spent on you rather than on the product.
  - Spring physics everywhere. A spring on a modal is delightful; a spring on a severity bar chart makes a deterministic, weighted, auditable score look wobbly and approximate. PRGuard's entire thesis is determinism — use linear/expo eases for anything that represents a computed value, and save springs for direct manipulation.
  - Marketing-grade motion leaking into the dashboard. The site should feel expensive; the dashboard should feel instantaneous. If a dashboard transition exceeds 220ms, it is a bug.
FACT-CHECK CORRECTIONS:
    [REFUTED] CodeRabbit ships Rive (two case-insensitive 'rive' matches in served HTML); Graphite ships GSAP (gsap-prefooter-split-text, gsap-decorative-dot) -> The CodeRabbit Rive claim is FALSE and it fails in exactly the way the researcher feared. I re-fetched www.coderabbit.ai (816,597 B) and the only two case-insensitive 'rive' matches are both the substring inside the word "AI-driven". There is no rive.js, no rive.wasm, no .riv asset, no @rive-app reference anywhere in the served markup. Drop 'CodeRabbit ships Rive' and the 'their heavy Rive payload is a weakness you can beat' line from recommendation 2 — the rationale is built on a false positive. The Graphite GSAP claim is fully CONFIRMED: 576 occurrences of gsap-decorative-dot plus gsap-prefooter-split-text and gsap-prefooter-ctas. The separate 'Greptile ships Lottie' claim in recommendation 2 is also CONFIRMED — greptile.com serves /features/agent/continuous-learning.lottie, /features/agent/full-codebase-context.lottie, /features/agent/swarm-agents.lottie and /greptile-lizard.lottie.
    [CORRECTED] ScrollSmoother lacks find-in-page/keyboard accessibility while Lenis retains it; Lenis README caveats (60fps Safari cap, 30fps low-power, no native scroll-snap, iframe issues) -> The Lenis half is CONFIRMED verbatim from the README: 'no support for CSS scroll-snap, you must use (lenis/snap)', 'capped to 60fps on Safari ... and 30fps on low power mode', 'smooth scroll will stop working over iframe since they don't forward wheel events', plus a bonus caveat the researcher omitted — 'position fixed seems to lag on MacOS Safari pre-M1'. Subpaths lenis, lenis/react, lenis/snap all exist in the exports map, @studio-freight/lenis last published 2024-03-17 (abandoned, confirmed), lenis@1.3.26 is MIT and the repo was pushed today. But the ScrollSmoother half is CONTRADICTED by GSAP's own docs, which state ScrollSmoother 'leverages NATIVE scrolling' and 'doesn't suffer from many of the accessibility annoyances common with smooth-scrolling sites'. The docs make no find-in-page or keyboard claim. What IS documented is the position:fixed problem: 'since the content has a CSS transform applied, browsers create a new containing block and that means position: fixed elements will be fixed to the content rather than the viewport'. Rewrite the rationale: choose Lenis for (a) MIT vs proprietary licence and (b) the transform/containing-block problem — not for find-in-page, which is a third-party blog claim the vendor disputes.
    [CORRECTED] OKLCH→sRGB hex pairs computed with an OKLab→linear-sRGB matrix, out-of-gamut values clamped; notably #F27700 for oklch(0.70 0.18 52) may not round-trip -> I recomputed all 17 pairs independently (OKLab→linear sRGB matrix, gamma encode, clamp) and every single one matches the researcher's hex exactly: #090A0D, #111316, #191B20, #24262C, #32353D, #EFF0F3, #ADB1B9, #767A83, #548AFF, #ED3345, #F27700, #E2B42C, #2CB2BF, #7A808D, #35C26D, #A780E6, and CodeRabbit's #121014. The palette is safe to use as written. The self-caution names the WRONG colour, though: oklch(0.70 0.18 52) → #F27700 is fully IN gamut and round-trips cleanly.
