The one value that required clamping is --brand-proof, oklch(0.66 0.190 264) → #548AFF — i.e. the cobalt brand accent, the single most important colour in the system. If you want an exactly round-tripping brand accent, drop chroma to roughly 0.17 at L 0.66.
    [CORRECTED] REC 1 tradeoff: ~55kB gz GSAP on marketing, ~20kB gz Motion on dashboard -> The Motion side is right (LazyMotion m 4.6 + domAnimation 15 = 19.6 KiB ≈ 20). The GSAP side is understated by about 25%. Summing gzip -9 of the exact plugin set the recommendation specifies {core, ScrollTrigger, SplitText, CustomEase, DrawSVGPlugin, Flip, ScrambleTextPlugin} = 28,314 + 17,977 + 3,653 + 3,668 + 2,200 + 9,701 + 3,938 = 69,451 B = 67.8 KiB gzip, not ~55. Recommendation 2 additionally requires MotionPathPlugin (9,695 B / 9.5 KiB), taking it to ~77 KiB. If Flip is moved to the dashboard surface, marketing drops to ~58 KiB. Budget ~68–77 KiB gz for marketing. This also softens recommendation 8's 'roughly four times the entire GSAP marketing bundle' — 200 KiB vs 68 KiB is about 3×, not 4×.
    [CORRECTED] REC 8 premise: Motion 13.4.0's AnimateView wraps React 19.3's ViewTransition if you would rather stay in Motion's API -> Substantially right but the gating is now better than described. motion@13.4.1's exports map does contain './react-animate-view', and dist/es/animate-view.mjs is the one top-level ESM file carrying a first-line "use client". Per the docs, AnimateView 'was originally in Motion+ early access' but is now in the open-source library — 'AnimateView no longer requires a Motion+ membership or access token'. So it is FREE, not a Motion+ perk. Critically, it 'requires React and React DOM 19.3 or later' — which collides with the React Three Fiber pin described in my additional notes.
    [CORRECTED] REC 9 reference: three.js manual at https://threejs.org/manual/en/webgpurenderer.html still calls WebGPURenderer experimental; three@0.186.0 is 87.7kB gz, webgpu build ~200kB gz -> The size facts are exactly right: three@0.186.0 published 2026-09-08, MIT; three.module.min.js 392,831 B / 89,850 B gz = 383.6 / 87.7 KiB; three.webgpu.min.js 820,842 B / 204,790 B gz = 801.6 / 200.0 KiB. But the cited URL is dead — https://threejs.org/manual/en/webgpurenderer.html returns HTTP 404, as do /manual/en/webgpu-renderer.html, the raw GitHub path, and the WebGPURenderer docs page. The three.js manual is hash-routed; https://threejs.org/manual/#en/webgpu-renderer returns 200 but serves an SPA shell I could not extract prose from. So the specific 'the manual still calls WebGPURenderer experimental' wording is UNVERIFIABLE as cited and the reference URL must be replaced. The practical advice (stay on WebGLRenderer for a hackathon hero) is unaffected by this, but do not quote the manual.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] REC 4 premise: ScrollTrigger works identically in every browser; Motion is the only library driving scroll-linked animation on native ScrollTimeline where available -> ScrollTrigger's dist genuinely contains `batch`, `anticipatePin` and `invalidateOnRefresh` so the API surface quoted in the references is real. But motion.dev/docs/react-scroll returns HTTP 404, so I could not verify Motion's native-ScrollTimeline / off-main-thread claim from a primary source, and I had exhausted the session's web-search budget. Treat 'Motion is the only library that drives scroll-linked animation on native ScrollTimeline' as an unsourced vendor-adjacent claim.
VERIFIER ADDITIONS:
    + BLOCKING DEPENDENCY CONFLICT THE RESEARCHER MISSED. @react-three/fiber@9.7.0 declares peerDependencies {react: '>=19 <19.3', react-dom: '>=19 <19.3'} — it explicitly EXCLUDES React 19.3.0, which the report itself names as the npm latest and assumes the project targets. The upper bound was introduced in 9.5.0 (2025-12-30) and is still in place in 9.6.0, 9.6.1 and 9.7.0 (2026-07-31). Worse, this collides head-on with recommendation 8's own suggestion of Motion's AnimateView, whose docs require 'React and React DOM 19.3 or later'. You cannot have R3F 9.7.0 and AnimateView in the same tree without --legacy-peer-deps or a package.json override. For a hackathon this is an argument for taking the 'skip Three entirely' branch the recommendation already offers, or for pinning React to 19.2.0 (2025-10-01) and giving up AnimateView. For contrast, @react-three/drei@10.7.8 (peer react ^19) and @react-three/postprocessing@3.1.1 (peer react ^19.0.0, @react-three/fiber >=9.7.0) have no such upper bound — the pin is R3F's alone. Verify with `npm view @react-three/fiber@9.7.0 peerDependencies`.
    + THE CODERABBIT-RIVE CLAIM IS A FALSE POSITIVE AND IT LOAD-BEARS RECOMMENDATION 2. The only two 'rive' matches on www.coderabbit.ai are inside the word 'AI-driven'. There is no Rive on that site. Recommendation 2's rationale ('CodeRabbit ships Rive ... beating them on payload while looking better is a defensible differentiator') and the CodeRabbit reference entry's 'Next.js + Rive (rive.wasm is 775.8kB gzipped on top of 98.3kB of JS)' are both wrong — those are Rive's generic payload numbers, which I confirmed independently, but they are not CodeRabbit's. Delete the claim. The Greptile-ships-Lottie half survives (four .lottie assets served) and the Graphite-ships-GSAP half is very strong (576 gsap-decorative-dot elements), so the 'competitors ship heavy animation runtimes' story still has two legs — just not three.
    + RIVE'S FREE TIER CANNOT EXPORT. rive.app/pricing shows the $0 tier as 3 collaborative files + 1 project with 'No exports, libraries, or hosting features' — .riv export starts at Cadet ($9/seat/mo). The report rejects Rive on payload and on 'you don't have an artist'; the stronger and simpler argument is that you cannot ship a .riv at all without paying. Also update the download figure: @rive-app/canvas is at 800,647 weekly, not ~710k.
    + THE GSAP LICENCE ANSWER IS NOW BULLETPROOF — USE THE ACTUAL WORDING. Because the plain-English 'no fee to end users' summary is verifiably dead HTML (every line individually comment-wrapped; it vanishes when comments are stripped), the one-line answer for a judge should quote Section I directly: Permitted Uses = use 'on any website, web application, or digital interface by any person or entity'. Pair it with the one genuinely uncomfortable fact, Section V: 'Webflow may terminate this GSAP License and revoke your access in its discretion if you fail to comply with any of these terms.' Also note the report's own reference entry for @gsap/react omits that its npm license field is 'SEE LICENSE AT https://gsap.com/standard-license' — the hook is proprietary too, so a package.json licence grep surfaces two non-OSI entries, not one.
    + RECOMMENDATION 5 AND RECOMMENDATION 8 CONTRADICT EACH OTHER ON MOTION'S OWN TIER LIST. Recommendation 5 adopts Motion's tier list as a hard lint contract. That same tier list places the View Transitions API in C-TIER (paint-triggering), alongside background-color and border-radius. Recommendation 8 then makes same-document View Transitions the dashboard navigation mechanism. Both positions are defensible — a once-per-navigation paint is not a per-frame paint — but the report should say so explicitly rather than leave a reviewer to find its own cited authority arguing against its own recommendation.
    + THE 8ms FIGURE IS AN ANECDOTE, NOT A BENCHMARK. Motion's article says: 'I recently found a site updating a global CSS variable every frame. It forced style recalculations on 1300+ elements, costing a whopping 8 ms per frame.' The general rule ('changing one will always trigger paint on affected elements') is sound and independently well-established, but 8ms is contingent on 1300+ affected elements. Quoting '~8ms/frame for CSS variables' as a flat cost will not survive a technically literate judge. State it as 'a global :root variable forced recalc on 1300+ elements and cost 8ms/frame in one measured case'.
    + THE LENIS-OVER-SCROLLSMOOTHER ARGUMENT NEEDS REBUILDING. GSAP's own ScrollSmoother docs claim it 'leverages NATIVE scrolling' and 'doesn't suffer from many of the accessibility annoyances common with smooth-scrolling sites' — directly contradicting the find-in-page/keyboard rationale, which came from third-party blogs. What IS documented and does support Lenis: ScrollSmoother applies a CSS transform to #smooth-content, which 'create[s] a new containing block and that means position: fixed elements will be fixed to the content rather than the viewport'. Combine that with Lenis being MIT vs GSAP's revocable proprietary licence and the recommendation stands — but on different evidence. Also add the README caveat the report omitted: 'position fixed seems to lag on MacOS Safari pre-M1'.
    + MOTION+ IS A WEAKER MOAT THAN THE REPORT ARGUES, IN BOTH DIRECTIONS. AnimateView has graduated out of Motion+ early access and is now free ('AnimateView no longer requires a Motion+ membership or access token'), so the £299 figure buys less than recommendation 1's rationale implies. Conversely, motion/vgpu's and motion/three's code ships completely unguarded inside the MIT npm tarball — I downloaded dist/es/vgpu.mjs (8,136 B) and dist/es/three.mjs (6,238 B) and neither contains any token, licence or entitlement check. Motion+ is a docs-and-examples paywall, not a technical one. Neither fact changes the GSAP-primary conclusion, but both should be stated accurately.
    + BUDGET THE MARKETING BUNDLE AT ~68–77 KiB gz, NOT ~55. The exact plugin set recommendation 1 specifies sums to 67.8 KiB gzip, and recommendation 2 adds MotionPathPlugin for another 9.5 KiB. This is still less than half of a realistic R3F hero and the argument is unharmed, but the stated number is wrong by ~25% and is the kind of thing a judge checks with one devtools panel.
    + VERSION WATCH FOR DEMO WEEK. motion@13.4.1 and next@16.3.6 were BOTH published today (2026-09-22, 16:40Z and 16:19Z). Pinning exact versions in package.json matters more than usual — you are adopting same-day releases of two core dependencies. tw-animate-css is the opposite risk: latest release v1.4.0 is from 2025-09-24, almost a year old, with the repo last pushed 2026-02-28 and only a 1.3.0-canary.35 tag since. The v2 breaking-change warning is real but has been sitting in the README a long time; pinning 1.4.0 is right and a dependabot bump during demo week is the actual hazard, exactly as the report says.
    + NAMING NOTE FOR THE package.json A JUDGE WILL READ. framer-motion@13.4.1 and motion@13.4.1 are the same release published minutes apart, and framer-motion still carries 33.6M weekly downloads against motion's 15.4M. If you install `motion`, expect `framer-motion` to appear in the lockfile anyway (motion/react-client is literally `export * from 'framer-motion/client'`). Not a problem, but worth being able to explain rather than being surprised by it on stage.

### AREA: Component ecosystems — 21st.dev, React Bits, and everything adjacent (component-kits)
FINDINGS:
  - THE BIGGEST FIND: Vercel AI Elements (elements.ai-sdk.dev, github.com/vercel/ai-elements, Apache-2.0, ~2.5k stars) ships components that are almost literally PRGuard's spec. Its 'Code' group contains Terminal, Test Results, Stack Trace, Commit, File Tree, Code Block, Sandbox, Snippet, Schema Display, Package Info, Environment Variables, Web Preview, Agent, Artifact, JSX Preview. Its 'Chatbot' group has Chain of Thought, Task, Plan, Reasoning, Tool, Checkpoint, Confirmation, Queue, Context, Shimmer. Its 'Workflow' group has Canvas, Node, Edge, Connection, Controls, Panel, Toolbar. Install: `npx ai-elements@latest add <name>` or `npx shadcn@latest add https://elements.ai-sdk.dev/api/registry/all.json`.
  - AI Elements <Terminal> is the only terminal in the whole ecosystem built for REAL output, not a scripted demo: props are `output` (string, "supports ANSI codes"), `isStreaming` (bool, animated cursor), `autoScroll` (default true), `onClear`. Docs state "Full ANSI color support (256 colors, bold, italic, underline)" via the `ansi-to-react` library. Magic UI's <Terminal> by contrast is a scripted <TypingAnimation duration=60ms/char> + <AnimatedSpan> sequence with `sequence=true`/`startOnView=true` — fine for marketing, wrong for PRGuard's S7 PROOF pane.
  - AI Elements <TestResults> maps 1:1 onto PRGuard's proof_status. Props: `summary {passed, failed, skipped, total, duration}`; four statuses with fixed color coding — passed=green, failed=red, skipped=yellow, running=animated blue; subcomponents TestResultsProgress, TestSuite (`name`,`status`,`defaultOpen`), Test (`name`,`status`,`duration` ms), TestError/TestErrorMessage/TestErrorStack. <StackTrace> takes a raw `trace` string, parses frames, dims node_modules frames (`showInternalFrames`, default true), `maxHeight` 400px, `onFilePathClick` callback, plus StackTraceCopyButton/ExpandButton. That is the 'here's the failing test and the stack trace' demo moment, pre-built.
  - AI Elements <Commit> is built for PRGuard's base-SHA/head-SHA and the 'agent deleted a test' moment: CommitFileStatus takes status="added|modified|deleted|renamed" with green/yellow/RED/blue coding, CommitFileAdditions/CommitFileDeletions render the '+12 −5' pair, CommitHash + CommitCopyButton (`timeout` 2000). A deleted test file renders red by default with no custom work.
  - shadcn/ui changed its DEFAULT primitive from Radix to Base UI in July 2026 (changelog `2026-07-base-ui-default`); the community picked Base UI 2:1 on shadcn/create. Radix is NOT deprecated — pin it with `pnpm dlx shadcn init -b radix`, and registry authors pin with a `registry:base` config. September 2026 added a dedicated `cn` npm package replacing the hand-copied `twMerge(clsx(...))` helper; migrate with `pnpm dlx shadcn@latest migrate cn` (lib/utils.ts still re-exports `cn`). Practical consequence: most motion registries (Magic UI, Aceternity, React Bits) still emit `import { cn } from "@/lib/utils"` and assume Radix, so init PRGuard with `-b radix` unless you want to hand-patch.
  - Magic UI's live registry at magicui.design/r/registry.json reports name="magicui", 250 items, MIT with NO Commons Clause. I verified every PRGuard shortlist item is actually present: animated-beam, border-beam, shine-border, magic-card, terminal, code-comparison, file-tree, number-ticker, marquee, bento-grid, dock, orbiting-circles, scroll-progress, dot-pattern, grid-pattern, flickering-grid, particles, globe, text-reveal, hyper-text, animated-list, warp-background, light-rays, noise-texture, floating-3d-particles, glyph-matrix, backlight, dia-text-reveal, progressive-blur, animated-theme-toggler, safari/iphone/android mocks. Zero missing.
  - THE MAGIC UI FINGERPRINT (read from registry source, not docs): AnimatedBeam and BorderBeam both default to `gradientStartColor="#ffaa40"` → `gradientStopColor="#9c40ff"` (orange→violet). MagicCard defaults to `gradientFrom="#9E7AFF"` → `gradientTo="#FE8BBB"` (violet→pink), `gradientColor="#262626"`, `gradientSize=200`, `gradientOpacity=0.8`. ShineBorder: `borderWidth=1, duration=14, shineColor="#000000"`. AnimatedBeam full defaults: `curvature=0, duration=5, delay=0, pathColor="gray", pathWidth=2, pathOpacity=0.2, repeat=Infinity`. Shipping any of those two gradient pairs untouched is THE tell that a site was assembled from Magic UI.
  - Aceternity itself concedes its flagship effect is burnt out. The ui.aceternity.com/components/spotlight-new page's own description reads: "A subtle yet effective spotlight effect, because the previous version is used a bit too much these days." Its registry.json has 284 items under namespace "aceternity". Pricing as of Sept 2026: $0 free tier, $169/yr, $199 lifetime (one-time), $1,590 team/10 seats. Its newer shader family (dither-shader, spotlight-shader, dot-distortion-shader, lines-gradient-shader, cloud-shader, dotted-glow-background, noise-background) is far less saturated than the 2023-24 spotlight/beams/3d-card generation.
  - React Bits is MIT + Commons Clause — the clause reads you may use it "including for any commercial purpose, so long as you do not sell, sublicense, or redistribute the components themselves." That is FINE for PRGuard (a product) and would only bite if PRGuard shipped a component library. ~47.9k GitHub stars, ~150 free components in 4 variants each (JS-CSS / JS-TW / TS-CSS / TS-TW), install `npx shadcn@latest add @react-bits/<Name>-TS-TW` or jsrepo. Pro tier (Next.js 16 + React 19 + TW4) adds 280 blocks / 300 app-UI / 15 templates / 20 agent skills.
  - React Bits engine reality, verified by reading the actual source files rather than the docs: FaultyTerminal, DarkVeil and Aurora all import `{ Renderer, Program, Mesh, Triangle } from 'ogl'` — OGL, a tiny WebGL lib, NOT three.js. ElectricBorder is pure Canvas 2D with ZERO dependencies (`color='#5227FF', speed=1, chaos=0.12, borderRadius=24`). DotGrid needs `gsap` + `InertiaPlugin`. Repo-level deps pin motion ^12.23.12, gsap ^3.13.0, three ^0.180.0, ogl ^1.0.11, matter-js ^0.20.0, shiki ^4.4.3, tailwindcss ^4.0.3, react ^19.
  - GSAP has been 100% free since April 2025 under Webflow, including every formerly Club-only plugin — SplitText, MorphSVG, DrawSVG, ScrollTrigger, InertiaPlugin — with no license key or auth token. This kills the historic reason to avoid GSAP-dependent components (e.g. React Bits DotGrid, ScrollFloat, ScrollReveal, AnimatedContent).
  - Motion is at 13.4.0 (14 Sept 2026); import path is `motion/react`, not `framer-motion` (the old package still resolves but is no longer developed). v13.0.0 dropped the optional @emotion/is-prop-valid dep; 13.2.0 added `animate.addEffect()` with Three.js/GPU effect support; 13.3.0 cut spring retarget time ~80% and startup ~20%; 13.4.0 added `AnimateView` for React 19.3 view transitions. Magic UI source confirms it already imports from `motion/react`.
  - Origin UI NO LONGER EXISTS at originui.com — it 301-redirects to coss.com/ui. Origin UI merged into COSS (coss.com, the new holding company of Cal.com); the library was rebuilt from the ground up on Base UI and rebranded 'particles' (~508 components, still early). The legacy Origin UI catalog is parked at coss.com/origin. Any 2025-era recommendation to use originui.com is stale.
  - LINEAR'S EXACT DARK TOKENS, scraped live from linear.app's computed :root — this is the premium benchmark to calibrate against. Surfaces: body `#08090A`, level-1 `#0F1011`, level-3 `#191A1B`, quinary `#282828`. Text: `#F7F8F8` / `#D0D6E0` / `#8A8F98` / `#62666D`. Borders: `#23252A` / `#34343A` / `#3E3E44` / `#FFFFFF0D`; hairlines `#37393A` / `#202122` / `#18191A` / `#141515`. Accent `#7170FF`, hover `#828FFF`, tint `#18182F`. Header `#0B0B0BCC` with `--header-blur: 20px`. Shadows: low `0 2px 4px #0000001A`, medium `0 4px 24px #0003`, high `0 7px 32px #00000059`. Easings: `--ease-out-quart cubic-bezier(.165,.84,.44,1)`, `--ease-out-expo cubic-bezier(.19,1,.22,1)`, `--ease-out-quint cubic-bezier(.23,1,.32,1)`, `--ease-in-out-quart cubic-bezier(.77,0,.175,1)`; speeds `.1s` quick / `.25s` regular. Type: Inter Variable with `font-feature-settings: "cv01","ss03"` and `font-variation-settings: "opsz" auto`; bold is weight 680 (not 700); h1 is 64px / weight 510 / letter-spacing -1.408px (= -0.022em); mono is Berkeley Mono.
  - CODERABBIT'S ACTUAL SITE — the bar to beat — is far more restrained than its ARR suggests. Scraped: 137 CSS custom properties in Radix-Colors-style 12-step ramps (`--color-cui-mauve-1 #fdfdfe` … `--color-cui-mauve-12 #211f24`), brand orange `--color-cui-cr-orange-9: #ff570a`, success green `--color-cui-secondary-9: #46e1a5`, red `#e54666`, amber `#ffc53d`. Near-black body, elevated sections `rgb(24,22,28)` = `#18161C`. Fonts: Geist + a mono literally named `hack`. h1 is only 48px / weight 500 / -2% tracking, animating per-letter through "reviewing it." → "securing it." → "prioritizing it." 153 inline SVGs, 2 canvases, ZERO <video>, and no GSAP/three/lottie/Rive script tags at all. It is a competent Geist-and-Radix-Colors site, not a cinematic one.
  - The category is NOT uniformly dark, which means there is differentiation room. Greptile is now LIGHT: body `#e9e9e9`, panels `#eef2f5` / `#dbe5ed` / `#cfd7e7`, deep navy type `#172b76`, hot-pink accent `#ff488b`, display face 'ABC Favorit Trial' + 'Suisse Int'l' / 'Suisse Int'l Mono', h1 96px/700 in 'Anybody'. Graphite is near-black (`lab(2.75 0 0)`) in 'matterFont', h1 60px / 500 / -1.5px, driven by a hero <video> with zero canvases. Meanwhile the frontier labs (OpenAI, Anthropic, Perplexity, Mistral, Cohere, xAI, Scale) have all converged on off-white/cream + oversized sans + serif accents + blurred gradient abstraction — i.e. the 'expensive' signal in 2026 has moved AWAY from dark-violet-gradient, which is now the mid-tier AI-startup uniform ("dark hero section", "gradient somewhere between indigo and violet", "sparkle or star icon denoting Intelligence").
  - Paper Shaders (`@paper-design/shaders-react`, Apache-2.0, ~3.5k stars, zero runtime dependencies) is the credible premium alternative to Aceternity backgrounds — 30+ real GPU shaders you tune live on shaders.paper.design and export with your settings baked in: mesh-gradient, static-mesh-gradient, static-radial-gradient, grain-gradient, dithering, god-rays, liquid-metal, smoke-ring, neuro-noise, perlin, simplex-noise, voronoi, warp, spiral, swirl, waves, metaballs, dot-orbit, dot-grid, color-panels, pulsing-border, heatmap, gem-smoke, plus image filters (paper-texture, fluted-glass, water, image-dithering, halftone-dots, halftone-CMYK, lens-distortion). Cult UI's Hero Dithering / Hero Liquid Metal / Hero Color Panels / Hero Heatmap / Hero Static Radial Gradient are thin wrappers over these.
  - Magic UI <CodeComparison> is a ready-made base-SHA-vs-head-SHA diff: props `beforeCode`, `afterCode`, `language`, `filename`, `lightTheme` (default "github-light"), `darkTheme` (default "github-dark"), `highlightColor` (default "rgba(101, 117, 133, 0.16)"), side-by-side layout, and it supports Shiki transformer annotations `[!code ++]`, `[!code --]`, `[!code highlight]`, `[!code focus]` (i.e. it's Shiki + @shikijs/transformers under the hood). Font licensing note for the mono you pair with it: JetBrains Mono, Geist Mono and Commit Mono are all SIL OFL 1.1 (free commercial), but Berkeley Mono — the font Linear uses and the one that reads most 'expensive dev tool' — is paid (from ~$75 personal), tiered commercially by company size, and explicitly not open-source-compatible.
REFERENCES:
  - [must-steal] Vercel AI Elements — Terminal https://elements.ai-sdk.dev/components/terminal :: STEAL: The entire S7 PROOF pane in the dashboard. This is the only ecosystem terminal that renders real program output rather than a scripted typing demo. :: SPECIFICS: Props: `output` (string, ANSI-aware), `isStreaming` (bool → animated cursor), `autoScroll` (default true), `onClear` (presence enables TerminalClearButton), `className`. Subcomponents: TerminalHeader, TerminalTitle, TerminalStatus, TerminalActions, TerminalCopyButton, TerminalClearButton, TerminalContent. Docs: "Full ANSI color support (256 colors, bold, italic, underline)" via `ansi-to-react`. Example given is literally `output="\x1b[32m✓\x1b[0m Success"`. Apache-2.0. Install `npx ai-elements@latest add terminal`. Wire TerminalStatus to proof_status and TerminalTitle to `pytest -q tests/test_repro_<fingerprint>.py @ <sha>`.

Run it twice per finding — once at base SHA, once at head SHA — and put them side by side. That IS the signature demo moment. :: SPECIFICS: `<TestResults summary={{passed, failed, skipped, total, duration}}>`; statuses passed(green)/failed(red)/skipped(yellow)/running(animated blue). Parts: TestResultsHeader, TestResultsSummary, TestResultsProgress (progress bar), TestResultsContent, TestSuite(`name`,`status`,`defaultOpen`), TestSuiteName, TestSuiteStats(`passed`,`failed`,`skipped`), Test(`name`,`status`,`duration` ms), TestStatus, TestName, TestDuration, TestError, TestErrorMessage, TestErrorStack. Uniforms include iTime, iResolution, uScale, uGridMul, uDigitSize. MIT + Commons Clause (fine for PRGuard; you just can't resell the component). Tune it DOWN hard — 8-12% opacity behind the hero, desaturated to your single accent hue — so it reads as texture, not as a Matrix screensaver. Sibling CRT Warp, Grid Scan, Radar and Letter Glitch are in the same family.

Wrap a CRITICAL / proof_status=proven card in it and it reads as 'this is live and dangerous' without a single third-party dependency. :: SPECIFICS: Source-verified: pure Canvas 2D, ZERO dependencies (no motion, no gsap, no ogl).
