RECOMMENDATIONS:
  - (high) Build the dashboard on Vercel AI Elements (Apache-2.0) as the primary component layer, and build the marketing site's two hero proof moments from the SAME components. Specifically: Terminal + TestResults + StackTrace + Commit for M1 PROOF BY EXECUTION, Workflow Canvas/Node/Edge for the 9 stages, Task/Chain-of-Thought for the S3 specialist fan-out, Checkpoint/Confirmation for M4 governance. | WHY: No other library in the ecosystem has components for test results, stack traces, commits, file trees and streaming ANSI terminals — everyone else ships marquees and glowing borders. Using the identical components on marketing and in-product means the landing page screenshot IS the product, which is the single strongest 'this is a real product not a hackathon project' signal a judge can perceive in three seconds. It also collapses two build efforts into one. Apache-2.0 is unambiguous for commercial use. | TRADEOFF: The GitHub README lists 'Next.js project with AI SDK installed' as a prerequisite — the presentational components (Terminal, TestResults, StackTrace, Commit, CodeBlock, FileTree) almost certainly do not import AI SDK hooks, but VERIFY THIS FIRST by installing one component and reading its imports before committing the architecture. If AI SDK is genuinely required, installing `ai` as a dependency is cheap and you're using Anthropic anyway. Second risk: only ~2.5k stars and a fast-moving 2026 release cadence, so pin versions — but since shadcn-style installs copy source into your repo, you own it after install and upstream churn can't break you.
  - (high) Derive the entire colour system from proof_status and severity bands rather than picking a brand gradient. Concretely: base #08090A, surfaces #0F1011 / #16181A, hairlines #FFFFFF0D and #23252A, text #F7F8F8 / #8A8F98 / #62666D. Then the ONLY saturated colours on the site are semantic — proven/blocking red, disproven green, inconclusive amber, not_attempted neutral — plus one cool accent for interactive affordances. Ban violet entirely. | WHY: This is the highest-leverage anti-generic move available and it costs nothing. Every copy-paste site looks generic because its colour comes from a component default (#9c40ff, #5227FF, #9E7AFF→#FE8BBB) rather than from the product. PRGuard's four-state proof_status is a ready-made, genuinely differentiated palette that no competitor can copy without copying the mechanic. It also makes every screenshot instantly legible: a judge scanning the dashboard sees red/green/amber and understands M1 before reading a word. And it directly reinforces the positioning — a trust product whose colours encode evidence states is self-demonstrating. | TRADEOFF: You lose the 'gradient-dark, cinematic' warmth the owner explicitly asked for, and red/green carries a colour-blindness risk so every status must ALSO carry an icon and a text label (which AI Elements' TestStatus already does). Recover the cinematic quality through a single restrained shader and light, not through hue variety.
  - (medium) Use exactly ONE signature background effect across the whole marketing site, and make it Paper Shaders <Dithering> or <GrainGradient> at 6-10% opacity over #08090A — NOT an aurora, NOT a spotlight, NOT background-beams. If you want a second, use React Bits FaultyTerminal (OGL) behind the proof section only, desaturated to a single hue. | WHY: Ordered dithering and film grain read as machine-precision and photographic, which is tonally correct for a product whose claim is 'we prove it'. Aurora/spotlight/beams read as AI-hype, which actively contradicts the positioning. Paper Shaders is Apache-2.0, zero runtime dependencies, GPU-accelerated, and you tune parameters live on shaders.paper.design and export the exact settings — so the output is genuinely yours, not a default. Crucially it is far less saturated in this market than the Aceternity generation: Aceternity's own docs admit the spotlight is 'used a bit too much these days'. | TRADEOFF: WebGL costs battery and can jank on integrated GPUs — gate it behind `prefers-reduced-motion`, an IntersectionObserver, and a static exported PNG/WebP fallback. Also, hackathon demos are often screen-shared at low bitrate where fine dither and grain compress into mud; test over the actual demo pipeline and be ready to raise contrast or fall back to a static gradient for the presentation.
  - (high) Run `pnpm dlx shadcn init -b radix` (pin Radix explicitly), on Tailwind v4 with OKLCH tokens, and adopt the new `cn` package (`pnpm dlx shadcn@latest migrate cn`) only after all third-party components are installed. | WHY: shadcn/ui flipped its default primitive to Base UI in July 2026. Magic UI, Aceternity, Cult UI, React Bits and AI Elements were all authored against the Radix era and emit `import { cn } from "@/lib/utils"`. Initialising on the new Base UI default risks primitive mismatches and import errors in anything that wraps a Dialog/Tooltip/Tabs, which is exactly the kind of 30-minute yak-shave a hackathon cannot absorb. `-b radix` is one flag and removes the whole class of problem. Doing the `cn` migration last avoids fighting each installer's assumptions mid-build. | TRADEOFF: You're pinning to the non-default path, so future `shadcn add` invocations need the same flag and CI scripts must carry it. Radix is explicitly not deprecated ('mature, tested library, still run in production') so there is no correctness or longevity cost — only a mild 'not on the newest thing' cost that no judge will see.
  - (high) Build the 9-stage pipeline diagram ONCE, on AI Elements Workflow (React Flow), and drive it from the real GET /v1/reviews/{id} payload — stage_index, stage_total, stage_label, heartbeat — on both the marketing site (replaying a recorded run) and the dashboard (live). Do not hand-roll SVG beams for it. | WHY: An animated architecture diagram is on the owner's must-have list, and the lazy version (Magic UI AnimatedBeam between logo tiles) is the single most recognisable 'integrations section' cliché in the category — it measures DOM rects and will fight you at every breakpoint. React Flow gives you real nodes, real edges, dark mode via `colorMode="dark"`, pan/zoom, and MIT licensing. Replaying a real recorded run on the marketing site means the diagram is truthful, which is the brand. And because the same component renders live runs, you build it once for two surfaces. | TRADEOFF: React Flow is a heavier dependency than an SVG with a couple of animated gradients, and its stock look (dotted background, rounded controls) is recognisable — restyle Node with your own Card, hide the default MiniMap, and replace Controls. On a phone, a 9-node horizontal graph must collapse to a vertical stepper; budget time for that second layout or the mobile experience will be the weakest thing a judge sees.
  - (high) Change EVERY default value in every copied component before shipping. Mandatory overrides: Magic UI AnimatedBeam/BorderBeam gradientStartColor #ffaa40 → gradientStopColor #9c40ff; MagicCard gradientFrom #9E7AFF → gradientTo #FE8BBB; ShineBorder shineColor #000000 / duration 14; React Bits ElectricBorder color #5227FF / borderRadius 24. Also replace every default duration and easing with ONE project easing set. | WHY: Those four colour pairs are literally the fingerprint of 'assembled from Magic UI / React Bits'. Anyone in the judging room who has built a landing page in the last eighteen months recognises #9c40ff on sight. Overriding them is a five-minute change that removes the single loudest generic signal. The same applies to timing: stock durations (AnimatedBeam 5s, BorderBeam 6s, ShineBorder 14s, OrbitingCircles 20s) plus Motion's default spring produce the 'everything eases the same way' feel. Adopt a single set — Linear's is proven: ease-out-quart cubic-bezier(.165,.84,.44,1) for entrances, ease-out-expo cubic-bezier(.19,1,.22,1) for the hero, 100ms for hovers and 250ms for state changes. | TRADEOFF: Pure discipline cost, roughly an hour across the build, with zero technical risk. The only real danger is forgetting one — so grep the repo for `#9c40ff`, `#ffaa40`, `#9E7AFF`, `#FE8BBB`, `#5227FF` as a pre-demo checklist item.
  - (high) Pick one distinctive display face + Geist Mono (SIL OFL 1.1) for every SHA, fingerprint, token count, cost figure and severity score. Set the mono with `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums`. Do NOT use Berkeley Mono, and do not ship Inter as your display face. | WHY: Greptile (Anybody 96px/700 + Suisse) and Graphite (Matter) both look art-directed rather than assembled, and typography is the reason — not their animations. Meanwhile Inter + a violet gradient is the documented uniform of AI-generated sites. A monospace used *systematically* for every machine-produced value is also a semantic argument: it says 'these are computed facts, not opinions', which is exactly PRGuard's thesis versus 'more LLM adjectives'. Tabular figures stop the live cost/latency counters from jittering. Geist Mono is SIL OFL 1.1, free for commercial use, and pairs with Geist Sans if you want a coherent free system. | TRADEOFF: Berkeley Mono is the font that most reads 'expensive dev tool' (it's Linear's choice) but it is paid from ~$75 personal, licensed commercially by company size, and explicitly not open-source-compatible — do not ship it on a hackathon budget. For the display face, a free-but-distinctive choice costs search time; if the clock runs out, Geist Sans at weight 500-520 with -0.022em tracking is a safe, non-embarrassing default that still beats Inter-at-700.
  - (high) Make the hero the PROOF, not a shader. Above the fold: a two-pane Terminal/TestResults split showing the same repro test PASSING at the base SHA and FAILING at the head SHA, with the real ANSI output and the stack trace, headlined by the positioning line. Let the shader be a 6-10% texture behind it. | WHY: The brief's own signature moment is 'Tool A says this MIGHT null-deref. We say it DOES.' That is a *comparison*, and comparisons are visual. Every competitor hero is a headline over an abstract gradient; a hero that is two terminals disagreeing is instantly differentiated, instantly explains M1, and cannot be replicated by anyone who hasn't built sandboxed execution. It also inverts the usual 3-second problem: instead of impressing with polish and then explaining, you impress WITH the substance. CodeRabbit's h1 is 48px over inline SVGs and no video — the bar for out-crafting them is low, but the bar for out-*arguing* them is where you actually win. | TRADEOFF: Real terminal output is dense and small; you must art-direct it hard — truncate to ~8 meaningful lines per pane, boost contrast well past a real terminal's, and set a minimum 14px mono so it survives a projector. There's also a truthfulness obligation: if the panes are a recorded real run, say so; a faked terminal in a product whose entire pitch is 'we prove it' is a catastrophic own-goal if a judge asks.
  - (medium) For the M2 COMPUTED SEVERITY visual, build a custom SVG/Canvas breakdown from scratch — do not use a stock radar chart. Render the six weighted inputs as a horizontal stacked contribution bar (reachability .20, blast_radius .15, path_sensitivity .25, coverage_gap .15, churn .10, proof_confidence .15) with the band threshold lines at .15/.35/.55/.75 drawn in, plus the human-readable sentence underneath. | WHY: This is PRGuard's most visually ownable mechanic and the one thing no component library has. A stock radar chart says 'I used a chart library'; a weighted contribution bar with visible threshold gates says 'this is a deterministic scoring function' — which is literally the claim (severity is never an LLM adjective). A stacked bar also reads correctly at thumbnail size in a bento tile and animates honestly: each segment grows to its weighted contribution and the band label snaps as the total crosses a threshold. Use Tremor or shadcn charts for the boring dashboard charts (cost, latency, stage durations) and reserve bespoke work for this one signature. | TRADEOFF: Bespoke dataviz is the easiest place to burn a day. Cap it: one component, six segments, four threshold lines, one Motion `animate` on width. Radar is genuinely more 'impressive-looking' at first glance and is what most people expect from a six-input score, so you're trading a small amount of immediate wow for a large amount of legibility and credibility — the right trade for a trust product, but be deliberate about it.
  - (medium) Use Cult UI's Dynamic Island as the persistent live-run status surface across the dashboard, driven by the heartbeat: compact while polling, tall when a finding lands, large + blocking-red when a proven finding turns the gate red. Add cmdk (via shadcn `command`) for a Cmd+K palette over repos, PRs, findings and fingerprints. | WHY: The brief asks for a UI that shows 'Stage 3/9 — specialists, 12s since last output', and Dynamic Island is purpose-built for exactly that: 14 size presets, a `useDynamicIslandSize()` hook exposing `state.size` / `state.previousSize` / `setSize()`, and `useScheduledAnimations([{size, delay}])` for queued transitions. It is MIT, widely available, and yet almost nobody in dev-tooling uses it for run status — so it's a recognisable pattern deployed in an unexpected place, which is the definition of tasteful borrowing rather than cliché. cmdk is what shadcn's Command wraps, has tens of millions of weekly downloads, and delivers the 'keyboard-fast' requirement for near-zero cost. | TRADEOFF: Dynamic Island is an Apple visual metaphor and a subset of viewers will read it as derivative or as iOS cosplay in a B2B dev tool — mitigate by squaring the radius toward your system and dropping the pill silhouette's most Apple-specific proportions. cmdk is genuinely table stakes now (2026 SaaS dashboards all have it), so it earns no points on its own; its value is purely that its absence would be noticed.
  - (high) Hard-avoid list, enforced as a pre-demo grep: Aceternity Spotlight / Spotlight New / Background Beams / Background Beams With Collision / Aurora Background / 3D Card / Wobble Card / Lamp / Meteors / Infinite Moving Cards; Magic UI Globe, Orbiting Circles used as an 'integrations' ring, and Marquee used for logos you don't have; React Bits Hyperspeed / Ballpit / Balatro / Liquid Chrome / Splash Cursor. | WHY: These are the documented, named clichés of the 2023-26 AI-startup era — Aceternity itself ships spotlight-new because 'the previous version is used a bit too much these days', and the same spotlight/3D-card/animated-beam trio appears across dozens of Product Hunt launches. Several are additionally *dishonest* for PRGuard: a spinning globe implies global infrastructure you don't have, orbiting circles imply an integrations ecosystem you don't have, and a logo marquee implies customers you don't have. At a hackathon, an empty-signifier section is worse than no section — it invites the question 'is any of this real?', which is fatal for a trust product. | TRADEOFF: You give up the fastest available route to a visually busy page, and busy pages do photograph well in a 3-second flyby. Compensate with density of REAL artifacts — terminal output, diffs, hash chains, severity breakdowns, audit rows — which fills the same screen area and is far harder to fake.
  - (medium) Treat 21st.dev as a search index filtered by author, not as a component source. Trust Dillion Verma (Magic UI), Hayden Bleasel (Kibo UI / AI Elements), Julien Thibeaut (motion-primitives) and Serafim (21st founder); ignore everything else on first pass. | WHY: 21st.dev's quality distribution is bimodal and visible in the raw listings: a 'terminal' search returns solid work by the above authors interleaved with entries named 'nm', 'mine', 'e43wr' and six duplicate 'Snippet' uploads from a single account. Its real value is coverage — it indexes the whole shadcn ecosystem with category counts (Heroes 1152, Backgrounds 365, File Trees 61, Numbers 54, Globes 41) so you can see what's saturated before you build it — plus an MCP server for agent-driven retrieval. The free tier's 2 copies/day is enough for research; you install the actual code from the source registry. | TRADEOFF: Author-filtering means you'll miss genuinely good work from unknown contributors. Acceptable at hackathon speed: the cost of auditing an unknown component's accessibility, SSR-safety and dependency footprint exceeds the upside. If you do pay, Builder at $6-8/mo for unlimited installs is trivially worth it; skip the AI credit tiers ($15-80/mo) — you have Claude.
ANTI-PATTERNS / AVOID:
  - The Aceternity Spotlight. Burnt out to the point that Aceternity SHIPPED A REPLACEMENT and said so in the docs: spotlight-new is described as 'A subtle yet effective spotlight effect, because the previous version is used a bit too much these days.' Note that spotlight-new is now getting used the same way, so it is following the original down. Avoid both.
  - Background Beams and Background Beams With Collision. Together with Spotlight and 3D Card, this is the trio that 'shows up on half the AI-startup landing pages' and recurs across Product Hunt / Hacker News launches from 2025-26. Instantly dates a site to 2023.
  - The Aceternity Aurora Background, and wobbly/blurred blob gradient heroes generally. This is THE canonical AI hero: 'dark hero section' + 'gradient somewhere between indigo and violet' + 'a sparkle or star icon denoting Intelligence'. Stripe, Linear and Vercel popularised restrained variants; ten thousand seed-stage sites then ran it into the ground.
  - Shipping Magic UI's default gradients untouched. #ffaa40 → #9c40ff (AnimatedBeam, BorderBeam) and #9E7AFF → #FE8BBB (MagicCard) are the exact hex pairs that tell a reviewer 'this was assembled, not designed'. Same for React Bits ElectricBorder's #5227FF. Grep for all five before demoing.
  - Orbiting Circles / Animated Beam used as an 'integrations' ring with logo tiles. The single most copied Magic UI pattern. For PRGuard it is also a lie — you have GitHub and a sandbox, not an ecosystem. An empty-signifier section is worse than no section in a product whose pitch is 'we prove our claims'.
  - A spinning 3D globe (Cobe / GitHub Globe / Magic UI Globe). Implies global infrastructure and worldwide users. You are a hackathon entry with one GitHub App. Judges notice.
  - Logo marquees and testimonial carousels with no logos or testimonials. Placeholder social proof reads as desperation and directly undermines a trust positioning. Replace with real artifacts: a real audit chain, a real suppression ledger row, a real cost breakdown.
  - The typewriter hero headline. CodeRabbit already does the animated-rotating-headline thing (per-letter, cycling 'reviewing it.' / 'securing it.' / 'prioritizing it.'). Doing it too reads as direct imitation of the incumbent you're trying to unseat.
  - Stacking multiple hero effects: aurora + spotlight + beams + meteors + grid + noise + tracing beam, all at once. This is the #1 cause of 'assembled from copy-paste' perception — each effect is fine, the pile-up is not. One signature effect per page, everything else is layout and type.
  - The default 8-tile bento grid with a big tile top-left. Bento is still working in 2026 (the documented sweet spot is 6-9 tiles) but the stock Magic UI / Aceternity arrangement is recognisable on sight. If you use bento, make tile sizes follow information density — the severity breakdown deserves 2x2, a stat deserves 1x1 — not the template's rhythm.
  - Everything at border-radius 12-16px with a soft glow. The 2024 'friendly SaaS' look. Dev tools in 2026 read more precise at 4-8px with 1px hairline borders at #FFFFFF0D-#FFFFFF14 and shadow instead of glow. Linear's ladder is 8 / 12 / 32 / 9999, used sparingly.
  - Inter at weight 700 as your display face. Documented as part of the AI-generated-site uniform ('purple gradients, Inter font, four cards in a grid, faint hover states'). If you must use Inter, use it the way Linear does — Inter VARIABLE at weight 505-520 with `font-feature-settings: "cv01","ss03"` and -0.022em tracking — or pick a genuinely distinctive display face like Greptile and Graphite did.
  - Mixing three or more animation engines. React Bits alone can pull in motion + gsap + ogl + three + matter-js + postprocessing. Shipping motion AND gsap AND three AND ogl for four different components is a bundle and a jank problem. Pick Motion 13 as the default and allow exactly one WebGL engine (OGL if you take React Bits backgrounds, or Paper Shaders standalone) — never three.js unless a component genuinely needs it.
  - Juvenile physics/game backgrounds: React Bits Hyperspeed, Ballpit, Balatro, Liquid Chrome, Splash Cursor, Meta Balls, Blob Cursor. Impressive tech, wrong register entirely for a governance and audit product. They make the site feel like a shader showcase, not software an engineering org would buy.
  - Cursor-follower effects (glow cursor, target cursor, crosshair, image trail, swarm cursor). Comprehensively overexposed in 2025-26, actively hostile on touch devices, and they fight with the text selection people actually need when reading terminal output.
  - Recommending or using originui.com. It no longer exists — it 301-redirects to coss.com/ui after Origin UI merged into COSS (Cal.com's holding company) and was rebuilt on Base UI. Any 2025-era blog post naming Origin UI is stale.
  - Leaving copied components on their stock timings. AnimatedBeam 5s, BorderBeam 6s, ShineBorder 14s, OrbitingCircles 20s, Magic UI Terminal 60ms/char — plus Motion's default spring everywhere — produces the 'everything eases the same, nothing has intent' feel that separates assembled sites from designed ones.
  - Faking the terminal output. For a product whose entire positioning is 'we PROVE what we claim', a scripted TypingAnimation pretending to be sandbox execution is the one unrecoverable mistake. Magic UI's Terminal is explicitly a pre-scripted sequence — use it only where it is obviously an illustration, and use AI Elements' streaming Terminal anywhere the content is, or implies it is, real.
FACT-CHECK CORRECTIONS:
    [CORRECTED] Vercel AI Elements README lists 'Next.js project with AI SDK installed' as a prerequisite; unverified whether presentational components (Terminal, TestResults, StackTrace, Commit, CodeBlock, FileTree) import AI SDK hooks or work standalone. -> Both halves now resolved. The README prerequisite list is verbatim accurate: 'Node.js 18 or later', 'Next.js project with AI SDK installed', 'shadcn/ui initialized in your project', 'Tailwind CSS configured (AI Elements supports CSS Variables mode only)'. BUT the presentational components do NOT import the AI SDK. I read the raw registry JSON for four of them. terminal.json: dependencies ['ansi-to-react','lucide-react'], registryDependencies ['button'], imports only React, @/lib/utils, the shadcn button, ansi-to-react and lucide-react — zero matches for `from 'ai'` or `from '@ai-sdk/*'`. test-results.json: dependencies ['lucide-react'], registryDependencies ['badge','collapsible'], no AI SDK import. stack-trace.json: dependencies ['@radix-ui/react-use-controllable-state','lucide-react'], registryDependencies ['button','collapsible'], no AI SDK import. commit.json: dependencies ['lucide-react'], registryDependencies ['avatar','button','collapsible'], no AI SDK import. The architecture is safe. IMPORTANT CAVEAT THE RESEARCHER MISSED: this only holds for per-component installs. The bulk manifest at /api/registry/all.json declares dependencies ['ai','lucide-react','media-chrome','@xyflow/react','@radix-ui/react-use-controllable-state','shiki','tokenlens','use-stick-to-bottom','react-jsx-parser','@streamdown/cjk','@streamdown/code','@streamdown/math','@streamdown/mermaid','streamdown','@rive-app/react-webgl2','nanoid','motion','ansi-to-react'] plus 24 registryDependencies. Installing via all.json DOES pull `ai`, media-chrome, streamdown and a Rive WebGL2 runtime. Install per-component, never all.json.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] CodeRabbit body background computed as lab(4.9349 1.26021 -1.99696); hex conversion #050102 unreliable. CSS var hexes (#ff570a, #46e1a5, #211f24, #18161C, mauve ramp, #e54666, #ffc53d) reliable. Graphite lab(2.75381 0 0) ≈ #0A0A0A is an estimate. -> These are getComputedStyle snapshots of a live marketing site taken in a browser session I cannot reproduce. I have no way to re-read them without a browser. The researcher's own self-flagging is the correct treatment: use the hex strings, discard the lab()-derived hex. Note lab(4.93 1.26 -1.997) does in fact convert to roughly #0a0607–#0b0709 in sRGB, not #050102, so the researcher's instinct that the conversion was wrong is well founded — but do not ship either number without re-reading it.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] Greptile's computed fonts included 'ABC Favorit Trial' and 'Suisse Int'l'; do not repeat the trial-licensing implication as fact. -> Live-DOM snapshot, not reproducible without a browser. The researcher's caution is correct and should be kept: a font-family string in a stylesheet proves declaration, not that the webfont is served or that a trial licence is in use. This claim should simply be dropped from the report — it carries legal-accusation risk against a named competitor and has zero bearing on PRGuard's design decisions.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] Magic UI Pro at $199 one-time lifetime comes from search summaries; magicui.design/pricing and pro.magicui.design/pricing both returned 404. -> I independently reproduced both 404s today (magicui.design/pricing → HTTP 404, pro.magicui.design/pricing → HTTP 404). The $199 lifetime figure has no first-party source I can reach. Treat as unknown. It is also irrelevant to the recommendations, since every Magic UI component actually used (AnimatedBeam, BorderBeam, MagicCard, ShineBorder, NumberTicker, DotPattern, OrbitingCircles, ScrollProgress, CodeComparison) is in the free MIT core registry.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] Berkeley Mono pricing '$75 personal', commercial tiered by company size, not compatible with open-source apps, commercial use restricted to UI elements only. -> usgraphics.com is behind a Cloudflare JS/cookie interstitial and returned HTTP 403 to every fetch I made (/products/berkeley-mono, /berkeley-mono, and the berkeleygraphics.com redirect). I did not attempt to bypass the bot check. No first-party price or licence text could be read, so neither the $75 figure nor the company-size tiering nor the 'UI elements only' restriction is verified. The RECOMMENDATION to avoid Berkeley Mono still holds on risk grounds alone: it is unambiguously a paid commercial typeface with no public licence text I could read, which is exactly the wrong profile for a hackathon deliverable. Geist Mono is the correct substitute and its licence IS verified.
    [CORRECTED] Motion 13.4.0 dated 14 Sept 2026; v13.0.0 dropped @emotion/is-prop-valid; 13.2.0 added animate.addEffect() with Three.js/GPU effects; 13.3.0 ~80% spring retarget improvement; 13.4.0 AnimateView for React 19.3 view transitions. The 'React 19.3' reference should be double-checked. -> Every changelog detail is CONFIRMED, including the React 19.3 reference — motion.dev/changelog for 13.4.0 reads 'View transitions for React 19.3, built on React's ViewTransition'. 13.0.0 (Aug 5 2026) 'Removed optional @emotion/is-prop-valid dependency in favour of explicit <MotionConfig>'. 13.2.0 (Sept 3 2026) added animate.addEffect() for non-DOM subjects with Three.js and VGPU support (note: 'VGPU', not 'GPU'). 13.3.0 (Sept 14 2026) Motion Editor hooks, 80% spring retargeting speedup, 10% smaller bundle. THE ONE CORRECTION: 13.4.0 is no longer latest. npm shows motion@13.4.1 published 2026-09-22T16:40:39Z — today, hours ago. License MIT. Pin explicitly; this library is shipping multiple releases per month.
    [REFUTED] Tremor was acquired by Vercel — announced January 2025, not 2026. Tremor Blocks are now free under MIT; Tremor has ~35+ components, ~16k stars, ~300k monthly downloads. -> The acquisition date is right and the rest is substantially wrong. (a) DATE: 'Vercel acquires Tremor' published January 22, 2025 — CONFIRMED, the researcher's self-correction was right. (b) LICENSE: the blog says only 'Tremor's Pro product—Tremor Blocks—will be free and open source for all users under an MIT license'. It does NOT say the main library is MIT. tremorlabs/tremor is Apache-2.0; only tremorlabs/tremor-blocks is MIT. Saying 'all products under MIT' is wrong. (c) STARS: current repo tremorlabs/tremor has 3,629 stars, not ~16k. The 16,488-star figure belongs to the LEGACY repo tremorlabs/tremor-npm (the old @tremor/react v3), last pushed 2025-01-13. The researcher conflated two repos. (d) DOWNLOADS: @tremor/react actually does ~1.51M downloads/month (2026-08-23→2026-09-21), not ~300k — but that traffic is all on the frozen v3.18.7 published 2025-01-13. (e) 35+ components CONFIRMED from tremor.so. (f) MAINTENANCE RISK the researcher missed entirely: tremorlabs/tremor was last pushed 2025-10-10, ~11 months stale; tremor.so still carries the 'Tremor is joining Vercel' banner from Jan 2025, still advertises '300+ Premium Blocks' (i.e. the site does NOT reflect the free-blocks claim), and its footer reads '© 2025 Tremor Labs, Inc.'. Do not put Tremor on the critical path. Use shadcn chart primitives instead — the researcher already offers that alternative and it is the right one.
    [CORRECTED] HeroUI v3: March 2026 release, Apache-2.0, 75+ web components / 21 new, 37 React Native components, React Aria Components + Tailwind v4, OKLCH, BEM modifiers, all animations moved to CSS with no JS runtime — from an InfoQ summary, not first-party. -> The two load-bearing facts check out; the feature specifics do not. RELEASE DATE CONFIRMED: @heroui/react 3.0.0 was published to npm 2026-03-21. LICENSE CONFIRMED as Apache-2.0, with a metadata trap worth knowing — the repo's default branch is `v3` and its LICENSE is Apache-2.0, but the legacy `main` branch LICENSE is MIT and the published package.json for @heroui/react@3.2.6 still declares "license": "MIT". If licence provenance ever matters, cite the v3 branch LICENSE file, not npm. Repo has 30,790 stars; latest release v3.2.6 (2026-09-17), actively maintained. UNVERIFIED: the 75+/21-new/37-React-Native counts, BEM modifiers, OKLCH tokens and the 'zero JS animation runtime' claim — heroui.com/blog returns 404 and I found no first-party release notes. Since the recommendation is to NOT use HeroUI, none of this is load-bearing.
    [CORRECTED] Paper Shaders: Apache-2.0, ~3.5k stars, 'zero-dependency' is the project's own marketing tagline; no bundle-size figure; React version requirements unverified. -> Apache-2.0 CONFIRMED. Stars CONFIRMED at 3,471 (~3.5k), last pushed 2026-09-17, actively maintained. 'ZERO-DEPENDENCY' NEEDS ONE ASTERISK: @paper-design/shaders-react@0.0.81 declares dependencies {'@paper-design/shaders': '0.0.81'} — its own sibling core package, which itself has zero dependencies. So: zero THIRD-PARTY runtime dependencies, two packages installed. The claim is substantively true, phrase it precisely. REACT REQUIREMENT NOW VERIFIED: peerDependencies are react '^18 || ^19' and @types/react '^18 || ^19' — React 19 is fine. THE MATERIAL RISK THE RESEARCHER MISSED: the package is at version 0.0.81, published 2026-09-17. It is pre-1.0 with dist-tags for canary, next, experimental and debug channels. A 0.0.x package can break its API in any release. Pin the exact version (0.0.81) in package.json — do not use a caret range — for anything the demo depends on. Bundle size still unverified.
    [CORRECTED] shadcn Base UI default: `pnpm dlx shadcn init -b radix` pins Radix (quoted from changelog); `registry:base` config key quoted but schema not found; components.json field name for primitive choice unconfirmed; claim that third-party motion registries are unaffected is reasoned inference. -> The command is VALID and now verified from the CLI reference, not just the changelog. ui.shadcn.com/docs/cli documents the init flag verbatim as: '-b, --base <base>          the component library to use. (base, radix, aria)'. So `pnpm dlx shadcn init -b radix` works. THE RESEARCHER MISSED A THIRD OPTION: `aria` (React Aria) is also accepted — worth knowing but not worth using here. 'Base UI as the Default' shipping in July 2026 CONFIRMED from the changelog. The `cn` package and `pnpm dlx shadcn@latest migrate cn` CONFIRMED verbatim from the September 2026 changelog entry: 'cn is a drop-in replacement for twMerge(clsx(...)). It is smaller, faster and ships the same API, so nothing changes at the call site.' STILL UNVERIFIABLE: the `registry:base` key (absent from the changelog I read) and the components.json field name for the primitive choice. The 'third-party motion registries unaffected' claim remains untested inference — but it is now better supported, because I read the actual sources: AnimatedBeam, BorderBeam, ShineBorder, DotPattern, OrbitingCircles and ElectricBorder declare no Radix dependencies at all. Among AI Elements, only stack-trace pulls a Radix package (@radix-ui/react-use-controllable-state), which is a headless state hook with no primitive/DOM surface — it will not conflict with a Base UI default.
    [CORRECTED] Eldora UI's '150+ free components' is a marketed figure; the live docs sidebar showed roughly 40 items. React Bits' README says '200+ components' vs '150 Components' on the site footer. -> The inconsistency is real; the count is slightly off. The live Eldora docs sidebar at eldoraui.site/docs/components lists 33 components, not ~40: Animated Badge, Animated Frameworks, Animated List, Animated Shiny Button, Blur In Text, Browser, Card Flip Hover, Clerk OTP, Cobe Globe, Dock Text, Fade Text, Font Weight Text, GitHub Inline Comments, Gradual Spacing Text, Grid, Hacker Background, Integrations, iPad, iPhone 17 Pro, Letter Pull Up Text, Live Button, Logo Timeline, MacBook Pro, Map, Multi Direction Slide Text, Novatrix Background, Photon Beam, Safari Browser, Scale Letter Text, Separate Away Text, Terminal, Testimonial Slider, Wavy Text, Word Pull Up Text. No '150+' claim appears on the docs site itself. React Bits README line 39 does read '**200+ components** — text animations, UI elements, micro interactions, and backgrounds, growing weekly', confirming the README-vs-footer inconsistency. Neither library is on the critical path, so this is cosmetic.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] Uiverse.io MIT licensing and ~3,000-3,500 element count come from third-party mirrors, not uiverse.io itself (which returned 403). -> I reproduced the 403 today — uiverse.io returns HTTP 403 Forbidden to fetches. Neither the MIT claim nor the element count can be confirmed from the first-party source. The researcher's own characterisation ('decorative buttons/loaders, not product UI') means this is correctly excluded from the build anyway, so leave it as an unverified long-tail mention or drop it.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] Linear's token values were read directly from getComputedStyle on the live :root at linear.app on 2026-09-22, but are a marketing-site snapshot; --font-monospace: "Berkeley Mono" being declared does not prove the webfont is served. -> Not reproducible without a browser session. The researcher's caveat is exactly right and should be preserved verbatim in the report. One thing to add: the values the recommendations actually DEPEND ON are the two easing curves (ease-out-quart cubic-bezier(.165,.84,.44,1) and ease-out-expo cubic-bezier(.19,1,.22,1)) and the 100ms/250ms durations. Those are standard published Penner-derived curves, not Linear-proprietary, so the recommendation stands on its own merit regardless of whether the Linear snapshot is accurate. The surface hexes are aesthetic starting points where precision does not matter.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] The 'frontier labs went light/cream while mid-tier AI went dark/violet' characterisation is synthesised from a DesignRush trend piece and a DEV Community opinion piece — a defensible reading of secondary sources, not a first-party survey. -> This is an aesthetic generalisation over a hand-picked sample (OpenAI, Anthropic, Perplexity, Mistral, Cohere, xAI, Scale AI) sourced from opinion journalism. It cannot be confirmed or refuted as a factual claim. The researcher labelled it correctly. Keep it framed as an observation, never as a finding, and do not let a design decision rest on it alone — the anti-violet recommendation is independently justified by the verified component-default fingerprints (#9c40ff, #5227FF, #9E7AFF→#FE8BBB), which is a much stronger argument.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] 'Aceternity UI shows up on half the AI-startup landing pages' is a quoted assertion from a comparison blog, not a measured statistic. The Aceternity self-deprecating spotlight-new quote IS verbatim from ui.aceternity.com/components/spotlight-new. -> The 'half of all AI-startup landing pages' figure is an unmeasured blog assertion and should be struck from the report — it is the kind of fake-precision number that damages credibility if challenged. Say 'widely used' or cite the verified registry size (284 items) instead. I did not separately re-verify the spotlight-new self-deprecating quote; the researcher marked it verbatim from the live page and it is not load-bearing.
    [CORRECTED] Kibo UI's licence and dependency list are unverified — its components page does not state them. The 41-component list IS directly observed. -> The licence IS now verifiable and the component list is stale. LICENSE: the repo is MIT. NOTE THE OWNERSHIP CHANGE — the repo is now shadcnblocks/kibo, not haydenbleasel/kibo; GitHub reports 3,943 stars, MIT, last pushed 2026-05-04 (~5 months stale). The components page now brands itself 'Shadcn UI Blocks'. The researcher's attribution 'By Hayden Bleasel, who also authors AI Elements — so the two share house style' is therefore at least partly stale and the 'shared house style / composes cleanly' inference is weaker than stated. Separately, the npm package `kibo-ui` (v1.1.5, published 2025-10-29) declares NO license field at all. COMPONENT LIST: the count of 41 is right but the researcher's enumeration is stale — it omits ten components now listed (Choicebox, Mini Calendar, Tags, Image Zoom, Ticker, Announcement, Typography, Glimpse, Relative Time, Status) and includes some no longer shown. Re-read the page before quoting the list.
    [CORRECTED] REC 6 premise — Magic UI AnimatedBeam gradientStartColor #ffaa40 / gradientStopColor #9c40ff; BorderBeam same pair; MagicCard gradientFrom #9E7AFF / gradientTo #FE8BBB; ShineBorder shineColor #000000 duration 14; React Bits ElectricBorder color #5227FF borderRadius 24; stock durations AnimatedBeam 5s, BorderBeam 6s, ShineBorder 14s, OrbitingCircles 20s. -> Nearly all source-verified from the live registry JSON today — the grep checklist is sound. CONFIRMED EXACTLY: AnimatedBeam duration = 5, gradientStartColor = "#ffaa40", gradientStopColor = "#9c40ff", curvature = 0, pathColor = "gray", pathWidth = 2, pathOpacity = 0.2. ShineBorder shineColor = "#000000", duration = 14, borderWidth = 1. OrbitingCircles duration = 20, radius = 160, path = true, iconSize = 30, speed = 1, and zero dependencies (pure CSS) — confirmed. ElectricBorder color = '#5227FF', speed = 1, chaos = 0.12, borderRadius = 24, zero JS deps. MagicCard source does contain #9E7AFF and #FE8BBB. THREE CORRECTIONS: (1) BorderBeam's props are NOT named gradientStartColor/gradientStopColor — they are colorFrom = "#ffaa40" and colorTo = "#9c40ff", with duration = 6, size = 50, borderWidth = 1, initialOffset = 0. Using the wrong prop name silently leaves the violet default in place, which is the exact failure mode the recommendation exists to prevent. (2) MagicCard's source carries THREE more hardcoded hexes the grep list misses: #6b21ef, #ee4f27 and #262626. (3) ElectricBorder also writes a CSS custom property '--electric-border-color' and imports './ElectricBorder.css' — so it is not a single self-contained .jsx file; the colour must be overridden in both places. EXTEND THE PRE-DEMO GREP to: #9c40ff, #ffaa40, #9E7AFF, #FE8BBB, #5227FF, #6b21ef, #ee4f27, and the prop names colorFrom/colorTo.
    [REFUTED] REFERENCE — Magic UI Code Comparison props: lightTheme="github-light", darkTheme="github-dark", highlightColor="rgba(101, 117, 133, 0.16)"; beforeCode/afterCode/language/filename all required; supports [!code ++] etc. via @shikijs/transformers; MIT. -> The default values are wrong — they are stale docs values, not what the registry ships. I read the current source at magicui.design/r/code-comparison.json. The actual signature is: ({ beforeCode, afterCode, language, filename, lightTheme, darkTheme, highlightColor = "#ff3333" }). lightTheme and darkTheme have NO defaults at all — they are required props typed `lightTheme: string; darkTheme: string` with no `?`, so omitting them yields `undefined` passed to Shiki, not "github-light"/"github-dark". The strings 'github-light' and 'github-dark' do not appear anywhere in the source. highlightColor's default is "#ff3333", not rgba(101,117,133,0.16). The only rgba values in the source are rgba(16,185,129,.16) for additions and rgba(244,63,94,.16) for deletions. ALSO UNDISCLOSED: dependencies are ['shiki','next-themes'] and the component calls useTheme() from next-themes — it will not render correctly without a next-themes ThemeProvider in the tree. @shikijs/transformers import CONFIRMED. MIT CONFIRMED. The recommendation to swap in a custom Shiki theme is still right, and is in fact mandatory rather than optional, since there is no theme default to fall back on.
    [CORRECTED] REFERENCE — Magic UI NumberTicker props: value(0), startValue(0), direction "up"|"down", delay(0), decimalPlaces(0), startOnView. DotPattern: width=16, height=16, x=0, y=0, cx=1, cy=1, cr=1. OrbitingCircles: radius=160, duration=20, delay=10, iconSize=30, speed=1, path=true. ScrollProgress uses useScroll from motion/react. -> Mostly right, two phantom props. NumberTicker actual signature: ({ value, startValue = 0, direction = "up", delay = 0, className, decimalPlaces = 0, ...props }) — there is NO `startOnView` prop in the shipped source, and `value` has no default (it is required). OrbitingCircles actual signature: ({ className, children, reverse, duration = 20, radius = 160, path = true, iconSize = 30, speed = 1, ...props }) — there is NO `delay = 10` prop; the researcher invented it. DotPattern CONFIRMED exactly (width=16, height=16, x=0, y=0, cx=1, cy=1, cr=1) plus an undocumented `glow = false` prop the researcher missed. ScrollProgress CONFIRMED — source imports { motion, useScroll, type MotionProps } from "motion/react". Dependency footprints: NumberTicker ['motion'], ScrollProgress ['motion'], DotPattern and OrbitingCircles have NO dependencies.
    [CORRECTED] REFERENCE — Origin UI merged into COSS (coss.com), rebuilt on Base UI, components renamed 'particles', ~508 listed, still explicitly early development, legacy catalogue at coss.com/origin. -> The site exists and the core facts hold: coss.com/ui describes itself as 'A new, modern UI component library built on top of Base UI' and its navigation reads 'Browse 508 particles' — so the Base UI foundation, the 'particles' naming and the 508 count are all CONFIRMED. UNVERIFIED on the page I fetched: the explicit 'early development' warning (no such warning appeared), the coss.com/origin legacy catalogue, and the Cal.com-holding-company framing. The components index itself lists roughly 60 named entries (Accordion through Tooltip), so '508 particles' counts variants, not distinct components — do not quote 508 as a component count. None of this is load-bearing since the recommendation is to not use it before the deadline.
    [CORRECTED] REFERENCE — React Bits text animations use GSAP, 'now fully free incl. SplitText/ScrollTrigger'. -> 'Free' yes, 'open source' no — and the distinction matters for a commercial product. npm reports gsap's license field literally as "Standard 'no charge' license: https://gsap.com/standard-license" — it is NOT MIT and not an OSI licence. GSAP's standard no-charge licence permits use in most commercial products but restricts products where the animation itself is what is being sold. PRGuard is fine under it. Also a version correction: gsap's current latest is 3.15.0 (published 2026-04-13); React Bits pins ^3.13.0 in its docs-site package.json, which the caret range will resolve up to 3.15.0. The recommendation already limits GSAP usage to AnimatedContent/ScrollFloat/ScrollReveal, none of which are on the critical path.
VERIFIER ADDITIONS:
    + BIGGEST SINGLE MISS — the AI Elements bulk install is a trap that would break the flagship recommendation. Recommendation 1 rests on 'presentational components don't need the AI SDK', which I confirmed is true per-component. But the README's headline command `npx ai-elements@latest` (no args) and `npx shadcn@latest add https://elements.ai-sdk.dev/api/registry/all.json` both install ALL components, and all.json declares dependencies ['ai','lucide-react','media-chrome','@xyflow/react','@radix-ui/react-use-controllable-state','shiki','tokenlens','use-stick-to-bottom','react-jsx-parser','@streamdown/cjk','@streamdown/code','@streamdown/math','@streamdown/mermaid','streamdown','@rive-app/react-webgl2','nanoid','motion','ansi-to-react'] plus 24 shadcn registryDependencies. That pulls in a Rive WebGL2 runtime, a Mermaid renderer, a math renderer and media-chrome for a dashboard that needs six components. ALWAYS install per-component: `npx ai-elements@latest add terminal test-results stack-trace commit file-tree code-block`.
    + HOW THE ai-elements CLI ACTUALLY WORKS — this resolves the install-command contradiction the researcher flagged and could not settle. I downloaded and read the published tarball (ai-elements@1.9.0). index.js is a ~40-line shim: it detects the package manager from npm_config_user_agent (pnpm→'pnpm dlx', yarn→'yarn dlx', bun→'bunx', else 'npx -y'), then does `components = args.length >= 2 ? args.slice(1) : ['all']` and spawns `shadcn add <registry-url>` for each. Because it slices off argv[0] WITHOUT checking its value, the verb is ignored entirely — `ai-elements add terminal`, `ai-elements install terminal` and `ai-elements banana terminal` are all identical. Neither docs form is outdated; both work by accident. TWO REAL GOTCHAS: (1) the package declares bin as {'elements': 'index.js'} — the executable is named `elements`, not `ai-elements`, so `npx ai-elements@latest` depends on npm's single-bin fallback and may prompt or fail on some npm versions; `npx -p ai-elements elements add terminal` is the deterministic form. (2) The npm package has not been republished since v1.9.0 on 2026-03-12 — six months stale — but since it is only a URL shim that resolves components live from elements.ai-sdk.dev, staleness is harmless.
    + TREMOR SHOULD BE REMOVED FROM THE REPORT, not merely corrected. Beyond the four factual errors (Apache-2.0 not MIT for the core library; 3,629 stars not 16k, since 16,488 belongs to the frozen legacy tremorlabs/tremor-npm repo; ~1.51M monthly downloads not 300k, all of it on the frozen v3.18.7 from Jan 2025; blocks-only MIT) the maintenance picture is the real story: tremorlabs/tremor last pushed 2025-10-10, @tremor/react last published 2025-01-13, tremor.so still shows the Jan-2025 'joining Vercel' banner, still sells '300+ Premium Blocks', and carries a '© 2025 Tremor Labs' footer. Recommendation 9's fallback — shadcn's own chart primitives, whose dark OKLCH defaults I verified exactly — is strictly better and has no third-party maintenance risk. Delete the Tremor branch.
    + PIN @paper-design/shaders-react EXACTLY. The whole 'one signature background effect' recommendation rests on a package at version 0.0.81, published five days ago (2026-09-17), with active canary/next/experimental/debug dist-tags. A caret range will happily pull a breaking 0.0.82 mid-build. Write "@paper-design/shaders-react": "0.0.81" with no range operator, and commit the lockfile. Same discipline applies to motion: the researcher's report says 13.4.0 but 13.4.1 shipped today (2026-09-22T16:40Z) — this library ships multiple releases a month.
    + A DEPENDENCY THE REPORT NEVER MENTIONS: next-themes. Two recommended Magic UI components — CodeComparison and MagicCard — declare next-themes as a dependency and call useTheme() at render. If PRGuard ships a single forced-dark theme (which the whole colour-system recommendation implies) and therefore skips installing a next-themes ThemeProvider, both components will read theme as undefined. For CodeComparison this is fatal rather than cosmetic, because lightTheme/darkTheme have no defaults — it will pass undefined to Shiki. Either install next-themes with a ThemeProvider forcedTheme="dark", or strip the useTheme() call when you copy the source in (which shadcn-style installs make trivial).
    + THE ONE LICENCE THAT ACTUALLY CONSTRAINS THIS BUILD is React Bits' MIT + Commons Clause, and the report understates how to read it. The actual text grants use 'as part of an application, website, or product' and restricts: 'You may use this Software, including for any commercial purpose, so long as you do not sell, sublicense, or redistribute the components themselves—whether alone, in a bundle, or as a ported version.' Using FaultyTerminal and ElectricBorder inside PRGuard is unambiguously fine. But note the second-order implication the report misses: if PRGuard ever open-sources its own frontend, the copied React Bits source cannot go into that public repo under an OSI licence, because publishing the component source IS redistribution of the component. At hackathon scope this is a non-issue; flag it before any 'we'll open-source the UI' moment.
    + LICENCE-BY-LIBRARY, VERIFIED FROM PRIMARY SOURCES — worth putting in the report as a table, since licensing was the single weakest area: AI Elements = Apache-2.0 (LICENSE file read directly). Magic UI core = MIT, no Commons Clause (LICENSE.md read directly). Cult UI = MIT (LICENSE.md read directly). Paper Shaders = Apache-2.0. @xyflow/react = MIT. cmdk = MIT. Geist / Geist Mono = SIL OFL 1.1 (both OFL.txt and LICENSE.txt in vercel/geist-font). HeroUI v3 = Apache-2.0 (v3 branch LICENSE; npm metadata wrongly says MIT). Kibo UI = MIT (repo now shadcnblocks/kibo; npm package declares no licence). React Bits = MIT + Commons Clause. GSAP = proprietary no-charge standard licence, not OSI. ansi-to-react = BSD-3-Clause. Tremor core = Apache-2.0; Tremor Blocks = MIT. NOT ESTABLISHED: Aceternity free components (see below), 21st.dev components (per-author), Uiverse, Tailark.
    + ACETERNITY'S FREE-COMPONENT LICENCE IS PROBABLY WORSE THAN 'UNKNOWN' — sharpen the warning. The /licence page opens: 'This agreement outlines the permissions granted by our Aceternity License for each item available for purchase OR DOWNLOAD on ui.aceternity.com.' The words 'or download' plausibly sweep in the free copy-paste components rather than excluding them, which would put the free tier under the same no-redistribution Aceternity License rather than under anything MIT-like. Combined with there being no LICENSE file anywhere for the free tier, the practical guidance is stronger than the researcher's 'don't assume MIT': prefer not to copy Aceternity source into PRGuard at all. Recommendation 11 already hard-avoids Aceternity's component catalogue on taste grounds, so simply extend that to the shader family listed as 'must-steal' in the references — those seven shader components are the only Aceternity items the report still proposes using, and they are the ones this licence ambiguity lands on.
    + 21st.dev's ToS gives you a better argument than the researcher's author-filtering heuristic. It states components are 'the sole and exclusive property of their respective authors and 21st Labs Inc.' and requires that authors 'keep their licence and give visible credit to the original author on the component page' when building on someone else's work — and it explicitly warns against republishing from restrictive commercial libraries like Tailwind Plus. Two consequences: (1) Recommendation 12's 'treat it as a search index, install from the source registry' is not just a quality heuristic, it is the licence-clean path, because installing from Magic UI's or Kibo's own registry gets you a known licence instead of an unstated one. (2) Any component you DO take from 21st.dev needs its individual page checked for licence and attribution — there is no blanket grant to fall back on.
    + MINOR BUT SHIP-BLOCKING IF MISSED: BorderBeam's violet is behind prop names colorFrom/colorTo, not gradientStartColor/gradientStopColor as Recommendation 6 states. AnimatedBeam uses the gradient* names; BorderBeam does not. Someone following the checklist literally will override nothing on BorderBeam and ship #9c40ff — precisely the failure the recommendation exists to prevent. Also add #6b21ef and #ee4f27 to the pre-demo grep list; both are hardcoded in MagicCard alongside the two hexes already listed.
    + TWO PROPS IN THE REFERENCE SECTION DO NOT EXIST and will fail typecheck or silently no-op: NumberTicker has no `startOnView` prop, and OrbitingCircles has no `delay` prop. I read both sources. NumberTicker's real signature is ({ value, startValue = 0, direction = "up", delay = 0, className, decimalPlaces = 0 }) and OrbitingCircles' is ({ className, children, reverse, duration = 20, radius = 160, path = true, iconSize = 30, speed = 1 }). If the live cost/latency counters were going to rely on startOnView to defer animation until scroll, that needs an IntersectionObserver written by hand.
    + KIBO UI CHANGED HANDS AND THE REPORT'S RATIONALE FOR IT NO LONGER HOLDS. The repo is now shadcnblocks/kibo, not haydenbleasel/kibo, and the components page brands itself 'Shadcn UI Blocks'. The report's argument for Kibo was 'By Hayden Bleasel, who also authors AI Elements — so the two share house style and will compose cleanly'. That shared-authorship premise is now questionable, and the repo has been quiet since 2026-05-04. The good news is the licence question is answered — it IS MIT. If Kibo is only being used as a fallback, downgrade the confidence in the 'composes cleanly with AI Elements' claim and verify visually rather than assuming.
    + THREE CLAIMS I RECOMMEND CUTTING ENTIRELY rather than caveating, because each carries downside risk and none supports a decision: (a) the Greptile 'ABC Favorit Trial' observation — an unverifiable font-licensing insinuation about a named competitor, with zero bearing on PRGuard; (b) 'Aceternity UI shows up on half the AI-startup landing pages' — fake precision from a blog, easily challenged, and the verified 284-item registry count makes the same point honestly; (c) the CodeRabbit lab()-derived hex #050102 — the researcher already knows it is wrong, and a wrong hex in a report is worse than no hex. The anti-generic argument in Recommendation 2 is fully carried by the verified component defaults (#9c40ff, #ffaa40, #9E7AFF, #FE8BBB, #5227FF, #6b21ef, #ee4f27) and needs none of these three.
    + UNCHECKABLE BY DESIGN — set expectations. Four claim clusters are live-browser getComputedStyle snapshots that no fetch tool can reproduce: Linear's token set, CodeRabbit's 137 CSS vars and mauve ramp, Greptile's and Graphite's computed fonts and type scales, and the Aceternity spotlight-new self-deprecating quote. I could not verify any of them and neither can a reviewer without opening a browser. Two of them matter to decisions — Linear's easings and the Berkeley Mono declaration — so note that the easing curves (cubic-bezier(.165,.84,.44,1) and cubic-bezier(.19,1,.22,1)) are standard published curves independent of Linear, and that the Berkeley Mono recommendation is 'avoid', which is the safe direction regardless of whether the snapshot is right. Also note usgraphics.com is Cloudflare-gated and returned 403 to every fetch, so Berkeley Mono's actual pricing and licence text remain unread — do not restate the $75 / company-size / 'UI elements only' specifics as fact anywhere in the report.

### AREA: Typography system for a technical, forensic, premium brand (typography)
FINDINGS:
  - COMPETITIVE KILL SHOT: coderabbit.ai loads exactly ONE webfont file — caa3a2e1cccd8315-s.p.0wgildi0cnwt9.woff2, which I downloaded and read the name table of: 'Geist Regular', Version 1.800, variable wght 100-900. Their CSS declares only three @font-face families: Geist, Lato, and 'hack'. That is Geist (2023 Vercel Swiss geometric) + Lato (2010 humanist, one of the most-used free Google fonts) + Hack (2015 free mono) — three unrelated free typefaces. Their letter-spacing values are unramped and self-contradictory (.08em, .06em, .12rem, -.075rem, -1.92px, .12em, -.1rem, -.02em, -.03em all coexist) and their font-weights are all round (400/500/600/700/800/900). The $1.5B competitor has no typographic system at all. This is winnable on day one.
  - THEREFORE: DO NOT USE GEIST SANS AS PRGUARD'S BRAND FACE. CodeRabbit ships Geist v1.800 as its only typeface. Vercel ships Geist v1.800 + Geist Mono v1.701. Using Geist reads as 'Vercel template' at best and 'CodeRabbit clone' at worst. This is the single most important constraint on this decision and it is based on the competitor's actual font binary, not a guess.
  - Linear's production CSS gives you their entire type system verbatim: `--font-regular: "Inter Variable"`, `--font-monospace: "Berkeley Mono", ui-monospace, "SF Mono", "Menlo", monospace`, `--font-settings: "cv01", "ss03"`, `--font-variations: "opsz" auto`, and non-round variable weights `--font-weight-medium: 510`, `--font-weight-semibold: 590`, `--font-weight-bold: 680`. They self-host static.linear.app/fonts/InterVariable.woff2 (343.9 KB, 2937 glyphs, axes opsz 14-32 + wght 100-900, Inter v4.001) and static.linear.app/fonts/Berkeley-Mono-Variable.woff2 (157.1 KB).
  - Linear's full title ramp, lifted from their CSS: title-1 1.0625rem/1.4/-.012em, title-2 1.25rem/1.33/-.012em, title-3 1.5rem/1.33/-.012em, title-4 2rem/1.125/-.022em, title-5 2.5rem/1.1/-.022em, title-6 3rem/1.0/-.022em, title-7 3.5rem/1.1/-.022em, title-8 4rem/1.06/-.022em, title-9 4.5rem/1.0/-.022em. Body: text-regular .9375rem/1.6/-.011em, text-small .875rem/calc(21/14)/-.013em, text-mini .8125rem/1.5/-.01em, text-micro .75rem/1.4/0. Note tracking CAPS at -0.022em and never goes further, and line-height hits 1.0 at 3rem.
  - Vercel's Geist tracking ramp is a pure percentage function, confirmed by paired font-size/letter-spacing rules in their CSS and by two CSS vars `--kerning-percent:-.02` and `--kerning-percent:-.06`: 14px/-.28px, 16px/-.32px, 20px/-.4px (all -0.02em); 24px/-.96px, 32px/-1.28px (all -0.04em); 40px/-2.4px, 48px/-2.88px, 56px/-3.36px, 64px/-3.84px, 72px/-4.32px (all -0.06em). Line-height reaches exactly 1.0 at 56px and above (56/56, 64/64, 72/72). Vercel is roughly 3x more aggressive on display tracking than Linear.
  - GEIST MONO HAS A 1/l DEFECT THAT IS DISQUALIFYING FOR A FORENSIC PRODUCT — AND AN EXACT ONE-LINE FIX. I rasterized the binary: Geist Mono's default 'l' is a bare stem with a top-left flag and a FULL-WIDTH BASELINE BAR, geometrically near-identical to '1' (both 480 units wide, 710 tall, 1 contour, 13 vs 14 points). This is open GitHub issue vercel/geist-font#102. The fix: `font-feature-settings: "ss03" 1` — I rendered glyph `l.ss03` and it replaces the baseline bar with a right-hooking tail, making it unmistakable. Ship ss03 or do not ship Geist Mono.
  - GEIST MONO'S CODE LIGATURES CANNOT BE DISABLED THE WAY EVERYONE THINKS, INCLUDING VERCEL. Its ligatures live in `liga` (GSUB lookup 198, LigatureSubst) — which is ON BY DEFAULT in every browser — not in `calt`. Geist Mono has NO `calt` feature at all. Vercel's own production rule is `font-feature-settings: "rlig" 1, "calt" 0, "ss11" 1`, which does NOT turn off Geist Mono's ligatures. I extracted 40 symbol-only ligatures including `!=` `!==` `->` `<=` `<==>` `<!--` `/=` `:=` `</>`. In a diff or terminal pane that silently rewrites `!=` into one glyph. To actually kill them you need `font-variant-ligatures: none` or `font-feature-settings: "liga" 0`.
  - JetBrains Mono is the opposite and is correctly engineered for this: its ligatures are in `calt` (ChainContextSubst, 158 lookups), so `font-variant-ligatures: no-contextual` / `"calt" 0` genuinely disables them. It also ships `zero`, `cv01` and `cv05` (two different tailed-l alternates), `ss19` (alternate `!=`/`==` forms), version 2.211. I rendered it: 'l' has a clear bottom-right tail, '0' has a centre dot. Graphite.dev and Raycast both ship JetBrains Mono v2.211.
  - SLEEPER PICK — GOOGLE SANS CODE: released August 2025 under SIL OFL 1.1 (Copyright 2025 The Google Sans Code Project Authors), Version 7.000, variable wght 300-800, and only 33.7 KB for the latin variable cut. I rendered it: slashed zero BY DEFAULT (3 contours vs O's 2), 'l' with a right-hooking tail, 'I' with top and bottom bars, 0.6 em advance. Its only GSUB features are ccmp/locl/mark/ss01 — meaning ZERO code ligatures exist to fight. For a product that renders diffs and terminal output as legal evidence, 'no ligatures by construction' is a feature, not a limitation.
  - MONO OPTICAL MATCHING, MEASURED FROM THE BINARIES (x-height / em): Geist Sans 0.530, Geist Mono 0.530 (IDENTICAL — and identical cap 710, ascent 1005, descent -295; a true metric-matched superfamily), Inter 0.546, JetBrains Mono 0.550, Commit Mono 0.540, Bricolage 0.528, Google Sans Code 0.523, Berkeley Mono 0.518, IBM Plex Mono 0.516, Instrument Sans 0.510, Space Mono 0.496, DM Mono 0.496, Source Code Pro 0.486. Pair Inter with JetBrains Mono and the optical mismatch is 0.7%. Pair Inter with Source Code Pro (what Supabase and Stripe do) and the mono renders 11% optically smaller — that is why code on those sites looks undersized.
  - ADVANCE WIDTH IS THE HIDDEN COST OF A MONO IN A DENSE DASHBOARD. Measured: Geist Mono, JetBrains Mono, IBM Plex Mono, Commit Mono, Source Code Pro, Berkeley Mono and Google Sans Code are all exactly 0.600 em per character. Martian Mono is 0.750 em (0.700 in the wght-only cut) — 25% wider, meaning a 64-char SHA line needs 25% more horizontal space. Martian Mono is disqualified for diffs and hash tables on that number alone.
  - BERKELEY MONO IS A THREE-AXIS VARIABLE FONT AND IS WATERMARKED PER LICENSEE. I pulled Linear's shipped file: 'Berkeley Mono Variable', Version 2.002, 157.1 KB, 644 glyphs, axes wght 100-900 + wdth 60-100 + slnt -16-0. Its embedded name table contains the licence string 'LT-07 Enterprise Business License. License classification: Commercial. License ID: JKZP-Y6J4-V1W0-RY21'. Two consequences: (a) self-hosted web use IS permitted under a commercial tier, (b) every file is individually traceable, so you cannot reuse anyone else's. The wdth axis down to 60 is genuinely useful — it gives you a condensed cut for dense audit/hash tables from the same file.
  - CLERK SHIPS A 5,232-BYTE DIGITS-ONLY GEIST SUBSET. The file /_next/static/immutable/media/GeistVariableVF_numbers.p.3we0i3od0iush.woff2 contains 43 glyphs whose entire cmap is `0123456789`, still variable wght 100-900, at 5.1 KB. Steal this exact technique for PRGuard's animated hero counters (findings proven, blast radius, severity score) — you get a full weight axis for numerals at 1/6th the cost of Clerk's real text face (they pair it with Suisse Int'l).
  - REAL SHIPPED BYTE BUDGETS, measured over the wire with compression on: Clerk digits-only Geist 5.1 KB; Vercel's Geist Mono subset (264 glyphs) 22.5 KB; Fontsource Geist Mono VF latin 22.5 KB; Instrument Sans VF 29 KB; Roboto Mono VF 32 KB; Google Sans Code VF 33.7 KB; Fira Code VF 35 KB; JetBrains Mono VF latin 39.4 KB; IBM Plex Sans VF 44 KB; Commit Mono 400 latin 47.0 KB; Inter VF latin (wght only) 47.1 KB; Vercel's Geist VF (975 glyphs) 68.1 KB; Bricolage Grotesque VF 75 KB; Linear's full InterVariable (opsz+wght, 2937 glyphs) 343.9 KB; Google Sans Flex full latin 1,368 KB. A two-font latin-subset system should cost you 60-90 KB total.
  - FONTSHARE IS A LICENSING TRAP FOR A SELF-HOSTED NEXT.JS APP. Satoshi, General Sans, Switzer and Cabinet Grotesk are under the ITF Free Font License, NOT SIL OFL. Commercial use of designs is allowed, but redistribution and modification of the files is not, and self-hosting as a webfont reportedly requires ITF's written consent — you are expected to call the Fontshare CDN API. That means a third-party render-blocking dependency on your hero, which is exactly the wrong trade for a 3-second first impression. Via the Fontshare API: Satoshi v1.0 has 12 styles / 2 variable cuts, Switzer v1.2 has 20 styles / 2 VF, General Sans v1.0 has 14 / 2, Cabinet Grotesk v1.0 has 9 / 1 (no italic VF). None of the four ships a `zero` (slashed zero) feature.
  - KLIM (Söhne) IS ACTUALLY VIABLE, CONTRARY TO THE SUMMARY ON THEIR OVERVIEW PAGE. Their Web Font Licence Agreement states the fonts may be used via the CSS @font-face rule for the licensed domain, WOFF2 only, and subsetting to reduce file size is explicitly permitted. Pricing is $60 USD for the first style with multi-style discounts (two styles = $102, a 15% saving). Web tiers are sized by traffic, minimum tier up to 20,000 monthly page views or 5,000 monthly unique users. Stripe self-hosts 'Sohne VF Buch' v2.002 from b.stripecdn.com with a wght axis of 1-1000, which corroborates that self-hosting is allowed.
  - INSTRUMENT SERIF HAS BECOME THE NEW CLICHÉ. I found it loaded on BOTH raycast.com and warp.dev. It is also static-only (no variable axes, 20 KB, single weight 400 + italic). The 'one tasteful serif accent in an otherwise sans site' move is now a signal that you followed a trend, not that you have taste. Instrument Sans is also more limited than people assume: its variable range is wght 400-700 only — no light weights, no 800/900.
  - WHAT THE BEST SITES ACTUALLY SHIP, RESOLVED BY READING EACH HASHED woff2's INTERNAL NAME TABLE (not by guessing): Linear = Inter Variable v4.001 + Berkeley Mono Variable v2.002. Vercel = Geist v1.800 + Geist Mono v1.701. Raycast = Inter v4.001 + JetBrains Mono v2.211 + Geist Mono v1.400 + Instrument Serif + VT323. Neon = Inter v4.001 + ES Build Neutral (paid) + Geist Mono v1.400. Mintlify = Inter + Arizona Flare + Geist Mono v1.701 + Paper Mono. Stripe = Söhne VF Buch v2.002 + Source Code Pro. Clerk = Suisse Int'l (11 static cuts) + digits-only Geist. Resend = Inter Variable + ABC Favorit + Domaine + Commit Mono. Depot = PP Neue Montreal + ABC Favorit Mono + Red Hat Text/Mono. Warp = Matter + Matter Mono + The Future + Azeret Mono + Hack. Modal = ABC Diatype + Inter Variable + DM Mono/Fira Mono. Supabase = Inter + Manrope + Source Code Pro + Departure Mono. Graphite (competitor) = Matter + Inter v4.001 + JetBrains Mono v2.211 + DM Mono. Greptile (competitor) = DM Sans + Space Mono + Anybody + Nanum Pen. Cursor = custom 'Cursor Gothic'. Val Town = Sprig Sans + IBM Plex Sans. Sentry = Rubik + 'Dammit Sans'. Arc = Marlin Soft SQ + Space Mono.
  - COMMIT MONO'S REPO LICENSE FILE IS MIT, NOT OFL. The LICENSE at github.com/eigilnikolajsen/commit-mono is a standard MIT License, Copyright (c) 2023 Eigil Nikolajsen, with no Reserved Font Name clause (several font directories incorrectly list it as OFL 1.1). Practically: you may modify it, rename it, and ship it as 'PRGuard Mono'. Combined with its official customizer at commitmono.com — which bakes your chosen alternates and merged features into a single static file — this is the cheapest way in the industry to own a proprietary-looking brand mono for $0. I verified from the binary that it has cv01/cv02/cv05/cv10, ss01-ss05, calt-based (disableable) ligatures, a tailed 'l' (18 points vs '1' at 10), a slashed zero, and 0.600 em advance.
  - SLASHED ZERO IS NOT ON BY DEFAULT WHERE IT MATTERS MOST. Measured contour counts: Inter's default '0' is PLAIN (2 contours, same as 'O') and only `zero.slash` (3 contours) fixes it — so `font-variant-numeric: slashed-zero` is mandatory if you print a SHA in Inter. Geist Sans is the same (0 and O both 2 contours). Geist Mono, JetBrains Mono, IBM Plex Mono, Commit Mono, Source Code Pro, Berkeley Mono and Google Sans Code all ship a marked zero by default. Beware Geist Mono's `ss09`, which SWITCHES THE SLASH OFF (zero.ss09 has 2 contours) — never enable it.
  - DEFAULT FIGURES ARE PROPORTIONAL IN EVERY SANS YOU WOULD CONSIDER, which will make every number column in the dashboard jitter. Measured digit advance widths: Inter {833,1159,1215,1249,1265,1267,1270,1292,1323}/2048, Geist Sans {384,524,593,604,613,615,619,626,663}/1000. Both expose `tnum`. IBM Plex Sans is the rare exception — its digits are already all equal width. Linear handles this with `font-variant-numeric: lining-nums tabular-nums` and, in one place, `lining-nums tabular-nums slashed-zero!important`.
REFERENCES:
  - [avoid] CodeRabbit (the site to beat) https://coderabbit.ai :: STEAL: Nothing. Study it as the baseline you must visibly exceed, and take the negative lesson: never mix three unrelated free typefaces. :: SPECIFICS: Loads ONE woff2: caa3a2e1cccd8315-s.p.0wgildi0cnwt9.woff2 = 'Geist Regular' v1.800, variable wght 100-900. @font-face families are exactly: Geist, Lato, hack (plus their Fallback twins). Mono stack is 'hack, hack Fallback, ui-monospace, monospace'. Letter-spacing is unramped chaos: .08em, .06em, .12rem, .12em, -.075rem, -.1rem, -1.92px, -.02em, -.03em. Weights all round: 400/500/600/700/800/900. No opsz, no tnum, no slashed zero, no variable-weight nuance.
  - [must-steal] Linear https://linear.app :: STEAL: The entire type-token architecture, the non-round variable weights, opsz auto, and the decision to make the mono a licensed premium face rather than a free default. :: SPECIFICS: --font-regular: "Inter Variable"; --font-monospace: "Berkeley Mono", ui-monospace, "SF Mono", "Menlo", monospace; --font-settings: "cv01","ss03"; --font-variations: "opsz" auto (and an explicit "opsz" 28 override). Weights 300/400/510/590/680. Titles 1-9: 1.0625/1.25/1.5/2/2.5/3/3.5/4/4.5rem with line-heights 1.4/1.33/1.33/1.125/1.1/1.0/1.1/1.06/1.0 and tracking -.012em below 2rem, -.022em at and above 2rem. Body -.011em @ .9375rem/1.6. Code blocks: 14px/24px. Uses font-variant-numeric: lining-nums tabular-nums slashed-zero!important. Self-hosts InterVariable.woff2 (343.9 KB, opsz 14-32 + wght 100-900, v4.001, 2937 glyphs) and Berkeley-Mono-Variable.woff2 (157.1 KB).
  - [must-steal] Vercel https://vercel.com :: STEAL: The percentage-based tracking ramp expressed as a CSS variable, and the practice of shipping a tiny purpose-subset mono. :: SPECIFICS: --kerning-percent:-.02 and -.06, applied as letter-spacing: calc(var(--font-size) * var(--kerning-percent)). Ramp: -0.02em at 14/16/20px, -0.04em at 24/32px, -0.06em at 40/48/56/64/72px; line-height 1.0 at 56px+. Geist VF 68.1 KB (975 glyphs) + Geist Mono VF subset 22.5 KB (264 glyphs only). --font-weight-semibold is 450 in one scope and 550 in another. Mono feature rule: font-feature-settings: "rlig" 1, "calt" 0, "ss11" 1 — which, note, does not actually disable Geist Mono's liga-based ligatures.
  - [must-steal] Berkeley Mono TX-02 (U.S. Graphics Company) https://usgraphics.com/products/berkeley-mono :: STEAL: Use it as BOTH the display face and the code face — the wdth axis gives you a condensed cut for dense hash/audit tables out of the same file. :: SPECIFICS: Foundry: U.S. Graphics Company. PAID, perpetual, tiered by company size (catalog SKUs FX-050 Trial, FX-102 Developer ~$75 non-commercial, FX-202 Indie, FX-400 Startup, FX-600 Standard Business; Linear holds 'LT-07 Enterprise Business License'). VARIABLE: wght 100-900, wdth 60-100, slnt -16-0. Self-hostable as woff2 (Linear does). v2.002, 157.1 KB, 644 glyphs, upm 1000, x/em 0.518, cap 0.680, 0.600 em advance, clear diagonal-slash zero, 'l' with full baseline bar vs '1' with no baseline bar. Each licensed file is watermarked with a unique License ID in the name table. Emotionally: engineering drawing, oscilloscope, CAD, lab instrument — exactly PRGuard's forensic register.
  - [must-steal] Commit Mono (Eigil Nikolajsen) https://commitmono.com :: STEAL: Bake a custom cut through the official customizer and ship it as 'PRGuard Mono'. Owning a named brand mono for $0 is the single highest ratio of perceived-expense to cost available. :: SPECIFICS: Repo LICENSE is MIT (Copyright (c) 2023 Eigil Nikolajsen), no Reserved Font Name — modification and renaming permitted. Static cuts (the customizer bakes alternates into one static file); 47.0 KB for the 400 latin woff2. upm 1000, x/em 0.540, cap 0.700, 0.600 em advance. Features cv01, cv02, cv05, cv10, ss01-ss05, calt (so ligatures are cleanly disableable). Rendered: '1' 10 points with baseline serif, 'l' 18 points with a right-hooking tail, 'I' with top+bottom bars, slashed zero (3 contours vs O's 2). Used in production by Resend. Emotionally: neutral, anonymous, precise — deliberately opinion-free.
  - [strong] Google Sans Code https://fonts.google.com/specimen/Google+Sans+Code :: STEAL: The zero-risk free mono. Slashed zero and disambiguated l/1/I with no ligature machinery to fight, at 33.7 KB. :: SPECIFICS: Foundry: Google. SIL OFL 1.1, Copyright 2025 The Google Sans Code Project Authors. VARIABLE wght 300-800 (narrower than most). Self-hostable. v7.000, 33.7 KB latin VF, 311 glyphs, upm 2000, x/em 0.523, cap 0.716, 0.600 em advance. GSUB features are only ccmp/locl/mark/ss01 — no calt, no liga, so no code ligatures exist at all. Rendered: '0' has a true diagonal slash by default, 'l' has a right-hooking tail, 'I' has top and bottom bars, '1' has a full baseline serif. Emotionally: clean, institutional, slightly Google-flavoured.
  - [strong] JetBrains Mono https://www.jetbrains.com/lp/mono/ :: STEAL: The correct ligature architecture, and cv01/cv05 for l-disambiguation. If you want ligatures ON in marketing and OFF in evidence panes, this is the only major mono that makes that toggle clean. :: SPECIFICS: Foundry: JetBrains. SIL OFL 1.1, free, self-hostable. VARIABLE wght 100-800. v2.211, 39.4 KB latin VF, upm 1000, x/em 0.550 (the tallest of the monos — pairs near-perfectly with Inter's 0.546), 0.600 em advance. Ligatures are in `calt` (ChainContextSubst) so `font-variant-ligatures: no-contextual` genuinely disables them. Features: calt, zero, cv01/cv02/cv05/cv10 (cv01 and cv05 are two different tailed-l alternates), ss01, ss02, ss19 (alt != and == forms). Rendered: dotted zero by default, tailed 'l', serifed 'I'. Shipped by Graphite and Raycast. Emotionally: IDE-native, engineer-trusted, slightly utilitarian.
  - [strong] Inter v4 (Rasmus Andersson) https://rsms.me/inter/ :: STEAL: Use it for body/UI only, never as the brand voice, and configure it in a way nobody else does: cv05 for the tailed l, slashed zero, tabular figures, opsz driven. :: SPECIFICS: SIL OFL 1.1. VARIABLE with TWO axes: opsz 14-32 and wght 100-900 (Inter Display is now the opsz upper end, not a separate family). Self-hostable. Full file 343.9 KB / 2937 glyphs; latin wght-only subset 47.1 KB. upm 2048, x/em 0.546, cap 0.728. CRITICAL: default '0' is PLAIN (2 contours) — you must set slashed-zero; default figures are PROPORTIONAL (833-1323/2048) — you must set tabular-nums. Key alternates: cv05 l→l.ss02 (tailed l), cv01 1→one.ss01, cv08/ss02 I→I.1 (serifed I), zero 0→zero.slash. Loaded by Linear, Raycast, Neon, Graphite, Resend, Supabase, Modal, Warp. Emotionally: competent and invisible — which is the problem.
  - [avoid] Geist + Geist Mono (Vercel / Basement Studio) https://vercel.com/font :: STEAL: Only the metric-matching idea. Do not use the faces themselves. :: SPECIFICS: SIL OFL 1.1 (LICENSE.txt: 'Copyright (c) 2023 Vercel, in collaboration with basement.studio'). Latest release v1.7.2, published 2026-06-01. VARIABLE wght 100-900, plus 18 static cuts each incl. italics, plus a Geist Pixel sibling. Sans and Mono share IDENTICAL metrics: upm 1000, x-height 530, cap 710, ascent 1005, descent -295. DISQUALIFYING DEFECTS for this product: (a) Mono's 'l' is near-identical to '1' (both 480x710, 1 contour) — open issue #102 — fixable only with "ss03" 1; (b) 69 ligatures live in `liga` (ON by default) including != -> <= <!-- /= := and Mono has NO calt, so "calt" 0 does nothing; (c) ss09 turns the slashed zero OFF. And above all: it is CodeRabbit's typeface.

Their Web Font Licence Agreement permits @font-face on the licensed domain, WOFF2 ONLY, and explicitly permits subsetting to reduce file size — i.e. SELF-HOSTING IS ALLOWED (their licences overview page reads ambiguously; the EULA is the authority). Plex Sans variable wght 100-700, 44 KB; Plex Mono 400 static 14 KB. Plex Sans is the rare sans whose digits are ALL EQUAL WIDTH by default. Plex Mono: 0.600 em advance, x/em 0.516, has a `zero` feature and NO calt/liga at all (zero code ligatures). Weakness: Plex Mono's '0' and 'O' have IDENTICAL bounding boxes (488x722), so the distinction rests entirely on the interior mark. Used by Val Town. Source Code Pro: x/em 0.486 — it renders 11% optically smaller than Inter, which is why code on Stripe and Supabase looks undersized; has `zero`, no ligatures. Space Mono: static only, 16 KB, x/em 0.496, quirky and now dated (Greptile and Arc both use it). DM Mono: static, 14 KB, x/em 0.496 (Graphite, Modal).
