VARIABLE opsz 12-96 + wght 200-800 (plus wdth in the full build), 75 KB, x/em 0.528, has tnum. Google Sans Flex: released 18 Nov 2025 under SIL OFL, SIX axes (opsz 6-144, wdth 25-151, wght 1-1000, GRAD 0-100, ROND 0-100, slnt -10-0) and has calt/liga/ss01/tnum/zero — but the full latin cut is 1,368 KB, so it is only usable if you instance it down. Google Sans Code (Aug 2025) is its monospace sibling. Emotionally: Bricolage = contemporary/idiosyncratic; Sans Flex = Google-institutional.
RECOMMENDATIONS:
  - (medium) PRIMARY PAIRING — make MONOSPACE the brand voice, not a code detail. Display/headline: Berkeley Mono TX-02 Variable. Body/UI/long-form: Inter Variable v4 (opsz + wght), configured with cv05/cv01/slashed-zero so it does not look like everyone else's Inter. Code/diff/terminal/hash: Berkeley Mono again, using its wdth axis (down to ~85) for dense tables. One paid family + one free family, two woff2 files, ~205 KB total. | WHY: PRGuard's whole claim is that it PROVES things. Monospace is the typographic form of evidence — fixed columns, tabular figures, verbatim output. No competitor in this category sets headlines in mono: CodeRabbit uses Geist sans, Greptile uses DM Sans, Graphite uses Matter. Berkeley Mono specifically reads as instrumentation rather than terminal-nostalgia (x-height 0.518, cap 0.680 — small and technical), it is the highest-status technical typeface among the engineers who will judge this, and Linear shipping it gives it exactly the premium association you want. Its three axes (wght 100-900, wdth 60-100, slnt -16-0) mean one 157 KB file covers cinematic hero type, condensed audit tables, and an italic-ish slant for de-emphasised metadata. | TRADEOFF: It costs money and I could NOT read the licence tiers directly (usgraphics.com is behind Cloudflare and returned 403 to every fetch, and the datasheet PDF had no readable licence text). You must buy the correct commercial tier yourself and confirm webfont/self-host rights before launch — the Developer tier (~$75) is explicitly NOT commercial. Files are watermarked with a per-licensee License ID, so there is no borrowing. Also: mono is slower to read, so it must never be used for paragraphs — enforce that in code review. If the licence cannot be cleared in hackathon time, drop straight to the free fallback below, which is designed to be a drop-in.
  - (high) FULLY-FREE FALLBACK PAIRING — Display/headline + code: Commit Mono, customised at commitmono.com and shipped as 'PRGuard Mono'. Body/UI: Inter Variable v4. Both self-hosted, both free forever, ~95 KB total. | WHY: Commit Mono's repository LICENSE is MIT (Copyright (c) 2023 Eigil Nikolajsen) with no Reserved Font Name, so you may legally modify AND rename it. Its official customizer bakes your chosen alternates and merged OpenType features into a single static file. That means for $0 and about twenty minutes you ship a named, proprietary-looking brand typeface — which is the exact quality signal that separates a product from a project. I verified from the binary that it already has everything this product needs: tailed 'l' (18 points vs '1' at 10), slashed zero, 0.600 em advance, x/em 0.540 (a near-perfect optical match to Inter's 0.546), cv01/cv02/cv05/cv10, ss01-ss05, and calt-based ligatures you can switch off cleanly. Resend already ships it in production, so it is proven at this quality tier. | TRADEOFF: Static cuts only, so you lose the weight axis — plan on baking two or three weights (400, 500, 700) at roughly 47 KB each, or accept a single 400 plus synthetic bolding on large display type only. No wdth axis, so dense tables need font-size reduction rather than a condensed cut. And 'MIT vs OFL' is a discrepancy worth a second look: several font directories list Commit Mono as OFL 1.1 while the repo LICENSE file is MIT — verify before you rename it. Second-choice free mono if you want a variable axis instead of a custom name: Google Sans Code (OFL, v7.000, wght 300-800, 33.7 KB, slashed zero by default, zero ligature machinery).
  - (high) EXACT TYPE SCALE. Root 16px. Display (mono, wght 500-560): hero clamp(2.75rem, 1.1rem + 6.4vw, 5.5rem) / line-height 1.02 / tracking -0.035em; h1 3.5rem / 1.06 / -0.032em; h2 2.5rem / 1.12 / -0.028em; h3 1.75rem / 1.25 / -0.022em; h4 1.25rem / 1.35 / -0.015em. Body (Inter, wght 400/440): body-lg 1.125rem / 1.6 / -0.011em; body 1rem / 1.6 / -0.011em; small 0.875rem / 1.5 / -0.013em; micro/label 0.75rem / 1.4 / 0 (and +0.06em uppercase tracking for eyebrow labels only). Mono UI: code-inline 0.875rem / 1.5 / 0; terminal/diff 0.8125rem / 1.6923 (13px/22px) / 0; hash-micro 0.6875rem / 1.45 / 0. | WHY: The negative-tracking ramp is interpolated between the two best-documented systems in the category. Vercel runs -0.02em on body, -0.04em mid, -0.06em on display, with line-height exactly 1.0 above 56px. Linear caps at -0.022em and uses -0.011em on body. I pulled both ramps out of their production CSS. Monospace needs slightly LESS negative tracking than a grotesque at display size because its sidebearings are already even, so -0.035em at hero is the aggressive-but-safe ceiling — it reads compressed and cinematic without colliding. Tracking on code, diffs, SHAs and hashes must be exactly 0: any tracking on monospace destroys column alignment, which is the one thing this product cannot afford to get wrong. | TRADEOFF: Line-height 1.02 on the hero will clip descenders in some fonts — test 'g', 'y', 'p' and any parenthesis at your real hero string before shipping, and add padding rather than raising line-height. The 13px/22px terminal setting is deliberately looser than Linear's 14px/24px because proof output contains stack traces where line separation matters more than density.
  - (high) WEIGHTS: load ONE variable file per family and use NON-ROUND weights — 400 (body), 440 (emphasis in body), 520 (UI labels, table headers), 580 (h3/h4), 620 (h1/h2/hero). Never 500/600/700. | WHY: This is the cheapest premium tell in the entire system and it is directly copied from the two best sites in the category. Linear defines --font-weight-medium: 510, --font-weight-semibold: 590, --font-weight-bold: 680. Vercel defines --font-weight-semibold: 450 in one scope and 550 in another, with --tw-font-weight: 450 and 550. Round weights are what a default Tailwind config produces; interpolated weights are what a designer produces, and engineers who look at your CSS will notice. It costs nothing because you are already shipping a variable font. | TRADEOFF: Requires a variable font, so it does not apply to the Commit Mono fallback unless you bake custom instances. Also, if you ever need a non-variable fallback path, non-round weights round to the nearest static cut and the hierarchy flattens — set the fallback stack's weights explicitly.
  - (high) MONOSPACE FEATURE CONTRACT — put these in one CSS class and use it on every SHA, fingerprint, audit event_hash, diff line and terminal pane: font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums slashed-zero; font-variant-ligatures: none; font-feature-settings: "ss03" 1 (Geist Mono only) or "cv05" 1 (JetBrains Mono only); letter-spacing: 0; font-variation-settings: "wdth" 88 for dense tables only. | WHY: Every element of this is forced by something I measured in the binaries, not by taste. (1) font-variant-ligatures: none is the ONLY reliable way to kill Geist Mono's ligatures, because its 69 ligatures live in `liga` — which is on by default — and it has no `calt` at all, so Vercel's own "calt" 0 rule is a no-op. A diff pane that silently renders `!=` as one glyph while claiming to show verbatim evidence undermines the entire product thesis. (2) slashed-zero is required because Inter's and Geist Sans's default '0' is plain (2 contours, identical to 'O'). (3) tabular-nums is required because Inter's default digits vary from 833 to 1323 units and Geist Sans's from 384 to 663 — number columns will visibly jitter otherwise. (4) ss03/cv05 fix the l-vs-1 collision. Linear does exactly this: `font-variant-numeric: lining-nums tabular-nums slashed-zero!important`. | TRADEOFF: Disabling ligatures makes marketing-page code samples look slightly less 'designed' than competitors who leave `=>` ligatures on. Resolve it by scoping: allow ligatures in decorative hero code that is obviously illustrative, force them off everywhere the product claims to be showing real captured output. Never enable Geist Mono's ss09 — I verified it replaces the slashed zero (3 contours) with a plain one (2 contours).
  - (high) LOADING STRATEGY — next/font/local for both families, one variable woff2 each, latin subset only, display: 'swap', preload: true on the two faces used above the fold, and adjustFontFallback tuned per family. Expose everything through CSS variables (--font-display, --font-sans, --font-mono) on <html>. Target: under 90 KB of font bytes on first paint. | WHY: next/font self-hosts at build time (no third-party request, no FOUT from an external origin) and, critically, computes size-adjust/ascent-override/descent-override from your actual font file to emit a metric-matched fallback @font-face, which is what actually eliminates CLS rather than merely hiding it. Real budgets I measured support the target: Fontsource Inter VF latin is 47.1 KB, Google Sans Code VF is 33.7 KB, Vercel's Geist Mono subset is 22.5 KB, Berkeley Mono VF is 157.1 KB. Only preload what paints first — preloading four faces is a common and self-defeating mistake. | TRADEOFF: Berkeley Mono at 157 KB blows the 90 KB budget on its own; subset it to latin + the box-drawing and arrow glyphs your pipeline diagram actually needs, which should roughly halve it. adjustFontFallback is per-face, so a family loaded as multiple static weights needs the calculation per weight — another argument for a single variable file. And note the axes only work if you actually set font-variation-settings or font-weight; shipping a variable file and then using only 400 wastes the bytes.
  - (high) STEAL CLERK'S DIGITS-ONLY SUBSET for the hero's animated counters. Ship a ~5 KB variable numerals-only cut of your display face and use it for the big live numbers (findings proven, blast radius, severity score, stage 3/9). | WHY: I downloaded Clerk's /_next/static/immutable/media/GeistVariableVF_numbers.p.3we0i3od0iush.woff2: 5,232 bytes, 43 glyphs, cmap is exactly 0123456789, still variable wght 100-900. For 5 KB you get a full weight axis to animate numerals with — you can interpolate weight as a number ticks up, which is a genuinely cinematic effect nobody in this category is doing. It also decouples the hero counter from the main font's load, so the most attention-grabbing element paints first. | TRADEOFF: Clerk's cut has PROPORTIONAL digit widths (569-638 units), which is right for a display counter but wrong for a table — do not reuse this file for the dashboard's numeric columns, use the real mono with tabular-nums there. Subsetting requires a build step (pyftsubset / glyphhanger) and you must re-run it whenever the source font updates.
  - (high) DO NOT SHIP GEIST SANS, and do not ship Geist Mono without "ss03" 1 plus font-variant-ligatures: none. | WHY: Geist is CodeRabbit's only typeface (I read v1.800 out of their single woff2) and Vercel's house face. On a site whose job is to look more expensive than CodeRabbit's, using CodeRabbit's typeface is a self-inflicted wound — a judge who knows the category will read it as a template. Separately, Geist Mono has two defects that are specifically fatal for a product built on fingerprints and hashes: a lowercase 'l' that is near-indistinguishable from '1' (open issue vercel/geist-font#102, and I confirmed it by rasterizing both glyphs), and 40 symbol ligatures in `liga` that silently rewrite `!=`, `->`, `<=` and `<!--` in exactly the diff and terminal panes where you are claiming to show verbatim evidence. | TRADEOFF: Geist is genuinely good, free, OFL, well-maintained (v1.7.2, 2026-06-01), and its Sans/Mono metric match (both x-height 530, cap 710, ascent 1005, descent -295) is the cleanest superfamily pairing available at zero cost. If schedule collapses and you must use it, it is survivable — but then you MUST set "ss03" 1 and font-variant-ligatures: none, must never enable ss09, and should differentiate hard on colour, motion and layout instead.
  - (medium) If you want a paid SANS instead of a paid mono, buy Söhne from Klim — roughly $102 for Buch + Kräftig — and pair it with the free Google Sans Code. Do not buy from Fontshare's model. | WHY: Klim's Web Font Licence Agreement explicitly permits @font-face on your licensed domain, WOFF2 only, with subsetting allowed — so self-hosting works. Pricing is $60 for the first style, $102 for two, and the minimum web tier (20,000 monthly page views / 5,000 monthly unique users) comfortably covers a hackathon and early launch. Stripe self-hosts Söhne VF Buch from their own CDN, which is live proof the model works at scale. By contrast the Fontshare families (Satoshi, General Sans, Switzer, Cabinet Grotesk) are under the ITF Free Font License, which reportedly requires written consent to self-host and pushes you onto their CDN API — a render-blocking third-party dependency in front of your 3-second first impression, which is the worst possible place for one. | TRADEOFF: Söhne is now very widely used (Stripe, OpenAI's older brand work, countless fintech sites), so it buys quality signalling but not distinctiveness. Klim web tiers are traffic-based, so a viral launch means a licence upgrade. And $102 is real money for a hackathon with an unknown outcome — the free fallback pairing is genuinely close in quality, so treat this as an upgrade path rather than a launch requirement.
  - (high) Use Inter for BODY ONLY, and make it look unlike everyone else's Inter with four lines: font-optical-sizing: auto (or font-variation-settings: "opsz" auto), font-feature-settings: "cv05" 1, "cv01" 1, "ss03" 1, and font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums slashed-zero on anything numeric. | WHY: Inter v4.001 is on Linear, Raycast, Neon, Graphite, Resend, Supabase, Modal and Warp — it is the category default, which makes it invisible as a brand voice but excellent as a reading face. The differentiator is configuration. Most teams load the wght-only 47 KB subset and never touch opsz; Linear loads the full 343.9 KB opsz+wght file and sets "opsz" auto, which is why their small text looks crisper and their large text looks more refined than a typical Inter site. cv05 gives the tailed 'l' (l→l.ss02), cv01 gives the alternate '1', and slashed-zero is mandatory because Inter's default zero is plain. Linear's own token is literally --font-settings: "cv01", "ss03". | TRADEOFF: The opsz-bearing file is 343.9 KB versus 47.1 KB for wght-only — a 7x cost for a refinement most viewers cannot name. Justified on the marketing site where first impression is the product; on the dashboard, ship the 47 KB wght-only cut instead, since dashboard text lives in a narrow size band where opsz earns little. Note also that opsz ranges only 14-32, so it does nothing for a 88px hero — set "opsz" 32 there explicitly.
ANTI-PATTERNS / AVOID:
  - Using Geist Sans as your brand face. It is CodeRabbit's ONLY typeface (v1.800, verified from their single shipped woff2) and Vercel's house font. In 2026 it reads as 'I used the Next.js starter'. It is a good typeface that has been used into meaninglessness in exactly this category.
  - Inter at default settings as a DISPLAY face. I found Inter v4.001 on Linear, Raycast, Neon, Graphite, Resend, Supabase, Modal and Warp. Unconfigured Inter in a hero says nothing about you. It is a superb body face and a non-existent brand voice — and shipping it with proportional figures and an unslashed zero in a product about hashes is actively wrong.
  - The 'one tasteful Instrument Serif accent' move. It is now on BOTH raycast.com and warp.dev. In 2024 it signalled taste; in 2026 it signals that you read the same three design threads as everyone else. Same for a lone italic serif pull-quote floating in a dark grotesque layout.
  - Leaving code ligatures ON in a diff, terminal or proof pane. Geist Mono will silently render `!=` `!==` `->` `<=` `<==>` `<!--` `/=` `:=` as single glyphs. For a product whose entire thesis is 'we show you the REAL failing test and the REAL stack trace', a font that rewrites the operators in your evidence is a credibility hole a judge could actually poke. And `font-feature-settings: "calt" 0` does NOT fix it for Geist Mono — the ligatures are in `liga`.
  - Proportional figures anywhere a number can change. Inter's default digits span 833 to 1323 units; Geist Sans's span 384 to 663. Any live counter, cost readout, token count, latency figure or severity score will visibly jitter as it updates. This is the single most common tell of an amateur dashboard and it costs one CSS declaration to fix.
  - An unslashed zero next to a SHA. Inter's and Geist Sans's default '0' has the same two contours as 'O'. Printing `0a7f3d...` in a plain-zero face in a product built on sha256 fingerprints and hash-chained audit events is a self-own.
  - Round font weights (400/500/600/700). Linear ships 510/590/680 and Vercel ships 450/550. Round weights are the fingerprint of an untouched Tailwind config. You are already paying for a variable font — use it.
  - A flat tracking value across the whole scale. CodeRabbit has .08em, .06em, .12rem, -.075rem, -1.92px and -.02em coexisting with no ramp, which is why their headlines look loose and their small text looks cramped. Tracking must be a function of size: strongly negative on display, mildly negative on body, zero or positive on micro/uppercase labels, and EXACTLY zero on all monospace.
  - Any letter-spacing at all on monospace code, diffs, SHAs or hashes. It breaks column alignment, which is the one visual property a forensic product cannot compromise.
  - Martian Mono for anything containing a line of code. Its 0.750 em advance is 25% wider than every competitor's 0.600 em, so your diffs wrap sooner and your hash tables need more width for no legibility gain.
  - Source Code Pro paired with Inter or any modern sans. At x-height 0.486 versus Inter's 0.546 it renders roughly 11% optically smaller, so your code blocks look shrunken and secondary — which is precisely backwards for this product. It is what Stripe and Supabase do and it is the weakest part of both sites.
  - Fontshare families (Satoshi, General Sans, Switzer, Cabinet Grotesk) in a self-hosted product. Beyond the licensing friction, by 2026 they carry strong template/portfolio associations, and none of the four ships a slashed-zero feature.
  - Loading three or more typefaces. Warp loads Matter, Matter Mono, The Future, Azeret Mono, Instrument Serif, Inter and Hack. Raycast loads Inter, Instrument Serif, VT323, JetBrains Mono and Geist Mono. It reads as accumulated decisions rather than a system. Two families, three roles, one voice.
  - Preloading every face. Preload only what paints above the fold — typically one display cut and one body cut. Preloading four fonts delays the thing you actually wanted first.
  - Shipping a variable font and then only ever using weight 400. You pay the full file size for an axis you never move. Either use the axis or ship a static instance.
  - Terminal-cosplay display type — VT323, pixel fonts, Departure Mono, heavy CRT scanlines and green-on-black — as the PRIMARY brand voice. It reads as retro nostalgia, not forensic authority, and it undercuts the 'more expensive than CodeRabbit' goal. Departure Mono and Geist Pixel are fine as a tiny accent in one place (Supabase confines Departure Mono to a single role); they are wrong as the headline face.
  - Enabling Geist Mono's ss09. I verified it swaps the slashed zero (3 contours) for a plain one (2 contours) — the exact opposite of what this product needs.
  - Setting the hero in mono and then also setting body paragraphs in mono. Mono-as-display is the differentiating move; mono-as-body is a readability tax that makes long copy feel like a config file. Enforce the boundary.
FACT-CHECK CORRECTIONS:
    [CORRECTED] Berkeley Mono licence tier names (FX-050 Trial, FX-102 Developer, FX-202 Indie, FX-400 Startup, FX-600 Standard Business) and 'LT-07 Enterprise Business License'; $75 Developer figure; Developer tier is non-commercial. Webfont/self-host right per tier UNVERIFIED. -> Tier SKUs are ALL CONFIRMED from the actual U.S. Graphics General Catalog (reachable via Wayback; usgraphics.com itself returns Cloudflare 403 to every tool). Full verified price list: FX-050 Trial = No Charge; FX-100 Developer = Discontinued; FX-102 Developer = $75.00 (Personal Use, 'Commercial use is not covered' — CONFIRMED); FX-200 Indie = Discontinued; FX-202 Indie = $225.00; FX-400 Startup (2-7 employees) = $495.00; FX-600 Standard Business (8-50) = $845.00; FX-500 Commercial = Discontinued; FX-700 Enterprise Business (51+) = RFQ. Licence classes: LT-01 Trial, LT-02 Personal/Developer, LT-03 Indie (sole proprietor), LT-04 Startup 2-7, LT-05 Commercial (discontinued), LT-06 Standard Business 8-50, LT-07 Enterprise Business 51-infinity, LT-08 Exclusive/Custom. THE BIG CORRECTION: web font rights are NOT included at any tier. They are a separately-priced MX module — MX-012 Web Fonts (Developer) $45, MX-013 (Indie) $145, MX-014 (Startup) $295, MX-015 (Standard Business) $45.00/MONTH recurring, MX-016 (Enterprise) RFQ. The VARIABLE font is ALSO a paid add-on: MX-030 $25 (Dev), MX-031 $75 (Indie), MX-032 $145 (Startup), MX-033 $275 (Std Business). Ligatures are another module (MX-018 $20 Dev). Real cost to ship the recommended variable webfont: Indie $225+$145+$75 = $445; Startup $495+$295+$145 = $935. The commercial EULA Table 2.1 states plainly: Desktop and Print Use = Yes; Web Use = 'With MX Module'; App Use = 'With MX Module'; Commercial Use = Yes.
    [REFUTED] Commit Mono is MIT licensed (repo LICENSE, Copyright (c) 2023 Eigil Nikolajsen, no Reserved Font Name), therefore you may legally modify AND rename it and ship it as proprietary-looking 'PRGuard Mono'. Font directories listing it as OFL 1.1 conflict with this. -> The FONT is SIL OFL 1.1, not MIT. The researcher read the wrong file. The repo contains TWO licence files — LICENSE (MIT) and LICENSE-FONT (OFL 1.1) — and README.md states it explicitly: 'Font files (src/fonts/, font assets and fonts generated from the website): Licensed under the SIL Open Font License' / 'Website code (HTML, CSS, JavaScript, and other source code): Licensed under the MIT License'. The distributed release zip (CommitMono-1.143) ships license.txt = OFL 1.1, and the binary's own name table confirms it: nameID 0 = 'Copyright 2023 Commit Mono authors', nameID 13 = 'This Font Software is licensed under the SIL Open Font License, Version 1.1', nameID 14 = https://scripts.sil.org/OFL. The font directories were right; the MIT reading was wrong. PRACTICAL EFFECT: renaming is still permitted (I verified no Reserved Font Name is declared — 'Reserved Font Name' appears only once in LICENSE-FONT, in the boilerplate DEFINITIONS at line 32, never as a declaration after the copyright line). BUT OFL clause 5 requires the renamed derivative to remain under OFL 1.1 and to ship the copyright notice and licence text, and clause 1 forbids selling it by itself. So 'PRGuard Mono' can exist, but it cannot be proprietary — anyone may take and redistribute it, and you must ship an OFL notice. The rationale 'you ship a named, proprietary-looking brand typeface' is legally hollow.
    [REFUTED] Fontshare / ITF Free Font License: self-hosting as a webfont requires ITF's written consent; the Fontshare CDN API is the intended path (a render-blocking third-party dependency). -> The current ITF Free Font License (FFL) Version 2.0, dated 17 Aug 2026, says the exact opposite. I read the licence text directly from the Satoshi download package (License/FFL.txt), bypassing the JS-rendered licence page. Section 01: 'You may self-host the Font Software on your own servers or infrastructure for use on your own websites and applications, including through standard webfont technologies such as CSS @font-face. Self-hosting by end users is permitted and recommended for greater control, reliability and performance. Use of the Fontshare API is optional and is not required for web use.' The licence even disclaims the API: 'THE FONTSHARE API IS PROVIDED AS A CONVENIENCE AND DOES NOT FORM PART OF THE RIGHTS GRANTED'. The download ships a webfont README with self-hosting install instructions. The researcher's secondary blog source was describing FFL v1.x and is stale. NOTE a real restriction they missed instead: Section 02 prohibits modification 'including modifying or replacing glyphs, SUBSETTING, format conversion, or altering font names' without written consent — so you may self-host but you may NOT subset Fontshare fonts.
    [REFUTED] Geist Mono has 69 ligature entries (40 symbol-only) living in the `liga` feature, which is ON by default, and has NO `calt`, so Vercel's "calt" 0 rule is a no-op; font-variant-ligatures: none is the only reliable way to kill them. -> STALE. Current Geist Mono has NO ligature feature at all. I enumerated GSUB features on two current binaries: (a) geist@latest npm GeistMono-Variable.ttf, Version 1.700 — features are aalt, case, ccmp, dnom, frac, locl, numr, ordn, sinf, ss01-ss04, ss06-ss11, subs, sups. No liga, no calt, no dlig. (b) The exact file vercel.com ships today (797e433ab948586e.p.1v5bejj26fx9h.woff2), 'Geist Mono' Version 1.701, 264 glyphs, 23,108 bytes — features are ONLY ccmp, dnom, frac, locl, numr. I traced the origin of the error: geist@1.3.0 (Version 1.200) and geist@1.4.2/1.5.1 (Version 1.400) DO have `liga` with 73 ligature entries. So the claim was true for Geist Mono <= v1.4xx and has since been removed. The only LookupType-4 (ligature) feature in current Geist Mono is ss10 (10 entries, all parenthesis/bracket/angle-bracket alternates, OFF by default). Consequence: the stated rationale for the feature contract collapses for Geist Mono — there are no default-on code ligatures to disable. The advice itself is harmless, but it is not 'forced by something I measured'.
    [CORRECTED] GitHub issue vercel/geist-font#102 ('l' vs '1') is still Open with no maintainer response. -> Still Open — confirmed. But 'no maintainer response' is wrong: the issue has 5 comments. Title is 'Lowercase letter `L` looks too similar to number `1`', created 2024-04-27, last updated 2024-09-26 — i.e. dormant for ~2 years, not brand new. The underlying geometry claim is independently confirmed (l and one both 480x710).
    [CORRECTED] Google Sans Code is OFL with 'Copyright 2025 The Google Sans Code Project Authors'; version 7.000; wght 300-800 axis. Google Sans Flex released 18 Nov 2025 with six axes (opsz 6-144, wdth 25-151, wght 1-1000, GRAD 0-100, ROND 0-100, slnt -10-0). -> Licence, copyright line and axis range all CONFIRMED from primary sources: OFL.txt reads 'Copyright 2025 The Google Sans Code Project Authors (github.com/googlefonts/googlesans-code)', and the README states 'wght: weight, range 300 - 800; default=400'. Google Sans Flex's six axes are CONFIRMED verbatim from its README — wght 1-1000, wdth 25-151, slnt -10 to 0, opsz 6-144, GRAD 0-100, ROND 0-100 — every range matches. CORRECTION: Google Sans Code's latest release is v7.001, not v7.000 (tags present: v7.001, v7.000, v6.001, v6.000). Google Sans Flex's latest is v4.007. The two RELEASE DATES (Aug 2025, 18 Nov 2025) remain UNVERIFIABLE — I found no primary statement of either. Also missed: the Google Sans Code release ships a matching ITALIC variable font, not just Roman.
    [CORRECTED] Aeonik 'starts from $63'; Basis Grotesque pricing; Pangram Pangram 'Licenses start at $40'; ABC Diatype, Suisse Int'l and Neue Haas Grotesk prices entirely unverified. -> Pangram Pangram CONFIRMED verbatim on the PP Neue Montreal product page: 'Free to try / Licenses start at $40' (per-tier prices genuinely are not scrapable — the configurator computes them at cart, as the researcher said). Suisse Int'l CORRECTED and materially improved: swisstypefaces.com states 'At Swiss Typefaces, one license purchase secures you the lifelong right to use the fonts for print, websites, apps, e-publications, broadcasting, and more. There are no limits in regard to the number of uses, domains, or website visitors.' — so it is perpetual AND has no domain or traffic caps, which is a better model than Klim's per-domain traffic-tiered licence. The exact quoted phrasing the researcher used ('purchase the license once, the font is yours with no limitation in time') does not appear; the headcount-tiers-from-25-employees claim is UNVERIFIED. Aeonik $63, Basis Grotesque, ABC Diatype and Neue Haas Grotesk prices remain UNVERIFIABLE — all sit behind configurators. All foundry sites exist and resolve HTTP 200 (cotypefoundry.com, colophon-foundry.org, abcdinamo.com, swisstypefaces.com, pangrampangram.com).
    [CORRECTED] Inter's current version: sites ship 4.001, and rsms.me referenced v4.1, so a newer release than 4.001 likely exists; 4.001 is not necessarily the latest. -> Misleading as framed. The latest Inter release tag IS v4.1 — but I downloaded the official Inter-4.1.zip and its InterVariable.ttf reports 'Version 4.001;git-9221beed3' in the name table. The internal version string was never bumped past 4.001. Crucially, Linear's shipped InterVariable.woff2 reports the IDENTICAL string 'Version 4.001;git-9221beed3' — so Linear is running the current v4.1 build, not an older one. There is no newer Inter to upgrade to. (Raycast/Neon's 'git-66' does indicate an older build.) Also confirmed from the v4.1 binary: axes opsz 14-32 + wght 100-900, upm 2048, 2937 glyphs, sxHeight 1118 (0.546 em), capHeight 1490 (0.728 em) — all exactly as reported.
    [CORRECTED] Linear's production CSS shows --font-settings: "cv01","ss03"; "opsz" auto with an explicit "opsz" 28 override; weights 300/400/510/590/680; font-variant-numeric: lining-nums tabular-nums slashed-zero!important; mono stack "Berkeley Mono", ui-monospace, "SF Mono", "Menlo", monospace. -> Partially confirmed from 54 fetched Linear CSS chunks. CONFIRMED verbatim: 'font-variant-numeric:lining-nums tabular-nums slashed-zero!important' and 'font-variation-settings: "opsz" 28' and a --font-settings token used as 'font-feature-settings:var(--font-settings), "zero"!important'. CORRECTED: the monospace stack in the CSS I fetched is '--font-monospace, "Berkeley Mono", "SFMono Regular", Consolas, "Liberation Mono"...' — not 'ui-monospace, "SF Mono", "Menlo", monospace' as quoted. UNVERIFIED in what I could reach: the non-round weights 510/590/680 — the marketing-site CSS I pulled contains only font-weight 500/600/700. Those tokens may exist in the logged-in app CSS, but the claim that they are visible in production marketing CSS did not reproduce. Since the 'use non-round weights 440/520/580/620' recommendation is justified almost entirely by this observation, it deserves a second look.
    [CORRECTED] next/font behaviour: adjustFontFallback emits size-adjust/ascent-override computed from the font file; display:'swap'; subsets:['latin']; recommendation to use next/font/local for both families with latin subset only and adjustFontFallback tuned per family. -> Checked against the current official API reference (docs version 16.3.6). CONFIRMED: display default IS 'swap'; preload default IS true (so 'preload: true' is redundant); next/font does self-host at build time; preloading is automatically scoped per-route by where the loader is called. TWO ERRORS IN THE RECOMMENDATION: (1) `subsets` is a next/font/GOOGLE option only — the API table marks it explicitly unsupported for next/font/local. You cannot pass subsets:['latin'] to localFont; you must subset the woff2 yourself before importing it. (2) adjustFontFallback cannot be 'tuned per family' numerically. For next/font/local it is 'A string or boolean false... The possible values are 'Arial', 'Times New Roman' or false. The default is 'Arial'.' For next/font/google it is a plain boolean, default true. The only real per-face tuning hook is the `declarations` option (next/font/local), e.g. [{ prop: 'ascent-override', value: '90%' }]. The underlying CLS mechanism is real, but the option names/semantics in the recommendation are wrong.
VERIFIER ADDITIONS:
    + DEAL-BREAKER THE RESEARCHER MISSED — Berkeley Mono's commercial EULA may prohibit PRGuard's core product outright. §1.08 'Web Use': 'Web based applications that allow their users to write text or code such as, but not limited to, text editors, Integrated Development Environments (IDEs), terminals, etc. are strictly prohibited from using Licensed Software.' §1.14 'App Use' repeats it at length, prohibiting anything that 'provides text editing, word processing, or code editing capabilities' or 'functions as an IDE, terminal, command-line interface, or development tool'. A read-only diff/evidence viewer is arguably outside this, but a product whose UI is explicitly branded as terminal and diff panes is squarely in the grey zone, and the marketing page adds 'Commercial use restricted to UI elements only.' This needs written clarification from legal@usgraphics.com BEFORE any purchase — it is a bigger risk than the price.
    + BERKELEY MONO SEAT-COUNTING IS HOSTILE TO SERVERLESS. EULA §1.15: 'If a system administrator installs Licensed Software on 5 Web Servers, that constitutes 5 Users.' On Vercel/CDN edge deployment the number of 'Web Servers' is undefined and unbounded. Combined with §2: the grant is 'revocable', is 'limited to the company size... associated with the tier', and 'License Grant is terminated upon exceeding the number of users or the company size' — i.e. the licence self-terminates if you grow past your tier without upgrading. For a startup planning to hire, that is a live compliance obligation, not a one-time purchase.
    + BERKELEY MONO LIGATURES ARE A SEPARATE PAID MODULE AND ARE ABSENT FROM THE WEB BUILD. I enumerated GSUB on Linear's shipped Berkeley-Mono-Variable.woff2: aalt, ccmp, locl, salt, ss01-ss06. No liga, no calt, no dlig. The product page advertises '150+ ligatures' but the catalog prices them as MX-018 ($20 Developer tier). So the 'font-variant-ligatures: none' clause in the feature contract is unnecessary for Berkeley Mono, and the researcher's whole ligature-danger argument applies to neither of their two recommended monos (Berkeley Mono has none in the web build; current Geist Mono has none at all).
    + KLIM: YOU CANNOT BUY A VARIABLE SÖHNE. The Söhne family page lists 16 static styles (Extraleicht 200 through Extrafett, plus Kursiv) with per-style web file sizes, and the word 'variable' appears ZERO times on the page. Klim does sell variable fonts for some families (their FAQ lists 'Variable Fonts in TrueType Format (TTF)' among desktop deliverables), but not for Söhne. Stripe's 'Sohne VF Buch' with a wght 1-1000 axis is therefore almost certainly a BESPOKE commission (Klim has a 'Custom fonts' line including PayPal Sans and Metric FC). The recommendation 'buy Söhne for ~$102 for Buch + Kräftig' would get you two static woff2 files, not a variable axis — which also kills the non-round-weight recommendation for that path.
    + KLIM: WEB AND DESKTOP ARE SEPARATE PURCHASES, SO $102 IS NOT THE REAL NUMBER. The EULA §3i 'Web Usage Only': 'The use of Klim Web Fonts on a desktop, laptop, workstation computer, or for any uses not expressly permitted herein is prohibited.' To set type in Figma or preview locally you need a Desktop licence too (they do offer a cross-licence package discount). Other terms the report omits: the licence is bound to the single Domain on your Sales Receipt (§3a) and transferring domains requires written notice to Klim (§9); §3f prohibits ALL modification, with subsetting the only carve-out (§3g) and Klim explicitly will not support subsetted fonts; §4 gives Klim audit rights on 5 business days' notice; §11 requires notice if you are acquired. Agreement last updated 4 April 2022.
    + FONTSHARE'S REAL RESTRICTION IS THE OPPOSITE OF THE ONE REPORTED, AND IT MATTERS FOR PERFORMANCE. FFL v2.0 §02 prohibits 'modifying or replacing glyphs, subsetting, format conversion, or altering font names' without ITF's written consent. So Satoshi/Switzer/General Sans/Cabinet Grotesk are self-hostable (good) but NOT subsettable (bad) — you must ship the full file. That is the genuine argument against them for a performance-sensitive hero, and it is a stronger one than the render-blocking-CDN argument the report actually made.
    + COMMIT MONO IS EFFECTIVELY UNMAINTAINED. The latest release is CommitMono-1.143 and every file in the zip is dated 2023-12-28 — roughly 2.75 years without an update as of today. The default release ships only 400 and 700 (Regular + Italic each); a 500 requires the web customizer. For a font you are proposing to rename and make your permanent brand asset, with no upstream bugfix path, that is worth stating in the tradeoffs.
    + INTER'S ss03 IS A NO-OP FOR AN ENGLISH PRODUCT. The recommendation sets font-feature-settings: "cv05" 1, "cv01" 1, "ss03" 1 on Inter as the way to 'make it look unlike everyone else's Inter'. I dumped Inter 4.1's ss03 lookup: it maps uni0122->uni0122.ss03, uni0136, uni013B, uni0145, uni0156 — i.e. the cedilla-vs-comma-accent forms for Ģ Ķ Ļ Ņ Ŗ (Latvian/Livonian). It touches no ASCII glyph. cv05 (l->l.ss02) and cv01 (one->one.ss01) do real work; ss03 does nothing visible in English and should be dropped or replaced with cv08/ss02 (I->I.1, the serifed capital I), which is the alternate that actually helps in a forensic/hash context.
    + GEIST MONO'S l/1 COLLISION IS NOT FIXABLE ON VERCEL'S SHIPPED FILE. The recommendation says Geist Mono is survivable 'but then you MUST set "ss03" 1'. The subset Vercel serves (v1.701, 264 glyphs) contains only ccmp/dnom/frac/locl/numr — ss03 is not in it. If the team ever pulls Geist Mono from Vercel's CDN rather than the npm package, the prescribed fix silently does nothing. Use the npm geist package (v1.700 variable has ss03) if this path is taken.
    + CHEAPEST CREDIBLE PATH TO THE STATED GOAL, GIVEN THE ABOVE. Google Sans Code survives this fact-check better than either recommended mono: genuinely OFL with no Reserved Font Name (so renaming is as available as with Commit Mono, under the same OFL obligations), actively maintained (v7.001, ahead of the v7.000 in the report), a real wght 300-800 variable axis (which Commit Mono lacks entirely), a matching Italic variable, zero ligature machinery to fight, and a slashed zero by default. It gets you the variable-axis and non-round-weight recommendation for $0 and no licence risk, which is the one place where the report's own logic points somewhere it did not go.
    + EVIDENCE ACCESS NOTE FOR ANYONE RE-CHECKING THIS. usgraphics.com returns Cloudflare HTTP 403 to curl (any UA), to WebFetch, to its Shopify-style .json/.js endpoints and to berkeleygraphics.com. Everything I verified about Berkeley Mono came from the Internet Archive snapshot of 2026-09-19 (three days old), including the catalog price list and the commercial-licence PDF at /static/legal/licenses/commercial-license.90c5ad9f125a.pdf, whose text I extracted from the PDF's compressed streams. This is a legitimate primary source but it is a snapshot — confirm prices at checkout. Separately, the researcher's note that the datasheet PDF 'had no readable licence text' is correct; the licence text lives in the two EULA PDFs, which they appear not to have found.

### AREA: Dark gradient colour system, tokens, and semantic encoding (color-system)
FINDINGS:
  - RADIX'S DARK SCALES ARE SECRETLY AN OKLCH LIGHTNESS LADDER. I parsed radix-ui/colors src/dark.ts and converted all 12 steps of 11 chromatic dark scales to OKLCH. Mean L per step across hues: s1 0.189, s2 0.211, s3 0.267, s4 0.310, s5 0.352, s6 0.400, s7 0.461, s8 0.535, s9 0.644, s10 0.683, s11 0.788, s12 0.912. Spread within steps 1-8 is tiny (step1 range 0.185-0.194; step2 0.205-0.216). Only steps 9/10 break the ladder (L 0.540 for irisDark9 vs 0.854 for amberDark9) because step 9 is 'the brand colour', not a ladder rung. IMPLEMENTATION: do not hand-pick darks — fix an L ladder and vary only chroma per hue. My PRGuard ladder: [0.150, 0.185, 0.240, 0.285, 0.330, 0.380, 0.440, 0.530, 0.620, 0.680, 0.790, 0.930].
  - I VERIFIED RADIX'S OWN APCA CLAIM. Radix docs say steps 11 and 12 are 'guaranteed to Lc 60 and Lc 90 APCA contrast on top of a step 2 background'. I implemented APCA 0.1.9 and measured grayDark11 #b4b4b4 on grayDark2 #191919 = Lc 60.6, and grayDark12 #eeeeee on #191919 = Lc 95.5. Claim holds. This is the contrast contract to copy: step 11 = Lc >= 60 (body text floor), step 12 = Lc >= 90 (headings/high-emphasis).
  - THE sRGB GAMUT PHYSICALLY FORBIDS A CLEAN SEVERITY RAMP. I mapped max in-gamut chroma per (L, hue): at hue 27 (red), max C is 0.240 at L 0.60 but collapses to 0.150 at L 0.75 and 0.080 at L 0.85. At hue 80 (amber) max C is 0.110 at L 0.55 rising to 0.165 at L 0.80. Consequence: you CANNOT build a critical-to-low ramp that is simultaneously monotone in lightness and monotone in chroma while ending on a saturated red — a saturated red is always DARKER than a saturated amber. Every red-amber-green severity scale in every competitor dashboard is non-monotone in perceived lightness and therefore unreadable in greyscale. Do not fight this; encode severity with a meter, not a hue.
  - HARD NUMBER FOR 'HOW MANY ACCENT HUES': I brute-forced every hue combination at 15-degree steps, simulated protanopia/deuteranopia/tritanopia with the Machado 2009 severity-1.0 matrices, and measured minimum pairwise OKLab separation. At a fixed L=0.80: best 3-hue set scores minDeltaE 11.7, best 4-hue 7.8, best 5-hue 6.1. With lightness free and hues constrained to (heat / ice / signal) families: 3 families score 28.7. Adding a 4th (plum) drops it to 11.8 — a 2.4x collapse. THE ANSWER IS 3 CHROMATIC FAMILIES IN PRODUCT CHROME.
  - CALIBRATION ANCHOR: the canonical Okabe-Ito 7-colour 'colourblind-safe' palette scores a minimum pairwise separation of only 7.5 on the same metric (worst pair: bluish-green #009E73 vs reddish-purple #CC79A7 = 7.5; bluish-green vs blue = 8.4). So 7.5 is the accepted industry floor. My recommended PRGuard 3-family set scores 15.6 minimum — roughly double the floor.
  - KILLER NEGATIVE RESULT: indigo/violet brand + magenta/plum status CANNOT COEXIST. signal-text oklch(0.80 0.125 274) #a5b7ff vs plum-text oklch(0.80 0.135 314) #dda3fa separates by only deltaE 1.9 under deuteranopia. They are the same colour to ~5% of male users. Any design that uses a violet brand AND a magenta 'suppressed/dismissed' badge is broken. This is why I kill plum.
  - DIFF COLOURS ARE DECORATIVE FOR CVD USERS AND ALWAYS HAVE BEEN. jade-add oklch(0.72 0.130 166) #3dbe92 vs rose-remove oklch(0.70 0.150 17) #ed737c separate by deltaE 3.6 at worst under CVD. GitHub's +/- gutter glyphs and line positioning are doing 100% of the semantic work. Keep jade/rose inside the code surface only, always with the glyph, and never let those two hues escape into UI chrome.
  - LINEAR'S REAL SURFACE LADDER, pulled from static.linear.app CSS on 2026-09-22 (not from a blog post): --color-bg-marketing #010102 = oklch(0.069 0.008 284); --color-bg-level-0 #08090a = oklch(0.139 0.003 246); level-1 #0f1011 = oklch(0.172 0.003 248); level-2 #141516 = oklch(0.195 0.003 248); level-3 #191a1b = oklch(0.217 0.003 248). Borders: --color-border-primary #23252a, secondary #34343a, tertiary #3e3e44, PLUS translucent variants #ffffff0d / #ffffff14 / #ffffff1f / #ffffff26. Text: fg-primary #f7f8f8, secondary #d0d6e0, tertiary #8a8f98, quaternary #62666d. Accent: #5e6ad2 (app) and #7170ff (marketing), hover #828fff.
  - LINEAR'S GRAIN IS A 512x512 PNG, NOT feTurbulence, AND IT COSTS 696,402 BYTES. I downloaded https://static.linear.app/web/_next/static/media/grain-default.mxaw2efG.png — 512x512, 8-bit, colortype 6 (RGBA), 680 KB. Applied as `.grain { opacity:.9; mix-blend-mode:overlay; background-size:256px 256px; position:absolute; inset:0 }`, with `.grainSubtle { opacity:.6 }` and a marketing overlay at `opacity:.25`. Note the 256px background-size on a 512px asset = deliberate 2x retina downsample. DO NOT COPY THIS — 680 KB for texture is indefensible; an inline feTurbulence data-URI is under 1 KB.
  - LINEAR'S GLOW AND EDGE RECIPES, verbatim from their CSS: glow = `radial-gradient(50% 50%, #ffffff0a 0%, #fff0 90%)` on a 400x400 circle, with `--glow-opacity: .03` / `.04`; edge-glow tint = `#ffffff08` with color-mix stops at 94%/58%/35%/16%/3% then transparent; edge-highlight ring = `1px solid #ffffff0f` (or #ffffff14 / #ffffff1f); a cursor-tracked 'shine' uses `mask-image: radial-gradient(ellipse 200px 200px at var(--mask-x) var(--mask-y), #000 0%, #0009 30%, #0003 50%, #0000 70%)`. Shadows: --shadow-low `0px 2px 4px #0000001a`, --shadow-medium `0px 3px 12px #00000017`, --shadow-high `0px 7px 32px #00000059`. Header blur: `backdrop-filter: saturate(1.8) blur(20px)`.
  - VERCEL GEIST SHIPS OKLCH WITH AN HSLA FALLBACK AND A SEPARATE ALPHA SCALE. Dark mode: --ds-background-100 #000 (pure black — Vercel is the one premium site that really does use #000), --ds-gray-100 #1a1a1a, 200 #1f1f1f, 300 #292929, 400 #2e2e2e, 500 #454545, 600 #878787, 700 #8f8f8f, 800 #7d7d7d, 900 #a0a0a0, 1000 #ededed. Crucially there is a parallel --ds-gray-alpha-* scale: 100 #ffffff0f, 200 #ffffff17, 300 #ffffff21, 400 #ffffff24, 500 #ffffff3d, 1000 #ffffffeb. Geist step semantics are explicitly documented: 100 bg, 200 hover bg, 300 active bg, 400 default border, 500 hover border, 600 active border, 700/800 high-contrast bg, 900 secondary text, 1000 primary text.
  - OTHER REAL VALUES CAPTURED 2026-09-22: Raycast --color-bg-100 #101111 / bg-200 #18191a / bg-300 #313133 / border #242728 / fg #f4f4f6 / fg-200 #c2c7ca / fg-300 #78787c, backdrop-filter blur(24px) saturate(1.5). Resend uses a custom Radix-shaped 12-step: gray-1 #141517, gray-2 #191b1e, gray-3 #212629, gray-4 #293034, gray-12 #f0f0f0. Clerk gray-950 #131316, gray-900 #212126, gray-850 #27272d, gray-800 #2f3037, borders #ffffff1f. Warp --color-background #121212, --color-surface #08090a, accent #00c2ff and #7267ff, and Warp defines greys as `oklch(L% var(--gray-chroma) hue)` with a shared chroma variable. Cursor's dark theme is WARM: --color-theme-bg #14120b = oklch(0.182 0.014 94), with all borders as alpha of #26251e. GitHub Primer's dark base black is #010409 = oklch(0.104 0.019 248). Anthropic's site accent is --swatch--accent #c6613f (clay).
  - CODERABBIT IS LITERALLY RUNNING RADIX COLORS WITH A `--color-cui-*` PREFIX. Their CSS contains --color-cui-amber-1 #16120c through -12 #ffe7b3, which are byte-identical to Radix amberDark. Their brand is orange #ff570a = oklch(0.678 0.215 39) plus mint #19d59e = oklch(0.776 0.158 166) on cream/brown (#311c15 .. #f7f2ef) with bar colours #062619, #28684a, #4d60c7, #171d37. STRATEGIC READ: their palette is warm, friendly, off-the-shelf Radix. If PRGuard ships stock Radix darks it will look like a CodeRabbit reskin. Go cold violet/cyan on a blue-tinted near-black and you are visually the opposite brand on the same page.
  - BANDING IS THE #1 REASON AMATEUR DARK GRADIENTS LOOK CHEAP, AND THE FIX IS ONE LINE. A large radial gradient across a near-black is exactly where 8-bit sRGB quantisation shows contour rings. The fix is dither at an amplitude of ONE 8-bit step: add (1.0/255.0) * noise and subtract (0.5/255.0) to preserve mean brightness. In CSS terms that is a noise layer at ~0.03-0.05 opacity with mix-blend-mode: overlay or soft-light — normal blend mode greys the gradient out. Above ~0.08 opacity it stops being a dither and reads as deliberate grain. Interleaved Gradient Noise beats white noise: fract(52.9829189 * fract(dot(uv, vec2(0.06711056, 0.00583715)))).
  - feTurbulence PARAMETERS AND THE PAINT CLIFF: the canonical CSS-Tricks recipe is `<feTurbulence type='fractalNoise' baseFrequency='0.65' numOctaves='3' stitchTiles='stitch'/>`. numOctaves above 4 is a paint-cost cliff for a difference nobody sees at UI opacity. Inlined as a data URI it is under 1 KB vs Linear's 680 KB PNG. The trap: an SVG filter is COMPUTED per-pixel rather than decoded, so a full-viewport fixed grain layer on a 3x phone is a multi-megabyte GPU texture. Bound the noise to the hero region, never `position: fixed; inset: 0` across the whole document, and never animate baseFrequency or seed per frame.
  - BACKDROP-FILTER BUDGET: backdrop-filter is heavier than filter because it must read and re-process the content behind the element, and cost scales with BOTH blurred area and radius. Stacked layers multiply: 8 layers at 1/2/4/8/16/32/64/128px over 1920x1080 can hit ~200ms/frame on mid-tier GPUs. Practical rule: stop at 24px (Linear uses blur(20px) saturate(1.8) on the header, Raycast blur(24px) saturate(1.5), Cursor blur(14px) saturate(1.45)); at most 2 concurrent glass surfaces; never animate the radius.
  - ANIMATED GRADIENT BORDERS NEED @property OR THEY SILENTLY DO NOTHING. An unregistered custom property is a string and cannot be interpolated — this is the single most common reason the effect fails. Register `@property --grad-angle { syntax: '<angle>'; inherits: false; initial-value: 0deg }` then animate it inside conic-gradient(). @property has been Baseline since Firefox 128 (2024). The browser interpolates one value and the compositor rotates a pre-painted layer, so it is cheap — but it repaints every frame, so gate it behind prefers-reduced-motion and pause when off-screen.
  - TOOLING FACTS AS OF 2026-09-22, PULLED FROM THE NPM REGISTRY API (not memory): tailwindcss latest = 4.3.3, MIT, published 2026-07-16 (v3 LTS is 3.4.19). @radix-ui/colors latest = 3.0.0, MIT, LAST PUBLISHED 2023-10-02 — nearly three years stale, so treat it as a values source to vendor, not a live dependency. culori 4.0.2, MIT. Tailwind v4 dark neutrals: neutral-950 oklch(14.5% 0 0), zinc-950 oklch(14.1% 0.005 285.823), slate-950 oklch(12.9% 0.042 264.695), gray-950 oklch(13% 0.028 261.692), stone-950 oklch(14.7% 0.004 49.25). I verified my OKLCH->hex converter reproduces Tailwind exactly: oklch(0.145 0 0) -> #0a0a0a and oklch(0.21 0.006 285.885) -> #18181b.
  - PURE #000 + PURE #FFF IS A 21:1 RATIO AND IT IS THE WRONG ANSWER. The excess contrast triggers halation — a glow/ghosting halo around light text — which affects roughly half of people with astigmatism and produces trailing afterimages when scrolling. Use a near-black (L 0.145-0.185 in OKLCH) and cap your brightest text at L ~0.93 rather than 1.0. Separately, WCAG 2.x's luminance ratio materially OVERSTATES contrast for near-black colours: 4.5:1 can be functionally unreadable on a dark background. Design to APCA Lc (60 body / 90 heading) and report WCAG only for compliance paperwork.
  - OKLCH IS SAFE TO SHIP RAW. oklch() is Baseline 'widely available', across all engines since May 2023, and color-mix() is Baseline widely available since May 2023. Relative colour syntax (`oklch(from var(--x) l c h / 0.2)`) is the one with a ragged edge — Chrome 119+/Safari 16.4+/Edge 119+ with Firefox later — so use it for authoring convenience but not for anything load-bearing without a fallback.
