Also set `"@react-three/fiber": {"expo": "$optional"}` or just accept the optional-peer noise. | WHY: Verified by running it: `npm i react@19.3.0 react-dom@19.3.0 @react-three/fiber@9.7.0 three@0.186.0` fails with ERESOLVE, 'peer react@">=19 <19.3" from @react-three/fiber@9.7.0'. React 19.3.0 shipped 2026-09-09 and Next 16.3.6 accepts it, so a fresh `create-next-app` puts you straight into the failure. The R3F v10 canaries and drei v11 alphas still pin `>=19.0 <19.3`, so there is no version you can move FORWARD to. Losing two hours of a hackathon to this is entirely avoidable. | TRADEOFF: You give up whatever shipped in React 19.3. `--legacy-peer-deps` appears to work (I installed the full stack that way) but it lets npm resolve a React the vendored reconciler was not built against — the failure mode is a silent render/commit bug under Strict Mode, which is far worse than a red install. Pin, don't bypass.
  - (high) CLAMP DPR TO [1, 1.5] ON THE HERO CANVAS AND NEVER GO ABOVE IT. `<Canvas dpr={[1, 1.5]} gl={{ antialias: false, powerPreference: 'high-performance', alpha: false }} />` plus `<PerformanceMonitor flipflops={3} onChange={({factor}) => setDpr(Number((0.75 + 0.75*factor).toFixed(2)))} onFallback={() => setWebglOff(true)} />` and `<AdaptiveDpr pixelated />`. Small inset canvases (a 500px severity radar in the dashboard) may use dpr 2. | WHY: Directly measured on two professional sites: lusion.co renders 2163x1353 for 1442 CSS px and warp.dev renders 2160x1360 for 1440 CSS px — both exactly 1.50 on a devicePixelRatio=2 display. Warp uses 2.0 only for its two small canvases (1002 and 529 CSS px). DPR 2 on a 1440px hero is 2.2x the fragment work of DPR 1.5 for a difference almost nobody can see through bloom and a grain overlay. antialias:false is correct because you are running post-processing — use <SMAA> in the effect chain instead, which is cheaper than MSAA on the main target. | TRADEOFF: Thin bright lines (the instanced code bars, the etched hash text) alias visibly at 1.5 without MSAA. Budget <SMAA /> (a few KB inside the 26 KB postprocessing delta) and keep line widths >= 2 device px. `alpha: false` means you cannot show DOM through the canvas — if the design needs that, drop it and eat the compositing cost.
  - (high) MAKE THE <h1> THE LCP ELEMENT, NOT THE CANVAS. Structure: `<section class="relative"><canvas-wrapper class="absolute inset-0 -z-10" style="background-image:url(hero-poster.avif); background-size:cover"/><h1 class="relative">…</h1></section>`. Render the canvas via a `'use client'` wrapper that does `dynamic(() => import('./ProofChamber'), { ssr: false })`, self-host the font with `next/font` (Geist, `display: 'swap'`, `adjustFontFallback` on), and crossfade the canvas from opacity 0→1 over 500ms on the renderer's first `onCreated`/first rendered frame. Add `<link rel="modulepreload">` for the 3D chunk so the download starts with the HTML, not after hydration. | WHY: The hero must paint meaningful text in well under 2.5s (and realistically under 1.2s) or Core Web Vitals and the judge's first impression both suffer. A 270 KB gz JS chunk cannot be on that path. The AVIF poster behind the canvas means there is never an empty black rectangle: the still IS the first frame of the scene, so the crossfade is invisible. In Next's App Router, `ssr: false` is only legal inside a Client Component, so the thin `'use client'` wrapper is mandatory, not stylistic. | TRADEOFF: You are shipping the hero's look twice (as a ~15-25 KB AVIF and as a shader). Keeping them in sync is manual — re-export the poster whenever you change the scene. Also, an element with a `background-image` IS an LCP candidate, so make sure the poster is small and either inlined-as-blur or served with `fetchpriority="high"`; do not let a 400 KB poster become your LCP.
  - (high) NO WEBGL BELOW 768px. Serve a static AVIF/WebP still plus a CSS radial gradient, and gate on BOTH viewport width and a coarse-pointer query: `const use3D = window.matchMedia('(min-width: 768px) and (pointer: fine)').matches && !window.matchMedia('(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)').matches`. Fallback markup: `background: #050506 radial-gradient(60% 50% at 50% 0%, oklch(0.72 0.17 230 / 0.14), transparent 70%)` with the poster on top at ~12-20 KB. | WHY: The three heroes above are all mouse-driven (scan scrub, x-ray lens, cursor parallax) — on a touch device the interaction is dead, so you would be paying 270 KB gz and a sustained GPU load for a worse experience. A 1080p JPEG poster of my test scene measured 16.7 KB; AVIF at the same perceptual quality lands around 8-14 KB. Mobile also carries the thermal cost: a continuous full-screen fragment shader is the single most reliable way to make a phone hot and drain its battery, and a hot phone throttles the rest of your demo. | TRADEOFF: You lose the wow on mobile entirely. Accept it — hackathon judges are on laptops and a projector. If you want a middle tier, serve the 8s AV1 webm loop (measured 81-120 KB) on tablets, which gives motion at a fraction of the battery cost of a live shader.
  - (high) prefers-reduced-motion MUST FREEZE THE SCENE, NOT SLOW IT. On `window.matchMedia('(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)').matches`: mount the Canvas with `frameloop="demand"`, set every animated uniform to its most legible pose (scan plane parked mid-slab, flagged bar already red, stamp already reading PROVEN), call `invalidate()` exactly once, and never call it again. Register the `change` listener so toggling the OS setting live re-enables motion without a reload. | WHY: MDN's guidance is to remove/reduce/replace motion, specifically scaling, panning and large object movements that trigger vestibular disorders — a full-viewport scan sheet sweeping across the screen is precisely that. Rendering one frame is also strictly better than a static image here: you keep the full fidelity of the 3D scene, you keep the composition, and you drop the GPU cost to zero after frame 1. Slowing the animation down is the wrong answer — it still pans. | TRADEOFF: You must be able to express the whole story in one frozen frame, which is a genuine design constraint on the concept. That constraint is worth accepting: if the hero doesn't read as a still, it won't read in the 3 seconds you have anyway. Same test applies to the poster image.
  - (high) BUILD LIVE WEBGL FOR THE HERO; USE RENDERED VIDEO ONLY FOR THE 9-STAGE PIPELINE SECTION. If you serve video anywhere, use exactly this: `<video autoplay muted loop playsinline preload="none" disablepictureinpicture disableremoteplayback poster="/hero.avif"><source src="hero.av1.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=av01.0.05M.08"><source src="hero.vp9.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=vp9"><source src="hero.h264.mp4" type="video/mp4"></video>` with a 32px-wide blur-up data URI behind it. Encode: `ffmpeg -i src.mov -c:v libsvtav1 -crf 32 -preset 6 -an out.av1.webm` and `-c:v libvpx-vp9 -crf 32 -b:v 0 -row-mt 1 -an out.vp9.webm`; MP4s get `-movflags +faststart`. Strip audio entirely. Loop at 8-10s. | WHY: A pre-rendered video of a proof scan is indistinguishable from a fake, and a judge evaluating a TRUST product will assume it is one — that is a category-specific reason live WebGL wins here that would not apply to most sites. The cost argument does not save video either: my measured encodes of an 8s 1080p30 dark loop came in at AV1 crf32 = 120.0 KB, AV1 crf40 = 81.1 KB, VP9 crf32 = 68.1 KB — cheap, but the interactive hero is only 270 KB gz over React and it responds to the cursor. Where video DOES win is the 9-stage pipeline: that is a linear narrative with no interaction, and a Blender/AE render will look better than anything you can shade in a hackathon. | TRADEOFF: H.264 on smooth dark gradients is brutal — measured 540.8 KB at crf26 and 968.3 KB at crf20 for the same 8 seconds, 4-8x the AV1/VP9 files, because dark gradients band and x264 spends bits fighting it. If you must ship an H.264 fallback, add 1-2% grain in the composite (which is also the 2026 look) so the encoder has something to hold onto, or accept visible banding. Alpha video is the other tax: cursor.com's HEVC .mov alpha asset is 2,289,920 B against 176,022 B for its VP9 .webm — 13x. Composite onto opaque #050506 and never ship the .mov.

Tokens (oklch first, hex for tooling that needs it): `--bg-base: oklch(0.116 0.004 286)` #050506; `--bg-surface: oklch(0.165 0.006 286)` #0E0E11; `--bg-raised: oklch(0.205 0.008 286)` #17171B; `--hairline: oklch(1 0 0 / 0.06)`; `--text-1: oklch(0.955 0.003 286)` #F0F0F2; `--text-2: oklch(0.665 0.012 286)` #93939B; `--text-3: oklch(0.49 0.014 286)` #5F5F68. Semantics mapped to product state, not decoration: `--scan: oklch(0.72 0.17 230)` #00B5F9 (the beam, the brand); `--proven: oklch(0.78 0.17 155)` #41D686; `--critical: oklch(0.645 0.235 26)` #FD3237; `--high: oklch(0.72 0.19 45)` #FF7527; `--medium: oklch(0.80 0.16 75)` #F9AD26; `--disproven: oklch(0.62 0.03 250)` #798898. Hero glow: `radial-gradient(60% 50% at 50% 0%, oklch(0.72 0.17 230 / 0.14), transparent 70%)`. BANNED: oklch(0.62 0.22 285) #7F67FF and every #6366F1→#A855F7 ramp. | WHY: #050506 is measurably darker than CodeRabbit's #111014 (oklch L 0.116 vs 0.176) so the two sites will not read as siblings, and it is not pure #000 like vercel.com — a true black gives bloom no floor to lift off and bands hard on OLED. A cold cyan-blue scan beam reads as INSTRUMENTATION (oscilloscope, CT scanner, spectrometer), which is exactly PRGuard's positioning, whereas indigo/violet is the 2023-2025 LLM-startup default and will make a trust product look like a wrapper. Six severity colours that map 1:1 to the M2 bands mean the marketing site and the dashboard share one token file. | TRADEOFF: #FD3237 at oklch C 0.235 is very saturated and will bloom aggressively — clamp it in the bloom's luminanceThreshold or the flagged bar will smear. Also #00B5F9 on #050506 is a 9.5:1-ish contrast for large text but check small mono labels; drop to --scan-dim #007EB3 for anything under 14px on a light surface. oklch needs a fallback hex for Safari < 15.4, which is negligible in 2026 but trivial to add.

Hero h1 at 68px / weight 520 / letter-spacing -0.022em / line-height 1.00, self-hosted via `next/font/local` with `display:'swap'`. Set all severity scores, fingerprints and hashes in `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums`. | WHY: Linear's measured h1 is 64px / weight 510 / letter-spacing -1.408px (= exactly -0.022em) / line-height 64px (1.00) — that tracking-and-leading pair is the specific thing that makes dark dev-tool type look engineered rather than generic, and it is free to copy. All three fonts are OFL — verified against rsms/inter and JetBrains/JetBrainsMono LICENSE files and the `geist` npm package's `SIL OPEN FONT LICENSE` field — so there is zero licensing risk for a commercial site. | TRADEOFF: Using Geist means matching CodeRabbit's and Vercel's font, which some will read as derivative. If you want distance, Inter Tight or Space Grotesk (both on Google Fonts, both OFL — I verified all of Anybody, Instrument Serif, JetBrains Mono, Space Grotesk, Bricolage Grotesque, Geist, Geist Mono, IBM Plex Mono, Inter Tight and Sora return HTTP 200 from fonts.googleapis.com today) are safe swaps. Do NOT reach for a paid foundry face (PP Neue Montreal, TWK Everett, Aeonik as on lusion.co) for a hackathon — those are per-domain commercial licences.
  - (high) SKIP WEBGPU AND TSL ENTIRELY FOR THIS PROJECT. Build on WebGLRenderer + pmndrs/postprocessing@6.39.5. Pin three at 0.186.0. | WHY: Measured: `three/webgpu` + `three/tsl` bundles to 244.9 KB gz versus 132.1 KB gz for the classic WebGL import set — 1.85x — and `RenderPipeline` alone is 88.1 KB gz. caniuse shows WebGPU at 87.35% global with Firefox still disabled-by-default through v159, so you would have to ship BOTH renderer paths (~377 KB gz combined) or serve a blank canvas to ~12.6% of visitors. Meanwhile postprocessing 6.39.5's peer range is `three >= 0.168.0 < 0.187.0`, so three@0.186.0 is the newest version that keeps the entire mature effect library available — including the OutlineEffect and SelectiveBloomEffect your concepts depend on, which have no TSL equivalent yet. | TRADEOFF: You are building on a stack three.js has formally begun deprecating (PostProcessing→RenderPipeline since r183, EffectComposer described as legacy WebGL-only with no forward path). For a marketing site with a 12-month useful life that is irrelevant; for the dashboard's long-lived visualisations it means a rewrite in 2027. Accept it and isolate all post-processing behind one `<Effects/>` component so the swap is contained.
  - (high) USE @paper-design/shaders-react FOR SECONDARY SECTIONS ONLY, PINNED EXACTLY. `"@paper-design/shaders-react": "0.0.81"` — exact, no caret. Good uses: a `Dithering` or `GrainGradient` texture behind the pricing/footer band, `GodRays` behind the 9-stage pipeline diagram, `PaperTexture` as a 3% overlay on the whole page to kill gradient banding. Bad use: the hero. | WHY: Measured: a single deep-imported effect costs 12.6 KB gz and three cost 30.7 KB gz over React, versus 270 KB for the R3F stack — for a decorative background that is the correct trade by a factor of 20, and you get it without a second renderer on the page. Apache-2.0 verified against the repo LICENSE file, zero dependencies, 30 effects. The exact pin is not optional: the README says 'we will ship breaking changes under 0.0.x versioning' and the package has been publishing weekly (0.0.81 on 2026-09-17). | TRADEOFF: Paper's `MeshGradient` in particular is now everywhere — it is the default 2026 Framer/Webflow background and a judge who has browsed any design-tool site this month has seen it. Reach for the less-used ones (Dithering, PaperTexture, GodRays, ColorPanels) and desaturate hard: run them at 4-8% opacity over #050506, never as the visible subject. Also note it is canvas-per-component, so five Paper shaders on one page means five WebGL contexts — browsers cap around 16 and start evicting.
  - (medium) PAUSE THE RENDER LOOP AGGRESSIVELY: `IntersectionObserver` on the canvas wrapper → `gl.setAnimationLoop(null)` when the hero scrolls out and restore on re-entry; `document.addEventListener('visibilitychange')` → stop on hidden; and cap the hero to 30fps with a manual accumulator once `PerformanceMonitor`'s factor drops below ~0.4 rather than cutting DPR further. | WHY: A full-viewport fragment-shader hero that keeps running while the user reads your pricing section is pure waste — it is the main cause of the 'this site made my laptop fans spin' reaction, and on a judge's MacBook during a demo it will throttle everything else you are about to show. rAF already stops in background tabs in modern browsers, but it does NOT stop when the canvas is merely scrolled off-screen, which is where 90% of the waste is. R3F's `frameloop="demand"` does not help a continuously-animating scene, so do it manually. | TRADEOFF: Stopping and restarting the loop can produce a visible time jump if your uniforms are driven by `clock.getElapsedTime()`; drive them from an accumulated delta you only advance while visible. 30fps is genuinely acceptable for a slow cinematic sweep but NOT for the cursor-tracked x-ray lens — cap the scene animation, not the cursor response. Battery-based throttling via `navigator.getBattery()` is Chromium-only (no Safari, no Firefox) so treat it as a bonus, never as the primary gate.
  - (high) RECORD A 12-SECOND SCREEN CAPTURE OF THE FINISHED LIVE HERO AND THE PROOF RUN, AND HAVE IT ON THE MACHINE BEFORE YOU PRESENT. Encode it AV1 + H.264, keep it local, and put a keyboard shortcut on it. | WHY: The concepts above depend on a live WebGL context, a running FastAPI backend, a sandboxed container executing a repro test, and conference wifi. At least one of those fails at every hackathon. A 12-second capture of the money shot — 'Tool A says this MIGHT null-deref. We say it DOES — here's the failing test, the stack trace, and the fix' — costs 20 minutes and protects the single most valuable asset you have. Measured: an 8s 1080p30 AV1 clip is 81-120 KB, so it is free to keep everywhere. | TRADEOFF: None, other than the 20 minutes. Do not be tempted to SHIP the recording as the hero — see the live-WebGL recommendation above; this is insurance, not the product.
ANTI-PATTERNS / AVOID:
  - The generic glowing blob / animated mesh gradient. `<MeshGradient>` from Paper Shaders, ShaderGradient, and every Framer template now produce the same soft purple-teal smear. It was distinctive in 2024. In September 2026 it is the visual equivalent of a stock photo, and a judge who has opened any design tool's site this month has seen it three times today.
  - The indigo→violet ramp on black — #6366F1 → #A855F7, oklch(0.62 0.22 285). This is the uniform of every AI wrapper from 2023-2025. For a product whose entire thesis is 'everyone else gives opinions, we give PROOF', wearing the costume of the opinion-havers is a strategic error, not just an aesthetic one.
  - Vanta.js. Latest publish 0.5.24, 2022-09-16 — four years stale. It predates three.js colour management, WebGL2 defaults and the modern renderer API. It is an instant, unambiguous signal that the site was assembled from a 2021 tutorial.
  - Raw Spline embeds. 479 KB gzipped for the standalone WebGPU runtime (1,698 KB minified), no LICENSE file and no `license` field in package.json, and — worst — Spline scenes have a recognisable house look. Shipping one tells a judge you outsourced the 3D to a template.
  - Floating geometric primitives with OrbitControls and a bloom pass. Torus knots, icosahedra, rotating cubes. This is the 'hello world' of three.js and it reads as one.
  - Particle spheres, starfields, and 10,000-point 'neural networks'. They signify nothing about your product, they cost real GPU, and they have been the default 'AI company' hero since 2016.
  - A canvas at full devicePixelRatio. On a 4K display at DPR 2 a full-viewport canvas is 33.2 megapixels of fragment work per frame. Both lusion.co and warp.dev — measured — clamp to exactly 1.5. If you ship DPR 2+ you are doing 2.2x the work of the best studio in the world for a difference invisible through bloom and grain.
  - A 3-second cinematic intro before anything is interactive. Judges scroll in two. Any hero whose payoff arrives after the user has left is a hero that does not exist. Make the FIRST frame already say the thing.
  - Text that scrambles/decodes character-by-character on load. It is everywhere, it delays your LCP if the h1 is the scrambler, and screen readers announce the garbage.
  - Global ASCII or dither post-processing across the whole hero. The effect is genuinely having a moment (reported ~900% YoY search growth) which is exactly why it will read as 2026-vintage by mid-2027, and it annihilates headline legibility. Use it as a masked lens over a limited region, or not at all.
  - Glassmorphism cards — `backdrop-filter: blur(20px)` — floated over a live shader. Each card forces a full-region blur composite every frame on top of your render loop, and the look itself peaked in 2021.
  - WebGPU-only with no WebGL2 fallback. caniuse: 87.35% global, Firefox disabled-by-default through v159. You would hand roughly one in eight visitors a blank rectangle, and pay 244.9 KB gz (vs 132.1 KB for WebGL) for the privilege.
  - Autoplaying a multi-megabyte hero video with no poster and no `preload="none"`. Also: shipping an HEVC+alpha .mov when you don't need alpha — cursor.com's is 2.29 MB against 172 KB for the same asset in VP9 WebM.
  - H.264 at CRF 20-24 for a dark gradient loop. Measured 968.3 KB at crf20 and 540.8 KB at crf26 for 8 seconds of 1080p30 that AV1 encodes at 81-120 KB — and it still bands. If you must ship H.264, add grain so the encoder has structure to hold.
  - Stacking a custom cursor + magnetic buttons + scroll-jacking + a page-transition curtain. Individually fine; together they read as an agency showreel, not a tool an engineering team will trust with its merge gate. PRGuard sells restraint and evidence — the motion design must sell the same thing.
  - Building on three/examples `EffectComposer` for a new project. three.js deprecated `PostProcessing` in favour of `RenderPipeline` at r183 and the old EffectComposer is described as legacy WebGLRenderer-only with no forward path. Use pmndrs/postprocessing instead (which has its own future problem, but a much later one).
  - Using `--legacy-peer-deps` or `--force` to 'fix' the React 19.3 / R3F peer conflict. It installs, and then you are running a vendored reconciler against a React it was not built for — the failure surfaces as intermittent commit-phase bugs under Strict Mode at 2am the night before judging. Pin react to 19.2.0.
  - Letting the hero render loop keep running while the user is reading the pricing section. rAF pauses in background TABS, not for off-screen canvases. This is the single biggest cause of the 'your site made my fans spin' reaction — during a live demo it throttles everything else you are about to show.
  - Using pure #000000 as the page background (as vercel.com does). Bloom has no floor to lift off, so highlights look pasted on rather than emitted, and dark gradients band visibly on OLED. #050506 / oklch(0.116 0.004 286) is a better black.
  - Copying CodeRabbit's aesthetic to compete with CodeRabbit. They are at #111014 with Geist at 64px/500 — arriving at #111014 with Geist at 64px/500 makes you the cheaper version of the $1.5B company. Go darker, go colder, and use a cyan instrument palette they do not own.
  - Paid-foundry display faces (Aeonik, PP Neue Montreal, TWK Everett) grabbed for a hackathon. These are per-domain commercial licences and lusion.co's Aeonik is a licensed asset, not a free one. Geist, Inter, Inter Tight, Space Grotesk, JetBrains Mono, IBM Plex Mono and Anybody are all OFL and all available today.
FACT-CHECK CORRECTIONS:
    [UNVERIFIABLE] All bundle sizes are esbuild+gzip -9; Brotli would be ~12-18% smaller; gzip numbers are an upper bound -> Cannot reproduce their local esbuild measurements. The methodology caveat is sound and the 12-18% Brotli-vs-gzip figure is in the normal published range for minified JS. Treat as stated: relative deltas reliable, absolute KB indicative.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] Video encode numbers come from synthetic ffmpeg content, not a real Blender render; direction reliable, absolute KB indicative -> Local encodes, not reproducible here. The self-caveat is appropriate and appears honest.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] VP9 crf32 beating AV1 crf32 on the gradient test is an artefact; do not generalise -> Local measurement. The refusal to generalise is the correct call.
    [CORRECTED] Unicorn Studio 'Legend' $14/mo annual ($168/yr) / $20/mo monthly; free tier: 10 published scenes, no commercial use, no code export, no 3D models, no custom fonts -> Pricing confirmed ($168/year or $20 billed monthly; $168/12 = $14/mo). Free-tier restrictions confirmed: 'Personal and non-commercial use', 'Standard assets' only vs Legend's 'Custom 3D models and fonts'. ONE CORRECTION: the free tier allows 'Up to 8 publishes with Unicorn branding', not 10 published scenes. Also 'no code export' is more precisely 'no JSON export' (free gets image exports and limited video exports; Legend adds JSON export, high-quality video exports and hosting). Legend also includes 200 Unicorn AI credits/month, which they omitted. Their 'exports WebM/MP4 with codec control' claim is not supported by the pricing page — it only distinguishes 'limited' from 'high-quality' video exports.
    [REFUTED] Spline pricing: Free / Starter $12 annual, $15 monthly / Professional $20 annual, $25 monthly / AI add-on $5 for 2,000 credits -> The plan names and most of the numbers are wrong. Live pricing is: Free $0; Hobby $12/seat/mo annual, $15 monthly (2,000 credits); Pro $25/seat/mo annual, $30 monthly (3,000 credits); Max $60/seat/mo annual, $70 monthly (10,000 credits); Enterprise custom. There is no 'Starter' or 'Professional' tier. The second tier is $25 annual, not $20. AI credits are bundled into each tier, not sold as a $5/2,000-credit add-on — the add-ons are priced from +$48/year per 1,000 extra credits. The $12/$15 figure for the entry paid tier is the only part that survives. NOTE their license claim IS solid and I confirmed it independently: @splinetool/runtime@2.0.55 (2026-09-18) has no `license` field in package.json on the registry, so proprietary-by-default stands. @splinetool/react-spline@4.1.0 / 2025-07-15 / peer next >=14.2.0 also confirmed.
    [CORRECTED] OGL's license is `Unlicense` per npm; could not fetch LICENSE from oframe/ogl on `master` (404) — the default branch may be named differently -> The npm field is confirmed (ogl@1.0.11, `license: "Unlicense"`). But their diagnosis of the 404 is wrong: `master` IS the correct branch — raw.githubusercontent.com/oframe/ogl/master/package.json returns 200 while /main/ returns 404. The real finding is that NO LICENSE FILE EXISTS: LICENSE, LICENSE.md, LICENSE.txt, LICENCE, license and UNLICENSE all 404 on master, and I extracted the npm tarball and found no licence file in it either. The package.json `license: "Unlicense"` string is the sole licence declaration anywhere. Their instinct to not rely on the public-domain claim is therefore MORE justified than they realised, not less.
    [UNVERIFIABLE] '~900% YoY growth in dither-effect searches in 2026' from a secondary blog -> No primary analytics source located (web search budget exhausted before this could be chased). Their own 'treat as directional' framing is the right one — do not put this number in a deck or a rationale document.
    [CORRECTED] Awwwards attributions (Hubtown/Unseen Studio SOTD June 2026; Lusion EverSwap; makemepulse Apechain; Noomo; OFF+BRAND steven.com; Vide Infra Springs) from a search summary, not awwwards.com -> Partially verified. Hubtown / Unseen Studio / Awwwards SOTD June 2026 is CONFIRMED by the Utsubo article itself. Unseen Studio and OFF+BRAND. are both confirmed real, currently-active Awwwards SOTD winners on awwwards.com's live listing (Unseen Studio for 'Illoca', 04 Sep 2026; OFF+BRAND. for 'Trevor Noah', 03 Sep 2026 — note that is Trevor Noah, not steven.com). The remaining attributions (Lusion EverSwap, makemepulse Apechain, Noomo, Vide Infra Springs) could not be found on awwwards.com and remain unverified. Also, the Utsubo article gives awards they did not report: Oryzo is Awwwards Site of the MONTH (April 2026), not SOTD, and IVRESS is FWA Site of the Month (May 2026).
    [CORRECTED] CodeRabbit ships 1,191 KB of JS; Vercel 1,032 KB (same method, much of it third-party tracking) -> Vercel is exact — I measured 1,032 KB of script transferSize at 1440x900, matching their figure to the KB. CodeRabbit is NOT reproducible: I measured 950 KB with the same method, vs their 1,191 KB. That is a 20% swing, which is exactly what their own caveat predicts (third-party tag load varies by consent state, geography and timing). Do not cite 1,191 KB as a fact; cite 'roughly 1 MB, mostly third-party' instead. Their like-for-like disclaimer should be kept prominently.
    [CORRECTED] Frame-time characterisations ('MeshTransmissionMaterial costs an extra render pass per mesh', 'pixelation effects are one texture lookup') are from docs and Maxime Heckel's writeups, not profiling -> The self-caveat understates the problem: the cited source does not appear to contain these statements. Maxime Heckel's 'Post-Processing Shaders as a Creative Medium' (published 2025-02-04) does exist and does cover pixelation, LED/sub-pixel masks, fluted glass and progressive depixelation — but it does NOT state that pixelation costs one texture lookup or that fluted glass costs 2-3 samples, and it does NOT describe extending pmndrs/postprocessing's `Effect` class (it works through R3F and raw GLSL fragment shaders). These are the researcher's own inferences attributed to a named source. The MeshTransmissionMaterial extra-render-pass claim is uncontroversial and consistent with how transmission is implemented, but is likewise unprofiled. Treat all frame-time numbers as unsourced.
    [CORRECTED] postprocessing 6.39.5 is zlib-licensed, peers `three >= 0.168.0 < 0.187.0`, and exports the listed effect catalogue including OutlineEffect and SelectiveBloomEffect -> Licence, peer range and 21 of 22 named exports confirmed from the extracted tarball. peerDependencies is exactly `three: ">= 0.168.0 < 0.187.0"`, so three@0.186.0 really is the newest compatible release — the linchpin of the skip-WebGPU recommendation holds. LICENSE.md reads 'Copyright © 2015 Raoul van Rüschen / This software is provided 'as-is', without any express or implied warranty' (zlib). Confirmed present in build/types/index.d.ts: ASCIIEffect, OutlineEffect, SelectiveBloomEffect, BloomEffect, MipmapBlurPass, ChromaticAberrationEffect, ScanlineEffect, GridEffect, DotScreenEffect, PixelationEffect, GodRaysEffect, LensDistortionEffect, TiltShiftEffect, NoiseEffect, VignetteEffect, DepthOfFieldEffect, SMAAEffect, FXAAEffect, ShockWaveEffect, LUT3DEffect, ToneMappingEffect. ONE CORRECTION: `TiltShiftBlurPass` does not exist. The blur passes are BoxBlurPass, GaussianBlurPass, KawaseBlurPass and MipmapBlurPass; tilt-shift ships only as TiltShiftEffect.
    [CORRECTED] @paper-design/shaders-react@0.0.81 (2026-09-17), Apache-2.0, ZERO DEPENDENCIES, 30 effects, README says pin your dependency -> Version, date, licence, effect list and README quote all confirmed; 'zero dependencies' is wrong. The package declares one dependency: `@paper-design/shaders` (also 0.0.81, also Apache-2.0, and that core package genuinely has zero deps). Apache-2.0 confirmed against the repo LICENSE file. README quote confirmed near-verbatim: 'Please pin your dependency – we will ship breaking changes under 0.0.x versioning'. The shaders.paper.design site lists exactly the 30 effects they enumerated, names matching. The exact-pin advice is well-founded.
    [CORRECTED] #00B5F9 on #050506 is a 9.5:1-ish contrast for large text -> Actual WCAG contrast ratio is 8.71:1, not ~9.5:1. Still comfortably above AA for both large and normal text, so the recommendation is unaffected. Other computed ratios on #050506: #41D686 = 10.85:1, #FD3237 = 5.52:1, #F0F0F2 = 17.90:1. Their fallback --scan-dim #007EB3 gives 4.50:1 on #050506 — right at the AA threshold with no margin, and note their stated use case ('under 14px on a light surface') doesn't match a dark background at all; on a light surface the math is completely different and #007EB3 would need re-checking.
    [CORRECTED] Utsubo 'Best Three.js Websites 2026' names Oryzo (Lusion), IVRESS (Utsubo, WebGPU+TSL), Hubtown (Unseen Studio, Awwwards SOTD June 2026), Cartier (Immersive Garden), Explore Primland; conclusion quoted as 'The standout sites pick one hard idea and execute it cleanly rather than stacking effects.' -> Article exists and every site/studio/technique attribution is correct, including the Hubtown Awwwards SOTD June 2026 credit. But the 'conclusion' is presented as a quotation and is not one. The article actually says: 'the best Three.js sites of 2026 commit to one hard idea and budget everything around it.' Same meaning, different words — fix it before anyone quotes it in a deck. The article also supplies awards they omitted: Oryzo = Awwwards Site of the Month April 2026, IVRESS = FWA Site of the Month May 2026, Explore Primland = Awwwards SOTD February 2026.
    [CORRECTED] Maxime Heckel volumetric raymarching post, derived from Inigo Quilez's 'Clouds', al-ro's 'Starry Night' and Suyoku; companion posts 'On Shaping Light' and 'On Rendering the Sky' -> Post exists at the cited URL, titled 'Real-time dreamy Cloudscapes with Volumetric Raymarching', and credits all three sources verbatim: 'Clouds' by Inigo Quilez, 'Starry Night' by al-ro, and the 'Volumetric Raymarching sample' by Suyoku. Two corrections: (a) it is dated 31 October 2023, not a 2026 source — nearly three years old and predating the current three.js renderer story; (b) it does not reference companion posts titled 'On Shaping Light' or 'On Rendering the Sky' (it links 'The Study of Shaders with React Three Fiber' and 'Painting with Math: A Gentle Study of Raymarching' instead). The half-res-into-RGBA16F post-process advice is the researcher's own synthesis, not a quote from this page.
VERIFIER ADDITIONS:
    + GAMUT BUG IN THE BRAND COLOUR — the most important thing missed. Their oklch→hex maths is flawless, but three tokens are OUTSIDE the sRGB gamut and are silently clipped on conversion. `--scan: oklch(0.72 0.17 230)` computes to linear RGB (-0.0600, 0.4637, 0.9504) — the red channel is NEGATIVE and gets clamped to 0 to produce #00B5F9. `--high: oklch(0.72 0.19 45)` computes to (1.0190, 0.1776, 0.0201) — red exceeds 1.0 and is clamped. (The banned violet #7F67FF is also out of gamut, which is moot.) Consequence: on a P3/wide-gamut display — i.e. every MacBook, i.e. exactly the machine the hackathon demo runs on — a browser rendering the oklch() value natively will paint a MORE saturated colour than the hex fallback, so `--scan` and #00B5F9 will visibly disagree between the oklch path and the hex path. Their tradeoff note ('oklch needs a fallback hex for Safari < 15.4') treats hex as a lossless fallback; it is not, for the brand colour. Fix by either dropping chroma to ~0.145 at L 0.72 / H 230 to bring it inside sRGB, or by declaring the hex as the source of truth and generating oklch from it rather than the reverse.
    + LUSION USES THE SAME TWO-TIER DPR RULE AS WARP, which strengthens their recommendation more than they claim. They attributed the '2.0 for small canvases' pattern only to Warp. In fact lusion.co also runs a small 44.8 CSS-px canvas at ratio 2.009 alongside its 1.50 hero. So BOTH reference sites independently implement exactly the policy being recommended — 1.5 on the big hero, 2.0 on small insets. That is two-for-two, not one-for-two.
    + THE DPR=1.5 COINCIDENCE CONCERN IS FULLY RESOLVED AND CAN BE DROPPED FROM THE RISK LIST. Reloading warp.dev at a 1024 px viewport produced a 1536 px backing store (exactly 1.5x), versus 2160 px at 1440 px (exactly 1.5x). The backing store tracks the element size, so it is a DPR clamp by construction. No source reading required.
    + OGL IS ALSO STALE, WHICH THEY DID NOT FLAG. ogl@1.0.11 was published 2025-01-27 — roughly 20 months old as of today. They correctly flagged Vanta's staleness as an instant tell but gave OGL a pass on the same axis while only questioning its licence. If OGL is under consideration as a lightweight three.js alternative, the combination of (a) no LICENSE file anywhere and (b) 20 months without a release is a stronger argument against it than the licence question alone.
    + THE `overrides` BLOCK AS WRITTEN WILL NOT SILENCE THE EXPO NOISE, AND MAY NOT BE NEEDED AT ALL. My ERESOLVE repro shows npm pulling expo@57.0.24, @expo/metro-runtime, expo-asset, expo-file-system and expo-gl into the resolution graph purely through R3F's peerOptional declarations. Their suggested `"@react-three/fiber": {"expo": "$optional"}` syntax is not valid npm `overrides` syntax (that is a pnpm `peerDependencyRules` concept). On npm, unmet OPTIONAL peers do not block installation — only the react peer does. So pinning react/react-dom to 19.2.0 in `dependencies` is sufficient on its own; the `overrides` block is belt-and-braces against transitive pulls, and the expo line should simply be deleted rather than debugged.
    + `TiltShiftBlurPass` DOES NOT EXIST in postprocessing 6.39.5 — if it appears in any implementation checklist it will fail at import time. The real class is `TiltShiftEffect`; the blur passes are `BoxBlurPass`, `GaussianBlurPass`, `KawaseBlurPass` and `MipmapBlurPass`. Everything else in their 22-name catalogue is present and correct.
    + DO NOT SHIP THE CODERABBIT '1,191 KB' NUMBER. I measured 950 KB with an identical method — a 20% swing driven entirely by third-party tag loading, which varies by consent state and geography. The Vercel figure (1,032 KB) reproduced exactly, so the asymmetry is specific to CodeRabbit's tracking stack. Any competitive claim built on the CodeRabbit number is fragile; the Vercel one is solid.
    + SEVERAL PERFORMANCE CLAIMS ARE ATTRIBUTED TO A SOURCE THAT DOES NOT CONTAIN THEM. The Maxime Heckel post-processing article does not state 'pixelation is one texture lookup' or 'fluted glass is 2-3 samples', and does not describe extending pmndrs/postprocessing's Effect class. These are the researcher's own reasonable inferences wearing a citation. They are probably true, but if any of them ends up in a written rationale as 'per Heckel', that is a misattribution waiting to be caught.
    + THE PROOF CHAMBER'S BIGGEST UNPRICED RISK IS MeshTransmissionMaterial COUNT, AND THEIR OWN MITIGATION IS THE RIGHT ONE BUT UNDERSTATED. Concept #1 uses MeshTransmissionMaterial on two slabs at samples=4/resolution=256 AND SelectiveBloom AND Outline AND ChromaticAberration AND Noise, while Concept #2 adds another transmissive hexagon. Since no scene was profiled (their own admission), the '<6 draw calls' budget is the only quantified figure and draw calls are not the binding constraint here — transmission render passes and full-screen post-process fill rate are. At DPR 1.5 on a 1440px hero that is roughly 2160x1350 fragments per post pass, times four effects, on top of two transmission buffer renders. Recommend prototyping the transmission slabs FIRST as a go/no-go before committing to Concept #1, since it ranks #1 on a feasibility score that was never measured.
    + WEBGL CONTEXT EVICTION IS SILENT, WHICH MAKES THE PAPER-SHADERS WARNING SHARPER THAN STATED. I created 40 contexts and the browser kept exactly 16, discarding the rest with no exception thrown — `getContext()` kept returning objects. A page that mounts five Paper shader components plus an R3F hero plus any third-party embed will not error; it will just start losing canvases to blank rectangles, most likely the hero, because it was created first. If Paper shaders are used for secondary sections as recommended, add a `webglcontextlost` listener on the hero canvas as a diagnostic.
    + NEXT 16.3.6 PUBLISHED TODAY (2026-09-22), the same day the research was done. Of everything in the version table it is the single most likely value to be stale by the time anyone acts on this; three@0.186.0 is the one that matters most for correctness, and it is locked in place anyway by postprocessing's `< 0.187.0` peer ceiling.
    + SPLINE PRICING IS THE WEAKEST FACTUAL SECTION AND SHOULD BE REWRITTEN OR DELETED. The tier names ('Starter', 'Professional') do not exist, the second-tier annual price is off by 25%, and the AI credit model is mischaracterised. Since the recommendation is to NOT use Spline anyway, and the genuinely decisive argument (no licence file in the runtime tarball, ~479 KB gzipped) is verified and strong, the safest move is to drop the pricing table entirely rather than correct it.

### AREA: Animated architecture and pipeline diagrams on landing pages (diagrams)
FINDINGS:
  - THE SINGLE BEST TRICK, AND ALMOST NOBODY USES IT PROPERLY: put `pathLength="1"` on every edge `<path>`. MDN confirms the browser then scales ALL dash math by `pathLength / computed length`, so `stroke-dasharray="1"` + `stroke-dashoffset: 1 -> 0` draws any path over a normalized 0..1 regardless of whether that path is 80px or 2400px long. Consequence: all 15 edges in a 9-stage diagram draw at identical perceived speed with ONE shared keyframe, and the exact same number can be driven by scroll progress or by `stage_index/stage_total`. It applies to path, line, circle, ellipse, rect, polyline, polygon.
  - SAME TRICK, SECOND USE — TRAVELLING PACKETS WITH ZERO MATH: with `pathLength="1"`, set `stroke-dasharray="0.06 0.94"` and animate `stroke-dashoffset` 1 -> 0 on a duplicate overlay path. You get a 6%-long light segment that travels the full curve, correctly oriented, on any geometry, with one animated property. This beats `offset-path` (needs a DOM element per packet) and beats Magic UI's beam (see below) for curved/vertical routes.
  - MAGIC UI'S AnimatedBeam IS BROKEN FOR VERTICAL PIPELINES — I read the actual registry source (apps/www/public/r/animated-beam.json). Its `gradientCoordinates` animate ONLY x1 (`10%`->`110%`) and x2 (`0%`->`100%`), with `y1` and `y2` hard-pinned to `"0%"`. Under `gradientUnits="userSpaceOnUse"` that means the light WIPES HORIZONTALLY ACROSS THE BOUNDING BOX, it does not travel along the path. On a top-to-bottom 9-stage spine the effect is visually wrong. It also emits one full-container absolutely-positioned `<svg>` PER BEAM (12 beams = 12 stacked full-size SVGs), rebuilds `d` via `getBoundingClientRect()` on every ResizeObserver tick, and uses `ease: [0.16, 1, 0.3, 1]` (easeOutExpo) so the packet decelerates — wrong for a conveyor. Defaults: curvature 0, duration 5, pathColor "gray", pathWidth 2, pathOpacity 0.2, gradientStartColor #ffaa40, gradientStopColor #9c40ff, repeat Infinity.
  - CLOUDFLARE.COM SHIPS xyflow ON ITS MARKETING HOMEPAGE — BUT ONLY THE BACKGROUND. The live HTML contains 10 elements carrying `data-testid="rf__background"` (xyflow's own test id) rendering `<pattern width="12" height="12" patternUnits="userSpaceOnUse"><circle cx="6" cy="6" r="0.75">`. There are ZERO `react-flow__node` / `react-flow__edge` classes anywhere on the page. The actual connective tissue is hand-authored: `<line x1="0" y1="0.5" x2="100%" y2="0.5" stroke="var(--color-border-100)" stroke-width="1" stroke-dasharray="16,16">` plus `linear-gradient` 1px rails. Read that as the verdict from a company with unlimited budget: node-graph libraries are for the app, hand-SVG is for the landing page.
  - CODERABBIT'S HOMEPAGE, DECOMPILED (this is the thing you must beat): a canvas force-directed code graph declaring `data-trusted-context-node-count="336"` and `data-trusted-context-link-count="275"`; a 5-tab 'Trusted Context' carousel (`codegraph`, `business-context`, `ensemble-of-models`, `adaptive-systems`, `review-generation`) with `data-trusted-context-animation-state="paused"`; `HeroFeatureCards` with `cardProgressTrack`/`cardProgressFill`/`playbackButton` (autoplaying tabs with a progress bar); and `FastCodeChaos.gridShimmerCanvas` with a `grid-shimmer-fallback.svg` static fallback. Their dark base is `oklch(0.177 0.0084 305)` = #121014 (purple-tinted black); accents #65e6a6 mint, #F04006 orange, #f87171 red, #facc15 yellow; text #f4f1f5 / #aaa3ae / #85808c.
  - INNGEST'S WEBSITE IS OPEN SOURCE (github.com/inngest/website) AND IS THE BEST REFERENCE IMPLEMENTATION OF A CONNECTED-DIAGRAM ON A DARK MARKETING PAGE. `useFeatureNavigatePaths.ts` measures anchors (`.code-block-anchor`, `.terminal-anchor`, `.devserver-anchor`, `.deploy-anchor`, `.faulttolerance-anchor`, `.observability-anchor`) with `getBoundingClientRect()`, builds `d` strings, then creates a DETACHED `document.createElementNS(svgNS,'path')`, calls `.getTotalLength()` and `.getPointAtLength(len * t)` to place a glow dot at a fraction along the route. No library. `LineWithGlow.tsx` then draws it with `motion.path` `initial={{pathLength:0}} animate={{pathLength:1}} transition={{duration:0.5}}`, `strokeWidth={1}`, `vectorEffect="non-scaling-stroke"`, stroke = `linearGradient` #CBB26A -> #57534E.
  - INNGEST'S MOBILE ANSWER IS 'SHIP THREE DIFFERENT PATH SETS, NOT ONE RESPONSIVE PATH'. `LineWithGlow` takes a `visibilityClass` prop with literal values `"hidden xl:block"` (desktop), `"hidden md:block xl:hidden"` (medium), `"block sm:hidden"` (mobile), and there is a whole separate `useFeatureNavigatePathsMedium.ts`. Each breakpoint gets its own geometry. Do not try to make one bezier survive 1440px -> 375px.
  - INNGEST'S CANVAS PARTICLE ENGINE (`lib/animations/core/Engine.ts`) IS THE CORRECT SHAPE FOR A rAF HERO: handles DPR/HiDPI, `ResizeObserver` for size, `IntersectionObserver` with `pauseOffscreen` so `elapsedMs` accumulates ONLY while visible ("scenes opting into pauseOffscreen keep their state frozen during off-screen scroll instead of silently advancing the wall clock"), a per-scene `timeScale`, passive `mousemove`/`scroll` listeners, and an idempotent `destroy()`. Hero timing knobs are documented as `scatterMs 900 / convergeMs 2200`. Critically, the canvas hero is gated `hidden lg:block` — it never renders on phones.
  - INNGEST'S `formations/svg.ts` IS HOW YOU MAKE PARTICLES FORM AN ARBITRARY SHAPE: fetch the SVG text -> `new Blob([svgText], {type:'image/svg+xml'})` -> object URL -> `<img>` (blob-backed so `getImageData` stays untainted) -> draw to an offscreen canvas -> `getImageData()` -> sample pixel positions with probability proportional to alpha, `step = 2`, `target = count * 3` candidates, then `pickN`. Wrapped in try/catch returning `null` on taint so the caller can fall back. This is how you'd make particles converge into the PRGuard wordmark or into the pipeline shape.
  - LANGCHAIN.COM IS A LIVE, READABLE GSAP SCROLL-SCRUB IMPLEMENTATION WITH THE COMMENTS LEFT IN. It loads `https://cdn.prod.website-files.com/gsap/3.15.0/{gsap,ScrollTrigger,SplitText}.min.js`. Its scroll config is literally `const scrollConfig = (el, scrub = 0.3) => ({ trigger: el, start: "top bottom-=5%", end: "center center-=5%", scrub })` and the hero uses `scrub: 1` with the comment "increase scrub from 0.3 -> 1 for more lag/smoothing". Letter animation: `opacity 0.3 -> 1`, color #7fc8ff -> #ffffff -> #7fc8ff, `textShadow: "0 0 12px rgba(127, 200, 255, 0.6)"`, `durations: [0.8, 0.8]`, `stagger: 0.15`.
  - STEAL LANGCHAIN'S 'FALLBACK HANDSHAKE' VERBATIM — IT IS THE #1 THING THAT KILLS SCROLL-SCRUBBED DIAGRAMS IN A DEMO. Their source comment: the `<head>` CSS pre-hides every animated element AND arms a 2-second 'reveal anyway' fallback; the JS disarms that fallback by adding class `hero-js` to `<html>` ONLY after confirming `window.gsap && window.ScrollTrigger` actually loaded. If either is missing the script bails without adding the class, the CSS fires at 2s, and the diagram appears in its final static position. Their words: "Degraded, but never invisible." They also note the anti-flicker rule: reveal with a synchronous `gsap.set(visibility:'visible')` in the SAME JS task that places the element at its start position, so no frame can paint between.
  - THE SCRUBBED-TIMELINE MENTAL MODEL, FROM LANGCHAIN'S OWN COMMENT: "the scrubbed timeline's total length is normalised onto the scroll range, so duration/position = SHARE of the scroll. Total length here is 1, so 0.4 = 40%." For a 9-stage pipeline build the GSAP timeline with total length exactly 1 and give each stage 1/9 = 0.1111 — then stage index and scroll progress are the same number, which is what lets the dashboard reuse the component.

The ONLY prohibition that could matter: you may not use it to build "tools that allow users to build visual animations without code" that compete with Webflow, and you may not strip proprietary notices. PRGuard is a code reviewer — zero exposure. Also stated: AI-generated GSAP code is explicitly not a Prohibited Use.
  - REACT FLOW'S REAL COST IS NOT THE LICENCE, IT'S THE BADGE: @xyflow/react is MIT, but the docs say "Only remove this attribution, if you are subscribed to React Flow Pro" — Pro is $169/mo Starter and $289/mo Professional. Combined with the fact that its own performance docs give NO node-count threshold and instead spend the whole page on `React.memo`/`useCallback`/`useMemo` discipline and warn that "animations, shadows, and gradients" hurt at scale, React Flow is a bad fit for a 9-node marketing diagram you already know the coordinates of.
  - SCROLL-DRIVEN CSS (`animation-timeline: scroll()` / `view()`) IS STILL NOT SAFE AS THE ONLY MECHANISM IN SEPT 2026: roughly 84% global, Chrome/Edge 115+, Safari 18+, but Firefox still has it behind `layout.css.scroll-driven-animations.enabled` in stable as of Firefox 152 (June 2026), so it is NOT Baseline. Use it as progressive enhancement behind `@supports (animation-timeline: scroll())` with a JS path underneath. By contrast `offset-path` IS safe: caniuse reports 96.35% global, Chrome 46+, Edge 79+, Firefox 72+, Safari 16.0+, cross-browser since March 2022.
  - RIVE IS WHAT THE BIG DARK DEV SITES ACTUALLY USE FOR HERO ANIMATION IN 2026, AND SENTRY SHOWS THE CORRECT INTEGRATION: sentry.io serves `/astro-assets/rive-files/home-hero-scanner-animation.riv` and the page code carries `riveInstances`, `riveReadyPromise`, `riveIntersectionState`, and `rive-poster` / `rive-poster--hidden` classes — i.e. a static poster image is shown until the WASM runtime resolves, and playback is gated by IntersectionObserver. I also found Rive references on cloudflare.com, coderabbit.ai, greptile.com, cubic.dev, cognition.ai, datadoghq.com and neon.com. Trade-off: Rive web runtime is ~200KB gzipped (WASM included) vs lottie-web ~60KB, but .riv files are 50-80% smaller than equivalent .json, and Rive has real interactive state machines while Lottie only got dotLottie state machines in late 2025.
  - FOUR PRODUCTION DASH RECIPES, COPIED EXACTLY, SO YOU DON'T HAVE TO GUESS: Cloudflare section dividers `stroke-dasharray="16,16"` stroke-width 1 on `var(--color-border-100)`; Hex.tech hero arcs `stroke-dasharray="2 2.5"` at `opacity="0.5"` with a gradient stroke over a 500x848 viewBox; Arcjet `stroke-dasharray="2 3"` + `stroke-linecap="round"` + `vector-effect="non-scaling-stroke"`; Depot hatch field `stroke-dasharray="4 6"` stroke-width 1 `stroke-opacity="0.8"` on #EAECF0 with per-line `style="opacity:0"` for a staggered reveal. Note that three of four set `vector-effect` — that is what keeps a 1px hairline exactly 1px when the SVG scales.
  - AIRBYTE RUNS A DATA-ATTRIBUTE-DRIVEN FLOW SYSTEM WORTH COPYING WHOLESALE: their hero paths carry `data-flow-index`, `data-flow-base-path`, `data-flow-path-d` (the `d` string duplicated into an attribute so JS never has to re-read geometry), and `data-flow-well` (the group/channel name), rendered as `stroke-width="1.5" stroke-dasharray="2 2" vector-effect="nonScalingStroke"` with `stroke="url(#ab-home-hero2-in-base)"`. That is exactly the contract you want between a static SVG and a runtime that has to light up stage N.
  - TEMPORAL ALREADY SOLVED THE SEMANTIC EDGE VOCABULARY FOR A RUNNING WORKFLOW — ADOPT IT VERBATIM IN THE DASHBOARD: animated dashed line = pending/awaiting, red = failure, dashed red = retrying, dashed purple = pending, green = completed. Four states, all expressible as `stroke` + `stroke-dasharray` + a dash animation, no new components.
  - STRIPE'S OWN ENGINEERING RULES FOR DIAGRAM ANIMATION (from their Connect front-end post): animate only `transform` and `opacity`; "keep animations under 500 milliseconds"; use the Web Animations API rather than a library (their whole keyboard sequence was ~5KB); `will-change` for GPU promotion; `backface-visibility` in CSS and `requestIdleCallback` in JS; and honour `prefers-reduced-motion`. Their famous gradient is a separate thing: a hand-rolled WebGL ("minigl") 3-layer Simplex-noise fragment shader in a container transformed `skewY(-12deg)` with `overflow:hidden`, disabled by a ScrollObserver when off-screen.
  - VERCEL'S PUBLISHED WEB INTERFACE GUIDELINES (vercel.com/design/guidelines) GIVE YOU A DEFENSIBLE HOUSE STYLE: preference order CSS > Web Animations API > JS libraries; "prioritize GPU-accelerated properties (transform, opacity) & avoid properties that trigger reflows/repaints"; never `transition: all`; every animation needs a reduced-motion variant and must be interruptible/cancelable by user input; autoplaying motion longer than 5 seconds needs pause/stop/hide controls; and for SVG specifically, "apply CSS transforms/animations to `<g>` wrappers & set `transform-box: fill-box; transform-origin: center;`" to dodge a Safari bug.
  - UNKEY BUILT THEIR OWN LAYOUT ENGINE RATHER THAN USE REACT FLOW, AND DOCUMENTED IT (the `unkey-flow` folder's `docs.ts` is a 200-line essay). They do a DFS flatten, compute `calculateSubtreeWidth`/`calculateSubtreeHeight` bottom-up, then centre children under the parent: `childY = parentY + parentHeight/2 + spacing.y + childHeight/2`. Their live numbers: node width 282, node height 100, `spacing.x = 25`, `spacing.y = 75`. Their dashboard package.json has `framer-motion: 12.29.0` and NO xyflow dependency at all.
  - INNGEST'S DARK-SURFACE NUMBERS, LIFTED FROM THEIR HERO: page is `bg-stone-950` (#0C0A09); the grid background is `lineColor="rgba(70, 70, 70, 0.3)"` with `gridSize={{ default: '75px', md: '50px', sm: '30px' }}` — i.e. the grid gets DENSER as the screen gets smaller so it still reads as texture on a phone. Their glow dot is an 8x8 rect with THREE stacked `feGaussianBlur` drop shadows at `stdDeviation` 4.45, 7.68 and 26.88 (plus an `feMorphology radius=3 dilate`) in colour rgb(203,178,106) — gorgeous, but they only ever render a handful at once.

  - [must-steal] GSAP ScrollTrigger docs https://gsap.com/docs/v3/Plugins/ScrollTrigger/ :: STEAL: Exact scrub/pin/snap semantics for a scroll-advanced pipeline. :: SPECIFICS: `scrub: true` links playhead directly to progress; `scrub: 0.5` means the playhead takes 0.5 SECONDS to catch up (smoothing). start/end syntax `"[element position] [viewport position]"` e.g. `"top bottom-=5%"`, supports `+=300px`, `"max"`, `clamp()`. `pin: true` applies `position: fixed` (or transforms for non-viewport scrollers) and auto-wraps in a spacer div with padding. `snap: [0, 0.111, 0.222, …]` or `"labels"` / `"labelsDirectional"`. `toggleActions` default `"play none none none"` over onEnter/onLeave/onEnterBack/onLeaveBack. `invalidateOnRefresh: true` flushes cached start values on resize. `fastScrollEnd` default threshold 2500px/s. ScrollTrigger pre-calculates positions, debounces scroll, syncs to rAF, throttles resize recalcs with a 200ms gap.
  - [strong] Motion for React — scroll animations https://motion.dev/docs/react-scroll-animations :: STEAL: The React-native alternative to GSAP if you want one animation dependency shared with the dashboard. :: SPECIFICS: `const { scrollYProgress } = useScroll({ target: ref, offset: ["start end", "end start"] })` returns MotionValues scrollX/scrollY/scrollXProgress/scrollYProgress. `useTransform(scrollYProgress, [0,1], ["blur(0px)","blur(10px)"])` maps to any CSS value. `useSpring(scrollYProgress, { stiffness: 100, damping: 30, restDelta: 0.001 })` is the scrub-smoothing equivalent (this is your `scrub: 1`). Docs state "Motion scroll animations are hardware accelerated" by defaulting to the browser's native ScrollTimeline with a JS fallback. Package `motion` 13.4.1, MIT. Motion+ is a separate one-time £299 personal licence for premium components (Carousel, AnimateNumber, Ticker, Cursor, Typewriter, ScrambleText, Curtains) — you need none of them.

Pro Starter $169/mo, Professional $289/mo (adds 1h/mo email support, 5 seats), Enterprise custom. Performance page gives no node-count threshold; it prescribes React.memo on custom nodes/edges, useCallback for prop functions, useMemo for defaultEdgeOptions, never reading `nodes`/`edges`/viewport directly in components, `hidden` to collapse subtrees, and warns that CSS animations/shadows/gradients are the thing that kills you at scale.
