  - [reference-only] Arcjet.com https://arcjet.com :: STEAL: Fine-dash + non-scaling-stroke on curved decorative paths; heavy use of inline SVG (248 on the page) with zero animation libraries. :: SPECIFICS: `<path fill="none" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-dasharray="2 3" vector-effect="non-scaling-stroke" d="…C19.6159 20.0788 29.1428 12.2603 40.2891 7.64338…">` with `<circle r="10.6667">` endpoint caps inside a 128x128 viewBox.
  - [avoid] Datadog homepage https://www.datadoghq.com :: STEAL: Nothing — this is the counter-example. 31 mp4/webm assets. It is the 'pre-render everything' approach and it makes the product feel like a screencast. :: SPECIFICS: `<video preload=none loop playsinline muted title="Log Management" poster="…clickthrough-logs.png?w=804&fit=crop&fm=jpg&auto=format&dpr=1.75&q=40" src="….mp4?format=mp4">` with a parallel `?fm=webm` source; hero video is `autoplay muted loop preload=auto` with a 1700x956 JPEG poster. Sensible engineering (preload=none, posters, dpr-aware), wrong strategy for a system whose whole pitch is that it is live.
  - [strong] Warp.dev and Pinecone.io canvas heroes https://www.warp.dev and https://www.pinecone.io :: STEAL: Full-bleed canvas behind a hero, masked to nothing at the edges by a CSS gradient — so the canvas never has to be beautiful at its boundaries. :: SPECIFICS: Warp: `<div class="dot-twinkle" aria-hidden="true"><canvas></canvas></div>` inside `<section class="hero">`, plus a separate `<canvas class="roi-chart">` with an HTML legend (`compute` / `platform` / `inference`). Pinecone: `<canvas class="absolute inset-0 z-1 block h-full w-full">` under a `pointer-events-none absolute inset-0 z-1` gradient overlay driven by CSS custom properties `--side-a: 6%` and `--side-b: 74%`, with chart canvases `#cpk-c0..c2` and a legend swatch #002BFF.
  - [reference-only] Graphite.dev (direct competitor) https://graphite.dev :: STEAL: Reality-check: their 'heavy GSAP' footprint is 576 instances of a class named `gsap-decorative-dot` plus `gsap-prefooter-split-text` and `gsap-prefooter-ctas`. It is decorative dots and a split-text prefooter, not an animated architecture diagram. They have not solved this either. :: SPECIFICS: No ScrollTrigger references in the shipped HTML; 127 inline SVGs; one mp4 and two webm.
  - [reference-only] Trigger.dev, Convex, Liveblocks, Modal, Neon, Upstash, Browserbase, n8n, Prefect, Retool, Val.town, Clerk, Linear, Resend, Railway, Vercel — fingerprint sweep (fetched live 2026-09-22) :: STEAL: The distribution of what dev-tool marketing actually ships in 2026, so you can calibrate. :: SPECIFICS: vercel.com, railway.com, convex.dev: NO animation library and no canvas in the served HTML — their motion is CSS + inline SVG only. linear.app: 2 `three` refs, 2 dasharray. clerk.com: 8 dasharray, nothing else. resend.com: 1 spline ref. neon.com: 1 rive, 6 framer. trigger.dev: 46 zero-sized `<canvas>` elements (one per tab icon, `style="vertical-align:top;width:0;height:0"` — a vector-runtime icon per Radix tab item) and 269 inline SVGs. modal.com: a `logo.lottie…png` (Lottie-authored, shipped as PNG). browserbase.com: 116 inline SVGs, one `btn--animated` class, no library. Takeaway: inline SVG + CSS is the majority answer; Rive is the premium answer; nobody is using React Flow for storytelling.
RECOMMENDATIONS:
  - (high) BUILD ONE COMPONENT, `<Pipeline progress={0..1} mode="scrub"|"live"|"replay" />`, AND USE IT ON BOTH THE LANDING PAGE AND THE DASHBOARD. On the marketing site `progress` comes from scroll; in the dashboard `progress = (stage_index - 1 + stagePhase) / stage_total` where `stagePhase = clamp(secondsSinceHeartbeat / expectedSeconds[stage], 0, 0.92)` so the in-flight edge keeps creeping while a stage works and snaps to 1.0 when `stage_index` increments. Build the GSAP/Motion timeline with total length exactly 1.0 and give each of the 9 stages a 0.1111 share, exactly as LangChain's shipped comment describes ('duration/position = SHARE of the scroll'). This is the single highest-leverage architectural decision in the whole project. | WHY: It turns your marketing hero into a live product surface and vice versa. When a judge sees the identical diagram on the landing page and then watching a real run tick from stage 3/9 to 4/9 in the dashboard, the site stops being marketing and becomes evidence. It also halves your build cost. Your API already emits `stage_index`, `stage_total`, `stage_label` and a heartbeat — the contract is already there. | TRADEOFF: You must resist adding marketing-only flourishes that the dashboard cannot render, and dashboard-only density that ruins the hero. Solve it with a `density="hero"|"compact"` prop, not a fork.
  - (high) GEOMETRY: hand-author the diagram as a FIXED viewBox with HTML node cards absolutely positioned by percentage on top of an SVG edge layer in the SAME coordinate space. Desktop `viewBox="0 0 1200 760"`, tablet `0 0 780 1100`, mobile `0 0 390 1560`. Three hard-coded `d` string sets, switched by a CSS visibility class exactly like Inngest's `visibilityClass` (`hidden xl:block` / `hidden md:block xl:hidden` / `block sm:hidden`). DO NOT measure the DOM. | WHY: You control the layout, so measurement buys you nothing and costs you everything: getBoundingClientRect on every ResizeObserver tick, a first-paint frame with no lines, font-loading reflow that moves your anchors, and the classic 'beam attached to the wrong node on first render'. Inngest measures because their nodes are arbitrary content; yours are nine known boxes. Static `d` strings also make every path server-renderable — the diagram is fully drawn in the HTML before a single byte of JS runs. | TRADEOFF: You hand-author 3 geometry sets (~2 hours of work in Figma + a coordinate export). Copy changes that lengthen a node label can push text out of a card, so cap labels at a fixed character count.
  - (high) PUT `pathLength="1"` ON EVERY EDGE AND DRIVE EVERYTHING WITH `stroke-dashoffset`. Draw-on: `stroke-dasharray="1" stroke-dashoffset: 1 -> 0`. Travelling packet: a duplicate overlay path with `stroke-dasharray="0.06 0.94"`, `stroke-dashoffset: 1 -> 0`, `stroke-linecap="round"`. Stalled state: `stroke-dasharray="0.01 0.02"` with a slow looping dashoffset (marching ants). Add `vector-effect="non-scaling-stroke"` to every stroke so 1px hairlines stay 1px at every viewBox scale (Inngest, Arcjet and Airbyte all do this). | WHY: One normalized property expresses draw-on, packet flow, pending, retrying and stalled. It is geometry-independent, so a 2400px spine and an 80px fork animate at the same perceived speed under one keyframe. It needs no DOM element per packet (unlike offset-path), no JS sampling (unlike getPointAtLength), and no library. MDN's pathLength page confirms the browser scales all dash math by pathLength/computed-length. | TRADEOFF: stroke-dashoffset is a PAINT property, not a compositor property — each animating path re-rasterizes per frame. Budget: never animate more than ~6 paths simultaneously; the other ~9 sit in their static end state. `will-change` will not help here, so the cap is the control.
  - (high) DESIGN A — 'THE SPINE' (ship this one). A scroll-scrubbed vertical pipeline in a pinned viewport. ScrollTrigger: `{ trigger: '#pipeline', start: 'top top', end: '+=460%', pin: true, scrub: 0.6, snap: { snapTo: [0,.111,.222,.333,.444,.556,.667,.778,.889,1], duration: 0.25, delay: 0.05, ease: 'power1.inOut' }, invalidateOnRefresh: true }`. Nine stations down a 1px spine; the active station is the ONLY glowing element on screen; a right-hand rail shows the live stage label ('Stage 3/9 — specialists') and a latency chip. S3 forks the spine into four lanes that draw SIMULTANEOUSLY (same keyframe window) with completion staggered 0 / 60 / 120 / 180ms and per-lane latency chips counting up (bugs 4.1s, security 5.8s, tests 3.2s, quality 4.4s), captioned 'wall clock = max(lane), not sum'. S7 breaks the visual grammar: the node expands into a two-column terminal panel — left = base SHA, green `1 passed`; right = head SHA, red `1 failed` with a real traceback — a scanline sweeps once, then `proof_status: proven` STAMPS with a 0ms colour change and a 160ms scale 1.06 -> 1.0. S8 lights the delivery ladder (inline_exact -> inline_nearest -> file_level -> summary_only) with the taken rung solid and the others ghosted. S9 flips the merge gate: everything else desaturates to 30% opacity over 300ms while the gate pill goes red with a 0ms colour swap and a 220ms width expand revealing '1 proven finding blocks merge'. | WHY: Scroll-scrubbing is the right pattern because a 9-stage pipeline is a SEQUENCE and scroll is the only input that natively expresses 'how far through am I'. It also gives the judge control — they can scrub back to the proof moment. The vertical spine is the only topology that survives 1440px -> 375px without a redesign. Breaking the visual grammar exactly once, at S7, is what makes the proof stage read as the climax rather than as stage seven of nine. The desaturate-then-red gate is the highest-contrast single frame on the page and it is the frame a judge will remember. | TRADEOFF: Pinning + `end: '+=460%'` means ~4.6 viewport heights of scroll for one section — too long if the page has much else above it. Pin on iOS Safari fights the collapsing toolbar; disable pinning below `lg` and fall back to per-stage `toggleActions: 'play none none reverse'` reveals. Reduced-motion users get all nine stages rendered in their final state with the gate already red, no scrub.
  - (high) DESIGN B — 'THE CONVEYOR' (build this second; it IS the dashboard mode and the social-preview loop). The same geometry, but ambient: a 14s looping rAF clock, packets flowing continuously through all nine stages, three or four in flight at different phases. Packets are `stroke-dasharray="0.06 0.94"` overlay paths on a 2600ms linear cycle with 180ms stagger per lane. No scroll dependency, no pin, works in an `<iframe>`, works as an OG video, works as the dashboard's 'run in progress' widget. Pause it with an IntersectionObserver exactly like Inngest's Engine (`elapsedMs` accumulates only while visible) and expose a play/pause control because Vercel's guidelines require one for autoplay motion over 5 seconds. | WHY: You need a version of the diagram that works with zero scroll: on the dashboard, in a demo GIF, in the README, on a projector during judging when someone else is driving. Ambient loops are bad as a hero's only moment (nothing is earned) but perfect as the live-run surface, where the 'wow' comes from the data being real. | TRADEOFF: An always-running rAF loop costs battery; the IntersectionObserver gate and a `document.visibilityState` check are mandatory, not optional. Ambient motion also competes with the dense data in a dashboard — in `density="compact"` mode, drop packets and keep only the active-stage pulse.
  - (medium) DESIGN C — 'THE PROOF CHAMBER' (do NOT build this for the hackathon). A Rive state machine or a Three.js depth-layered scene, with the nine stages as parallax planes and the sandbox as a literal chamber the code drops into. If you ever do it, copy Sentry's integration exactly: a `rive-poster` static image shown until `riveReadyPromise` resolves, an IntersectionObserver gate, and `@rive-app/react-canvas` 4.34.3. | WHY: It would look the most expensive, and Rive state machines are genuinely the right long-term tool for 'diagram driven by named inputs' — you could expose `stageIndex`, `proofStatus` and `gateRed` as state-machine inputs and drive them from the API. But it requires a Rive designer, an authoring pass, and a ~200KB gzipped WASM runtime, and you cannot iterate on it at 2am. Listing it shows you considered it; not building it is the correct call. | TRADEOFF: Dropping it means your hero is DOM/SVG, which is slightly less 'cinematic' than a shader. Compensate with the background layer (recommendation below) rather than by making the diagram 3D.
  - (medium) PALETTE — deliberately do NOT look like CodeRabbit. They are purple-tinted black + mint + orange. Go blue-tinted black with a cyan proof accent. Exact tokens (oklch with computed sRGB fallbacks): `--bg: oklch(0.145 0.012 265)` #080A0F; `--surface-1: oklch(0.185 0.014 265)` #101319; `--surface-2: oklch(0.225 0.016 265)` #181C23; `--hairline: oklch(0.28 0.02 265)` #242933; `--flow: oklch(0.72 0.15 195)` #00C0C2 (the travelling packet / active stage — this colour appears ONLY on things that are moving or live); `--proven: oklch(0.75 0.18 150)` #40CC6D; `--blocking: oklch(0.60 0.22 25)` #E62B34; `--warn: oklch(0.80 0.15 85)` #EAB532; `--text: oklch(0.97 0.005 265)` #F3F5F9; `--text-2: oklch(0.72 0.015 265)` #A0A5AE; `--text-3: oklch(0.55 0.015 265)` #6D727B. Grid texture: `rgba(255,255,255,0.045)` at 72px desktop / 48px tablet / 32px mobile (Inngest ships 75/50/30 at `rgba(70,70,70,0.3)` — denser grid on smaller screens so it still reads as texture). Idle edges = `--hairline` at 0.55 opacity. | WHY: First impression in 3 seconds is mostly chroma and contrast. CodeRabbit's mint-on-purple-black is now the category default; matching it makes you look like a clone. A cold blue-black with a single cyan 'live' accent and a single green 'proven' accent encodes the product thesis directly: cyan means something is happening, green means something was PROVEN, red means something BLOCKS. Three chromas, three meanings, nothing decorative. oklch keeps perceptual lightness consistent when you shift hue, which is why the surfaces step cleanly. | TRADEOFF: Cyan/blue is common in infra branding (Cloudflare orange and Temporal purple are more distinctive). If you want more differentiation, rotate `--flow` to oklch(0.72 0.15 215) (a colder ice-blue) — but do not go violet, that is Temporal, and do not go mint, that is CodeRabbit.
  - (high) MOTION TOKENS — publish them as CSS custom properties and use nothing else. `--ease-out-expo: cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)` (node reveal, 420ms); `--ease-snap: cubic-bezier(0.2, 0, 0, 1)` (chips/hover, 120ms); `linear` for anything scrubbed (NEVER ease a scroll-scrubbed draw — easing fights the scroll input and reads as lag); packet loop 2600ms linear; lane stagger 180ms; verdict stamp = 0ms colour + 160ms scale; gate flip = 0ms colour + 220ms width. Obey Stripe's rule: nothing discrete exceeds 500ms. Wrap all SVG transform animation on `<g>` elements with `transform-box: fill-box; transform-origin: center;` per Vercel's Safari guidance, and never write `transition: all`. | WHY: A judge reads 'expensive' from consistency, not from complexity. Two easing curves plus linear-for-scrub is a system; fifteen ad-hoc durations is a student project. The 0ms colour change on verdicts is the deliberate exception: every other transition on the page is eased, so an instantaneous state flip reads as a machine returning a result rather than a UI animating. | TRADEOFF: Rigid tokens will occasionally feel slightly too fast on a large element. Fix it with distance, not duration.
  - (high) ENGINEER THE FAILURE MODE BEFORE THE HAPPY PATH — copy LangChain's fallback handshake verbatim. In `<head>`: CSS that pre-hides animated elements AND an armed 2-second 'reveal anyway' rule. In the bundle: only add `class="js-anim"` to `<html>` after verifying the animation runtime actually loaded. If it did not, the CSS fires at 2s and the diagram appears fully drawn in its final state. Additionally, server-render every path already at `stroke-dashoffset: 0` and have JS set it to 1 in the SAME synchronous task it starts the animation in, so no frame can paint a blank diagram. | WHY: The demo will run on conference wifi, possibly on a machine you do not control, possibly with an extension that blocks a CDN. 'Beautiful, or invisible' is the default outcome of a scroll-scrubbed diagram and it is how hackathon demos die on stage. 'Degraded, but never invisible' is a decision, and it costs about 20 lines. | TRADEOFF: You carry a small amount of duplicated state (the CSS end-state and the JS end-state must agree). Enforce it by generating the static CSS from the same geometry constants.
  - (high) ADD THE 'DISPROVEN' BEAT — it is the only moment on the page no competitor can copy. Somewhere in the S7 sequence, show a SECOND finding entering the sandbox, passing at the head SHA, and being publicly struck through with `proof_status: disproven` and the caption 'We refuted our own finding. It never reached your PR.' Then show the counter increment on a small self-report strip: `execution_verified_precision`, `self_caught_false_positive_rate`, each with its `n`. | WHY: Every competitor's site claims accuracy. Yours is the only one that can show the system publicly losing an argument with itself, and that single beat does more for the trust positioning than the other eight stages combined. It also directly visualises your `/v1/accuracy` endpoint, which proves the marketing claim is wired to real data rather than to a copywriter. | TRADEOFF: It costs ~8 seconds of scroll and risks diluting the 'proven' climax if it comes first. Order it strictly: proven stamp -> beat -> disproven strike-through -> gate red.
  - (medium) USE THE 'AI AGENT DELETED A TEST' SCENARIO AS THE SECTION IMMEDIATELY AFTER THE PIPELINE, RENDERED AS A DIFF, NOT AS A DIAGRAM. A two-pane diff where the right pane shows a test file deleted, then the PRGuard annotation lands with fingerprint `sha256(category|rule_key|symbol_path|shape_hash)` shown truncated, severity breakdown expanded as a horizontal weighted bar (reachability .20, blast_radius .15, path_sensitivity .25, coverage_gap .15, churn .10, proof_confidence .15) and the gate red. No pipeline geometry here — you already spent the diagram budget. | WHY: Judges need a second, concrete, emotionally legible moment and the diagram cannot carry two climaxes. A diff is instantly readable to every engineer in the room and the 'agent gamed CI' story is the most 2026-relevant threat in the category. The weighted-severity bar is your only genuinely novel data visualisation — show it as a stacked horizontal bar with the six weights as fixed-width segments and each segment filled 0..1 by its input, so the total width IS the score and the bands (critical .75 / high .55 / medium .35 / low .15) can be marked as tick lines on the track. That is far more legible than a radar chart at mobile width. | TRADEOFF: A radar chart looks more 'designed' in a screenshot; a weighted stacked bar is more honest and readable. Take the bar. If you want the radar, put it in the dashboard's finding-detail drawer where there is room.
  - (high) DEPENDENCY DECISION: `motion` 13.4.1 (MIT) as the single animation dependency for the whole product, plus `gsap` 3.15.0 + ScrollTrigger ONLY on the marketing route if you need pin + snap + scrub. Do NOT install `@xyflow/react` for the marketing site. Do NOT install `lottie-web` (frozen since 2025-05-21) — if you need a pre-rendered asset use `@lottiefiles/dotlottie-react` 0.19.16 or `@rive-app/react-canvas` 4.34.3. Do NOT install `reactflow@11` (dead package). If you need nice elbow/bezier edge generators, `d3-shape` (ISC) gives you `linkVertical()` and `curveBumpY` in a few KB without the graph library. | WHY: Motion is already the React-native choice, is MIT, ships scroll animations backed by native ScrollTimeline, and the dashboard needs it anyway for layout transitions. GSAP's pin + snap + `invalidateOnRefresh` + `fastScrollEnd` genuinely have no Motion equivalent for a pinned scrubbed section, it is now free for commercial use including all plugins, and PRGuard is nowhere near the one prohibited use (building a competing no-code animation tool). Everything else on the list is either dead, paid-to-de-badge, or solving a problem you do not have. | TRADEOFF: Two animation libraries on one route is ~70KB gz of GSAP+ScrollTrigger you would not otherwise ship. Route-split it so the dashboard bundle never sees GSAP. If you want a single dependency, `useScroll` + `useSpring({ stiffness: 100, damping: 30 })` + a `position: sticky` container reproduces ~85% of Design A without GSAP — you lose snap-to-stage and `fastScrollEnd`.
  - (high) MOBILE (375px) IS A DIFFERENT DESIGN, NOT A SCALED ONE. Ship `viewBox="0 0 390 1560"`: spine at x=28, cards full-width minus a 16px gutter each side, S3's four specialists become a 2x2 grid fed by four short 40px fork curves, the S7 terminal collapses from two columns to two stacked rows (base above, head below) with the head row's red border being the only chroma, and the latency chips drop to a single aggregate. Kill every canvas/particle layer below `lg` exactly like Inngest (`hidden lg:block`). Replace pin+scrub with per-stage `toggleActions: 'play none none reverse'` triggered at `start: 'top 75%'` — no pinning on mobile Safari. | WHY: A 9-across horizontal pipeline at 375px is 41px per stage. There is no responsive trick that saves it. Every reference that solved this — Inngest most explicitly with its three `visibilityClass` variants and a whole separate `useFeatureNavigatePathsMedium.ts` — solved it by authoring separate geometry. Pinning on iOS Safari also fights the collapsing URL bar, which produces the jump-and-stick artefact that instantly reads as 'broken'. | TRADEOFF: Three geometry sets to maintain. Mitigate by generating all three from one TS constants file of node coordinates per breakpoint, with the `d` strings produced by a tiny `elbow(from, to)` helper rather than hand-typed.
  - (medium) BACKGROUND LAYER: get the 'cinematic / expensive' feeling from a cheap static background, not from the diagram. Compose three layers behind the pipeline: (1) a CSS `linear-gradient` grid at `rgba(255,255,255,0.045)`, 72/48/32px; (2) two large-radius dashed arcs, Hex.tech style — `stroke-dasharray="2 2.5"`, `opacity: 0.5`, gradient stroke, over a ~500x850 viewBox, static, zero JS; (3) ONE radial glow positioned behind the active stage: `radial-gradient(60% 60% at var(--glow-x) var(--glow-y), oklch(0.72 0.15 195 / 0.14), transparent 70%)` where `--glow-x/--glow-y` are the only two values the scrub timeline writes to the background layer. If you want more, add a single `filter: drop-shadow()` on the active `<g>` rather than Inngest's three stacked feGaussianBlurs (stdDeviation 4.45 / 7.68 / 26.88 — beautiful, but they only ever render a handful). | WHY: Layers 1 and 2 cost nothing and are what actually makes Hex, Depot and Cloudflare look expensive. Layer 3 is one animated custom property that moves the eye to the active stage — it is the cheapest possible 'cinematic' effect and it carries information, which is the difference between design and decoration. | TRADEOFF: A moving radial glow repaints a large area. Keep it on its own compositing layer (`will-change: transform` on a translated div rather than animating gradient stops) and cap its size at ~60% of the viewport.
  - (high) LIVE-RUN EDGE VOCABULARY (dashboard): adopt Temporal's four states unchanged, expressed entirely through stroke properties so no new components are needed. Completed = solid `--proven`, dashoffset 0. In flight = `--flow`, `stroke-dasharray="0.06 0.94"`, looping dashoffset. Stalled (now - heartbeat > 20s) = `--warn`, `stroke-dasharray="0.01 0.02"`, slow marching ants, plus a '12s since last output' label. Failed / degraded = `--blocking` solid. Retrying = `--blocking` dashed. Adopt Airbyte's attribute contract for the runtime handoff: put `data-stage-index`, `data-stage-key` and `data-path-d` on each edge so the live driver never re-reads geometry from the DOM. | WHY: These five states cover every transition your S1..S9 pipeline can be in, they are all one-property changes on a path you already render, and Temporal has already proven the grammar is legible to engineers. The data-attribute contract is what lets the live driver be ~50 lines and the SVG be fully static and server-rendered. | TRADEOFF: Colour-only state encoding fails for colour-blind users; pair every state with a distinct dash pattern (which this does) and a text label in the stage rail.
ANTI-PATTERNS / AVOID:
  - Magic UI's `AnimatedBeam` as the centrepiece. It is THE tell that a site was assembled from a shadcn registry, judges have seen it hundreds of times, and it is technically wrong for your layout: y1/y2 are hard-pinned to "0%" so the light wipes horizontally across the bounding box instead of travelling along a vertical spine. Twelve beams also means twelve stacked full-container absolutely-positioned SVGs, each re-measuring on every ResizeObserver tick.
  - React Flow / xyflow on a marketing page. You ship pan, zoom, drag, selection, keyboard handlers, connection logic and a required stylesheet — then spend an afternoon disabling all of it — and you either keep an attribution badge or pay $169/mo. Cloudflare, with infinite budget, uses only its `Background` sub-component and hand-writes every actual line. Follow them.
  - Aurora / mesh-gradient blobs and glassmorphism cards. This was the 2021-2023 AI-startup uniform. In 2026 it reads as 'we bought a template'. A blurred purple blob behind frosted glass is now a negative signal.
  - A Spline scene, a rotating 3D logo, or a hero `<canvas>` with generic floating particles that mean nothing. Heavy, hurts LCP, and reads as portfolio rather than infrastructure. If particles appear, they must FORM something (Inngest's alpha-sampled SVG formation) or they are noise.
  - A typewriter-effect headline. Instantly dates the page and delays the one sentence you need a judge to read in 3 seconds.
  - Showing all nine stages at once with everything pulsing simultaneously. A pulse is only information if exactly one thing is pulsing. Nine breathing nodes is a screensaver.
  - Glow on everything. Glow must be earned by state. Exactly one element should glow at any instant: the active stage. Inngest's triple-feGaussianBlur glow dot (stdDeviation 4.45 / 7.68 / 26.88) is gorgeous precisely because they render one or two of them, never twenty.
  - A full-bleed autoplaying product video as the hero. Datadog ships 31 mp4/webm files on its homepage and the result feels like a screencast. For a product whose entire claim is that it executes real code and shows you real terminal output, a pre-rendered video actively undercuts the thesis.
  - Authoring the main diagram in Lottie or After Effects. A pre-rendered JSON cannot be driven by `stage_index`, so you would have to rebuild it from scratch for the dashboard, and the two would drift. Author once, in DOM/SVG, driven by a number.
  - ScrollSmoother, Lenis, or any scroll-jacking. Judges scroll fast and erratically; smoothed scroll feels broken on a trackpad and makes 'get me to the demo' hostile. `scrub: 0.6` gives you smoothing on the ANIMATION without hijacking the SCROLL. Those are different things.
  - Loading two copies of your animation library. LangChain.com literally ships GSAP 3.15.0 from the Webflow CDN and GSAP 3.12.5 from cdnjs on the same page. Check your network tab before you demo.
  - `transition: all`. Vercel's guidelines call this out explicitly. It animates properties you did not intend, including layout-triggering ones, and it is the single most common cause of 'why does this feel sluggish'.
  - Animating `filter: blur()`, `box-shadow`, `width`, `height`, `top` or `left` per frame. `transform` and `opacity` only. If you need a moving glow, translate a pre-rendered radial-gradient div; do not animate the gradient's stops.
  - A diagram that renders blank for the first 200ms while JS measures the DOM. This is the default outcome of the getBoundingClientRect approach and it is what you will see on a cold load on conference wifi. Server-render the geometry.
  - Pure `#000000` background with pure `#FFFFFF` text. 21:1 contrast causes halation on OLED and reads as unfinished. Use a near-black with a hue tint (#080A0F) and a near-white (#F3F5F9).
  - Copying CodeRabbit's colour language. Their base is oklch(0.177 0.0084 305) (#121014, purple-tinted black) with mint #65E6A6 and orange #F04006. If you land anywhere near that you have volunteered to be compared on their terms.
  - Leading with 'AI-powered code review' in the hero. Ten competitors say it, one of them is free inside GitHub Copilot, and it is not your positioning. Your hero sentence is about PROOF and TRUST, and the diagram exists to demonstrate that claim, not to look busy.
  - A radar/spider chart for the six severity inputs on the marketing page. It looks designed in a screenshot and is illegible at 375px. The weighted stacked bar (six fixed-width segments, each filled 0..1, total width = the score, band thresholds as tick marks) is both more honest and more readable. Save the radar for the dashboard drawer.
  - Emoji anywhere in the product UI or the diagram. Instantly cheapens a governance/compliance product.
  - An 'animated' diagram that is really a GIF or an `<img>` of a Figma export with a CSS pulse on top. Judges will open devtools.
  - Building the mobile layout by scaling the desktop viewBox. Nine stations across 375px is 41px per stage. Every reference that solved this authored separate geometry per breakpoint; Inngest ships three literal `visibilityClass` variants and a whole separate `useFeatureNavigatePathsMedium.ts` hook.
  - Autoplaying ambient motion for longer than 5 seconds with no pause control — Vercel's guidelines (and WCAG 2.2.2) require pause/stop/hide. A judge who cannot stop your loop to read a label will just leave.
FACT-CHECK CORRECTIONS:
    [CORRECTED] Unclear whether React Flow Pro is per-seat or per-org, and whether an annual discount applies; Professional 'adds 1h/mo email support, 5 seats' -> It is per-organisation with seat caps, not per-seat: Starter = 'Invite 1 team member', Professional = 'Invite 5 team members', Enterprise = 'Invite 10 team members'. A Monthly/Yearly toggle exists on the page (only /pro/subscribe/{starter,pro}/month links are in the static HTML). The Professional bullets today are 'Prioritized Github Issues' and 'Introduction call with one of the creators' — no '1 hour/month email support' line appears.
    [REFUTED] Cloudflare.com uses xyflow (10 data-testid="rf__background" occurrences) -> The 10 `rf__background` elements and the dot `<pattern>` are present, and there are zero react-flow__node/edge classes — but I downloaded all 19 /_astro/*.js chunks the homepage references and NONE contains the strings 'xyflow', 'reactflow', 'react-flow__' or 'rf__background'. There is no evidence the package is shipped; the markup is almost certainly copied/reimplemented as a static SVG background. Do not cite Cloudflare as a React Flow user.
    [CORRECTED] CSS scroll-driven animations: ~84% global, Chrome/Edge 115+, Safari 18+, Firefox behind a flag, not Baseline -> Safari is 26+, NOT 18+ (MDN BCD and web-features both give safari/safari_ios = 26 for animation-timeline, scroll-timeline, view-timeline and the ScrollTimeline/ViewTimeline APIs). Everything else holds: Chrome/Edge/Chrome-Android 115 (timeline-scope 116), Firefox = 'preview' (flag only), baseline: false. My own computation over caniuse usage data gives 84.42% global, so ~84% is right. Note Firefox is now 156.0.1 (2026-09-15) and still unsupported — 'as of Firefox 152' is stale but the conclusion is unchanged. The exact pref name layout.css.scroll-driven-animations.enabled could not be verified (web-search budget exhausted).
    [CORRECTED] offset-path: 96.35% global, Chrome 46+, Edge 79+, Firefox 72+, Safari 16.0+, cross-browser since March 2022 -> Browser versions match caniuse exactly (chrome 46, edge 79, firefox 72, safari/ios_saf 16.0). Global usage is 95.64% today, not 96.35% (usage_perc_a = 0). The 'March 2022' tension resolves: MDN/web-features gives css.properties.offset-path Baseline newly-available 2022-03-14 (Safari 15.4), while the full motion-path feature is Baseline low 2022-09-12 (Safari 16) and Baseline WIDELY available since 2025-03-12. So 'widely available since March 2025' is the accurate framing.
    [CORRECTED] CodeRabbit palette: #65E6A6, #F04006 and oklch(0.177 0.0084 305) are the highest-frequency values; #121014 is the sRGB conversion of that oklch -> Frequency counts on today's served HTML: #332F39 = 34 (highest), #65E6A6 = 27, #F04006 = 18, #AAA3AE = 18, #F87171 = 17, #F4F1F5 = 14, #D6F9DF = 12, #203929 = 12, oklch(0.177 0.0084 305) = 5. So the highest-frequency hex is the surface #332F39, not the mint. The #121014 conversion is correct — my independent oklch→sRGB implementation returns exactly #121014.
    [CORRECTED] All proposed PRGuard oklch values and their sRGB hex equivalents were computed locally; oklch(0.65 0.19 262) clipped out of gamut -> All 11 conversions match my independent implementation exactly (#080A0F, #101319, #181C23, #242933, #00C0C2, #40CC6D, #E62B34, #EAB532, #F3F5F9, #A0A5AE, #6D727B), and oklch(0.65 0.19 262) is indeed out of gamut (linear B = +1.018). BUT the recommended `--flow: oklch(0.72 0.15 195)` is ALSO out of sRGB gamut (linear R = -0.076) — #00C0C2 is a clipped value, not a faithful fallback. Max in-gamut chroma at L=0.72/H=195 is ~0.123. The suggested alternative oklch(0.72 0.15 215) is out of gamut too (linear R = -0.060; max chroma ~0.127). On a P3 display the oklch value and the hex fallback will visibly disagree. Drop chroma to ~0.12 or accept the clip deliberately. --proven, --blocking and --warn are all in gamut.
    [CORRECTED] Rive web runtime ~200KB gzipped vs lottie-web ~60KB; .riv 50-80% smaller than .json; dotLottie zip saves 70-80% -> Materially understated for Rive. @rive-app/react-canvas 4.34.3 JS is 202.8KB minified / 56.6KB gzipped — but the WASM is a SEPARATE runtime download: @rive-app/canvas 2.42.2 ships rive.wasm at 1,935,229 bytes raw → 794,396 bytes gzipped (~776KB gz); even canvas-lite's rive.wasm is 849KB raw. So the real first-load cost is roughly 800KB gz, not 200KB. lottie-web 5.13.0: lottie.min.js is 76.1KB gz (not ~60KB); lottie_light.min.js is 46.5KB gz; lottie_svg.min.js 62.4KB gz. The .riv-vs-.json and dotLottie-zip percentages are UNVERIFIABLE (no first-party source found). For comparison: @lottiefiles/dotlottie-react is 33.1KB gz, gsap 3.15.0 is 26.7KB gz, motion 13.4.1 is 46.6KB gz.
    [CORRECTED] dotLottie gained state machines 'in late 2025' -> Off by ~18 months. State machines shipped in @lottiefiles/dotlottie-web 0.25.0, published 2024-06-20 ('feat(web): dotLottie State Machines Integration #254', adding loadStateMachine/startStateMachine/stopStateMachine/postStateMachineEvent and upgrading dotlottie-rs to v0.1.23). The API was later refactored (loadStateMachineData, context setters, input callbacks).
    [UNVERIFIABLE] Airbyte's `data-flow-*` attributes (data-flow-index, -base-path, -path-d, -well) with stroke-dasharray="2 2" and vector-effect="nonScalingStroke" -> Not reproducible. Two fetches of airbyte.com today with different user agents returned identical 240,967-byte documents containing ZERO occurrences of 'data-flow', zero '<svg>' elements, zero 'stroke-dasharray' and zero 'vector-effect'. The page is a Webflow-ported shell (class 'ab-theme-dark ab-home ab-ported-pages') with SVG only in CSS data: URIs. Do not build the 'Airbyte attribute contract' recommendation on a citation you cannot re-fetch.
    [CORRECTED] Trigger.dev has 46 zero-sized <canvas> elements inside Radix tab items and 269 inline SVGs -> Today's served HTML has 23 <canvas> elements, all 23 zero-sized (width:0;height:0), not 46. 269 inline <svg> is exact. Confirmed: no '@rive-app', 'lottie' or 'dotlottie' string appears, so the vector-icon-runtime hypothesis remains unidentified.
    [CORRECTED] Stripe's 'keep animations under 500 milliseconds' and the ~5KB WAAPI figure, from a post that is 'several years old' -> The post is from 2017-06-19 — nine years old, not 'several'. Exact wording is 'in most cases you'll want to stay under 500 milliseconds'. The 5KB figure is 'the whole Express animation is about 5KB all included: scripts, images, etc.' — it is the size of the animation implementation, not of the Web Animations API. Everything else (transform/opacity only, will-change, backface-visibility, requestIdleCallback, prefers-reduced-motion) is present.
    [REFUTED] Temporal's four/five edge states (animated dashed = pending, red = failure, dashed red = retrying, dashed purple = pending, green = completed) come from temporal.io/blog/lets-visualize-a-workflow -> The post exists (Alex Tideman, 2023-10-10) but contains ZERO occurrences of 'dashed', 'dash', 'animated' or 'retrying'. The only colour statement is 'The color of the span indicates its outcome, such as green for Completed and red for Failed' — and it is about timeline SPANS, not edges. The dashed-line grammar is not sourced. The 'their 2026 site language is almost-black with electric-purple gradients / neon-green accents' description was not verified. The recommended edge vocabulary may still be good design, but do not present it as 'Temporal has already proven the grammar'.
    [CORRECTED] Fingerprint sweep: linear.app has 2 `three` refs; railway.com is CSS + inline SVG only; browserbase has 116 inline SVGs and no library -> linear.app's 2 'three' hits are the English word in product copy ('Three issues ranked by…', 'three most important cust…') — there is NO three.js reference. Same false-positive class on browserbase: its 3 'rive' hits are inside 'drive'. railway.com's served HTML has ZERO <svg> elements (and zero canvas/library refs) — it is a client-rendered shell, so 'CSS + inline SVG only' is wrong for Railway specifically. Correct and unchanged: vercel.com 0 canvas/0 library, convex.dev 0 canvas/0 library, clerk.com 8 dasharray only, resend.com 1 spline, neon.com 1 rive + 6 framer, modal.com 1 lottie reference, browserbase.com 116 inline SVGs (btn--animated appears 10 times, not once).
    [CORRECTED] Two animation libraries on one route is '~70KB gz of GSAP+ScrollTrigger' -> ~46KB gz, not ~70KB. gsap@3.15.0 dist/gsap.min.js is 72,927 B raw / 28,302 B gzip; dist/ScrollTrigger.min.js is 44,575 B raw / 17,961 B gzip. The route-splitting advice still stands, but the cost is materially lower than stated (and comparable to motion's own 46.6KB gz).
    [UNVERIFIABLE] Pipeline scroll figures (end '+=460%', scrub 0.6, 0.1111 per stage, 2600ms packet loop, 180ms lane stagger, 20s heartbeat stall threshold) are design proposals -> Correctly self-flagged. No external source; they are invented values. The only externally grounded number in the set is that a GSAP scrubbed timeline normalises total length onto the scroll range (confirmed from LangChain's shipped comment and ScrollTrigger docs), which makes the 1.0-total-length / 0.1111-share architecture sound.
VERIFIER ADDITIONS:
    + GAMUT BUG IN THE HEADLINE COLOUR: the single most-used accent in the whole proposal, `--flow: oklch(0.72 0.15 195)`, is out of sRGB gamut (linear R = -0.076). The researcher caught this for oklch(0.65 0.19 262) but not for their own recommended value, nor for the proposed alternative oklch(0.72 0.15 215) (linear R = -0.060). Max in-gamut chroma at L=0.72 is ~0.123 at H=195 and ~0.127 at H=215. Consequence: browsers clip, so the oklch declaration and the #00C0C2 fallback diverge on wide-gamut (P3) displays — the 'live' colour will look different on a MacBook than in the fallback. Either drop chroma to 0.12 or state the clip is intentional.
    + THE npm LICENSE FIELD IS A LANDMINE FOR CI: gsap's package.json `license` is the free-text string "Standard 'no charge' license: https://gsap.com/standard-license." — not an SPDX identifier. license-checker, FOSSA, Snyk license policies, `npm ls --json` license audits and most OSS-compliance gates will flag it as UNKNOWN/non-conformant. If the hackathon repo or the judges' pipeline runs a licence scan, GSAP will show up as an unidentified licence. Budget an allowlist entry.
    + SAFARI 26 CHANGES THE CALCULUS THE RESEARCHER DIDN'T ACCOUNT FOR: because Safari 26 shipped scroll-driven animations (Sept 2025), native CSS `animation-timeline: view()/scroll()` now covers ~84% of global traffic with Chrome+Edge+Safari, and Firefox is the only hold-out. That is a genuinely viable zero-JS path for the simpler reveals (station fade-ins, progress rail), with the GSAP pin+scrub reserved only for the pinned section. The research frames scroll-driven CSS as not-ready; it is closer to ready than the writeup implies, and it is the option Vercel's own guidelines push you toward (CSS > WAAPI > JS libraries).
    + OG 'VIDEO' LOOP WON'T PLAY WHERE IT MATTERS: Design B is justified partly as 'works as an OG video'. Slack, X/Twitter, LinkedIn and iMessage link previews render a static og:image; og:video autoplay is effectively limited to Twitter player cards with a whitelisted player. Plan on a static og:image (a frame of the diagram with the gate red) plus a separate hosted mp4 for the README, not an OG video.
    + rAF ALREADY PAUSES IN BACKGROUND TABS: the recommendation calls a `document.visibilityState` check 'mandatory' alongside the IntersectionObserver. requestAnimationFrame is already throttled to ~0fps in hidden tabs by every current engine; the visibilityState check is belt-and-braces. The IntersectionObserver gate (for the in-viewport-but-scrolled-past case) is the one that actually matters — exactly as Inngest's Engine.ts does it.
    + SNAP + SCRUB FIGHT EACH OTHER: `snap` with `scrub: 0.6` on a pinned trigger is a known friction point — on trackpads and momentum scrolling the snap tween competes with the scrub lag and can produce a rubber-banding feel. GSAP's docs expose `snap.directional`, `snap.inertia` and `snap.delay` for exactly this. Prototype it before committing to the 10-value snap array.
    + ACCESSIBILITY COST OF PINNING IS UNDER-STATED: a 4.6-viewport pinned scrub breaks browser find-in-page (content is fixed, not in flow), keyboard scrolling lands mid-stage, and Vercel's own guideline that 'animations are cancelable by user input' is hard to honour in a scrub. The reduced-motion fallback described (all nine stages in final state) is right, but consider also honouring it for keyboard-only navigation, not just prefers-reduced-motion.
    + `vector-effect="non-scaling-stroke"` DOES NOT NORMALISE DASHES: it scales stroke WIDTH only. Dash arrays/offsets are still computed in user-space path length, which is precisely why the pathLength="1" trick is needed — the two techniques are complementary, not redundant. Worth stating explicitly in the build doc so nobody drops pathLength thinking vector-effect covers it. (Also note Airbyte's markup as quoted used `vector-effect="nonScalingStroke"`, which is NOT a valid value — the correct token is `non-scaling-stroke`; another reason not to copy that citation.)
    + MOTION 13.x IS DAYS OLD: motion and framer-motion 13.4.1 were both published at 16:39-16:40 UTC on 2026-09-22 — i.e. hours before the research was written. A 13.x major is recent enough that ecosystem typings and React-19 interop edge cases may not be settled. Pin the exact version in package.json and do not use a caret for a hackathon build.
    + LOTTIE-WEB ISN'T 'FROZEN' SO MUCH AS STABLE: 5.13.0 (2025-05-21) is 16 months old but it remains the reference player and is still the thing dotLottie/LottieFiles interoperate with. The recommendation to prefer @lottiefiles/dotlottie-react is still right on size (33KB gz vs 76KB gz), but 'frozen since 2025' overstates abandonment.
    + THE CLEANEST 'WE USE XYFLOW' EVIDENCE IS UNKEY'S ABSENCE OF IT: having refuted the Cloudflare claim, the strongest supporting data point for the 'nobody uses React Flow for storytelling' thesis is that Unkey — which builds a genuine node graph in its dashboard — hand-rolled a layout engine and ships framer-motion with no xyflow at all. That is a better citation than Cloudflare and it is verifiable in source.
    + REFERENCE-FRAGILITY WARNING FOR THE WHOLE AREA: of the live-site fingerprints, three (Airbyte's data-flow attributes, Trigger.dev's canvas count, CodeRabbit's hex frequency ranking) already differ from what was recorded hours earlier. Any build doc that hard-codes 'copy Airbyte's attribute contract' should paste the actual markup inline rather than cite a URL, because the URL no longer reproduces it.

### AREA: Internal dashboard design — IA, screens, data density, code and diff UI (dashboard)
FINDINGS:
  - Linear's full dark token ladder is extractable from their live CSS and is the single best reference to copy: --color-bg-level-0 #08090a (page), level-1 #0f1011 (panel), level-2 #141516 (raised), level-3 #191a1b (hover); --color-bg-marketing #010102; text ladder #f7f8f8 / #d0d6e0 / #8a8f98 / #62666d; borders #23252a / #34343a / #3e3e44 AND alpha equivalents #ffffff14 / #ffffff1f / #ffffff26. They ship --border-hairline: .5px. Radii are 4/6/8/12/16/24/32px. Weights are non-standard: 400/510/590/680. Font sizes: micro .6875rem, mini .75–.8125rem, small .8125–.875rem, regular .9375rem. Fonts: 'Inter Variable' with font-feature-settings 'cv01','ss03', mono = 'Berkeley Mono'. Shadows are near-black at high alpha in dark: --shadow-high 0px 7px 32px #00000059.
  - Vercel Geist dark is the opposite bet and worth knowing: --ds-background-200 = #000 (pure black page) and --ds-background-100 = #0a0a0a (surfaces) — i.e. surfaces sit ABOVE a black floor, inverted from Linear. Geist dark grays: 100 #1a1a1a, 200 #1f1f1f, 300 #292929, 400 #2e2e2e, 500 #454545, 600 #878787, 1000 #ededed. Geist also ships every token in oklch (e.g. --ds-blue-700 oklch(57.61% .2321 258.23)) and in lab(). Scale semantics: 100–300 component backgrounds, 400–600 borders, 700–800 high-contrast backgrounds, 900–1000 text/icons.
  - Graphite.dev's marketing site ships literally unmodified shadcn/ui default dark tokens: --background #0a0a0a, --card #171717, --border #262626, --accent #262626, --chart-1 #f05100 … --chart-5 #ff2357, Inter + JetBrains Mono. CodeRabbit ships Radix Colors under a --color-cui-* prefix (amber-1 #16120c … amber-12 #ffe7b3, identical to Radix dark amber) with Geist + Lato and brand greens #19d59e / #46e1a5, orange #ff570a. Greptile uses Anybody + DM Sans + Geist Mono with an emerald grid (#10b981 at 0d/1f alpha). Conclusion: the entire competitive set is running default shadcn/Radix dark. A bespoke token layer is cheap, visible differentiation.
  - Trigger.dev publishes a complete dark 'charcoal' neutral ramp that is better tuned for dense dashboards than Tailwind neutral: 1000 #0b0c0f, 950 #0d0e12, 900 #121317, 850 #15171a, 800 #1a1b1f, 750 #212327, 700 #272a2e, 650 #31353a, 600 #3b3e45, 500 #5f6570, 400 #878c99, 350 #a4a8b0, 300 #b5b8c0, 200 #d7d9dd, 100 #e8e9ec — plus explicit grid-line tokens --color-grid-dimmed #1c1e21 and --color-grid-bright #272a2e. Note it is slightly blue-cool (hue ~250), which reads 'engineering' rather than 'neutral gray'.
  - GitHub rebuilt the PR Files-changed diff on TanStack Virtual and published hard numbers (Jan–Feb 2026 rollout, default since 2026-01-23): for 10k+ line PRs, ~10x reduction in JS heap and DOM nodes; INP went from 275–700ms to 40–80ms; a 10,000-line split diff went from 150–250MB to 80–120MB memory and ~450ms → ~100ms INP under 4x CPU throttle. They cut per-line React components from 8–13 down to 2–3 and per-line DOM nodes from ~10 (unified) / ~15 (split); they removed per-line event handlers in favour of a single top-level delegated handler keyed off data-attributes, and replaced :has() selectors and layout-forcing drag handling with GPU transforms. The experimental virtualised mode targets 3,000-file PRs.
  - Monaco is disqualified on size alone: monaco-editor@0.56.0 is 93.38 MB unpacked and pulls marked + dompurify. For comparison: @git-diff-view/react@0.1.7 = 1.25 MB, react-diff-view@3.3.3 = 1.41 MB, diff2html@3.4.56 = 1.93 MB, @codemirror/merge@6.12.2 = 0.17 MB, shiki@4.4.3 = 0.57 MB core (+ @shikijs/langs 8.25 MB and @shikijs/themes 1.41 MB, both tree-shaken per-language/per-theme).
  - @git-diff-view is the only React diff component with a pluggable HAST highlighter pipeline where Shiki is a first-class option: @git-diff-view/lowlight (highlight.js, default), @git-diff-view/shiki (TextMate/VS Code fidelity), wired via diffFile.initSyntax({ registerHighlighter }). Props that matter for us: diffViewMode Split|Unified, diffViewTheme light|dark, diffViewHighlight, diffViewWrap, diffViewFontSize, renderWidgetLine, renderExtendLine, extendData — renderExtendLine + extendData is exactly the hook needed to hang a PRGuard finding card under the offending line. MIT. Caveat: still 0.1.7 (published 2026-07-13).
  - react-diff-view@3.3.3 (MIT, last publish 2026-03-30) is the mature alternative but its highlighting is refractor v3 (Prism grammars), not Shiki. Its three useful hooks are `widgets` (keyed by change key — inline finding cards), `renderToken` (custom token rendering, the seam for injecting Shiki tokens), and `generateLineClassName({changes, defaultGenerate})` (per-line classes for severity tinting). It also ships tokenize() enhancers markEdits (diff-match-patch word-level intra-line diff), markWord and pickRanges, and is web-worker safe. gutterType accepts 'default' | 'anchor' | 'none'.
  - Shiki 4.4.3 (MIT, 2026-08-10) dual-theme is the right call for static code snippets: pass themes:{light,dark}, it emits --shiki-light / --shiki-dark (and --shiki-{name} / --shiki-{name}-bg for >2 themes) with cssVariablePrefix default '--shiki-'. defaultColor:false emits no inline colors so CSS owns everything; defaultColor:'light-dark()' uses the CSS light-dark() function but has limited browser support. Overriding inline styles requires !important. @shikijs/transformers gives transformerNotationDiff ([!code ++]/[!code --] → classes `diff add`/`diff remove`), transformerNotationHighlight ([!code highlight] → .highlighted), transformerNotationWordHighlight, transformerNotationFocus, transformerNotationErrorLevel (.error/.warning/.info), transformerMetaHighlight ({1,3-4}), transformerRenderWhitespace, transformerRenderIndentGuides, transformerRenderLineNumber, transformerStyleToClass. Transformers add classes only — you write the CSS.
  - xterm.js is the wrong tool for a static proof artifact. @xterm/xterm@6.0.0 is 5.65 MB unpacked and v6 removed the canvas renderer entirely (DOM is default, WebGL via @xterm/addon-webgl@0.19.0); it owns its own scrollback, sizing and selection, so it fights page layout, virtualization, SSR and copy-to-clipboard. The correct choice for captured stdout/stderr is anser@2.3.5 — 0.04 MB unpacked, zero dependencies, MIT — using ansiToJson with use_classes:true to emit your own CSS classes, rendered into a styled <pre> you virtualize yourself. ansi_up@6.0.6 (0.23 MB) is the sibling anser was forked from; ansi-to-html@0.7.2 is effectively abandoned (last publish 2021-10-05).
  - Charting: Recharts 3.10.1 (MIT, 2026-07-25, 7.11 MB unpacked, deps clsx/immer/reselect/es-toolkit/react-redux/eventemitter3) is the correct default because shadcn/ui's Chart layer (ChartContainer / ChartTooltip / ChartTooltipContent / ChartLegend / ChartLegendContent / ChartConfig) is built on Recharts v3 and gives CSS-variable theming (--chart-1..--chart-5, oklch values, per-theme {light,dark} config) for free. Reject: echarts@6.1.0 (Apache-2.0 but 57.5 MB unpacked, imperative/canvas), @nivo/core@0.99.0 (stale — last publish 2025-05-23), @observablehq/plot@0.6.17 (ISC, stale — 2025-02-14, imperative render-to-node), chart.js@4.5.1 (canvas, no SSR), @unovis/react@1.7.0 (Apache-2.0, 0.20 MB but requires @unovis/ts at 9.90 MB).
  - Tremor was acquired by Vercel on 2025-01-22 and all components and blocks were made free/open source; the @tremor/react npm package is now stale (latest 3.18.7, published 2025-01-13, license field 'Apache 2.0'). Use Tremor Blocks as a visual reference for dark dashboard composition, not as a dependency.
  - Temporal's Event History UI is the closest existing analogue to our hash-chained audit trail and offers four co-equal views of the same data — Timeline, All (full event table), Compact (Event Groups: ActivityTaskScheduled + Started + Completed collapse into one row spanning the activity duration), and JSON (full downloadable history). The Timeline is built on the open-source vis-timeline library wrapped in Svelte; green = Completed, red = Failed; the first row is always the overall Workflow Execution duration; retrying activities show attempt numbers inline. The lesson: give the same log three fidelities (human timeline / dense table / raw canonical JSON) and let the user download the raw form — that is what makes it read as evidence rather than as decoration.
  - Datadog's trace view gives four visualisations of one trace — Flame Graph (default, color-coded spans on a timeline, scroll-to-zoom, drag-to-pan, minimap, group-by service/host/container, toggle between % execution time and span counts), Waterfall (one row per span: bar length = relative duration, plus absolute ms, service/resource, HTTP status, expand/collapse descendants via chevrons), Span List (grouped by service, sorted by span count, filterable), and Map. Errors are encoded as red borders on entry spans and red edges on exit spans; inferred services get dashed outlines. 'Focus' narrows to a span and its children. Our 9-stage run with 4 parallel specialists is exactly a 2-level waterfall — copy the waterfall, not the flame graph.
  - Inngest's run viewer uses list + side panel with a full-page escape hatch, and the detail splits into trigger details / event payload / run details+timeline; expanded steps show the error message, timings AND every retry attempt's individual error. Trigger.dev's refreshed run timeline explicitly separates pre-execution steps from execution steps at three zoom levels (run / attempt / span) so you can see whether time went to queueing or to work — directly applicable to showing that S1 INGEST and S2 CONTEXT are cheap and S3/S7 are where the seconds go.
  - cmdk@1.1.1 (MIT, 0.08 MB, built on @radix-ui/react-dialog) is still the standard command palette. Its parts expose [cmdk-root], [cmdk-dialog], [cmdk-input], [cmdk-list], [cmdk-item], [cmdk-group], [cmdk-empty], [cmdk-loading], [cmdk-separator] data-attributes for styling and a --cmdk-list-height CSS variable for animating list height. Items accept a `keywords` prop for synonym matching; a custom filter(value, search, keywords) returns a numeric rank; shouldFilter={false} hands filtering to the server. Groups get `hidden` rather than unmounting during filtering.
  - GitHub's documented single-key shortcuts set the conventions users already have in their fingers for code review: `t` file finder, `l` jump to line, `y` canonicalise URL, `b` blame, `c` create issue (list) / open Commits dropdown (files-changed), `t` focus the 'Filter changed files' field, `u`/`l`/`m`/`a` filter by author/label/milestone/assignee, `o` or Enter to open, and ⌘⇧Enter to submit a review comment. Reusing `t`, `j/k`, `o`, `c`, `?` costs nothing and buys instant fluency.
  - TanStack Table has shipped a v9 (@tanstack/react-table 9.2.4, 2026-08-28, MIT, 0.13 MB) whose deps are now @tanstack/table-core + @tanstack/react-store, and TanStack Virtual is at 3.14.13 (2026-09-14, 0.06 MB) with a documented guide for reverse feeds (chat/AI streams/logs) — i.e. it now explicitly supports the append-at-bottom log case we need for live terminal output.
  - Real-time transport: resumable-stream@2.2.13 (ISC, zero dependencies, published 2026-09-16) is maintained by Vercel (repo vercel/resumable-stream, maintainer cramforce) and exists specifically to let a client reconnect to an in-flight SSE stream without losing output — which is the exact failure mode of demoing a 60-second pipeline run on conference wifi. Current runtime baselines if you are picking a stack today: React 19.3.0, Next 16.3.6, Vite 8.3.0, Tailwind CSS 4.3.3, TanStack Query 5.103.2, motion/framer-motion 13.4.1, radix-ui 1.6.7, react-resizable-panels 4.13.2, lucide-react 1.47.0, nuqs 2.10.1.
  - Radix Colors' 12-step contract is the right mental model for every semantic colour in the dashboard and should be stated explicitly in the token file: 1–2 app/subtle backgrounds, 3 component background, 4 hover, 5 pressed/selected, 6 subtle border on non-interactive (cards, sidebars), 7 subtle border on interactive, 8 strong border + focus ring, 9 solid (highest chroma — the fill for a severity chip), 10 solid hover, 11 low-contrast text, 12 high-contrast text, with 11 and 12 guaranteed to meet APCA contrast against step 2. Both CodeRabbit and Axiom ship Radix scales verbatim (Axiom: --background #090909, blue-1 #0d1520 … blue-12 #c2e6ff), which is why step-9 fills on step-2 backgrounds are the safest severity-chip recipe available.
REFERENCES:
  - [must-steal] Linear (app + marketing) — dark token ladder, hairlines, typography https://linear.app/now/how-we-redesigned-the-linear-ui :: STEAL: The entire neutral/elevation/border system and the typographic scale. This is the highest-quality dark dense UI shipping today and its tokens are readable straight off their CSS bundle. :: SPECIFICS: Surfaces: --color-bg-level-0 #08090a, level-1 #0f1011, level-2 #141516, level-3 #191a1b; marketing floor #010102; panel #0f1011/#161718. Secondary/tertiary/quaternary surfaces also exist as pure alphas (#ffffff08, #ffffff12, #ffffff26) so they compose over anything. Text: primary #f7f8f8, secondary #d0d6e0 (alt #b4bcd0), tertiary #8a8f98, quaternary #62666d. Borders: #23252a / #34343a / #3e3e44 or alphas #ffffff14 / #ffffff1f / #ffffff26; --border-hairline: .5px; separate line ladder --color-line-primary #37393a, secondary #202122, tertiary #18191a, tint #141516. Brand/accent: --color-brand-bg #5e6ad2 (light) / #7070ff (dark), --color-accent #7170ff, hover #828fff, tint #18182f. Status hues: red #eb5757, orange #fc7840, yellow #f0bf00, green #27a644, teal #00b8cc, blue #4ea7fc/#5eb0ff, indigo #5e6ad2. Diff colours (directly reusable): --diff-new-color #27A644 on rgba(39,166,68,0.07), --diff-old-color #F34E52 on rgba(243,78,82,0.1) — note the 7–10% tint, not 20%. Syntax tokens: text #E2E4E7, comment #8B93A1, keyword #F79CE0, string #ffdf9f, constant #8FA6FF, variable #f7bf8b, entity #83dcdc, punctuation #D2D7DE. Type: 'Inter Variable' with font-feature-settings 'cv01','ss03'; mono 'Berkeley Mono'; weights 300/400/510/590/680; sizes micro .6875rem, mini .75–.8125rem, small .8125–.875rem, regular .9375rem, title3 1.25rem, title2 1.5rem, title1 2.25rem. Radii 4/6/8/12/16/24/32 + 9999. Shadows: tiny 0 1px 1px #00000017, low 0 2px 4px #0000001a, medium 0 4px 24px #0003, high 0 7px 32px #00000059. Full easing set exposed as tokens (--ease-out-expo cubic-bezier(.19,1,.22,1), --ease-out-quint cubic-bezier(.23,1,.32,1), --ease-in-out-quart cubic-bezier(.77,0,.175,1)). Colour engine: LCH, 3 inputs per theme (base, accent, contrast) replacing 98 variables; selection colours are computed with color-mix(in lch, ...).

Also the 'black floor, lifted surfaces' inversion as a deliberate alternative to Linear. :: SPECIFICS: Dark: --ds-background-200 #000 (page), --ds-background-100 #0a0a0a (surfaces) — surfaces are LIGHTER than the page. Dark grays: 100 #1a1a1a (hsl 10%), 200 #1f1f1f (12%), 300 #292929 (16%), 400 #2e2e2e (18%), 500 #454545 (27%), 600 #878787 (53%), 1000 #ededed (93%). Every token is emitted three ways (hex, hsla(var(--x-value),1), and oklch/lab) e.g. --ds-blue-700 oklch(57.61% .2321 258.23), --ds-blue-600 oklch(64.94% .1982 251.813). Step semantics: 100–300 component backgrounds (default/hover/active), 400–600 borders (default/hover/active), 700–800 high-contrast backgrounds, 900–1000 text/icons (secondary/primary). Scales: backgrounds, gray, gray-alpha, blue, red, amber, green, teal, purple, pink.

Dedicated grid tokens: --color-grid-dimmed #1c1e21, --color-grid-bright #272a2e. Text aliases: --text-bright #e8e9ec, --text-light-dimmed #a4a8b0, --text-dimmed #878c99. Accent 'apple' green: 300 #bfff81, 400 #afff62, 500 #a8ff53, 700 #6fb12f, 800 #45711a with --color-accent-foreground #cfffa0. Timeline: three levels of detail (run level = steps before execution starts through completion; attempt level = intermediate phases; span level = e.g. a Wait), explicitly so you can separate queue time from work time. Their run page is OpenTelemetry-backed and every AI SDK call becomes a span carrying model, provider, token counts, cost and latency.
  - [must-steal] Temporal Web UI — Workflow Event History https://temporal.io/blog/lets-visualize-a-workflow :: STEAL: The four-view pattern for an event log (Timeline / All / Compact / JSON) and Event Groups. This is the direct template for PRGuard's hash-chained audit viewer. :: SPECIFICS: Views: Timeline (chronological or reverse-chronological, horizontal spans with tooltips giving start/end and ms duration; first row is always the overall execution duration; retry attempt numbers rendered inline; zoom via pinch or +/- buttons; horizontal + vertical scroll; Event Type filter from the table narrows the Timeline), All (every raw event), Compact (Event Groups — ActivityTaskScheduled + ActivityTaskStarted + ActivityTaskCompleted collapse to one Activity row spanning its duration; single events like Markers and Signals render as points; linear left-to-right by scheduled order), JSON (full history, downloadable — v2.28.0 added three download options). Colours: green = Completed, red = Failed. Implementation: open-source vis-timeline with HTML templates, wrapped in Svelte for large-history performance.
  - [must-steal] GitHub — pull request Files-changed rebuild https://github.blog/engineering/architecture-optimization/the-uphill-climb-of-making-diff-lines-performant/ :: STEAL: The exact performance architecture for rendering large diffs on the web, plus the interaction conventions reviewers already have muscle memory for. :: SPECIFICS: TanStack Virtual for diff lines. 10k+ line PRs: ~10x reduction in JS heap and DOM nodes; INP 275–700ms → 40–80ms. 10,000-line split diff: memory 150–250MB → 80–120MB, INP under 4x throttle ~450ms → ~100ms, component tree ~183,504 → ~50,004 rendered components (74% cut), per-line components 8–13 → 2–3, DOM nodes ~200,000 → ~180,000. Removed <code> wrappers on line numbers (20,000 nodes saved on a 10k-line diff). Replaced per-line handlers (20+ per line) with one delegated top-level handler reading data-attributes. O(1) Map lookups keyed commentsMap['path/to/file.tsx']['L8'] instead of O(n) scans. Removed heavy :has() selectors; drag/resize moved to GPU transforms to kill forced layout. Server hydrates only visible lines. Rollout: public preview 2025-06-26, default 2026-01-22/23, CODEOWNERS + perf 2026-02-05, side-by-side code+comments 2026-03-19, refreshed repo PR list 2026-09-10; experimental virtualised mode targets 3,000 files. Shortcuts: t file finder / filter changed files, l jump to line, b blame, y canonical URL, c commits dropdown, ⌘⇧Enter submit review comment, u/l/m/a list filters, o or Enter to open.

Highlighter is swapped through a unified HAST pipeline: const highlighter = await getDiffViewHighlighter(); diffFile.initSyntax({ registerHighlighter: highlighter }). Props: diffViewMode Split|Unified (default Split), diffViewTheme light|dark, diffViewHighlight, diffViewWrap, diffViewFontSize (px), diffViewAddWidget, renderWidgetLine, renderExtendLine, extendData, onAddWidgetClick; input via `data` (raw) or `diffFile` (preprocessed). Web Worker and Node/SSR supported; 'template mode' on by default. Risk: still 0.1.x; a @git-diff-view/lezer package is described in the README but I could not find it published on the npm registry.

API: parseDiff(text, {nearbySequences:'zip'}), <Diff viewType='split'|'unified' diffType hunks widgets selectedChanges gutterType='default'|'anchor'|'none' renderToken customEvents customClassNames optimizeSelection>, <Hunk gutterEvents codeEvents> (callbacks receive {change, side}), <Decoration> for inter-hunk headers, renderGutter({change, side, renderDefault, wrapInAnchor, inHoverState}), generateLineClassName({changes, defaultGenerate}). tokenize(hunks, {highlight:true, refractor, language, oldSource, enhancers}) with enhancers markEdits(hunks,{type}) (word-level intra-line diff via diff-match-patch), markWord(word,name,replacement), pickRanges(oldRanges,newRanges). Transformers and the classes they emit: transformerNotationDiff ([!code ++]/[!code --] → `diff add` / `diff remove`, matchAlgorithm v1|v3), transformerNotationHighlight ([!code highlight] / [!code highlight:N] → .highlighted), transformerNotationWordHighlight ([!code word:Text:N] → .highlighted-word), transformerNotationFocus (.focused), transformerNotationErrorLevel (.error/.warning/.info), transformerMetaHighlight ({1,3-4}), transformerMetaWordHighlight (/Hello/), transformerRenderWhitespace (all|boundary|trailing|leading → .tab/.space), transformerRenderIndentGuides (.indent), transformerRenderLineNumber, transformerStyleToClass, transformerRemoveComments, transformerRemoveLineBreak. Transformers apply classes only; you supply the CSS.

Parse ANSI server-or-client side into tokens, render your own spans, keep full control of typography, selection, virtualization and theming. :: SPECIFICS: v2.3.5, MIT, published 2025-12-15, 0.04 MB unpacked, zero dependencies. API: ansiToJson(text, {use_classes:true, json:true, remove_empty:true}) returns entries with {content, fg, bg, fg_truecolor, bg_truecolor, decoration, decorations}; ansiToHtml(text, {use_classes}) for a string path; linkify option turns bare URLs into anchors; escapeForHtml sanitises; supports 256-colour and 24-bit truecolour; use_classes emits CSS classes (ansi-red-fg etc.) instead of inline styles, which is what you want for theming. Our 9-stage run with 4 parallel S3 specialists is a two-level waterfall, not a flame graph. :: SPECIFICS: Waterfall row = bar whose length is relative duration + absolute ms + service/resource name + HTTP status, colour-coded by service/host/container, descendants expand/collapse via chevrons. Flame graph: scroll to zoom, drag to pan, minimap for navigation, group by service/base service/host/container, toggle between % execution time and span counts. 'Focus' narrows to a span plus its children. Search highlights matching spans; boolean, group and range expressions supported; no wildcards.
