Purpose per its own description: 'Library for resuming and following streams in distributed systems'. Pairs with a Redis-style pub/sub backing store in the documented usage; I did not verify the storage requirements first-hand.
RECOMMENDATIONS:
  - (high) Ship a bespoke token layer, not shadcn defaults. Build a three-file system: primitives.css (a 15-step cool-neutral ramp modelled on Trigger.dev's charcoal, authored in oklch), semantic.css (surface-0..3, border-subtle/default/strong, text-1..4, and PRGuard's own status families), and product.css (severity bands + proof states). Concretely: --surface-0 oklch(0.145 0.006 255) ≈ #0A0B0D (page), --surface-1 oklch(0.176 0.007 255) ≈ #101215 (panel/sidebar), --surface-2 oklch(0.205 0.008 255) ≈ #16181C (card/raised), --surface-3 oklch(0.238 0.009 255) ≈ #1D2025 (hover/selected); borders as pure alpha so they compose over any surface: --border-subtle oklch(1 0 0 / 0.06), --border-default oklch(1 0 0 / 0.10), --border-strong oklch(1 0 0 / 0.16); text --text-1 #F5F7FA, --text-2 #C6CCD6, --text-3 #8A929E, --text-4 #5E6672. Single chromatic accent: oklch(0.70 0.17 172) ≈ #17C9A4 (a verification teal-green, deliberately not violet and not CodeRabbit's #19d59e). Use Linear's hairline trick (--border-hairline: .5px) on table separators at >=2dppx. | WHY: Graphite ships raw shadcn dark (#0a0a0a / #262626 / --chart-1..5) and CodeRabbit ships raw Radix. Every judge has seen both palettes dozens of times this year. A custom ramp costs one afternoon and is the single cheapest signal that PRGuard is a product rather than a weekend scaffold — and it is visible in the first 400ms, before anyone reads a word. | TRADEOFF: You lose the ability to paste shadcn blocks unmodified, and you have to hand-tune contrast (target APCA Lc >= 60 for body text on surface-0/1, Lc >= 90 for primary text). Budget half a day for the ramp plus a contrast pass. Mitigation: keep shadcn's component structure and only swap the token values, so blocks still drop in with a find-and-replace.
  - (high) Make the run-detail screen (/runs/:id) the hero and build it as a fixed 9-slot horizontal stage rail above a two-level vertical waterfall — and use the exact same 9-node component on the marketing site. Stage rail: 9 equal slots, each with index, label, state (pending / running / done / degraded / failed) and duration; the running node gets a 1.5s pulse on its ring only and a 1px animated gradient sweep along the connector to the next node — never a spinner. Below it, a Datadog-style waterfall where S3 SPECIALISTS expands to four parallel child rows (bugs, security, tests, quality) with bar length = duration and left offset = start time. Right rail carries live counters (found / dropped / deduped / suppressed / proven), a ticking cost readout (usd_estimate, input, output, cache_read with a cache-hit %), and the degradations list. Bottom carries the delivery ladder rendered as a literal ladder (inline_exact → inline_nearest → file_level → summary_only) with a count on each rung and a '0 silently dropped' badge, then the gate ladder (check_run → commit_status → dry_run) showing which rung fired. | WHY: The pipeline is the product's credibility. Four parallel specialists is an invisible claim until you draw it as four bars starting at the same x. Reusing one component across marketing and dashboard means the shape a judge sees in the hero animation is the shape they see in the demo 90 seconds later — that repetition is what makes a hackathon entry read as a real product with a design system rather than two disconnected artifacts. | TRADEOFF: A fixed 9-slot rail does not generalise if the pipeline ever becomes dynamic; you would need a fallback layout. The shared component must be written framework-agnostically or duplicated. Also, horizontal rails compress badly below ~900px — you need a stacked mobile variant, though for an internal dashboard that is an acceptable second-class citizen.
  - (high) For the finding detail, use master-detail split inside a run plus a canonical full page at /findings/:id. Explicitly ban slide-over sheets for findings. A finding carries markdown, a diff, a six-input severity breakdown and two terminal captures — 900–1400px of vertical content across three interaction modes. Terminal output at 80–120 columns needs ~940px at 13px mono (~7.8px/char); a 480–600px sheet forces horizontal truncation of the single artifact the whole product is built on. Inside the detail, do NOT use tabs — use one scroll with sticky section headers in the fixed order Claim → Location → Why this score → Proof → Identity → Audit, and auto-anchor to Proof when arriving from a 'proven' badge. Implement the split with react-resizable-panels 4.13.2, persist sizes, and give the handle keyboard resize. | WHY: Tabs hide the proof, and the proof is the positioning. The narrative order 'we claim X / here is where / here is why it scored 0.82 / here is the failing test' is the demo script; making it a single scroll means the demo is literally one gesture. Slide-overs are also a trap for deep-linking and for the audit story — a finding must be a shareable URL because it is evidence. | TRADEOFF: A single long scroll means the severity breakdown and the proof compete for the same vertical space; you need a sticky mini-header showing severity + proof badge + fingerprint so context never scrolls away. Master-detail also costs you roughly 380px of horizontal room, so the diff inside the detail pane will usually be unified rather than split.
  - (high) Diff UI: use @git-diff-view/react@0.1.7 with @git-diff-view/shiki for anything that is a real git diff, and server-rendered Shiki 4.4.3 (dual theme, transformerNotationHighlight) for standalone snippets like the generated repro test. Anchor findings to lines with renderExtendLine + extendData, tint the offending lines at 7–10% alpha (Linear's ratio: --diff-new-bg rgba(39,166,68,0.07), --diff-old-bg rgba(243,78,82,0.1)) with a 2px left accent bar in the severity hue. Fall back to react-diff-view@3.3.3 (widgets + renderToken + generateLineClassName) if the 0.1.x API bites. Virtualize with TanStack Virtual above ~400 lines. Reject Monaco outright (93.38 MB unpacked, editor affordances, focus traps), reject diff2html (string→HTML, needs dangerouslySetInnerHTML, no React widget model), and reject CodeMirror merge (it is a two-document editor merge view, not a multi-file PR diff surface). | WHY: The finding must appear inline in the diff at its exact line, with a severity tint and a proof badge — that is a widget-per-line requirement, and only @git-diff-view (renderExtendLine/extendData) and react-diff-view (widgets) expose it. Shiki gives VS Code-grade grammar fidelity, which matters because reviewers will look closely at the exact line you are accusing. The 7–10% tint is the detail that separates a designed diff from a Bootstrap one — most implementations use 20–25% and the code becomes unreadable. | TRADEOFF: @git-diff-view is 0.1.7 from a single maintainer; API churn is a real risk and there is no LTS. Its README mentions a @git-diff-view/lezer package I could not find published, which suggests docs run ahead of releases. Mitigate by wrapping it in one PRGuardDiff component so a swap to react-diff-view touches one file. Shiki's langs bundle is 8.25 MB unpacked, so you must import only the 6–10 languages you support, not the barrel.
  - (high) Render the proof terminal with anser@2.3.5 into your own virtualized <pre>, never xterm.js — and render base SHA and head SHA as a synced two-column split with a verdict banner between them. Parse with ansiToJson(text, {use_classes:true, json:true}) and map tokens to CSS classes so the terminal inherits your theme. Strip \r-rewrites, \x1b[2K erase-line and cursor-movement sequences server-side during capture so progress bars collapse to their final frame. Typography: 12.5–13px mono at line-height 1.6 (20–21px rows), tabular-nums, a 40px gutter of dimmed line numbers, and a hard max of ~1200px content width with horizontal scroll rather than wrapping (wrapping destroys stack-trace alignment). Give every terminal a toolbar with copy-raw, download-raw, wrap toggle, and a search that highlights in place. Colour the split: base pane border-left 2px in the proven-green, head pane border-left 2px in the critical-red, and put a single full-width banner between them reading 'passes at base a91f3c2 · fails at head 4e8b1d0 · PROVEN'. | WHY: xterm.js@6.0.0 is 5.65 MB, removed its canvas renderer in v6, owns its own scrollback and sizing, and fights SSR, page virtualization, text selection and theming. None of that buys anything for a static artifact. anser is 0.04 MB with zero deps and gives you tokens, which means you control every pixel — and this artifact is the single most-photographed screen in the demo. The two-column base/head split is the visual form of 'we PROVE it', and it is not a layout any competitor currently ships. | TRADEOFF: You give up true terminal emulation: anything relying on cursor addressing (curses UIs, live-redrawing spinners) will render as garbage unless you sanitise at capture time, so the backend capture path needs to own that normalisation. You also hand-roll search and selection. If PRGuard ever adds an interactive shell into the sandbox, you will need xterm.js then — keep the terminal behind one component boundary.
  - (high) Visualise the M2 severity score as a horizontal weighted contribution bar, not a radar. Six segments whose widths are value x weight (reachability .20, blast_radius .15, path_sensitivity .25, coverage_gap .15, churn .10, proof_confidence .15) so the bar's total length IS the score and the band thresholds (.15 / .35 / .55 / .75) render as four vertical tick marks across it. Under it, a six-row table with columns input / raw 0..1 / weight / contribution, all tabular-nums to three decimals. Above it, render the generated sentence ('reachable from an unauthenticated route x 7 callers x untested x payments path x proven') as a 15px pull-quote — that sentence is the product, give it typographic weight. Keep a 20px radar only as a shape-glyph in list rows for at-a-glance similarity, never as the explanation. | WHY: Radar area scales as the square of the radius, so radars systematically misrepresent magnitude and invite the exact 'this is vibes' criticism PRGuard exists to refute. A stacked bar that literally sums to the score is self-verifying: a viewer can check the arithmetic by eye. Showing the threshold ticks on the same bar answers 'why is this critical and not high?' without a legend. | TRADEOFF: A six-segment stacked bar needs six distinguishable colours, which fights the 'one accent hue' discipline. Solve it with one hue at six lightness steps plus a hatch pattern on proof_confidence (the only input that is about the system's own certainty), rather than six hues. Also, small contributions (churn at .10 x 0.2 = 0.02) become sub-pixel; enforce a 3px minimum segment width and label on hover.
  - (high) Build the audit viewer as a vertical chain rail with per-row recomputation, and design the BROKEN state first. Left gutter: a continuous 2px vertical rail with a filled link glyph at each event; verified segments are solid in the proven-green, and a break at seq N replaces the rail below that point with a red dashed line plus a full-bleed red band reading 'CHAIN BROKEN AT SEQ 1428'. Row anatomy: seq (mono, tabular), timestamp (absolute + relative), actor with a human-vs-bot glyph, event-type chip, and a truncated hash rendered as first-8 + ellipsis + last-4 (a91f3c2e…8b4d) with click-to-copy. Expanding a row reveals prev_hash, the canonical JSON (Shiki, read-only), the recomputed hash, and an explicit per-row check showing sha256(prev_hash + canonical_json) == event_hash. Header strip: '1,428 events · chain verified · root a91f…8b4d · verified 3s ago' with a shield; when chain.verified is false the whole strip turns red, shows broken_at_seq, and the list auto-scrolls to it. Offer a 'download raw history (JSON)' button, copying Temporal. | WHY: Temporal proves the pattern: give the same log a human timeline, a dense table, and a downloadable canonical JSON, and it reads as evidence. GitHub's binary 'Verified' badge proves that a single high-contrast, clickable-for-detail state is more trustworthy than a probability. Per-row hash recomputation shown in the UI is the moment a judge believes the chain is real rather than a schema field — and designing the broken state first is what makes the verified state mean something. | TRADEOFF: Client-side recomputation is partly theatre unless you actually implement SHA-256 over the canonical JSON in the browser (Web Crypto makes this ~20 lines, so do it). Canonical JSON serialisation must match the backend exactly or you will show false breaks — pin the canonicalisation rules (key order, whitespace, unicode escaping) in a shared spec and test both sides against the same fixtures. Also resist any 3D chain-link or blockchain imagery; it reads as crypto and destroys the credibility you just built.
  - (medium) Real-time: SSE for the single live run screen, adaptive polling everywhere else, and make 'since last output' a first-class visible number rather than a spinner. On /runs/:id open an EventSource against a FastAPI text/event-stream endpoint with Last-Event-ID reconnection, and wrap it with resumable-stream (Vercel, ISC, v2.2.13, zero deps) so a mid-run reload rejoins rather than restarts. Everywhere else use TanStack Query 5.103.2 with refetchInterval as a function of the data: 1000ms while status is running, back off to 5000ms once heartbeat age exceeds 15s, return false on any terminal state. Render heartbeat age as a live 1Hz ticker with explicit thresholds — under 10s in text-3, 10–30s in amber, over 30s in red with a 'stalled?' affordance and a Re-run action. Show 'Stage 3/9 — specialists · 12s since last output' as literal text, exactly as the backend already emits it. | WHY: The backend already emits heartbeat plus stage_index/stage_total/stage_label, so the expensive part is done; the design decision is to expose the raw numbers instead of hiding them behind an indeterminate spinner. A visible, honest '12s since last output' is a trust signal that matches the product's entire thesis — it is the UI admitting what it does and does not know. SSE over WebSocket because the flow is one-way, it rides the same FastAPI app and the same auth, it survives proxies, and it reconnects for free. | TRADEOFF: SSE over HTTP/1.1 is capped at ~6 concurrent connections per origin, which matters if a user opens many run tabs (HTTP/2 removes this; verify your deploy terminates HTTP/2). resumable-stream needs a shared store (Redis-style pub/sub in its documented usage) — that is infrastructure you may not want for a hackathon, so make SSE additive: build polling first, ship it working, then layer SSE on the one demo screen. Do not let the transport become the critical path.
  - (high) Design the tables for density and keyboard, and make dismissal a four-keystroke flow in the command palette. Row height 36px default with a 28px compact toggle; body 13px / .8125rem, metadata 12px, chips and micro-labels 11px / .6875rem; line-height 1.4; tabular-nums on every number. Hairline separators at oklch(1 0 0 / 0.06) — never zebra striping. Hover oklch(1 0 0 / 0.03), selected oklch(1 0 0 / 0.06) plus a 2px left accent bar. Sticky header at surface-1 with backdrop-blur(8px) and a 1px bottom border. Findings columns: [checkbox][severity band][proof glyph][title, 1-line truncate][file:line, mono, middle-truncate][category][score, 3dp][age][status], freezing the first four. Cursor pagination with an explicit 'Showing 1–50 of 1,284' and a Load more button — never infinite scroll (it breaks deep links, browser find, and 'jump to finding 400'). Saved views are URL-first via nuqs 2.10.1, then nameable. Bulk suppress caps at 50, requires one reason, lists exactly which N will be affected, and writes N audit events. Shortcuts reuse GitHub's muscle memory: j/k move, o or Enter open, / focus filter, t file/repo finder, c comment, ? cheatsheet, ⌘K palette, ⌘B sidebar, e suppress, x select, ⌘Enter open in new tab. The palette must nest: typing 'suppress' pushes a sub-page collecting reason, scope (line|file|pattern|repo|tenant) and expiry without the mouse — then show the resulting audit event appearing live in the rail. | WHY: Keyboard-first suppression with a mandatory reason is M3 and M4 demonstrated in four seconds, and it is the flow no competitor has because none of them have a ledger. Reusing GitHub's key bindings means reviewers are fluent immediately. Hairlines over zebra because zebra striping at 36px rows in dark mode creates a visible moiré and reads as a 2010s spreadsheet; every reference product in this study (Linear, Vercel, Axiom, Trigger) uses hairlines. | TRADEOFF: Freezing four columns costs horizontal room and needs care with the sticky header's z-index and the shadow on the frozen edge. A nested palette is meaningfully more work than a flat one (state machine, back behaviour on Backspace, per-page placeholder text) — roughly a day. Bulk actions on an append-only ledger are inherently risky; the 50-cap and the explicit affected-list are the guardrails, and there must be a revoke path since the ledger never deletes.
  - (high) Charts: Recharts 3.10.1 wrapped in the shadcn ChartContainer pattern, and pick chart types that foreground uncertainty rather than hide it. Specifically: execution_verified_precision as a large tabular number with a Wilson score confidence interval drawn as a bar with an ErrorBar and the n printed underneath in 11px — never a bare percentage. proven_rate vs disproven_rate vs inconclusive vs not_attempted over time as a 100% stacked area by week (Recharts AreaChart with stackId). human_dismissal_rate by category as a horizontal bar sorted descending. cost per review as a histogram with p50 and p95 ReferenceLines, never a mean. Per-stage latency as a stacked horizontal bar across the 9 stages showing p50 and p95. Every metric card carries its definition as an always-visible one-line subtitle (not a hover tooltip) and its caveats in a strip, because the spec requires caveats and hiding them behind hover is the same sin as an LLM adjective. Reject ECharts (57.5 MB unpacked, imperative canvas), Nivo (stale since 2025-05-23), Observable Plot (stale since 2025-02-14, imperative), Chart.js (no SSR), and @unovis (Apache-2.0, but @unovis/ts is 9.9 MB and the React layer is thin). | WHY: A self-reported accuracy page is only a trust asset if the uncertainty is louder than the number. Showing a Wilson interval and an n next to 'execution-verified precision' is a differentiator no competitor offers, and it is exactly on-thesis: the product that refutes its own findings should also publish its own error bars. Recharts is the default because shadcn's chart layer already themes it from your CSS variables, so charts inherit the bespoke token ramp for free. | TRADEOFF: Recharts is SVG and will struggle past ~5,000 points; for the histogram bin server-side rather than streaming raw values. Its dependency list (immer, reselect, react-redux, eventemitter3, es-toolkit) is heavier than a hand-rolled visx composition, and 7.11 MB unpacked is not small — if bundle size becomes a problem, visx 4.0.0 (@visx/xychart is 0.41 MB) is the escape hatch at the cost of writing axes, tooltips and legends yourself. Confidence intervals also invite the question 'what is n?' on every metric, so the API must actually return n for all six.
  - (high) Shell/IA: a 48px top bar plus a 224px collapsible left rail (52px icon-only, persisted, ⌘B), with the breadcrumb acting as the switcher — 'PRGuard ▸ acme ▸ api-gateway ▸ #4821' where every segment is a combobox, Vercel-style. Rail groups: Overview / Runs / Findings / Repos — separator — a group explicitly LABELLED 'Governance' containing Suppressions / Audit / Accuracy — Settings pinned to the bottom. Full route inventory: / (fleet overview: live runs strip, 7d gate outcomes, proven-vs-disproven trend, cost, top repos by blocked findings); /runs (list) and /runs/:id (the 9-stage hero, sub-tabs Pipeline | Findings | Delivery | Cost & timing | Raw payload); /findings and /findings/:id; /suppressions (ledger, with a revoked-not-deleted filter); /audit (chain viewer, deep-linkable to a seq); /accuracy; /repos and /repos/:owner/:name (PR list, risk-tier defaults, policy) and /repos/:owner/:name/pulls/:number (all runs for one PR, run-to-run finding delta); /settings/{organization,members,policy,integrations,api-keys,cost-caps}; plus /login and /onboarding/install-app. | WHY: PRGuard's data is inherently three-level scoped (org → repo → PR), which is exactly the case where a breadcrumb-as-switcher beats a sidebar dropdown — it keeps the current scope and the ability to change it in the same glyph. Naming the second rail group 'Governance' sells M4 for free, every time anyone looks at the sidebar, at a cost of one string. A thin top bar plus a thin rail preserves vertical room for the waterfall and horizontal room for the terminal, which are the two screens that actually need it. | TRADEOFF: Combobox breadcrumbs are more work than a dropdown and need careful keyboard semantics (each segment must be reachable and typeable). Two chrome regions cost ~48px of height that a pure-sidebar layout would keep. Twelve route families is a lot of surface for a hackathon — if you must cut, cut /repos/:owner/:name/pulls/:number and the settings sub-pages first; never cut /audit or /accuracy, since those two screens are the positioning made visible.
  - (medium) Typography: Inter Variable for UI with font-feature-settings 'cv01','ss03' (Linear's exact settings — cv01 gives the single-storey lowercase g and ss03 the open-tail curves, which together de-Bootstrap Inter), Geist Mono or JetBrains Mono for code and all hashes, and font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums applied globally to tables, metric cards, timestamps, durations, costs and every hash. Set the mono at 12.5–13px / 1.6 in the terminal and the diff, and render every sha256 as first-8 + ellipsis + last-4 in mono with click-to-copy. Do not license Berkeley Mono for this. | WHY: Inter without feature settings is the single most recognisable 'generic SaaS' tell; two OpenType features cost nothing and change the texture of every label. Tabular numerals are the difference between a table that scans and one that shimmers — this is the highest ratio of perceived polish to effort in the entire dashboard. Geist Mono ships under the SIL Open Font License in the `geist` npm package (v1.7.2, which exports ./font/mono, ./font/sans and ./font/pixel); @fontsource-variable/jetbrains-mono 5.3.0 is OFL-1.1. | TRADEOFF: Linear's mono is Berkeley Mono, which is commercially licensed per-seat/per-domain — I could not retrieve its terms (the vendor page returned 403), so do not assume a free tier. Using Geist Mono makes you visually adjacent to Vercel and to CodeRabbit (which also uses Geist Sans), so differentiate on the neutral ramp and accent hue rather than on the typeface. Inter's cv01/ss03 features require the variable font with feature support, not a subsetted static cut.
ANTI-PATTERNS / AVOID:
  - Shipping shadcn/ui's default dark tokens unmodified. Graphite.dev literally serves --background:#0a0a0a, --card:#171717, --border:#262626, --accent:#262626 and --chart-1:#f05100 through --chart-5:#ff2357 on its production marketing site. If your dashboard's computed styles match those strings, a judge who has opened DevTools on three other entries this weekend has already seen it. This is the single most common tell in 2026.
  - Zebra striping table rows. At 36px rows on a dark background, alternating fills create a visible banding artefact and read as a 2010s admin template. Every reference product studied here — Linear, Vercel, Axiom, Trigger.dev, Temporal — uses a single-pixel (or half-pixel) hairline separator at 5–10% white alpha. Nobody serious stripes.
  - Glassmorphism as texture. backdrop-filter: blur() on every card is a 2021 tell, costs real GPU time at scale, and destroys the legibility of 12px text over a busy backdrop. Blur is a focus device — sticky headers, modal scrims, the command palette — nowhere else.
  - The 135deg purple-to-blue gradient primary button, and its cousin the purple-to-pink hero. Indigo→violet is now the default 'AI product' costume; it signals 'I picked the first Tailwind gradient' rather than 'I made a decision'. If you use a gradient at all, use it on one surface, at one angle, once per screen.
  - Indeterminate spinners for a pipeline whose stages you already know. The backend emits stage_index/stage_total/stage_label and a heartbeat. Rendering that as a rotating circle actively throws away the product's best trust signal. Never show a spinner for anything that takes more than ~800ms and has known structure.
  - Radar/spider charts as the primary explanation of the M2 severity score. Radar area scales as the square of the radius, so a 0.6 looks more than twice as big as a 0.3; that is precisely the 'trust me, it's serious' handwaving PRGuard exists to replace. Radars also misread when the axis order is arbitrary, which it is here.
  - Donut and pie charts for proven/disproven/inconclusive/not_attempted splits. Four-segment donuts with a big number in the hole are the default dashboard cliché and are strictly worse than a stacked bar for comparing across time or across repos.
  - Emoji as severity or status icons (🔴 🟠 🟡 ✅ ⚠️). They render inconsistently across platforms, break in dark mode, cannot be recoloured, and instantly read as a script's stdout rather than a designed interface. Use a single-weight icon set (lucide-react 1.47.0, ISC) and colour it with your tokens.
  - Monaco Editor for read-only code. 93.38 MB unpacked, drags in marked and dompurify, installs editor affordances (cursor, focus trap, context menu, IntelliSense scaffolding) that are wrong for evidence you are meant to read, and fights page scroll. It is the most expensive way to be worse.
  - xterm.js for a static captured log. 5.65 MB, owns its own scrollback and sizing, cannot be server-rendered, fights page virtualization, and makes copy/select awkward. It is a terminal emulator; you have a text artifact.
  - Rounded-2xl (16px) and larger radii on dense UI chrome. Marketing-site radii on a data table make rows look like chat bubbles and waste 8px of horizontal room per cell. Linear's dense surfaces live at 4–8px; reserve 12–32px for marketing-scale cards.
  - Non-tabular numerals anywhere a number changes or stacks. Costs, durations, scores, token counts, seq numbers, timestamps. Proportional digits make a live-updating cost readout visibly jitter, which reads as sloppiness in exactly the surface that is supposed to look precise.
  - "No data available" / "Nothing here yet" empty states. Every empty state in this product has a better sentence available: 'No findings above medium in this run — 4 were dropped by the coordinator as speculative. Show them.' An empty state is a routing opportunity, not an apology.
  - Infinite scroll on the findings list. It breaks deep links, breaks browser find-in-page, makes 'go to finding 400' impossible, orphans the footer, and interacts badly with a mutating feed. Use cursor pagination with an explicit count and a Load more button.
  - Animating everything. Motion inside a dense table is noise and makes scanning slower; row-enter animations on a live-updating list are actively hostile. Reserve motion for exactly three places: the pipeline stage rail, the chain-verification moment, and the command palette. Everything else gets a 120ms opacity change or nothing.
  - Dark mode implemented as a sprinkle of Tailwind dark: variants with no semantic token layer, or worse, as filter: invert(). You get unpredictable contrast, uncontrollable borders, and no way to tune elevation. Build surface-0..3 and border-subtle/default/strong first, then never write a raw colour in a component.
  - Showing an LLM-produced confidence percentage ('87% confident this is a bug'). This directly contradicts PRGuard's entire positioning — the whole thesis is that severity is computed and claims are executed, not asserted. Any float that came out of a model and is displayed as precision is a self-inflicted wound.
  - Surfacing raw model reasoning / chain-of-thought as a 'why' panel. It is long, non-deterministic, unverifiable, and it is exactly the 'more opinions' the product claims to be an alternative to. The 'why' panel is the six weighted inputs and the proof terminal.
  - Six unrelated hues for the six M2 severity inputs because it 'looks colourful'. Use one hue at six lightness steps plus one hatch pattern for proof_confidence. Multi-hue categorical palettes on a six-segment bar make it impossible to compare segment widths.
  - A sidebar with a tall logo lockup, a marketing CTA, and an upgrade banner. Internal tools get a 48px top bar and a 224px rail; every pixel spent on brand furniture is a pixel stolen from the waterfall and the terminal, which are the only two screens anyone will remember.
  - Hiding metric definitions and caveats behind hover tooltips on the /accuracy page. The spec requires every metric to ship its definition, its n and its caveats. Putting them behind hover is the same category of dishonesty as an LLM adjective — render the definition as an always-visible one-line subtitle.
  - Any 3D chain-link, block, or ledger imagery for the audit trail. It reads as crypto, it is the first thing a skeptical judge will roll their eyes at, and it undercuts a genuinely rigorous feature. The chain is a 2px vertical rail and a per-row hash check. That is all it should ever be.
FACT-CHECK CORRECTIONS:
    [CORRECTED] Berkeley Mono is commercially licensed with no confirmed free tier; vendor page returned 403 so unverified. Elsewhere described as 'per-seat/per-domain'. -> Retrieved the vendor pages via the Wayback Machine (usgraphics.com is Cloudflare-challenged, hence the 403). Three corrections. (1) There IS a free tier: a 'Trial / Trial Use' license and a free 'Trial Font Compiler' (SKU MX-001/TS-023) are listed. (2) Licensing is NOT per-seat/per-domain — it is tiered by COMPANY SIZE: Developer/Personal $75 (FX-102, commercial use not covered); Indie/sole proprietor $225 (FX-202); Startup 2-7 employees $495 (FX-400); Standard Business 7-50 employees $845 (FX-600); Enterprise 50+ is RFQ (FX-700), and only the Enterprise tier has a per-seat SKU (MX-035 'Enterprise Seats'). (3) Crucially, WEB FONTS ARE A SEPARATE PAID MODULE, not included: Developer +$45, Indie +$145, Startup +$295, Standard Business +$45.00/MONTH (a subscription). The two terms that actually kill this for PRGuard were missed entirely: 'Commercial licenses are not compatible with open-source apps' and 'Commercial use restricted to UI elements only. If you're building an IDE, Terminal app, Text Editor, etc., we generally do not allow it.' PRGuard's hero screen is a terminal-output view — this needs a written exception from the vendor. The recommendation 'do not license Berkeley Mono' is correct; the stated reason is not.
    [CORRECTED] All proposed oklch values for PRGuard's ramp are approximations; hex equivalents are indicative. E.g. --surface-0 oklch(0.145 0.006 255) ≈ #0A0B0D and accent oklch(0.70 0.17 172) ≈ #17C9A4. -> The surface ramp is fine — I converted all four and each is off by exactly 1/255 per channel (oklch(0.145 0.006 255)=#090A0D vs claimed #0A0B0D; 0.176→#0F1114 vs #101215; 0.205→#15171B vs #16181C; 0.238→#1C1F23 vs #1D2025). Invisible, ship it. But the ACCENT is materially wrong and will bite: oklch(0.70 0.17 172) converts to #00BF92, NOT #17C9A4. At L=0.70 and hue 172, chroma 0.17 is at/past the sRGB gamut boundary, so it clips to a much more saturated cyan-green than the hex implies. #17C9A4 is roughly oklch(0.745 0.129 169). Since the entire rationale for this accent is 'deliberately not CodeRabbit's #19d59e', note that the value as written (#00BF92) drifts further from #19d59e while also being a different colour from the one specified in the same sentence. Pick one representation and derive the other, and gamut-check every chromatic token before authoring.
    [CORRECTED] GitHub diff performance numbers (10x heap/DOM reduction, INP 275-700ms → 40-80ms, 150-250MB → 80-120MB, 183,504 → 50,004 components) are self-reported vendor numbers. -> The numbers are real and the vendor-source caveat is right, but two distinct experiments have been conflated into one. The post (published 2026-04-03) reports (a) a v1→v2 REWRITE on a 10,000-line split diff: components ~183,504 → ~50,004 (74%), memory 150-250 MB → 80-120 MB, INP ~450 ms → ~100 ms under throttle, per-line React components 8-13 → 2-3, component types 19 → 10, LOC 2,800 → 2,000; and (b) VIRTUALIZATION via TanStack Virtual on p95+ largest PRs: '10X reduction' in JavaScript heap, INP 275-700+ ms → 40-80 ms. The '10x heap AND DOM nodes' phrasing is wrong: DOM nodes fell only ~10%, from ~200,000 to ~180,000, and that was the rewrite, not virtualization. Does not change the recommendation (virtualize above ~400 lines), but do not quote '10x fewer DOM nodes' in a demo.
    [CORRECTED] Datadog trace-view specifics (red borders on entry spans, dashed outlines with purple backgrounds for inferred services, no wildcard support in span search) may be stale. -> Live docs confirm almost all of it, with one precision error. Confirmed: 'a service entry span is in an error state, the corresponding service node is marked with a red border', error on a service exit span highlights the outgoing edge red; 'Wildcards are not supported in the Trace search bar'; waterfall row = colour-coded relative-duration bar + absolute ms + service/resource + HTTP status, Color-by service/host/container; flame graph scroll-to-zoom, drag-to-pan, minimap, group by Service/Base Service/Host/Container, toggle %-exec-time vs span counts; Focus narrows to a span plus children; Span List groups by service, sorts by span count, sortable by RESOURCE/SPANS/AVG DURATION/EXEC TIME/% EXEC TIME. CORRECTION: inferred-service styling is surface-specific — dashed OUTLINE in flame graphs, dashed UNDERLINE in waterfall, and dashed outline WITH purple background only on Trace Maps. The study attributes the purple background to the waterfall/trace view generally, which is wrong.
    [REFUTED] APCA targets Lc >= 60 for body text and Lc >= 90 for primary are my own working guidance; Radix only claims steps 11 and 12 'guarantee sufficient APCA contrast against step 2' without stating thresholds. -> Radix states the exact numbers, and they are exactly the researcher's numbers. Verbatim from the page they cited: 'Steps 11 and 12—which are designed for text—are guaranteed to Lc 60 and Lc 90 APCA contrast ratio on top of a step 2 background from the same scale.' So Lc 60 for low-contrast/body text and Lc 90 for high-contrast/primary text is published, citable Radix guidance, not personal working guidance. Good news for the recommendation: the contrast pass now has an external authority behind it, and the confidence on that tradeoff should go up rather than down. (Also confirmed on the same page: step 9 is 'the highest chroma of all steps'; 1-2 backgrounds, 3/4/5 component bg/hover/pressed, 6/7/8 borders, 9/10 solid + hover, 11/12 text.)
    [UNVERIFIABLE] Semgrep's and Snyk's triage/ignore-reason taxonomies could not be verified (docs 404'd), so no claims were made about them. -> I could not verify these either — the web-search budget for this session was exhausted before I reached them, and I have no independent evidence about Semgrep's or Snyk's ignore-reason taxonomies, expiry semantics or audit behaviour. The researcher's restraint is correct and I am not able to improve on it. Any suppression-ledger competitive claim genuinely needs fresh research; do not assert differentiation ('no competitor has a ledger') on a demo slide without it, because that is an unverified competitive claim, not a design decision.
    [REFUTED] Inter's cv01 is the single-storey g and ss03 is open-tail curves (recollection); the fact that Linear sets font-feature-settings 'cv01','ss03' IS verified from their CSS. -> The Linear half is confirmed; the Inter half is wrong on both features. From Inter's own feature table at rsms.me/inter: cv01 = 'Alternate one' (an alternate glyph for the DIGIT 1) and ss03 = 'Round quotes & commas'. Neither touches the lowercase g. Inter's g is already single-storey by default and has no cv toggle; the character variant that visibly changes Inter's texture is cv11 = 'Single-story a'. (Others: cv02 Open four, cv03 Open six, cv04 Open nine, cv05 Lower-case L with tail, cv06 Simplified u, cv08 Upper-case i with serif, cv09 Flat-top three, cv10 Capital G with spur, cv12 Compact f, cv13 Compact t; ss02 Disambiguation.) Linear's setting is confirmed verbatim: --font-settings:"cv01", "ss03" applied via font-feature-settings:var(--font-settings), on --font-regular:"Inter Variable",… This REFUTES the rationale, not the tactic: 'two OpenType features change the texture of every label' is false, because cv01 only affects the digit 1 and ss03 only affects quotes and commas — most labels will render identically. If the goal is actually to de-generic Inter, add cv11 (single-storey a), and note that 'zero' (slashed zero) and tnum matter far more for a dashboard full of hashes and numbers than ss03 does.
    [CORRECTED] Recommendation premise: reject Nivo (stale since 2025-05-23), Observable Plot (stale since 2025-02-14), ECharts (57.5 MB), Chart.js (no SSR); visx 4.0.0 is the escape hatch. -> Staleness and size premises all confirmed from the registry: @nivo/core 0.99.0 published 2025-05-23 (16 months stale), @observablehq/plot 0.6.17 published 2025-02-14 (19 months stale), echarts 6.1.0 = 57.50 MB unpacked. Two corrections. (1) There is no npm package named bare 'visx' — the meta-package is @visx/visx@4.0.0 (2026-06-11, MIT); @visx/xychart@4.0.0 is 0.41 MB as claimed. Writing 'visx 4.0.0' in a dependency list will fail to install. (2) 'Chart.js (no SSR)' is imprecise: Chart.js 4.5.1 renders to <canvas> so it has no HTML SSR path, but chartjs-node-canvas exists for server-side PNG generation. The real argument against it for PRGuard is canvas rather than SSR — canvas means no DOM nodes to select, no per-segment hover targets from your own markup, and no theming from your CSS variables, which is exactly what the token-inheritance rationale for Recharts depends on. Same underlying conclusion, better reason.
VERIFIER ADDITIONS:
    + BIGGEST SINGLE ERROR: the Inter cv01/ss03 rationale. cv01='Alternate one' (digit 1 only) and ss03='Round quotes & commas'. The claim that these 'change the texture of every label' and 'de-Bootstrap Inter' is false — most UI labels contain no 1, no quote and no comma, so they render byte-identically. The typography recommendation is presented as 'the highest ratio of perceived polish to effort in the entire dashboard', and half of that ratio evaporates. Salvage: keep tabular-nums (that claim is entirely correct and is the real win), add cv11 (single-story a) if you actually want a visible texture change, and add 'zero' (slashed zero) — for a product whose screens are wall-to-wall SHA hashes, line numbers, scores to 3dp and seq counters, slashed zero and tnum are worth more than cv01 and ss03 combined.
    + BERKELEY MONO — the two terms that actually matter were missed, and they are stronger than the argument made. usgraphics.com states 'Commercial licenses are not compatible with open-source apps' and 'Commercial use restricted to UI elements only. If you're building an IDE, Terminal app, Text Editor, etc., we generally do not allow it, please contact us.' PRGuard's single most-photographed screen is a terminal-output view. Even at the Startup tier ($495 + $295 web-fonts module = $790), this needs vendor sign-off, and if PRGuard is ever open-sourced the commercial license does not cover it at all. Also note web fonts are never included in the base SKU and at the 7-50 employee tier become a $45/month subscription — a recurring cost for a design token. 'Do not license Berkeley Mono' is the right call; lead with the open-source/terminal-app restrictions rather than 'I could not retrieve its terms'.
    + THE ACCENT TOKEN IS OUT OF GAMUT. oklch(0.70 0.17 172) renders as #00BF92, not the #17C9A4 written beside it in the same recommendation — chroma 0.17 at L=0.70 near hue 172 clips against the sRGB boundary. Since the whole point of the accent is 'deliberately not CodeRabbit's #19d59e', shipping a token that silently resolves to a different, more saturated colour than the spec undermines the one bespoke decision the design rests on. The 15-step surface ramp is fine (all four surfaces are within 1/255 of their stated hexes). Recommendation: author the ramp in oklch, but run every chromatic token through a gamut check and regenerate the hex comments from the oklch, never the reverse. This matters more than it sounds because the rationale explicitly claims the palette is 'visible in the first 400ms'.
    + RADIX PUBLISHES THE Lc NUMBERS — upgrade this from 'my own working guidance' to a citation. Verbatim: 'Steps 11 and 12—which are designed for text—are guaranteed to Lc 60 and Lc 90 APCA contrast ratio on top of a step 2 background from the same scale.' The tradeoff paragraph on the token recommendation currently hedges on a number that is actually documented by the very source being referenced elsewhere in the study.
    + TANSTACK TABLE v9 IS ESM-ONLY — this was missed and it is the real migration risk, not the column API. v9 drops UMD and CJS builds entirely and targets ES2022. On a Next.js app that is fine; on anything with a Jest/CJS test path, a CommonJS script, or an older bundler config it is a hard break. Combined with 9.0.0 being only 7 weeks old (2026-08-04) and already at 9.2.4, consider whether a hackathon needs v9 at all. The reassuring part: per the official migration guide, 'column definitions, and rendering patterns remain familiar' and 'Table markup is largely unchanged'; the breaking surface is table CREATION (new `features` option, row model factories moved onto `tableFeatures()`), and `useLegacyTable` from '@tanstack/react-table/legacy' accepts the v8 API as a deprecated shim.
    + KEYBOARD SHORTCUT COLLISIONS with the stated 'reuse GitHub's muscle memory' rationale. GitHub binds c on Files changed to 'Open the Commits dropdown menu' (the recommendation assigns c to comment) and x on a pull request to 'Link an issue or pull request' (the recommendation assigns x to select). Two of the proposed bindings therefore do the opposite of what a fluent GitHub reviewer expects, on the two screens where they will be reflexive. j/k/n/p are not GitHub bindings at all (correctly flagged), and e is unbound so 'e suppress' is free. Suggest: keep j/k/o/Enter//? (borrowed or free), but move comment off c and select off x, or drop the 'reviewers are fluent immediately' claim.
    + THE GITHUB PERF NUMBERS MIX TWO EXPERIMENTS. '10X reduction' applies to JavaScript heap under virtualization on p95+ PRs; DOM nodes fell only ~10% (~200,000 → ~180,000) and that was the v1→v2 rewrite, not virtualization. The 183,504 → 50,004 component figure is also the rewrite. If any of these land on a slide, attribute them correctly — a judge who has read the post will notice, and the product's entire thesis is about not overstating evidence.
    + DATADOG INFERRED-SERVICE STYLING IS SURFACE-SPECIFIC: dashed outline in flame graphs, dashed UNDERLINE in waterfall, dashed outline + purple background only on Trace Maps. The waterfall recommendation borrows the purple-background treatment from the wrong surface.
    + RESUMABLE-STREAM HAS NO ZERO-INFRA PATH — confirmed from the README, which is stronger than the tradeoff paragraph implies. It ships exactly three entrypoints (node-redis, ioredis, generic) and no in-memory or Postgres implementation. 'Make SSE additive, build polling first' is therefore not a nice-to-have sequencing preference, it is the only way to have a working demo without standing up Redis. Also: npm reports the license as ISC while the repo LICENSE file is MIT text — pick the repo's answer for any attributions page.
    + TWO CLAIMS I COULD NOT CHECK AT ALL, flagged honestly rather than guessed: (a) Graphite's ~$290M last private valuation and 'priced way over' — the primary source (cursor.com/blog/graphite, 2025-12-19) discloses no terms, and Axios/InfoWorld/Yahoo URLs returned 403/404; this session's web-search budget was exhausted, so I have no corroboration. Drop the figure. (b) Semgrep/Snyk ignore-reason taxonomies — same budget exhaustion, no evidence either way; the researcher's restraint stands and the 'no competitor has a ledger' line remains an unverified competitive claim that should not be asserted on a demo slide.
    + SHIKI'S light-dark() CAVEAT IS DOUBLY STALE and the study can act on it more confidently than it does: MDN puts light-dark() at Baseline (newly available) since May 2024, and Shiki's live docs no longer say 'limited browser support' — the current wording is 'relatively new and may not be supported in older browsers'. Use defaultColor:'light-dark()'. Minor: the claim that cssVariablePrefix defaults to '--shiki-' is inferred from an example, not documented on the page cited.
    + CHART.JS REJECTION REASON IS WRONG BUT THE CONCLUSION IS RIGHT. 'No SSR' is imprecise (chartjs-node-canvas renders server-side PNGs). The real disqualifier is that it is canvas-based: no DOM nodes to hover, no per-segment targets in your own markup, and — decisively — no inheritance from your CSS custom properties. That last point is the entire stated rationale for choosing Recharts ('charts inherit the bespoke token ramp for free'), so framing it as a canvas-vs-SVG argument makes the rejection consistent with the selection.
    + MINOR BUT WILL BREAK AN INSTALL: there is no npm package named 'visx'. The meta-package is @visx/visx@4.0.0. Also react-resizable-panels 4.13.2 was published 2026-09-21 — one day before today; pin it exactly and commit a lockfile rather than accepting a caret range on a release that has existed for 24 hours.
    + WHAT THE STUDY GOT RIGHT AND SHOULD NOT SECOND-GUESS: every single registry figure I spot-checked was accurate to the byte (monaco 93.38 MB, echarts 57.50 MB, recharts 7.11 MB, @shikijs/langs 8.25 MB, @git-diff-view/react 1.25 MB, react-diff-view 1.41 MB, anser 0.04 MB, @unovis/ts 9.90 MB). Every Linear token I re-extracted from static.linear.app matched verbatim, including the 7%/10% diff tints that the diff-UI recommendation is built on. The @git-diff-view/lezer discrepancy is real and is genuine evidence for the 'wrap it in one component' mitigation. The CodeRabbit-ships-Radix inference is validated by value equality against two independent products. Graphite-ships-stock-shadcn is confirmed from their live CSS. The self-flagging discipline was well-calibrated: of 20 flagged claims, the researcher was right to doubt 4, wrong to doubt 2 (Radix Lc thresholds are published; light-dark() support is fine), and missed exactly one thing they were confident about (Inter's feature semantics).

### AREA: Implementation stack, performance, accessibility and shipping constraints (stack-perf)
FINDINGS:
  - VERSION GROUND TRUTH pulled live from registry.npmjs.org on 2026-09-22, not memory: next@16.3.6 (published 2026-09-22T16:19Z, a same-day critical security release alongside 15.5.26), react@19.3.0, tailwindcss@4.3.3 (2026-07-16), typescript@7.0.2 (2026-07-08, Apache-2.0 — TS7 is the 10x native port), @tanstack/react-query@5.103.2 (2026-09-21), shadcn@4.21.0, motion@13.4.1 (2026-09-22), three@0.186.0, @react-three/fiber@9.7.0, gsap@3.15.0, lenis@1.3.26, turbo@2.11.2, pnpm@12.5.1, eslint@10.11.0, @playwright/test@1.63.0, lucide-react@1.47.0, web-vitals@6.2.2. Pin these exact versions in package.json; do not use ^ ranges for a hackathon build.
  - Next.js 16 (blog publishedAt 2025-10-21) made Turbopack the DEFAULT bundler (2-5x faster prod builds, up to 10x faster Fast Refresh). Escape hatch is `next dev --webpack` / `next build --webpack`. Minimum Node.js 20.9.0 (Node 18 dropped), minimum TypeScript 5.1, browser floor Chrome/Edge/Firefox 111+ and Safari 16.4+. That browser floor is IDENTICAL to Tailwind v4's floor except Firefox (Tailwind needs FF 128+), so the union floor for PRGuard is Chrome 111 / Edge 111 / Firefox 128 / Safari 16.4.
  - Next.js 16 REMOVALS that will silently break copy-pasted 2024-2025 code: `middleware.ts` renamed to `proxy.ts` (now Node.js runtime by default, exported fn renamed to `proxy`); `next lint` deleted and `next build` no longer lints (codemod: `npx @next/codemod@canary next-lint-to-eslint-cli .`); `experimental.ppr` and `export const experimental_ppr` removed in favour of `cacheComponents: true`; `experimental.dynamicIO` renamed to `cacheComponents`; `experimental.turbopack` moved to top-level `turbopack`; sync `params`/`searchParams`/`cookies()`/`headers()`/`draftMode()` all removed — must be awaited; every parallel-route slot now REQUIRES an explicit `default.js` or the build fails; AMP gone; `serverRuntimeConfig`/`publicRuntimeConfig` gone; automatic `scroll-behavior: smooth` gone (opt back in with `data-scroll-behavior="smooth"` on <html>).
  - next/image defaults changed in 16 in ways that matter for a hero-image marketing site: `images.qualities` default went from [1..100] to `[75]` and the `quality` prop is now COERCED to the nearest allowed value (so `quality={90}` silently becomes 75 unless you add `images: { qualities: [75, 90] }`); `images.minimumCacheTTL` 60s -> 14400s (4h); `16` removed from default `imageSizes`; `images.maximumRedirects` capped at 3; `images.dangerouslyAllowLocalIP` now blocks local-IP optimization by default; `next/image` local `src` with a query string now requires `images.localPatterns`; `images.domains` deprecated in favour of `remotePatterns`; `next/legacy/image` deprecated.
  - Next.js 16.3 (2026-08-03) shipped the numbers worth quoting: up to 90% less dev-server RAM (vercel.com dashboard 21.5 GB -> 2 GB; nextjs.org 4,600 MB -> 840 MB) via disk caching + memory eviction now ON by default; `next build` disk caching on by default with vercel.com/geist going 30s -> 5.5s (5.5x); App Router SSR moved from web streams to native Node streams for ~22% more RPS under load; `pnpm add -D typescript@^7` makes `next build` typecheck with the native TS7 compiler. New APIs: `catchError` from `next/error` (custom error boundary with a `retry()` that re-runs Server Components), `import.meta.glob` in Turbopack, `next/root-params`.
  - Caching semantics in 16.x are now explicit and opt-in: nothing is cached by default, `fetch` is uncached and BLOCKS render unless wrapped in Suspense or marked `'use cache'`. `revalidateTag(tag)` single-arg is deprecated — it now requires a cacheLife profile as the 2nd arg (`revalidateTag('findings','max')`, or `{expire: 3600}`). Two new Server-Actions-only APIs: `updateTag(tag)` for read-your-writes (use this after a suppression write so the ledger reflects instantly) and `refresh()` for refreshing uncached data without touching cache. Instant Navigations needs BOTH flags: `{ cacheComponents: true, partialPrefetching: true }`.
  - Tailwind CSS v4.3.3 is current (docs header reads v4.3). CSS-first config: `@import "tailwindcss"` then `@theme { --color-*: ... }`. Three directives you must know: plain `@theme` (token -> utility), `@theme inline` (emits the RESOLVED value — REQUIRED when a token points at another variable, which is exactly the `--font-sans: var(--font-geist)` next/font pattern, and exactly what shadcn now generates), and `@theme static` (force-emit every variable even if unused — needed when a WebGL/canvas script reads tokens via `getComputedStyle`). `--color-*: initial` wipes the default palette; `--*: initial` wipes everything.
  - Tailwind v3->v4 port traps, exact: `ring` went 3px -> 1px (use `ring-3`); default ring colour blue-500 -> currentColor; default BORDER colour gray-200 -> currentColor (every `border` class in a pasted v3 snippet now renders in the text colour); `shadow-sm`->`shadow-xs`, `shadow`->`shadow-sm`, `blur`->`blur-sm`, `rounded`->`rounded-sm`, `drop-shadow`->`drop-shadow-sm` (all shifted one step); `outline-none`->`outline-hidden`; `!flex` -> `flex!` (important moved to the end); `bg-[--brand]` -> `bg-(--brand)`; `grid-cols-[max-content,auto]` -> underscore not comma; variant stacking flipped right-to-left -> left-to-right (`first:*:pt-0` -> `*:first:pt-0`); `space-y-*` selector changed to `> :not(:last-child)` with margin-BOTTOM; `hover:` is now wrapped in `@media (hover: hover)`; `@apply` in a Vue/Svelte/CSS-module file needs `@reference "../app.css"`; `corePlugins`, `resolveConfig` and Sass/Less/Stylus support are gone; PostCSS users must move to `@tailwindcss/postcss` and delete postcss-import + autoprefixer.
  - shadcn CLI v4 shipped March 2026 (npm `shadcn@4.21.0`). It adds `registry:base` (ship a whole design system — components + deps + CSS vars + fonts + config — as ONE install), `registry:font` as a first-class type, presets, `--dry-run`/`--diff`/`--view`, `init --template` for Next.js/Vite/TanStack Start/React Router/Astro/Laravel, and `init --base radix|base-ui`. CRITICAL: Base UI is still `@base-ui-components/react@1.0.0-rc.0`, last published 2025-12-04 — do NOT pick `--base base-ui` for a demo. Also note shadcn components no longer use `forwardRef` and every primitive carries a `data-slot` attribute, which is the cleanest hook for theming the dashboard without fighting class order.
  - Data-layer staleness is the deciding fact: the whole openapi-typescript monorepo (`openapi-typescript@7.13.0`, `openapi-fetch@0.17.0`, `openapi-react-query@0.5.4`) last published 2026-02-11 — seven months with no release. `@hey-api/openapi-ts@0.99.0` (2026-06-22, MIT) is the maintained option; its `@tanstack/react-query` plugin generates `getReviewOptions()`, `getReviewQueryKey()`, `suppressFindingMutation()` and infinite-query options directly from the FastAPI OpenAPI doc. Caveat: it declares `engines: { node: '>=22.18.0' }` and is still 0.x, so pin the exact version. `orval@8.36.0` (2026-09-21) is the alternative and is the only one that also emits MSW mocks, which is real demo-day insurance.
  - TanStack Query v6 does NOT exist for React. `@tanstack/react-query` latest is 5.103.2; only the Svelte adapter carries a v6 tag. Anyone telling you to 'use Query v6' is wrong. Separately, `@tanstack/react-table` IS on v9 (9.2.4, 2026-08-28) — its API differs from the v8 examples that dominate blog posts, so budget time if the findings table uses it.
  - Scroll-driven CSS animations are NOT safe as a sole mechanism. From MDN browser-compat-data 8.1.2 (extracted directly from the npm package): `animation-timeline`, `scroll-timeline`, `view-timeline` and `animation-range` = Chrome/Edge 115, Safari 26, Firefox **"preview"** (still behind `layout.css.scroll-driven-animations.enabled`), `timeline-scope` = Chrome 116. A CSS-only `animation-timeline: view()` reveal silently does nothing in Firefox — the element just sits at its @keyframes 0% state, which usually means invisible. Blog posts claiming 'Firefox 132+' or 'universal in 2026' are wrong. Gate with `@supports (animation-timeline: scroll())` and ship a JS/IntersectionObserver fallback.
  - View Transitions compat (same BCD source): `view-transition-name` / `document.startViewTransition` = Chrome 111, Safari 18, Firefox 144. Cross-document `@view-transition` = Chrome 126, Safari 18.2, Firefox **false**. React 19.2's `<ViewTransition>` is available through Next 16's React canary, so same-document transitions are cheap and safe; cross-document MPA transitions are not. Other safe-today primitives from the same data: `@starting-style` (Ch117/FF129/Saf17.5), `@property` (Ch85/FF128/Saf16.4), `content-visibility` (Ch85/FF125/Saf18), `color-mix()` (Ch111/FF113/Saf16.2), `oklch()` (Ch111/FF113/Saf15.4), `light-dark()` (Ch123/FF120/Saf17.5), `@scope` (Ch118/FF146/Saf26.4 — too new). `PerformanceLongAnimationFrameTiming` (LoAF) is Chrome-only (123+), Firefox/Safari false — so INP field data will be Chrome-only.
  - Animation library weights, measured by downloading from jsDelivr and gzipping locally (not from a size badge): three r186 `three.core.min.js` 415,816 B raw / 104,259 B gzip and `three.module.min.js` 392,831 B / 89,862 B; `three.webgpu.min.js` 820,842 B raw; motion@13.4.1 UMD 147,246 B / 49,070 B; gsap@3.15.0 72,927 B / 28,310 B; ScrollTrigger 44,575 B / 17,967 B; lenis@1.3.26 18,722 B / 5,427 B. A WebGL hero therefore costs ~90-105 KB gzip of three alone before your shaders, R3F (2.09 MB unpacked) and drei (1.67 MB unpacked). GSAP + ScrollTrigger together are ~46 KB gzip — cheaper than three by 2x and than Motion's full bundle.
  - GSAP licensing is NOT MIT. The npm `license` field on gsap@3.15.0 literally reads "Standard 'no charge' license: https://gsap.com/standard-license." Webflow now funds it and all plugins (ScrollTrigger, SplitText, MorphSVG, DrawSVG, Flip, Observer) are free including commercial use. The prohibited use is building a competing no-code visual animation tool, and reverse-engineering to build one. The license page explicitly states AI-generated code using GSAP is not a Prohibited Use. You also may not strip proprietary notices. For a hackathon this is fine, but it is a non-OSI license and a judge who checks will notice it is not MIT.
  - Core Web Vitals thresholds are unchanged at p75: LCP <= 2.5s, INP <= 200ms, CLS <= 0.1. The 2026 movement is soft navigations — Chrome 151 (Aug 2026) can measure CWV across client-side route changes, with a final origin trial around Chrome 147; Google has been explicit this does NOT yet feed CrUX or search signals. web.dev's LCP breakdown budget: TTFB ~40%, resource load delay <10%, resource load duration ~40%, element render delay <10%. Next's own streaming doc adds the trap: anything inside a <Suspense> boundary cannot be your LCP element without paying for the boundary swap, and React may fall back to a boundary you didn't expect under slow network/busy CPU.
  - Vercel hard numbers (docs last_updated 2026-08-24): Fluid compute is default for new projects since 2025-04-23. Hobby = 300s default AND max duration, 2 GB / 1 vCPU, single region (`iad1` default). Pro = 300s default, 800s max, 1800s extended (beta), 4 GB / 2 vCPU, up to 3 regions. Request AND response body cap is 4.5 MB (413 FUNCTION_PAYLOAD_TOO_LARGE) — relevant if you proxy proof-terminal blobs through a route handler. Bundle 250 MB uncompressed (5 GB via large-functions beta). Concurrency auto-scales to 30,000 on Hobby/Pro. 1,024 file descriptors shared across concurrent executions. Node 20+ gets bytecode caching for cold starts — but ONLY in production, explicitly NOT in preview deployments, so the preview URL you are tempted to demo from is the slow one.
  - Streaming will break on anything that buffers, and Next documents each: nginx buffers by default (set `X-Accel-Buffering: no` via `headers()` in next.config); CDNs may buffer entire responses; gzip/brotli buffer chunks before flushing; AWS Lambda needs response-streaming mode explicitly enabled; and Safari/WebKit buffers the first 1024 bytes of a streamed response (webkit bug 252413) so tiny SSE payloads paint all at once. Once the first chunk is sent the status code is locked at 200 — a `notFound()` mid-stream becomes a `<meta name="robots" content="noindex">` injection, and a `redirect()` becomes a client-side redirect.
  - Competitor stack fingerprints, taken from the actual fetched HTML/CSS: coderabbit.ai is Next.js on Vercel (Turbopack chunk names, `?dpl=dpl_...` deployment-id query on every asset), Tailwind v4 (`@layer theme`, `--tw-backdrop-*`), 127 `<script>` tags, 816 KB of HTML in the document, ~690 KB of CSS, and exactly ONE `oklch()` in the whole stylesheet. graphite.dev = Next.js + GSAP (buildId `0Ku2uBfu23Pa9kZxkrE8D`). linear.app, vercel.com, cursor.com, greptile.com all Next.js; vercel.com uses `tw-animate-css` (`--tw-animation-delay`). cubic.dev and codeant.ai are Framer-hosted template sites. Translation: the premium tier of this category is all hand-built Next.js, the weak tier is Framer, and CodeRabbit's own site is a heavy, un-optimised Tailwind v4 page — it is beatable on both craft AND measured performance.
