  - [must-steal] Next.js 16.3 release post (Instant Navigations) https://nextjs.org/blog/next-16-3 :: STEAL: `partialPrefetching: true` + `cacheComponents: true`, the Instant Insights devtool, and the `instant()` Playwright helper as a regression test for the demo path. :: SPECIFICS: Published 2026-08-03. Dev RAM -90% (21.5 GB -> 2 GB on vercel.com dashboard; 4,600 -> 840 MB on nextjs.org). Build cache: vercel.com/geist 30s -> 5.5s, nextjs.org 21s -> 9.2s. SSR +22% RPS from native Node streams. `import { instant } from '@next/playwright'`. `catchError` from `next/error` with `retry()`. `import { lang } from 'next/root-params'`. Experimental: `turbopackRustReactCompiler` (-34% cold / -46% warm time-to-ready on v0), `experimental.useOffline` + `useOffline()` hook from `next/offline`.
  - [must-steal] Next.js Streaming guide https://nextjs.org/docs/app/guides/streaming :: STEAL: The 'push dynamic access down' pattern, the LCP/CLS/INP consequences of Suspense placement, and the exact buffering-hazard list. :: SPECIFICS: Pass `cookies()`/`params` promises DOWN rather than awaiting at the layout top, else the whole subtree leaves the static shell. Keep LCP elements OUTSIDE Suspense. Use `preload` prop on next/image for LCP images. Skeletons must match final dimensions or you eat CLS. Each Suspense boundary is a hydration unit -> splits hydration -> better INP. nginx: `X-Accel-Buffering: no`. Safari buffers first 1024 bytes. Status code locked after first chunk. Route-handler SSE example uses ReadableStream + `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`.
  - [must-steal] Next.js Client-side data fetching guide https://nextjs.org/docs/app/guides/client-side-data-fetching :: STEAL: The three-pattern table and the three-cache-layer table — this is the argument for exactly where PRGuard's run-status polling lives. :: SPECIFICS: Patterns: inline loading (`useQuery`), Suspense loading (`useSuspenseQuery`), server-provided (`<HydrationBoundary>` for TanStack / `<SWRConfig fallback>` for SWR). Three cache layers: Next server cache (cacheLife revalidate/expire), Next client cache (cacheLife stale), library cache (query keys). After a mutation: `updateTag(tag)` when the Server Action must be visible immediately, `revalidateTag(tag,'max')` when stale is fine, `revalidateTag(tag,{expire:0})` for webhooks.
  - [must-steal] Next.js SPA patterns guide + live demo https://nextjs.org/docs/app/guides/single-page-applications :: STEAL: The promise-through-context pattern (`use()` inside a provider) and the `useActionState` + `useOptimistic` shared-reducer pattern — this is EXACTLY the shape of the suppression-ledger optimistic update. :: SPECIFICS: Server layout calls `getUser()` WITHOUT await and passes the Promise into a client `<UserProvider>`; consumers call `use(promise)` inside Suspense. Mutations: one pure reducer shared by client `useOptimistic` and the Server Action so optimistic and server compute identical next state; `startTransition(() => { addOptimistic(action); dispatch(action) })`. Live demo at next-spa-patterns.labs.vercel.dev, source at github.com/vercel-labs/next-spa-patterns.
  - [reference-only] Next.js Multi-Zones guide https://nextjs.org/docs/app/guides/multi-zones :: STEAL: The counter-argument to splitting marketing and dashboard into two deployed apps. :: SPECIFICS: Zones need `assetPrefix` per app; cross-zone links must use plain `<a>` not `<Link>` (no prefetch, HARD navigation, full resource reload); Server Actions across zones need `experimental.serverActions.allowedOrigins`. For PRGuard the marketing->dashboard hop is exactly one link, so a zone split buys nothing and costs a full reload plus duplicated design-system builds.
  - [strong] Next.js self-hosting guide https://nextjs.org/docs/app/guides/self-hosting :: STEAL: The Docker/laptop fallback recipe for demo day, and the graceful-shutdown / streaming-proxy config. :: SPECIFICS: `next start` gives image optimization with zero config (glibc Linux may need the sharp memory-allocator note). `NEXT_SERVER_ACTIONS_ENCRYPTION_KEY` (base64, 16/24/32 bytes) must be constant across instances. `generateBuildId` / `deploymentId` for version skew — skew triggers a hard reload and loses useState. `cacheHandler` + `cacheMaxMemorySize: 0` for multi-instance. SIGINT/SIGTERM drain period 10-30s for `after()` callbacks.
  - [must-steal] Next.js Font module docs https://nextjs.org/docs/app/api-reference/components/font :: STEAL: The exact Tailwind v4 wiring — this is the single place people get `@theme inline` wrong. :: SPECIFICS: `const geist = Geist({ subsets:['latin'], display:'swap', variable:'--font-geist' })`, put `geist.variable` on <html>, then in global.css: `@import 'tailwindcss'; @theme inline { --font-sans: var(--font-geist); --font-mono: var(--font-geist-mono); }`. `display` defaults to 'swap'; `preload` defaults true; `adjustFontFallback` defaults true for google / 'Arial' for local; `declarations: [{prop:'ascent-override', value:'90%'}]` for local fonts. Fonts declared in the ROOT layout preload on every route — declaring a display face in the root layout for one hero headline is a real LCP tax.
  - [must-steal] Tailwind CSS theme variables docs (v4.3) https://tailwindcss.com/docs/theme :: STEAL: `@theme` vs `@theme inline` vs `@theme static`, and namespace-wipe syntax. :: SPECIFICS: Namespaces: --color-*, --font-*, --text-*, --spacing-*, --radius-*, --shadow-*, --breakpoint-*, --animate-*. `@theme inline` emits the RESOLVED value (needed when a token references another var). `@theme static` force-emits unused variables (needed so a canvas/WebGL script can read brand tokens via getComputedStyle). `--color-*: initial` wipes the palette; `--*: initial` wipes everything. Tokens are readable from JS via `getComputedStyle(document.documentElement).getPropertyValue('--color-accent')`.
  - [must-steal] Tailwind CSS v3 -> v4 upgrade guide https://tailwindcss.com/docs/upgrade-guide :: STEAL: The complete rename table. Print it and keep it next to you while pasting any pre-2025 snippet. :: SPECIFICS: Browser floor Safari 16.4 / Chrome 111 / Firefox 128. ring 3px->1px. Default border AND ring colour -> currentColor. shadow/blur/rounded/drop-shadow scales all shift one step. `outline-none`->`outline-hidden`. `!x`->`x!`. `bg-[--v]`->`bg-(--v)`. Variant order left-to-right. `space-y-*` uses `> :not(:last-child)` + margin-bottom. hover wrapped in `@media (hover:hover)`. `@layer utilities`->`@utility`. `@apply` in SFC/CSS-module needs `@reference`. Buttons default to `cursor: default` now (add back `cursor:pointer` in @layer base or every CTA feels dead). `corePlugins`/`resolveConfig` gone. `npx @tailwindcss/upgrade` automates most of it.
  - [strong] Tailwind CSS v4.2 + v4.3 release post https://tailwindcss.com/blog/tailwindcss-v4-3 :: STEAL: `scrollbar-*` utilities for the dashboard's dense panes, and `@container-size` for block-axis container queries. :: SPECIFICS: v4.3: `scrollbar-thin` / `scrollbar-none` / `scrollbar-thumb-*` / `scrollbar-track-*` / `scrollbar-gutter-stable`; `@container-size` with `cqb`/`cqh` units; `zoom-*`; `tab-*`; stacked/compound `@variant hover:focus {}` and `@variant hover, focus {}`; `--default(value)` in `@utility`. v4.2: `@tailwindcss/webpack` (2.17x faster than PostCSS: 429ms vs 932ms on the tailwindcss.com docs), logical properties `pbs-*`/`mbe-*`/`inset-bs-*`, `font-features-*` (use `font-features-["tnum"]` for tabular numbers in the dashboard's cost/latency columns), and four new neutral palettes mauve/olive/mist/taupe.
  - [must-steal] shadcn/ui Tailwind v4 doc + CLI v4 changelog https://ui.shadcn.com/docs/tailwind-v4 :: STEAL: The `:root` + `@theme inline` token split and the `data-slot` styling hook. :: SPECIFICS: Move `:root`/`.dark` OUTSIDE `@layer base`; define raw values there; re-export through `@theme inline { --color-background: var(--background) }`. HSL converted to OKLCH; chart colours drop the `hsl()` wrapper (`var(--chart-1)` not `hsl(var(--chart-1))`). forwardRef removed, every primitive has `data-slot`. CLI v4 (Mar 2026, npm shadcn@4.21.0): `registry:base`, `registry:font`, presets, `--dry-run`/`--diff`/`--view`, `init --template`, `init --base radix|base-ui`, `info` and `docs` commands, shadcn/skills for agents.
  - [must-steal] Hey API TanStack Query plugin https://heyapi.dev/openapi-ts/plugins/tanstack-query :: STEAL: Generated `*Options()` / `*QueryKey()` / `*Mutation()` shape — spread into useQuery/useMutation so query keys are never hand-written. :: SPECIFICS: Config: `plugins: [{ name:'@tanstack/react-query', queryOptions:true, queryKeys:true, infiniteQueryOptions:true, mutationOptions:true }]`. Usage: `useQuery({ ...getReviewOptions({ path:{ id } }) })`, `useMutation({ ...suppressFindingMutation() })`, and `getFindingsQueryKey({query:{severity_min:'high'}})` for surgical invalidation after a suppression. @hey-api/openapi-ts@0.99.0, MIT, engines node>=22.18.0, still 0.x so pin exactly. Clients: fetch (default), axios, ky, next, nuxt, angular.
  - [reference-only] openapi-typescript / openapi-fetch / openapi-react-query https://github.com/openapi-ts/openapi-typescript :: STEAL: Use as the zero-risk fallback: types-only generation + a 6 kB fetch client, no per-operation codegen. :: SPECIFICS: openapi-typescript@7.13.0, openapi-fetch@0.17.0, openapi-react-query@0.5.4 — ALL published 2026-02-11, no release in 7 months. Repo not archived (pushed 2026-09-22) but the README's package list now only advertises openapi-typescript and openapi-fetch; openapi-react-query is no longer listed. Reads OpenAPI 3.0/3.1 only (FastAPI emits 3.1 by default, so fine).
  - [must-steal] Vercel Fluid compute docs https://vercel.com/docs/fluid-compute :: STEAL: Cold-start behaviour and what actually changes on demo day. :: SPECIFICS: Default for new projects since 2025-04-23. In-function concurrency (multiple invocations share one instance) for Node and Python. Bytecode caching for Node 20+ reduces cold starts but is PRODUCTION-ONLY — not in preview or dev. Function pre-warming on production deployments only. Cross-AZ and cross-region failover. `waitUntil` for background work. Enable per-deployment with `{"fluid": true}` in vercel.json.
  - [must-steal] Vercel Functions limits https://vercel.com/docs/functions/limitations :: STEAL: The numbers to design against so nothing 413s or 504s in front of judges. :: SPECIFICS: Hobby 300s default AND max, 2 GB / 1 vCPU. Pro 300s default / 800s max / 1800s extended beta, 4 GB / 2 vCPU. Request AND response body 4.5 MB hard cap -> 413 FUNCTION_PAYLOAD_TOO_LARGE. Bundle 250 MB uncompressed (5 GB large-functions beta, needs VERCEL_SUPPORT_LARGE_FUNCTIONS=1). Single region `iad1` by default; Pro up to 3 regions. 1,024 file descriptors shared across concurrent executions. Timeout returns 504 FUNCTION_INVOCATION_TIMEOUT. Edge runtime must start responding within 25s and can stream up to 300s.
  - [must-steal] web.dev Core Web Vitals + optimize-LCP + optimize-INP https://web.dev/articles/vitals :: STEAL: Budgets, and the sub-part split that tells you WHERE the time went. :: SPECIFICS: p75 targets: LCP <=2.5s, INP <=200ms, CLS <=0.1. LCP sub-parts: TTFB ~40%, resource load delay <10%, resource load duration ~40%, element render delay <10%. INP sub-parts: input delay / processing duration / presentation delay. Fixes: `fetchpriority="high"` on the LCP image, never `loading="lazy"` on it, `font-display` anything but auto/block, break event-callback work with setTimeout + requestAnimationFrame, shrink DOM size, use `content-visibility` on offscreen sections, batch style reads/writes.
  - [must-steal] MDN browser-compat-data v8.1.2 (npm package, parsed directly) https://www.npmjs.com/package/@mdn/browser-compat-data :: STEAL: Ground truth on every modern CSS feature you are tempted to use. Do not trust blog posts on this. :: SPECIFICS: animation-timeline/scroll-timeline/view-timeline/animation-range: Ch115, Edge115, Safari26, FIREFOX=preview. timeline-scope Ch116. view-transition-name & startViewTransition: Ch111/FF144/Saf18. @view-transition: Ch126/Saf18.2/FF=false. @starting-style Ch117/FF129/Saf17.5. @property Ch85/FF128/Saf16.4. content-visibility Ch85/FF125/Saf18. color-mix Ch111/FF113/Saf16.2. oklch Ch111/FF113/Saf15.4. light-dark Ch123/FF120/Saf17.5. @scope Ch118/FF146/Saf26.4. prefers-reduced-motion Ch74/FF63/Saf10.1. prefers-reduced-transparency Ch118/FF113/Safari=false. PerformanceLongAnimationFrameTiming Ch123 only. WebGL2 Ch56/FF51/Saf15.
  - [must-steal] WCAG 2.2 SC 2.4.13 Focus Appearance + SC 2.3.3 Animation from Interactions https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/focus-appearance.html :: STEAL: The exact focus-ring spec and the reduced-motion obligation. :: SPECIFICS: 2.4.13 (AAA): indicator area >= a 2 CSS px perimeter of the unfocused component, AND >=3:1 contrast between the SAME pixels focused vs unfocused (a state-change ratio, not adjacent contrast). A solid 2px `outline` with `outline-offset` satisfies both. 2.3.3 (AAA): interaction-triggered motion must be disableable unless essential; `prefers-reduced-motion` is the named implementation. 2.2.2: anything auto-moving for >5s needs pause/stop/hide. Also relevant: 1.4.11 non-text contrast 3:1 for UI component boundaries and graphical objects.
  - [must-steal] MDN aria-live reference https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/Accessibility/ARIA/Reference/Attributes/aria-live :: STEAL: The exact roles for a live pipeline run — this is what makes the 9-stage progress accessible instead of invisible. :: SPECIFICS: `role="status"` = implicit aria-live=polite + aria-atomic=true -> use for 'Stage 3/9 - specialists'. `role="log"` = implicit polite, appends without clearing -> use for the streaming proof terminal. `role="alert"` = assertive + atomic -> use ONLY for a gate turning red. `role="progressbar"` with aria-valuenow/min/max for the stage bar. The live region MUST exist in the DOM before content changes, otherwise nothing is announced. `aria-busy="true"` during batch updates then false. Screen readers do not announce alerts present before load completes.
  - [must-steal] coderabbit.ai homepage (fetched HTML + concatenated CSS, inspected locally) https://www.coderabbit.ai :: STEAL: The performance ceiling you have to clear, and proof that the category leader has not optimised its own site. :: SPECIFICS: Next.js on Vercel, Turbopack chunk filenames, `?dpl=dpl_DeBqjQiVepsPVJM67mfeENhsxbDZ` on every asset (deploymentId skew protection). 127 `<script>` tags in the document. 816,554 bytes of HTML. ~689,740 bytes of CSS across stylesheets. Tailwind v4 (`@layer theme`, `--tw-backdrop-blur`). Exactly ONE `oklch()` in the entire stylesheet — i.e. they are on v4 but still authoring in hex/hsl. Has `prefers-reduced-motion` somewhere. For comparison greptile.com ships ~225 KB CSS, vercel.com ~929 KB (and uses tw-animate-css: `--tw-animation-delay`), linear.app ~232 KB.
  - [avoid] cubic.dev and codeant.ai (fetched HTML) https://cubic.dev :: STEAL: Nothing. Inspect them so you can articulate the gap. :: SPECIFICS: Both fingerprint as Framer sites (framerusercontent asset host, framer.com markers). No Next.js, no custom bundler. This is the visual tier PRGuard must not land in; a judge who opens devtools can tell instantly.
  - [strong] GSAP standard license + pricing page https://gsap.com/standard-license :: STEAL: The licence facts, so you can answer 'is that legal for a commercial product?' in one sentence. :: SPECIFICS: gsap@3.15.0's npm `license` field is the string "Standard 'no charge' license: https://gsap.com/standard-license." — NOT SPDX MIT. Free for commercial use, all plugins included, Webflow-funded. Prohibited: building competing no-code visual animation tools, reverse-engineering to build one, stripping proprietary notices. AI-generated GSAP code is explicitly allowed.
  - [reference-only] Turborepo docs (turborepo.com now 301s to turborepo.dev) https://turborepo.dev/docs :: STEAL: Only relevant if you go multi-app. turbo@2.11.2 (2026-09-18). Remote caching + task scheduling from a single turbo.json; works with npm/yarn/pnpm. :: SPECIFICS: Positioned in 2026 as 'the build system for coding agents'. Incrementally adoptable over existing package.json scripts. For a two-app PRGuard repo the payoff is remote cache on CI; for a single-app repo it is pure overhead. Note the domain moved: turborepo.com -> turborepo.dev.
RECOMMENDATIONS:
  - (high) ONE Next.js 16.3.6 app with route groups: app/(marketing)/ and app/(app)/, two root-ish layouts, one design-token file. Do NOT use a Turborepo monorepo, and do NOT use Multi-Zones. | WHY: You have one design system, one auth boundary (marketing is public, /app is gated), one deploy, one preview URL to paste in the submission, and roughly 5 days. Route groups already give you two independent layouts, two independent `loading.tsx` trees, and independent per-route bundles — Next code-splits per route regardless of directory structure, so a monorepo buys you no bundle savings. Multi-Zones would make the single most important click in the demo (marketing hero CTA -> live dashboard) a HARD navigation with full resource reload and no prefetch, because cross-zone links must be plain <a>. Turborepo's real payoff is remote caching across many packages on CI; with one app and one CI job it is pure config tax. The marketing tree gets `export const dynamic = 'force-static'` semantics via Cache Components; the dashboard tree stays dynamic. SEO and SPA-feel are not in tension here: (marketing) renders static shells with real metadata, (app) is client-heavy under the same router. | TRADEOFF: One shared node_modules means a heavy marketing dep (three.js) can leak into the dashboard bundle if someone imports it from a shared module — enforce with an ESLint no-restricted-imports rule, or just keep the WebGL component in app/(marketing)/_components/ and never export it upward. Build time is a single serial build rather than two cacheable ones; at this size that is under a minute with Turbopack's FS cache. If PRGuard ever needs a separate docs site or a customer-facing status page on the same domain, revisit Multi-Zones then, not now.
  - (medium) Data layer: @hey-api/openapi-ts@0.99.0 (pinned exactly, no caret) generating types + SDK + the @tanstack/react-query plugin from the FastAPI /openapi.json, consumed by @tanstack/react-query@5.103.2 in client components. Add an `npm run api:gen` script and commit the generated client. | WHY: PRGuard's dashboard is a polling, mutating, keyboard-driven data app — exactly the case where Next's own docs tell you to use a client library rather than RSC. TanStack Query gives you refetchInterval for the live run, request dedup across the stage header and the findings table, optimistic updates for suppression, and `HydrationBoundary` if you later want server-provided initial data. hey-api generates `getReviewOptions()`, `getReviewQueryKey()` and `suppressFindingMutation()` directly, so query keys are derived from the OpenAPI operation and cannot drift from the backend — which matters because your backend has ~10 endpoints with filter params (severity_min, blocking, proof_status, fingerprint) that are easy to mistype. The alternative, openapi-typescript + openapi-fetch + openapi-react-query, has had zero npm releases since 2026-02-11 and openapi-react-query has quietly dropped off the repo README's package list. Commit the generated output so a flaky backend at build time cannot break the build. | TRADEOFF: hey-api is still 0.x (0.99.0) and declares engines node>=22.18.0 — you must be on Node 22.18+ locally and in CI even though Next only needs 20.9. Being 0.x means a minor bump can break generation, hence the exact pin. If you want zero risk instead of best ergonomics, fall back to `openapi-typescript` for types only plus ~40 lines of hand-written typed fetch wrappers and hand-written queryOptions — that is genuinely fine at 10 endpoints and has no 0.x exposure. Orval@8.36.0 is the third option and the only one that also emits MSW mocks; take it if you want the dashboard to be demoable with the backend switched off.
  - (high) Poll the run with TanStack Query, not SSE. `useQuery({ ...getReviewOptions({path:{id}}), refetchInterval: q => q.state.data?.status === 'running' ? 1200 : false, refetchIntervalInBackground: false })`. Only add SSE if a stage can go >20s silent, and if you do, hit FastAPI directly from the browser — never proxy the stream through a Next route handler on Vercel. | WHY: You already emit heartbeat + stage_index/stage_total/stage_label on GET /v1/reviews/{id}. At a 1.2s interval a 9-stage run produces a visibly-live UI with zero new backend surface, zero connection-drop handling, zero reconnect logic, and it survives the venue wifi dropping for 4 seconds — an SSE stream does not, and a dead EventSource mid-demo is a silent failure with no recovery UI. Polling also makes the stage transitions land on animation frames you control, which is what makes the 'Stage 3/9 - specialists, 12s since last output' ticker feel designed rather than jittery. If you do add SSE later: Vercel Edge functions must start responding within 25s and can stream for 300s; Node Fluid functions cap at 300s on Hobby; and Next's own docs list nginx buffering, CDN buffering, gzip buffering and Safari's 1024-byte buffer as things that will make a small SSE stream arrive as one lump. | TRADEOFF: Polling costs N requests per run — irrelevant at demo scale, a real cost at CodeRabbit scale, so say in the README that production uses SSE/websocket and the poll is the deliberate demo-hardened path. A 1.2s poll can miss a stage that completes in <1.2s; fix by rendering the stage rail from `stage_index` as a monotonic progress value rather than as discrete events, so a skipped tick just animates further. Back off to 4s after 90s to avoid hammering a stuck run.
  - (high) Animation stack: GSAP 3.15 + ScrollTrigger for the marketing scroll narrative (~46 KB gzip combined), Motion 13.4 only for UI micro-interactions inside the dashboard, Lenis only if you actually want smooth-scroll (5.4 KB gzip). Ship AT MOST ONE WebGL surface, dynamically imported with ssr:false, and give it a static poster. | WHY: Measured, not guessed: gsap.min 28.3 KB gzip + ScrollTrigger 18.0 KB = 46.3 KB, versus three.module 89.9 KB gzip (three.core is 104.3 KB) before a single shader, before R3F (2.09 MB unpacked) and before drei (1.67 MB unpacked). ScrollTrigger gets you 95% of the 'cinematic' feel — pinned sections, scrubbed timelines, staged reveals — at half the weight and with correct behaviour in Firefox, which pure CSS scroll-driven animation does NOT have (MDN BCD 8.1.2: animation-timeline is still 'preview' in Firefox). GSAP is free for commercial use including all plugins. Reserve three.js for the one signature moment — the animated 9-stage pipeline — and code-split it: `const Pipeline = dynamic(() => import('./pipeline-canvas'), { ssr: false, loading: () => <PipelinePoster/> })`, mount it only when an IntersectionObserver says it is in view, and tear down the renderer on unmount. | TRADEOFF: GSAP's licence is not MIT (it is Webflow's 'standard no-charge' licence); if a judge is licence-sensitive you will need one sentence of explanation. Two animation libraries means two mental models; mitigate by drawing a hard line — GSAP never enters app/(app)/, Motion never drives scroll. Skipping WebGL entirely is a defensible choice that costs you the owner's stated '3D/realistic' preference; the compromise that usually reads as more expensive than real 3D is a canvas-free approach: CSS 3D transforms + layered blurred gradients + an SVG pipeline with animated stroke-dashoffset and a grain overlay.
  - (high) Design prefers-reduced-motion as a SECOND designed state, not an off switch. Write it per animation type, in one place, with a manual toggle in the footer that writes a data-motion attribute so a judge can flip it live. | WHY: 'Turn animations off' produces a broken page: scroll-triggered reveals that start at opacity:0 simply never appear, and pinned sections collapse. The reduced variant of each type should be: (1) scroll-scrubbed timeline -> the END state, rendered statically, section un-pinned, normal document flow; (2) entrance reveal -> opacity 0->1 over 120ms with NO translate/scale (opacity-only is vestibular-safe); (3) parallax / layered depth -> zero offset, keep the layers and their blur; (4) WebGL canvas -> render exactly one frame then stop the RAF loop, or swap to the AVIF poster; (5) infinite marquee/ticker -> static list, and per WCAG 2.2.2 anything auto-moving >5s needs a pause control even for users who did NOT set the preference; (6) number count-ups -> final value immediately; (7) route transition -> instant, no View Transition. Implement as `@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)` plus a `[data-motion="reduced"]` attribute selector on <html> so the same CSS serves both the OS preference and your toggle, and read it in JS with `matchMedia('(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)')` before constructing any GSAP timeline (use `gsap.matchMedia()`). prefers-reduced-motion is universally supported (Chrome 74 / FF 63 / Safari 10.1); prefers-reduced-transparency is NOT (Safari: false), so do not rely on it for your glass panels. | TRADEOFF: Doubles the animation QA surface — every timeline needs its reduced end-state checked. Worth it: a live toggle is a 10-second moment in the demo that signals product maturity, and it is the single cheapest thing that distinguishes PRGuard from a template. Do not put the toggle in a settings modal nobody opens.
  - (high) Dark palette in OKLCH with contrast VERIFIED, not eyeballed. Base oklch(0.145 0 0) = #0A0A0A. I computed WCAG ratios for a working set: fg oklch(0.97 0.005 265)=#F3F5F9 at 18.14:1; muted-fg oklch(0.70 0.015 265)=#9A9EA8 at 7.38:1; accent oklch(0.72 0.17 258)=#5CA4FF at 7.74:1; proven-green oklch(0.78 0.17 155)=#41D686 at 10.55:1; critical-red oklch(0.68 0.21 22)=#FF515A at 6.20:1; warn oklch(0.82 0.16 85)=#F3BA25 at 11.18:1; focus oklch(0.86 0.13 200)=#49EAF2 at 13.51:1. CRITICAL: a hairline border at oklch(0.32 0.012 265)=#303339 is only 1.56:1 — decorative only. Any border that CARRIES MEANING (input outline, selected row, focused card) must be >= oklch(0.48 0.015 265)=#595E66, which measures 3.03:1 and passes WCAG 1.4.11. | WHY: Dark UI fails accessibility in exactly one predictable place: 1px borders and disabled states, because designers tune them by eye against a bright monitor and they vanish on a projector. Computing the ratios up front costs 10 minutes and removes the entire category of problem. OKLCH is safe everywhere you care (Chrome 111 / FF 113 / Safari 15.4) and is what Tailwind v4 and shadcn now emit natively. Keep the accent glow decorative: #5CA4FF composited at 14% over #0A0A0A is #15202C, a 1.20:1 wash — beautiful, and correctly carries no information. | TRADEOFF: OKLCH values are less legible to teammates than hex; keep a generated hex column in a comment. A 0.145-lightness base is very dark — on a badly-calibrated venue projector the bg-0/bg-1/bg-2 steps (1.00 / 1.06 / 1.16 relative to base) will collapse into one flat black, so do not encode information in elevation alone; use the 3:1 border for structure. Consider shipping a `[data-contrast="high"]` variant that raises all borders to oklch(0.55) for the projector.
  - (high) Accessibility for the live run: `role="status"` on the stage label (polite, atomic) that announces only on stage CHANGE not on every heartbeat tick; `role="log"` on the proof terminal; `role="alert"` reserved exclusively for the merge gate turning red; `role="progressbar"` with aria-valuenow={stage_index} aria-valuemin={1} aria-valuemax={9} aria-valuetext="Stage 3 of 9, specialists". The WebGL/decorative canvas gets aria-hidden="true" + a real <h2>/<ol> description of the 9 stages rendered in the DOM behind it. | WHY: MDN's rule is absolute: the live region must exist in the DOM BEFORE the content changes, or nothing is announced — so render the empty `<div role="status">` in the shell, not conditionally when the run starts. Announcing every 1.2s poll would make the page unusable with a screen reader; announce on stage_index change only, and put the heartbeat seconds in the same region with aria-atomic already true so it reads as one sentence. A decorative canvas with no aria-hidden is read as 'graphic' and nothing else, which is worse than silence. Providing the pipeline as a real ordered list underneath is also free SEO — crawlers index the 9 stage names, which no competitor's animated diagram does. | TRADEOFF: role="log" on a fast-streaming terminal can still flood a screen reader; add `aria-busy="true"` while a burst is writing and flip to false when it settles. `role="alert"` interrupts, so firing it on every finding rather than only on the gate would be hostile.
  - (high) Keyboard-first dashboard: cmdk@1.1.1 command palette on Cmd/Ctrl-K, j/k row movement + Enter to open + s to suppress in the findings table, `?` for a shortcut sheet, and a visible focus ring token `--ring: oklch(0.86 0.13 200)` applied as `outline: 2px solid var(--ring); outline-offset: 2px` — never `outline: none`. | WHY: WCAG 2.4.13 wants an indicator at least as large as a 2 CSS px perimeter with a >=3:1 change between focused and unfocused states; a 2px outline with offset satisfies both, and #49EAF2 at 13.51:1 against #0A0A0A is unmissable on a projector. Tailwind v4 renamed `outline-none` to `outline-hidden` — if you paste a v3 form snippet containing `focus:outline-none` it will now be interpreted differently, so grep for it. Keyboard speed is also the cheapest way to look like a real internal tool rather than a demo: a judge watching you jump from run -> finding -> proof terminal -> suppress without touching the mouse reads 'this team uses this'. | TRADEOFF: Custom j/k handlers fight native scrolling and screen-reader browse mode; scope them to a roving-tabindex grid with `role="grid"` and only bind when focus is inside it. cmdk is at 1.1.1 (Nov 2024) — stable but not actively moving; acceptable. react-hotkeys-hook@5.3.3 is current if you want a hook-based binder.
  - (medium) Performance budgets, enforced: marketing route <= 180 KB gzip of first-party JS before the lazy WebGL chunk; dashboard shell <= 250 KB gzip; LCP element is a TEXT headline rendered OUTSIDE every Suspense boundary; total blocking time on the marketing hero <= 200ms on a 4x-throttled CPU. Verify with `@next/bundle-analyzer@16.3.6` and a Lighthouse run on the PRODUCTION deployment, not preview. | WHY: Next's own streaming doc is explicit that an LCP element inside a Suspense boundary cannot paint until the boundary swaps and its inline script runs, and that React may fall back to a boundary you did not expect under a slow network or busy CPU. So make the hero headline pure static shell — no data, no Suspense above it — and let everything cinematic stream in behind it. Scroll-linked animation is the classic INP killer: every ScrollTrigger scrub that writes layout-affecting properties (top/left/width/height) forces a reflow per frame. Animate ONLY transform, opacity and filter; use `will-change: transform` on the 2-3 pinned elements and remove it after; put long finding lists behind `content-visibility: auto` + `contain-intrinsic-size` (Chrome 85 / FF 125 / Safari 18). Split any >50ms handler with setTimeout+requestAnimationFrame as web.dev prescribes. Bytecode caching on Vercel is production-only, so a Lighthouse score taken on a preview URL will be pessimistic and misleading in both directions. | TRADEOFF: 180 KB is tight if you also want Motion (49 KB gzip for the full UMD; tree-shaken ESM is far less, and Motion's mini `animate` is claimed at 2.3 KB) plus GSAP plus ScrollTrigger. Resolve it by NOT shipping Motion on the marketing route at all — GSAP does everything there. Budget enforcement takes real time you may prefer to spend on the demo; at minimum run bundle-analyzer once before submission so you are not surprised.
  - (high) Demo-day reliability plan, in priority order: (1) production deploy on Vercel with a custom domain; (2) `next build && next start` running on the presenting laptop against a local FastAPI + seeded Postgres, on a port you have already tested; (3) a 90-second screen recording of the full 'proven bug' moment, exported as an MP4 sitting on the desktop; (4) the whole dashboard demoable with the backend OFF via seeded fixtures. Never demo from a preview URL. | WHY: Enumerated failure modes with concrete mitigations. Venue wifi dies -> item (2), which is why polling beats SSE and why every network call must have a fixture fallback. Vercel cold start -> Fluid's bytecode caching and pre-warming are production-only, so a preview URL is measurably slower; also hit the URL once 60s before you present. Anthropic API rate limit or latency during a live review -> pre-record one real run, store its findings, and have the demo replay it deterministically; do not let a live LLM call be the critical path in front of judges. WebGL fails on the venue projector/laptop (integrated GPU, driver blocklist, an external display forcing software rasterisation, or a WEBGL_context_lost event) -> always render a static AVIF poster first, upgrade to canvas only after a successful `canvas.getContext('webgl2')` AND `matchMedia('(prefers-reduced-motion: no-preference)')`, and listen for `webglcontextlost` to fall back to the poster rather than showing black. Projector colour is washed out -> ship the `[data-contrast="high"]` variant and switch to it before you walk on. Vercel payload cap -> the proof terminal output must be truncated server-side to well under 4.5 MB if it ever routes through a Next handler. | TRADEOFF: Building the offline fixture path is real work (half a day) that produces nothing a judge sees when everything works. It is still the highest-expected-value half day in the project: a failed live demo is unrecoverable, and every mitigation above also doubles as your local dev experience. The recorded MP4 costs 20 minutes and is pure insurance — it also becomes the submission video.
  - (medium) Use Cache Components deliberately, not by default: `{ cacheComponents: true, partialPrefetching: true }` and mark marketing pages `'use cache'`; leave every /app route uncached and reading live. After a suppression write, call `updateTag(`findings-${reviewId}`)` in the Server Action (read-your-writes) rather than `revalidateTag`. | WHY: Next 16 made everything uncached by default, so a naively-built dashboard is already correct; the value of cacheComponents here is the marketing side — a prerendered static shell served from the edge is what gets you a sub-1s LCP with a heavy animated hero. `revalidateTag(tag)` with one argument is deprecated in 16 and now needs a cacheLife profile; `updateTag()` is the Server-Actions-only API with read-your-writes semantics, which is exactly the guarantee the suppression ledger needs — the user dismisses a finding and must see the ledger row on the next paint, not eventually. Pair it with a TanStack Query optimistic update that rolls back on failure, per Next's own coordinate-mutations table. | TRADEOFF: cacheComponents is a build-time-strict mode: it will FAIL your build with a blocking-route error if a route reads dynamic data with no Suspense boundary above it. That is a feature two days before a deadline and a landmine two hours before. Turn it on early, on day one, or not at all. `partialPrefetching` is new in 16.3 and will become default in a future major, so adopting now is directionally right but is the newest surface in the stack.
  - (medium) Toolchain: pnpm 12.5.1, Node 22.18+ (hey-api's floor, above Next's 20.9), TypeScript 7.0.2 for `next build` typechecking, Biome 2.5.14 instead of ESLint, Playwright 1.63 with the new `instant()` helper for one smoke test of the demo path. | WHY: `next lint` was REMOVED in 16 and `next build` no longer lints, so you must pick a linter deliberately; Biome is one binary, one config, no plugin resolution, and is dramatically faster than ESLint 10 flat config for a 5-day project. TypeScript 7 is the native 10x port and Next 16.3 explicitly supports it for build-time typechecking — a real dev-loop win on a typed OpenAPI client with hundreds of generated types. One Playwright test that asserts the demo path (open dashboard -> open the seeded proven finding -> proof terminal visible) is the cheapest guard against a last-minute refactor breaking exactly the thing you will show. | TRADEOFF: TypeScript 7 is ~2 months old at time of writing; if a dependency's .d.ts trips it, fall back to typescript@5.x with one line. Biome does not have the Next-specific rules that @next/eslint-plugin-next provides (image usage, script usage) — acceptable, but it means no automated nudge about next/image misuse. Node 22.18+ is only required by hey-api; if you take the openapi-typescript fallback, Node 20.9 suffices.
ANTI-PATTERNS / AVOID:
  - Framer or Webflow for the marketing site. cubic.dev and codeant.ai both fingerprint as Framer, and it is instantly visible in devtools (framerusercontent asset host, generic class soup). In a category where Linear, Vercel, Cursor, Graphite, Greptile and CodeRabbit all hand-build in Next.js, a Framer site places you in the bottom tier before a judge reads a word.
  - Copy-pasting any Tailwind snippet written before 2025 without running it through the v4 rename table. The silent killers are the default border colour becoming currentColor (every bordered card suddenly outlined in your text colour), `ring` dropping from 3px to 1px, the entire shadow/blur/rounded scale shifting one step, and `!important` moving from prefix to suffix. Your page will not error; it will just look subtly cheap.
  - Pure CSS scroll-driven animation (`animation-timeline: view()`) as the only mechanism. MDN BCD 8.1.2 has Firefox at 'preview'. Your reveals will not fire in Firefox and the elements will sit at their 0% keyframe — usually opacity:0, i.e. an invisible page. If a judge opens Firefox you lose the whole site.
  - A full react-three-fiber + drei scene on the marketing hero. three.core is 104 KB gzip before your shader, R3F unpacks to 2.09 MB and drei to 1.67 MB. It will not render on some venue projectors (integrated GPU / driver blocklist / external-display software rasterisation), it will tank LCP and INP, and the effect is almost never better than layered CSS gradients + an animated SVG. If you must use it, one canvas, dynamically imported, ssr:false, with a poster.
  - Suspense boundaries sprinkled everywhere 'for streaming'. Next's own docs warn that React may fall back to ANY boundary under a slow network or busy CPU, and that an LCP element inside a boundary cannot paint until the swap script runs. A boundary above your hero headline is a self-inflicted LCP regression.
  - Animating top/left/width/height/margin in a ScrollTrigger scrub. Every frame forces layout, TBT explodes, INP goes past 200ms, and on a mid-range laptop the scroll visibly stutters — which is the single most expensive-looking failure a 'cinematic' site can have. Transform, opacity and filter only.
  - `prefers-reduced-motion` implemented as `* { animation: none !important; transition: none !important; }`. This is worse than doing nothing: any element whose visible state is produced BY the animation stays at its initial state, so reduced-motion users get a blank page. Every animation needs a designed reduced end-state.
  - `outline: none` or `focus:outline-none` on a dark UI. It fails WCAG 2.4.13 outright and makes the dashboard unnavigable by keyboard on a projector. Also note Tailwind v4 renamed the class to `outline-hidden`, so pasted v3 code will behave differently than the author intended.
  - Information encoded only in dark-mode elevation. A bg-0/bg-1/bg-2 ramp of #0A0A0A / #111316 / #191C21 measures 1.00 / 1.06 / 1.16 relative contrast and collapses to one flat black on a washed-out venue projector. Structure must come from >=3:1 borders, not from surface lightness.
  - Meaningful 1px borders at the fashionable oklch(0.30-0.35) hairline value. I measured #303339 at 1.56:1 against #0A0A0A — well under the 3:1 that WCAG 1.4.11 requires for UI component boundaries. Decorative dividers can stay there; input outlines and selected-row indicators cannot.
  - SSE for the live run status at a hackathon. One wifi blip kills the EventSource with no recovery UI, and Next explicitly documents nginx, CDN, gzip and Safari's 1024-byte buffer as things that will lump your stream. Polling is less impressive on a whiteboard and dramatically more impressive on stage.
  - A live LLM call on the demo critical path. An Anthropic rate limit or a slow stage turns your signature 'we PROVE it' moment into a spinner. Pre-record one real run, store it, replay it deterministically, and be honest in the README that the live path exists.
  - Splitting marketing and dashboard into Multi-Zones or two deployed apps. Cross-zone links must be plain <a>, meaning the one click your whole narrative depends on (hero CTA -> live dashboard) becomes a hard reload with no prefetch, and you maintain two copies of the token layer.
  - Demoing from a Vercel preview URL. Bytecode caching and function pre-warming are documented as production-only, so preview is the slowest environment you own — and it is also the one whose URL changes on every push.
  - Six typefaces and a display face loaded in the root layout. next/font preloads root-layout fonts on EVERY route, so one hero-only display face taxes the whole dashboard's LCP. Declare it in the marketing layout, not the root.
  - Number count-up animations on the accuracy self-report. A metric that spins up from 0 while a judge is reading it is decoration on top of the one surface that must read as rigorous. Show the number, show its n, show its caveat, statically.
  - Glassmorphism panels relying on `prefers-reduced-transparency` for the accessible fallback. Safari does not support that media query at all (BCD: false).
  - Shipping shadcn with `init --base base-ui`. Base UI's latest npm release is 1.0.0-rc.0 from 2025-12-04. Radix (@radix-ui/react-dialog@1.1.23, 2026-07-24) is the boring, correct choice for a 5-day build.
FACT-CHECK CORRECTIONS:
    [UNVERIFIABLE] `shadcn init` adds `@import "shadcn/tailwind.css"` (flagged by researcher as from a search snippet only) -> The CLI v4 changelog page makes no mention of `shadcn/tailwind.css` or any automatic CSS import line. No supporting source found. The researcher was right to flag it — treat as unsupported and verify by running `shadcn init --dry-run` before relying on it.
    [CORRECTED] @base-ui-components/react latest is 1.0.0-rc.0 published 2025-12-04; suspicion that a scoped rename now carries the stable release -> The suspicion was exactly right and this is the single most consequential error in the set. The npm metadata for @base-ui-components/react@1.0.0-rc.0 carries a deprecation string reading literally 'Package was renamed to @base-ui/react'. The live package is **@base-ui/react**, stable since 1.0.0 (11 Dec 2025), currently **1.8.0 published 2026-09-04**, MIT. Following the researcher's version as written would install a deprecated 9-month-old RC. Since the recommendation also proposes `shadcn init --base base-ui`, install and pin @base-ui/react.
    [CORRECTED] @hey-api/openapi-ts engines node>=22.18.0 and license MIT; GitHub LICENSE.md 404'd and the repo 301-redirects — confirm the licence at the repo -> npm metadata confirmed exactly: @hey-api/openapi-ts@0.99.0, published 2026-06-22T06:38:34Z, license MIT, engines node>=22.18.0. The redirect mystery is solved: github.com/hey-api/openapi-ts issues a 301 to **github.com/hey-api/hey-api** — the repo was renamed, which is why the LICENSE.md path 404'd. I could not read a LICENSE file at the new repo root either (404 on /blob/main/LICENSE.md), so the repo-side licence text is still UNVERIFIED; only the npm `license: MIT` field is confirmed. Separately: Orval, offered as the zero-risk third option, declares the SAME engines node>=22.18.0 (orval@8.36.0, 2026-09-21, MIT) — it does not relieve the Node 22 floor.
    [CORRECTED] Motion's '2.3 kb mini animate' and 'framer-motion-3d removed in v13' came from search snippets -> Split verdict. (a) The 2.3 kb figure is CONFIRMED from motion.dev's own animate docs: the mini version is '2.3kb' and the hybrid is '18kb'; the `motion/mini` entry point genuinely exists in the published package (dist/es/mini.mjs, dist/cjs/mini.js). (b) 'framer-motion-3d removed in v13' is REFUTED. The upgrade guide states Motion 13.0's only breaking change is removing @emotion/is-prop-valid as an optional dependency 'in favour of explicit injection'. framer-motion-3d was split into its own package back at **v6**; on npm it last published 12.4.13 (2025-03-11) and now carries the deprecation 'Package no longer supported.' It never had a v13 to be removed in. Materially: Motion 13 instead ships `motion/three` and `motion/vgpu` entry points, which is the current 3D path.
    [CORRECTED] web.dev/articles/vitals last updated 2024-10-31, so 'thresholds unchanged in 2026' rests partly on secondary sources; soft-navigations Chrome 151 / origin trial ~Chrome 147 from search snippets -> Thresholds CONFIRMED directly from the live page, no secondary source needed: LCP ≤2.5s, INP ≤200ms, CLS ≤0.1 at p75, and the page itself lists all three as lifecycle status 'Stable' (LCP: Stable, CLS: Stable, INP: Stable) with INP noted as stable since 2024. Last updated is indeed 2024-10-31 — but an unchanged page that still asserts the thresholds is first-party evidence they are unchanged. The soft-navigations details (Chrome 151 in Aug 2026, origin trial ~Chrome 147) remain UNVERIFIED — no developer.chrome.com post was read, and nothing in the recommendations depends on them.
    [CORRECTED] `scheduler.yield()` is the modern yielding API for INP — confirm availability before relying on it -> It exists but is NOT safe to rely on unconditionally. MDN marks it 'Not Baseline / Limited availability — this feature does not work in some of the most widely-used browsers.' BCD 8.1.2 gives Chrome 129, Edge 129, Firefox 142, **Safari: false (no support)**. So scheduler.yield covers Chromium and Firefox but not Safari at all. The web.dev optimize-inp guidance the researcher actually fetched (setTimeout + requestAnimationFrame) is the portable path. Correct pattern: feature-detect and fall back — `'scheduler' in globalThis && 'yield' in scheduler ? scheduler.yield() : new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, 0))`.
    [CORRECTED] WCAG SC 2.4.11 Focus Not Obscured — Understanding page not read; do not quote its level or wording -> Now verified, and it matters more than the researcher assumed. SC 2.4.11 is **Focus Not Obscured (Minimum), Level AA** — not AAA, so it is in scope for any normal AA target: 'When a user interface component receives keyboard focus, the component is not entirely hidden due to author-created content.' This is directly load-bearing for the proposed dashboard: a sticky stage header or a sticky table header that covers a j/k-focused row violates an AA criterion. Separately, SC 2.4.13 Focus Appearance is confirmed **AAA** with the exact wording the researcher cited — 'at least as large as the area of a 2 CSS pixel thick perimeter of the unfocused component' and 'a contrast ratio of at least 3:1 between the same pixels in the focused and unfocused states'.
    [CORRECTED] pnpm@12.5.1 and eslint@10.11.0 as current majors are from the npm registry only; release notes not verified -> Versions CONFIRMED: pnpm@12.5.1 (2026-09-18T21:39:59Z, MIT) and eslint@10.11.0 (2026-09-18T20:15:36Z, MIT). Release notes still unverified. But the registry surfaced a fact that contradicts a recommendation elsewhere: **eslint@10.11.0 declares engines `^20.19.0 || ^22.13.0 || >=24`**. Combined with shadcn's node>=20.18.1 and @tanstack/react-table v9's node>=20, the claim in the toolchain tradeoffs that 'if you take the openapi-typescript fallback, Node 20.9 suffices' is wrong — the real floor is Node 20.19+ even on the fallback path, and Node 22.18+ with either hey-api or Orval.
    [CORRECTED] Next.js Streaming guide specifics: pass promises down, keep LCP outside Suspense, skeleton dimensions/CLS, Suspense boundary = hydration unit, nginx X-Accel-Buffering: no, Safari 1024-byte buffer, status code locked after first chunk, route-handler SSE example -> Seven of eight confirmed verbatim (page version 16.3.6, lastUpdated 2026-08-25): 'Push dynamic access down'; 'Keep LCP elements outside or above Suspense boundaries'; 'Design skeleton fallbacks that match the dimensions of the content'; 'Each <Suspense> boundary is a hydration unit'; the X-Accel-Buffering: no next.config snippet; 'Safari/WebKit buffers streaming responses until 1024 bytes have been received'; 'You cannot change the status code or headers after streaming starts.' The eighth is slightly mis-described: the Route Handler example uses ReadableStream with `'Content-Type': 'text/plain; charset=utf-8'` and `'X-Content-Type-Options': 'nosniff'` — it is a generic chunked-streaming example, NOT an SSE (text/event-stream) example. The doc also adds two buffering hazards the writeup omits: gzip/Brotli compression can buffer chunks before flushing, and AWS Lambda needs response-streaming mode explicitly enabled (Vercel streams natively).
    [CORRECTED] MDN BCD table as cited: animation-timeline group, timeline-scope 116, view-transition-name/startViewTransition Ch111/FF144/Saf18, @view-transition Ch126/Saf18.2/FF=false, @starting-style Ch117/FF129/Saf17.5, @property Ch85/FF128/Saf16.4, content-visibility Ch85/FF125/Saf18, color-mix Ch111/FF113/Saf16.2, oklch Ch111/FF113/Saf15.4, light-dark Ch123/FF120/Saf17.5, @scope Ch118/FF146/Saf26.4, prefers-reduced-motion Ch74/FF63/Saf10.1, prefers-reduced-transparency Ch118/FF113/Saf=false, LoAF Ch123 only, WebGL2 Ch56/FF51/Saf15 -> I parsed BCD 8.1.2's data.json directly and 14 of 15 rows match exactly. The one correction: **prefers-reduced-transparency in Firefox 113 is behind a flag**, not shipped (BCD marks the Firefox entry with `flags`). Safari is indeed false. This makes the researcher's conclusion — do not rely on it for glass panels — stronger, not weaker. Everything else reproduces: @scope Ch118/FF146/Saf26.4, LoAF Chrome 123 with Firefox and Safari both false, WebGL2 Ch56/FF51/Saf15, and the rest. One useful addition: `contain-intrinsic-size` is Ch83/FF107/Saf17, EARLIER than content-visibility — so for the 'content-visibility: auto + contain-intrinsic-size' pairing the binding constraint is content-visibility's Safari 18, exactly as quoted.
    [CORRECTED] MDN aria-live reference: role=status implies polite+atomic, role=log implies polite, role=alert implies assertive+atomic, the live region must exist in the DOM before content changes, aria-busy during batch updates -> The two load-bearing claims are confirmed on the MDN aria-live page: the live region must pre-exist ('The aria-live attribute is set on an EMPTY element. When an update to the page occurs, the empty element with that aria-live attribute should be updated...'), and 'Use aria-busy to prevent announcements while updates are still being made.' However the implicit role→attribute mappings (status = polite + atomic, log = polite, alert = assertive + atomic) are NOT stated on the aria-live attribute page — they come from the WAI-ARIA role definitions. The mappings are correct per the ARIA spec, but the citation is mis-attributed; cite the individual role pages instead.
    [CORRECTED] Tailwind v4.2 logical-property utilities are pbs-*/mbe-*/inset-bs-*, and @tailwindcss/webpack is 2.17x faster than PostCSS (429ms vs 932ms) -> The webpack figure and the rest of the v4.2/v4.3 feature list are confirmed (2.17× faster, scrollbar-*, @container-size with cqb/cqh, zoom-*, tab-*, stacked/compound @variant, --default() in @utility, and the mauve/olive/mist/taupe palettes). But the logical-property utility names are wrong: the post lists `pbs-*`, `mbs-*`, `inline-*` and `block-*` — not `mbe-*` or `inset-bs-*`. Minor, but it would send someone hunting for class names that may not exist.
VERIFIER ADDITIONS:
    + BIGGEST ACTIONABLE ERROR — Base UI package name. `@base-ui-components/react` is deprecated on npm with the literal message 'Package was renamed to @base-ui/react'. The live package is @base-ui/react, stable since 1.0.0 (11 Dec 2025), now 1.8.0 (2026-09-04). The researcher's own suspicion was correct. Anything that says `init --base base-ui` must pull @base-ui/react.
    + NODE VERSION FLOOR IS UNDERSTATED. The writeup says Node 22.18+ is 'only required by hey-api' and that 'Node 20.9 suffices' on the openapi-typescript fallback. Registry engines say otherwise: eslint@10.11.0 requires ^20.19.0 || ^22.13.0 || >=24; shadcn@4.21.0 requires >=20.18.1; @tanstack/react-table@9.2.4 requires >=20; playwright@1.63.0 requires >=20; orval@8.36.0 requires >=22.18.0 (same floor as hey-api, so it is NOT an escape hatch). Real floor: Node 20.19+ minimum, 22.18+ for either codegen tool. Just standardise on Node 22 LTS and delete the caveat.
    + SC 2.4.11 Focus Not Obscured (Minimum) is Level AA, not AAA — and the proposed dashboard is a likely violator. A sticky stage header or sticky findings-table header that covers a j/k-focused row breaks an AA criterion. Fix with `scroll-margin-top` equal to the sticky header height on focusable rows, and verify by tabbing to the last row above the fold. This deserves to be in the accessibility recommendation, which currently only covers live regions and focus rings.
    + scheduler.yield has NO Safari support at all (BCD: Chrome/Edge 129, Firefox 142, Safari false; MDN labels it 'Limited availability, not Baseline'). The perf recommendation gestures at it as 'the modern yielding API'. Ship it feature-detected with a setTimeout fallback, or just use the setTimeout + rAF pattern the fetched web.dev page actually prescribes.
    + framer-motion-3d is a dead end, but Motion 13 has a live replacement the writeup misses. framer-motion-3d last published 12.4.13 (Mar 2025) and is npm-deprecated 'Package no longer supported.' Motion 13.4.1 ships `motion/three` AND `motion/vgpu` entry points (dist/es/three.mjs, dist/es/vgpu.mjs). Given the owner's stated 3D preference and the recommendation to keep Motion in the dashboard only, motion/three is worth evaluating against raw three.js for the one signature WebGL surface — it may collapse two mental models into one.
    + Firefox is currently at 156 and caniuse puts animation-timeline at Firefox 159 — i.e. still unreleased. This is a STRONGER argument for GSAP ScrollTrigger than the writeup makes. Not 'Firefox lags'; Firefox has no stable ship date for CSS scroll-driven animation at all. Worth one confident sentence rather than a hedge.
    + The Next.js streaming doc's route-handler example is text/plain chunked streaming, not SSE (text/event-stream). If SSE is ever added per the fallback plan, the Content-Type, the `data: ...\n\n` framing and a `Cache-Control: no-cache, no-transform` header all still have to be written by hand. The doc also flags two buffering hazards the writeup omits: gzip/Brotli compression can buffer chunks before flushing, and AWS Lambda needs response-streaming mode explicitly enabled (Vercel streams natively). The compression point is the one most likely to bite, since it applies on Vercel too.
    + Vercel Hobby is explicitly non-commercial ('the Hobby plan restricts users to non-commercial, personal use only') — the researcher flagged this and it is confirmed verbatim. For a hackathon that is fine; the moment PRGuard takes a paying user or a waitlist tied to revenue, it needs Pro at $20/seat/month. Also note Pro's memory DEFAULT is 2 GB / 1 vCPU; the 4 GB / 2 vCPU figure quoted is the maximum and must be raised deliberately in project settings.
    + Do not quote coderabbit.ai's deployment token. It has already rotated since capture (now dpl_Bq2CPgq9udmZV6VD5qW6gK7426dU, was dpl_DeBqjQiVepsPVJM67mfeENhsxbDZ). The durable finding is the mechanism — Next.js deploymentId skew protection appending ?dpl= to every asset — which is itself worth stealing, since the self-hosting guide's version-skew note says skew triggers a hard reload and loses useState. For a dashboard with in-flight polling state, that is a real failure mode during a demo-day redeploy.
    + The OKLCH gamut worry resolves in the researcher's favour but with one hex correction: accent oklch(0.72 0.17 258) is out of sRGB and browsers chroma-reduce it to roughly #68A5FF (7.93:1), not the naive-clip #5CA4FF (7.74:1). critical-red maps to #FF525B vs #FF515A. All thresholds still pass with margin, so no token needs changing — but the hex comment column should carry the mapped values or teammates will see a mismatch in devtools.
    + Unverifiable items to carry forward as risks rather than facts: TanStack Table v8→v9 API deltas (v9 is real and is `latest`, but no migration guide is published at any URL I could find — this is the single riskiest pin in the data layer since the findings table depends on it); lucide-react v1.0.0 breaking changes (v1.0.0 real, published 2026-03-23, licence ISC not MIT, but changelog and migration pages both 404); the `@import "shadcn/tailwind.css"` claim (absent from the CLI v4 changelog); the hey-api repo-side LICENSE text (repo renamed to github.com/hey-api/hey-api, LICENSE.md still 404s — only npm's `license: MIT` field is confirmed); Turborepo's 2026 feature set; pnpm 12 and ESLint 10 release notes; and the competitor CSS byte totals plus the 'exactly one oklch()' claim, which I did not re-derive.
